| `FC08-C03-U047` | **Definition/convention — objects of C** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2129 | A category C consists of the following things: a class Ob.C/, whose elements are called objects of C , a class Hom.C/, whose elements are called morphisms of C , for each morphism f ∈ Hom ( C ) , two objects X; Y ∈ Ob ( C ) called the source and target of f , respectively, for each triple X , Y , Z ∈ Ob ( C ) , a mapping called composition: Hom _ℂ (X, Y) × Hom _ℂ (Y, Z) → Hom _ℂ (X, Z), written ( f , g ) ↦ g ∘ f , where Hom _C ( X , Y ) denotes the class of all morphisms with source X and target Y . The morphisms are required to satisfy the following axioms: (i) ASSOCIATIVITY: .f g/ h f .g h/. (ii) EXISTENCE OF IDENTITIES: For each object X ∈ Ob ( mathsf C ) , there exists an identity morphism I d _X ∈ Hom _ℂ ( X , X ) , such that Id Y ∘ f = f = f ∘ I d _X for all f ∈ Hom _C ( X , Y ) . | — |
| `FC08-C03-U048` | **Definition/convention — isomorphism in C** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2151 | A morphism f ∈ Hom _C ( X , Y ) is called an isomorphism in C if there exists a morphism g ∈ Hom _C ( Y , X ) such that f ∘ g = I d _Y and g ∘ f = I d _X | — |
| `FC08-C03-U049` | **Example 3.26** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2153 | (Categories). In most of the categories that one meets “in nature,” the objects are sets with some extra structure, the morphisms are maps that preserve that structure, and the composition laws and identity morphisms are the obvious ones. Some of the categories of this type that appear in this book (implicitly or explicitly) are listed below. In each case, we describe the category by giving its objects and its morphisms. | — |
| `FC08-C03-U050` | **Definition/convention — pointed set and pointed map** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2179 | There are also important categories whose objects are sets with distinguished base points, in addition to (possibly) other structures. A pointed set is an ordered pair .X; p/, where X is a set and p is an element of X. Other pointed objects such as pointed topological spaces or pointed smooth manifolds are defined similarly. If ( X , p ) and ( X ^prime , p ^prime ) are pointed sets (or topological spaces, etc.), a map F : X → X 0 is said to be a pointed map if F ( p ) = p ^prime ; in this case, we write F : ( X , p ) → ( X ^prime , p ^prime ) . Here are some important examples of categories of pointed objects. | — |
| `FC08-C03-U051` | **Definition/convention — small and locally small categories** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2189 | We use the word class instead of s e t for the collections of objects and morphisms in a category because in some categories they are “too large” to be considered sets. For example, in the category Set, Ob.Set/ is the class of all sets; any attempt to treat it as a set in its own right leads to the well-known Russell paradox of set theory. (See [LeeTM, Appendix A] or almost any book on set theory for more.) Even though the classes of objects and morphisms might not constitute sets, we still use notations such as X ∈ Ob ( mathsf C ) and f ∈ Hom ( C ) to indicate that X is an object and f is a morphism in C. A category in which both Ob.C/ and Hom.C/ are sets is called a small category, and one in which each class of morphisms Hom _C ( X , Y ) is a set is called locally small. All the categories listed above are locally small but not small. | — |
| `FC08-C03-U052` | **Definition/convention — covariant functor** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2191 | If C and D are categories, a covariant functor from C to D is a rule F that assigns to each object X ∈ Ob ( mathsf C ) an object F ( X ) ∈ Ob ( D ) , and to each morphism f ∈ Hom _C ( X , Y ) a morphism F ( f ) ∈ Hom _D ( F ( X ) , F ( Y ) ) /, so that identities and composition are preserved: F (Id _X ) = Id _F (X); F (g ∘ h) = F (g) ∘ F (h). | — |
| `FC08-C03-U053` | **Definition/convention — contravariant functor** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2197 | We also need to consider functors that reverse morphisms: a contravariant functor from C to D is a rule F that assigns to each object X ∈ Ob ( mathsf C ) an object F ( X ) ∈ Ob ( D ) , and to each morphism f ∈ Hom _C ( X , Y ) a morphism F ( f ) ∈ Hom _D ( mathcal F ( Y ) , mathcal F ( X ) ) , such that F (Id _X) = Id _F (X); F (g ∘ h) = F (h) ∘ F (g). | — |
| `FC08-C03-U054` | **Definition/convention — covariant functor** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2205 | One trivial example of a covariant functor is the identity functor from any category to itself: it takes each object and each morphism to itself. Another example is the forgetful functor: if C is a category whose objects are sets with some additional structure and whose morphisms are maps preserving that structure (as are all the categories listed in the first part of Example 3.26 except Set itself), the forgetful functor F : C → Set assigns to each object its underlying set, and to each morphism the same map thought of as a map between sets. | `FC08-C03-U049` |
| `FC08-C03-U055` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2207 | More interesting functors arise when we associate “invariants” to classes of mathematical objects. For example, the fundamental group is a covariant functor from T o p _* to Grp. The results of Problem 2-10 show that there is a contravariant functor from Diff to VecR defined by assigning to each smooth manifold M the vector space C ^∞ ( M ) , and to each smooth map F : M → N the linear map F * : C ^∞ ( N ) → C ^∞ ( M ) defined by F * ( f ) = f ∘ F | — |
| `FC08-C03-U056` | **Definition/convention — functor** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2209 | The discussion in this chapter has given us some other important examples of functors. First, the tangent space functor is a covariant functor from the category Diff of pointed smooth manifolds to the category VecR of real vector spaces. To each pointed smooth manifold ( M , p ) it assigns the tangent space T _p M _ℓ ; and to each pointed smooth map F : ( M , p ) ( N , F ( p ) ) it assigns the differential d F _p The fact that this is a functor is the content of parts (b) and (c) of Proposition 3.6. | `FC08-C03-U011` |
| `FC08-C03-U057` | **Definition/convention — tangent functor** | Ch. 3, § Categories and Functors; source L2211 | Similarly, we can think of the assignments M ↦ T M and F ↦ d F (sending each smooth manifold to its tangent bundle and each smooth map to its global differential) as a covariant functor from Diff to itself, called the tangent functor. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U001` | **Definition/convention — rank of F at p to be the rank of the linear map d F** | Ch. 4, § Maps of Constant Rank; source L2243 | Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary. Given a smooth map F M N and a point p ∈ M , ; we define the rank of F at p to be the rank of the linear map d F _p : T _p M → T _F ( p ) N ; it is the rank of the Jacobian matrix of F in any smooth chart, or the dimension of Im d F _p ⊆ T _F ( p ) N . I f F has the same rank r at every point, we say that it has constant rank, and write rank F = r . | — |
| `FC08-C04-U002` | **Definition/convention — linear map** | Ch. 4, § Maps of Constant Rank; source L2245 | Because the rank of a linear map is never higher than the dimension of either its domain or its codomain (Exercise B.22), the rank of F at each point is bounded above by the minimum of dim M; dim N . If the rank of d F _p is equal to this upper bound, we say that F has full rank at p, and if F has full rank everywhere, we say F has full rank. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U003` | **Definition/convention — smooth submersion** | Ch. 4, § Maps of Constant Rank; source L2247 | The most important constant-rank maps are those of full rank. A smooth map F : M → N is called a smooth submersion if its differential is surjective at each point (or equivalently, if rank F = dim N ) . It is called a smooth immersion if its differential is injective at each point (equivalently, rank F = dim M ) . | — |
| `FC08-C04-U004` | **Proposition 4.1** | Ch. 4, § Maps of Constant Rank; source L2249 | Suppose F : M → N is a smooth map and p ∈ M If d F _p is surjective, then p has a neighborhood U such that F ∣ _U is a submersion. I f d F _p i s injective, then p has a neighborhood U such that F ∣ _U is an immersion. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U005` | **Example 4.2** | Ch. 4, § Maps of Constant Rank; source L2255 | (Submersions and Immersions). (a) Suppose M _1 , … , M _k are smooth manifolds. In particular, the projection π : ℝ ^n + k ℝ ^n onto the first n coordinates is a smooth submersion. (b) If γ : J → M is a smooth curve in a smooth manifold M with or without bound- a r y , then is a smooth immersion if and only if γ ^prime ( t ) ≠ 0 for all t ∈ J (c) If M is a smooth manifold and its tangent bundle TM is given the smooth manifold structure described in Proposition 3.18, the projection π T M → M is a smooth submersion. To verify this, just note that with respect to any smooth local coordinates ( x ^i ) on an open subset U ⊆ M and the corresponding natural coordinates ( x ^i , v ^i ) on π ^- 1 ( U ) ⊆ T M (see Proposition 3.18), the coordinate representation of π is hat(π) ( x , v ) = x (d) The smooth map X : ℝ ^2 → ℝ ^3 given by X (u, v) = ((2 + cos 2 π u) cos 2 π v, (2 + cos 2 π u) sin 2 π v, sin 2 π u ) | `FC08-C03-U031` |
| `FC08-C04-U006` | **Definition/convention — local diffeomorphism** | Ch. 4, § Local Diffeomorphisms; source L2278 | If M and N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, a map F : M → N is called a local diffeomorphism if every point p ∈ M has a neighborhood U such that F .U / is open in N and F ∣ _U : U → F ( U ) is a diffeomorphism. The next theorem is the key to the most important properties of local diffeomorphisms. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U007` | **Theorem 4.5** | Ch. 4, § Local Diffeomorphisms; source L2280 | (Inverse Function Theorem for Manifolds). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds, and F : M → N is a smooth map. If p ∈ M is a point such that d F _p is invertible, then there are connected neighborhoods U 0 of p and V 0 of F ( p ) such that F ∣ _ U _0 : U _0 → V _0 is a diffeomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U008` | **Proposition 4.6** | Ch. 4, § Local Diffeomorphisms; source L2286 | (Elementary Properties of Local Diffeomorphisms). (a) Every composition of local diffeomorphisms is a local diffeomorphism. (b) Every finite product of local diffeomorphisms between smooth manifolds is a local diffeomorphism. (c) Every local diffeomorphism is a local homeomorphism and an open map. (d) The restriction of a local diffeomorphism to an open submanifold with or without boundary is a local diffeomorphism. (e) Every diffeomorphism is a local diffeomorphism. (f) Every bijective local diffeomorphism is a diffeomorphism. (g) A map between smooth manifolds with or without boundary is a local diffeomorphism if and only if in a neighborhood of each point of its domain, it has a coordinate representation that is a local diffeomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U009` | **Proposition 4.8** | Ch. 4, § Local Diffeomorphisms; source L2304 | Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds (without boundary), and F : M → N is a map. (a) F is a local diffeomorphism if and only if it is both a smooth immersion and a smooth submersion. (b) If dim M dim N and F is either a smooth immersion or a smooth submersion, then it is a local diffeomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U010` | **Example 4.11** | Ch. 4, § Local Diffeomorphisms; source L2322 | (Local Diffeomorphisms). The map ε : ℝ → mathbb S ^1 defined in Example 2.13(b) is a local diffeomorphism because in a neighborhood of each point it has a coordinate representation of the form t ↦ 2 π t + c , which is a local diffeomorphism. Similarly, the map ε ^n : ℝ ^n → mathbb T ^n defined in Example 2.13(c) is a local diffeomorphism because it is a product of local diffeomorphisms. // | `FC08-C02-U015` |
| `FC08-C04-U011` | **Theorem 4.12** | Ch. 4, § The Rank Theorem; source L2330 | (Rank Theorem). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds of dimensions m and n, respectively, and F : M → N is a smooth map with constant rank r. For each p ∈ M there exist smooth charts ( U , φ ) for M centered at p and ( V , ψ ) for N centered at F ( p ) such that F ( U ) ⊆ V in which F has a coordinate representation of the form hat(F) (x ^1, … , x ^r, x ^r + 1, … , x ^m ) = (x ^1, … , x ^r, 0, … , 0 ).tag4.1 In particular, if F is a smooth submersion, this becomes hat(F) (x ^1, … , x ^n, x ^n + 1, … , x ^m ) = (x ^1, … , x ^n ),tag4.2 and if F is a smooth immersion, it is hat(F) (x ^1, … , x ^m ) = (x ^1, … , x ^m, 0, … , 0 ).tag4.3 | — |
| `FC08-C04-U012` | **Convention/notation — Kronecker delta** | Ch. 4, § The Rank Theorem; source L2356 | where we have used the following standard notation: for positive integers i and j , the symbol delta _j ^i , called the Kronecker delta, is defined by delta_j ^i = beginarrayl l 1 & text if i = j , 0 & text if i≠ j . endarray .tag4.4 | — |
| `FC08-C04-U013` | **Corollary 4.13** | Ch. 4, § The Rank Theorem; source L2408 | Let M and N be smooth manifolds, let F M N be a smooth map, and suppose M is connected. Then the following are equivalent: (a) For each p ∈ M there exist smooth charts containing p and F(p) in which the coordinate representation of F is linear. (b) F has constant rank. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U014` | **Theorem 4.14** | Ch. 4, § The Rank Theorem; source L2419 | (Global Rank Theorem). Let M and N be smooth manifolds, and suppose F M N is a smooth map of constant rank. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U015` | **Theorem 4.15** | Ch. 4, § The Rank Theorem for Manifolds with Boundary; source L2434 | (Local Immersion Theorem for Manifolds with Boundary). Suppose M is a smooth m-manifold with boundary, N is a smooth n-manifold, and F : M → N is a smooth immersion. For any p ∈ ∂ M ; there exist a smooth boundary chart ( U , φ ) for M centered at p and a smooth coordinate chart ( V , ψ ) for N centered at F ( p ) with F ( U ) ⊆ V , in which F has the coordinate representation hat(F) (x ^1, … , x ^m ) = (x ^1, … , x ^m, 0, … , 0 ).tag4.7 | — |
| `FC08-C04-U016` | **Definition/convention — smooth embedding of M into N** | Ch. 4, § Embeddings; source L2458 | One special kind of immersion is particularly important. If M and N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, a smooth embedding of M into N is a smooth immersion F : M → N that is also a topological embedding, i.e., a homeomorphism onto its image F ( M ) ⊆ N in the subspace topology. A smooth embedding is a map that is both a topological embedding and a smooth immersion, not just a topological embedding that happens to be smooth. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U017` | **Example 4.17** | Ch. 4, § Embeddings; source L2462 | (Smooth Embeddings). (a) If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary and U ⊆ M is an open submanifold, the inclusion map U ↪ M is a smooth embedding. (b) If M _1 , … , M _k are smooth manifolds and p _i ∈ M _i are arbitrarily chosen points, each of the maps iota _j : M _j → M _1 × ·s × M _k given by iota_j (q) = (p _1, … , p _j - 1, q, p _j + 1, … , p _k) is a smooth embedding. In particular, the inclusion map ℝ ^n ↪ ℝ ^n + k given by sending ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) to ( x ^1 , … , x ^n , 0 , … , 0 ) is a smooth embedding. (c) Problem 4-12 shows that the map X : ℝ ^2 → ^ ^prime ℝ ^3 of Example 4.2(d) descends to a smooth embedding of the torus mathbb S ^1 × mathbb S ^1 into ℝ ^3 | `FC08-C04-U005` |
| `FC08-C04-U018` | **Example 4.18** | Ch. 4, § Embeddings; source L2478 | (A Smooth Topological Embedding). The map γ : ℝ → ℝ ^2 given by γ ( t ) = ( t ^3 , 0 ) is a smooth map and a topological embedding, but it is not a smooth embedding because γ ^prime ( 0 ) = 0 // | — |
| `FC08-C04-U019` | **Example 4.19** | Ch. 4, § Embeddings; source L2483 | (The Figure-Eight Curve). Consider the curve β : ( - π , π ) → ℝ ^2 defined by β (t) = (sin 2 t, sin t). Its image is a set that looks like a figure-eight in the plane ( F i g . ~ 4 . 3 ) ,sometimes called a lemniscate. (It is the locus of points ( x , y ) where x ^2 = 4 y ^2 ( 1 - y ^2 ) , as you can check.) It is easy to see that β is an injective smooth immersion because β ^prime ( t ) never vanishes; but it is not a topological embedding, because its image is compact in the subspace topology, while its domain is not. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U020` | **Example 4.20** | Ch. 4, § Embeddings; source L2491 | (A Dense Curve on the Torus). Let mathbb T ^2 = mathbb S ^1 × mathbb S ^1 ⊆ ℂ ^2 denote the torus, and let ˛ be any irrational number. The map γ : ℝ → mathbb T ^2 given by γ (t) = (e ^2 π i t, e ^2 π i α t ) is a smooth immersion because γ ^prime ( t ) never vanishes. It follows from Dirichlet’s approximation theorem (see below) that for every ε > 0 , there are integers n; m such that ∣ α n - m ∣ < ε . But this means that γ is not a homeomorphism onto its image, because ℤ has no limit point in R. In fact, it is not hard to show that the image set γ ( ℝ ) is actually dense in overline mathbb T ^2 (see Problem 4-4). | — |
| `FC08-C04-U021` | **Lemma 4.21** | Ch. 4, § Embeddings; source L2509 | (Dirichlet’s Approximation Theorem). Given α ∈ ℝ and any positive integer N , there exist integers n; m with 1 ≤ n ≤ N such that ∣ n α - m ∣ < 1 / N . | — |
| `FC08-C04-U022` | **Proposition 4.22** | Ch. 4, § Embeddings; source L2515 | Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, and F M N is an injective smooth immersion. If any of the following holds, then F is a smooth embedding. (a) F is an open or closed map. (b) F is a proper map. (c) M is compact. (d) M has empty boundary and dim M = dim N . | — |
| `FC08-C04-U023` | **Example 4.23** | Ch. 4, § Embeddings; source L2526 | Let ι mathbb S ^n ↪ ℝ ^n + 1 be the inclusion map. We showed in Example 2.13(d) that ι is smooth by computing its coordinate representation with respect to graph coordinates. It is easy to verify in the same coordinates that its differential is injective at each point, so it is an injective smooth immersion. Because mathbb S ^n is compact, ι is a smooth embedding by Proposition 4.22. // | `FC08-C02-U015`, `FC08-C04-U022` |
| `FC08-C04-U024` | **Theorem 4.25** | Ch. 4, § Embeddings; source L2530 | (Local Embedding Theorem). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, and F M N is a smooth map. Then F is a smooth immersion if and only if every point in M has a neighborhood U ⊆ M such that F ∣ _U : U → N is a smooth embedding. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U025` | **Definition/convention — topological immersion** | Ch. 4, § Embeddings; source L2536 | Theorem 4.25 points the way to a notion of immersions that makes sense for arbitrary topological spaces: if X and Y are topological spaces, a continuous map F : X → Y is called a topological immersion if every point of X has a neighborhood U such that F ∣ _U is a topological embedding. Thus, every smooth immersion is a topological immersion; but, just as with embeddings, a topological immersion that happens to be smooth need not be a smooth immersion (cf. Example 4.18). | `FC08-C04-U024`, `FC08-C04-U018` |
| `FC08-C04-U026` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 4, § Submersions; source L2540 | One of the most important applications of the rank theorem is to vastly expand our understanding of the properties of submersions. If π W M ! N is any continuous map, a section of π is a continuous right inverse for π, i.e., a continuous map σ : N → M such that π ∘ σ = I d _N : beginarrayc M π Big downarrow N. endarray σ | — |
| `FC08-C04-U027` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 4, § Submersions; source L2546 | A local section of π is a continuous map σ : U → M defined on some open subset U ⊆ N and satisfying the analogous relation π σ = I d _U . Many of the important properties of smooth submersions follow from the fact that they admit an abundance of smooth local sections. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U028` | **Theorem 4.26** | Ch. 4, § Submersions; source L2548 | (Local Section Theorem). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds and π : M → N is a smooth map. Then π is a smooth submersion if and only if every point of M is in the image of a smooth local section of π | — |
| `FC08-C04-U029` | **Definition/convention — topological submersion** | Ch. 4, § Submersions; source L2575 | This theorem motivates the following definition: if π : X → Y is a continuous map, we say π is a topological submersion if every point of X is in the image of a (continuous) local section of π . The preceding theorem shows that every smooth submersion is a topological submersion. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U030` | **Proposition 4.28** | Ch. 4, § Submersions; source L2579 | (Properties of Smooth Submersions). Let M and N be smooth manifolds, and suppose π : M → N is a smooth submersion. Then π is an open map, and if it is surjective it is a quotient map. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U031` | **Theorem 4.29** | Ch. 4, § Submersions; source L2585 | (Characteristic Property of Surjective Smooth Submersions). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds, and π:M→N is a surjective smooth submersion. For any smooth manifold P with or without boundary, a map F:N→P is smooth if and only if F∘π is smooth. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U032` | **Theorem 4.30** | Ch. 4, § Submersions; source L2601 | (Passing Smoothly to the Quotient). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds and π : M → N is a surjective smooth submersion. If P is a smooth manifold with or without boundary and F : M → P is a smooth map that is constant on the fibers of π, then there exists a unique smooth map tilde(F) : N → bar(P) such that tilde(F) ∘ π = F | — |
| `FC08-C04-U033` | **Theorem 4.31** | Ch. 4, § Submersions; source L2609 | (Uniqueness of Smooth Quotients). Suppose that M; N _1 , and N _2 are smooth manifolds, and π _1 : M → N _1 and π _2 : M → N _2 are surjective smooth submersions that are constant on each other’s fibers. Then there exists a unique diffeomorphism F : N _1 → N _2 such that F π _1 = π _2 | — |
| `FC08-C04-U034` | **Definition/convention — smooth covering map** | Ch. 4, § Smooth Covering Maps; source L2619 | In the context of smooth manifolds, it is useful to introduce a slightly more restrictive type of covering map. If E and M are connected smooth manifolds with or without boundary, a map π : E → M is called a smooth covering map if π is smooth and surjective, and each point in M has a neighborhood U such that each component of π ^- 1 ( U ) is mapped diffeomorphically onto U by π. In this context we also say that U is evenly covered. The space M is called the base of the covering, and E is called a covering manifold of M . If E is simply connected, it is called the universal covering manifold of M . | — |
| `FC08-C04-U035` | **Proposition 4.33** | Ch. 4, § Smooth Covering Maps; source L2623 | (Properties of Smooth Coverings). (a) Every smooth covering map is a local diffeomorphism, a smooth submersion, an open map, and a quotient map. (b) An injective smooth covering map is a diffeomorphism. (c) A topological covering map is a smooth covering map if and only if it is a local diffeomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U036` | **Example 4.35** | Ch. 4, § Smooth Covering Maps; source L2633 | (Smooth Covering Maps). The map ε : ℝ → mathbb S ^1 defined in Example 2.13(b) is a topological covering map and a local diffeomorphism (see also | `FC08-C02-U015` |
| `FC08-C04-U037` | **Proposition 4.36** | Ch. 4, § Smooth Covering Maps; source L2641 | (Local Section Theorem for Smooth Covering Maps). Suppose E and M are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, and π : E → M is a smooth covering map. Given any evenly covered open subset U ⊆ M . ; any q ∈ U , and any p in the fiber of π over q _: , there exists a unique smooth local section σ : U → E such that σ ( q ) = p | — |
| `FC08-C04-U038` | **Proposition 4.40** | Ch. 4, § Smooth Covering Maps; source L2653 | (Covering Spaces of Smooth Manifolds). Suppose M is a connected smooth n-manifold, and π : E → M is a topological covering map. Then E is a topological n-manifold, and has a unique smooth structure such that π is a smooth covering map. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U039` | **Proposition 4.41** | Ch. 4, § Smooth Covering Maps; source L2676 | (Covering Spaces of Smooth Manifolds with Boundary). Suppose M is a connected smooth n-manifold with boundary, and π : E → M is a topological covering map. Then E is a topological n-manifold with boundary such that ∂ E = π ^- 1 ( ∂ M ) , and it has a unique smooth structure such that π is a smooth covering map. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U040` | **Corollary 4.43** | Ch. 4, § Smooth Covering Maps; source L2680 | (Existence of a Universal Covering Manifold). If M is a connected smooth manifold, there exists a simply connected smooth manifold M , called the universal covering manifold of M , and a smooth covering map π tilde(M) → M The universal covering manifold is unique in the following sense: if tilde(M) ^prime is any other simply connected smooth manifold that admits a smooth covering map π ^prime : tilde(M) ^prime → M; then there exists a diffeomorphism ˚ tilde(M) → tilde(M) ^prime such that π 0 ˚ π. | — |
| `FC08-C04-U041` | **Proposition 4.46** | Ch. 4, § Smooth Covering Maps; source L2688 | Suppose E and M are nonempty connected smooth manifolds with or without boundary. I f π : E → M is a proper local diffeomorphism, then π is a smooth covering map. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U001` | **Definition/convention — embedded submanifold of M is a subset S M that** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2759 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary. An embedded submanifold of M is a subset S M that is a manifold (without boundary) in the subspace topology, endowed with a smooth structure with respect to which the inclusion map | — |
| `FC08-C05-U002` | **Definition/convention — regular submanifolds by some authors** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2761 | S ↪ M is a smooth embedding. Embedded submanifolds are also called regular submanifolds by some authors. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U003` | **Definition/convention — codimension of S in M** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2763 | If S is an embedded submanifold of M; the difference dim M dim S is called the codimension of S in M , and the containing manifold M is called the ambient manifold for S. An embedded hypersurface is an embedded submanifold of codimension 1. The empty set is an embedded submanifold of any dimension. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U004` | **Proposition 5.1** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2767 | (Open Submanifolds). Suppose M is a smooth manifold. The embedded submanifolds of codimension 0 in M are exactly the open submanifolds. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U005` | **Proposition 5.2** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2773 | (Images of Embeddings as Submanifolds). Suppose M is a smooth manifold with o r without boundary, N is a smooth manifold, and F : N → M is a smooth embedding. Let S = F ( N ) . With the subspace topology, S is a topological manifold, and it has a unique smooth structure making it into an embedded submanifold of M with the property that F is a diffeomorphism onto its image. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U006` | **Proposition 5.3** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2788 | (Slices of Product Manifolds). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds. For each p ∈ N , the subset M × p (called a slice of the product manifold) is an embedded submanifold of M N diffeomorphic to M . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U007` | **Proposition 5.4** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2792 | (Graphs as Submanifolds). Suppose M is a smooth m-manifold (without boundary), N is a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary, U ⊆ M is open, and f : U → N is a smooth map. Let Gamma ( f ) ⊆ M × N denote the graph of f : Gamma (f) = (x, y) ∈ M × N: x ∈ U, y = f (x) . Then Gamma ( f ) is an embedded m-dimensional submanifold of M × N (see Fig. 5.1). | — |
| `FC08-C05-U008` | **Definition/convention — properly embedded** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2808 | For some purposes, merely being an embedded submanifold is not quite a strong enough condition. (See, e.g., Lemma 5.34 below.) An embedded submanifold S ⊆ M is said to be properly embedded if the inclusion S ↪ M is a proper map. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U009` | **Proposition 5.5** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2810 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary and S ⊆ M is an embedded submanifold. Then S is properly embedded if and only if it is a closed subset of M . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U010` | **Corollary 5.6** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2814 | Every compact embedded submanifold is properly embedded. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U011` | **Proposition 5.7** | Ch. 5, § Embedded Submanifolds; source L2820 | (Global Graphs Are Properly Embedded). Suppose M is a smooth manifold, N is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and f : M → N is a smooth map. With the smooth manifold structure of Proposition 5.4, .f / is properly embedded in M × N . | `FC08-C05-U007` |
| `FC08-C05-U012` | **Definition/convention — k-dimensional slice** | Ch. 5, § Slice Charts for Embedded Submanifolds; source L2832 | Somewhat more generally, if U is an open subset of ℝ ^n and k ∈ 0 , … , n , a kdimensional slice of U (or simply a _k - s l i c e ) is any subset of the form S = (x ^1, … , x ^k, x ^k + 1, … , x ^n ) ∈ U: x ^k + 1 = c ^k + 1, … , x ^n = c ^n for some constants c ^k + 1 , … , c ^n . (When k = n , this just means S = U . ) Clearly, every k-slice is homeomorphic to an open subset of ℝ ^ check k . (Sometimes it is convenient to consider slices defined by setting some subset of the coordinates other than the last ones equal to constants. The meaning should be clear from the context.) | — |
| `FC08-C05-U013` | **Definition/convention — slice chart for S in M** | Ch. 5, § Slice Charts for Embedded Submanifolds; source L2840 | Let M be a smooth n-manifold, and let ( U , φ ) be a smooth chart on M . If S is a subset of U such that φ ( S ) is a k-slice of φ ( U ) , then we say that S is a kslice of U . (Although in general we allow our slices to be defined by arbitrary constants bar(c) ^k + 1 , … , bar(c) ^n , it is sometimes useful to have slice coordinates for which the constants are all zero, which can easily be achieved by subtracting a constant from each coordinate function.) Given a subset S ⊆ M and a nonnegative integer k, we say that S satisfies the local k-slice condition if each point of S is contained in the domain of a smooth chart ( U , φ ) for M such that S U is a single k-slice in U . Any such chart is called a slice chart for S in M , and the corresponding coordinates ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) are called slice coordinates. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U014` | **Theorem 5.8** | Ch. 5, § Slice Charts for Embedded Submanifolds; source L2842 | (Local Slice Criterion for Embedded Submanifolds). Let M be a smooth n-manifold. Conversely, if S ⊆ M is a subset that satisfies the local k-slice condition, then with the subspace topology, S is a topological manifold of dimension k, and it has a smooth structure making it into a k-dimensional embedded submanifold of M . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U015` | **Example 5.9** | Ch. 5, § Slice Charts for Embedded Submanifolds; source L2884 | (Spheres as Submanifolds). For any n ≥ 0 , mathbb S ^n is an embedded submanifold of ℝ ^n + 1 , because it is locally the graph of a smooth function: as we showed in Example 1.4, the intersection of mathbb S ^n with the open subset x : x ^i > 0 is the graph of the smooth function x ^i = f (x ^1, … , x ^i - 1, x ^i + 1, … , x ^n + 1 ), | `FC08-C01-U008` |
| `FC08-C05-U016` | **Theorem 5.11** | Ch. 5, § Slice Charts for Embedded Submanifolds; source L2897 | If M is a smooth n-manifold with boundary, then with the subspace topology, @M is a topological .n 1/-dimensional manifold (without boundary), and has a smooth structure such that it is a properly embedded submanifold of M . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U017` | **Definition/convention — zero set of clubsuit** | Ch. 5, § Level Sets; source L2905 | In practice, embedded submanifolds are most often presented as solution sets of equations or systems of equations. | `FC08-C05-U018` | **Theorem 5.12** | Ch. 5, § Level Sets; source L2917 | (Constant-Rank Level Set Theorem). Let M and N be smooth manifolds, and let Phi : M → N be a smooth map with constant rank r. Each level set of varPhi is a properly embedded submanifold of codimension r in M . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U019` | **Corollary 5.13** | Ch. 5, § Level Sets; source L2927 | (Submersion Level Set Theorem). If M and N are smooth manifolds and ˚ M → N is a smooth submersion, then each level set of ˚ is a properly embedded submanifold whose codimension is equal to the dimension of N . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U020` | **Definition/convention — surjective linear map** | Ch. 5, § Level Sets; source L2931 | This result should be compared to the corresponding result in linear algebra: if L : ℝ ^m → ℝ ^r is a surjective linear map, then the kernel of L is a linear subspace of codimension r by the rank-nullity law. The vector equation L x = 0 is equivalent to r linearly independent scalar equations, each of which can be thought of as cutting down one of the degrees of freedom in ℝ ^m , leaving a subspace of codimension r. In the context of smooth manifolds, the analogue of a surjective linear map is a smooth submersion, each of whose (local) component functions cuts down the dimension by one. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U021` | **Definition/convention — regular level set** | Ch. 5, § Level Sets; source L2933 | Corollary 5.13 can be strengthened considerably, because we need only check the submersion condition on the level set we are interested in. This means, in particular, that every point of M is critical if dim M < dim N , and every point is regular if and only if F is a submersion. Note that the set of regular points of varPhi is always an open subset of M by Proposition 4.1. A point c ∈ N is said to be a regular value of ˚ if every point of the level set scriptstyle φ ^- 1 ( c ) is a regular point, and a critical value otherwise. In particular, if φ ^- 1 ( c ) = emptyset , then c is a regular value. Finally, a level set φ ^- 1 ( c ) is called a regular level set if c is a regular value of varPhi ; in other words, a regular level set is a level set consisting entirely of regular points of ˚ (points p such that d φ _p is surjective). | `FC08-C05-U019`, `FC08-C04-U004` |
| `FC08-C05-U022` | **Corollary 5.14** | Ch. 5, § Level Sets; source L2938 | (Regular Level Set Theorem). Every regular level set of a smooth map between smooth manifolds is a properly embedded submanifold whose codimension is equal to the dimension of the codomain. | — |

| `FC08-C05-U024` | **Proposition 5.16** | Ch. 5, § Level Sets; source L2948 | Let S be a subset of a smooth m-manifold M . Then S is an embedded k-submanifold of M if and only if every point of S has a neighborhood U in M such that U ∩ S is a level set of a smooth submersion φ : U → overline ℝ ^m - k | — |
| `FC08-C05-U025` | **Definition/convention — defining map for S** | Ch. 5, § Level Sets; source L2955 | If S ⊆ M is an embedded submanifold, a smooth map Phi : M → N such that S is a regular level set of φ is called a defining map for S . Example 5.15 shows that f ( x ) = ∣ x ∣ ^2 is a defining function for the sphere. More generally, if U is an open subset of M and φ : U → N is a smooth map such that S ∩ U is a regular level set of varPhi , then varPhi is called a local defining map (or local defining function) for S . Proposition 5.16 says that every embedded submanifold admits a local defining function in a neighborhood of each of its points. | `FC08-C05-U023`, `FC08-C05-U024` |
| `FC08-C05-U026` | **Example 5.17** | Ch. 5, § Level Sets; source L2959 | (Surfaces of Revolution). Let H be the half-plane ( r , z ) : r > 0 and suppose C ⊆ H is an embedded 1-dimensional submanifold. At any point ( x , y , z ) ∈ S c , at least one of the components of D φ ( x , y , z ) is nonzero, so S _C is a regular level set of varPhi and is thus an embedded 2-dimensional submanifold of ℝ ^3 For a specific example, the doughnut-shaped torus of revolution D described in Example 4.2(d) is the surface of revolution obtained from the circle ( r - 2 ) ^2 + z ^2 = mathbb 1 . The set C is called its generating curve (see Fig. 5.7). If φ : U ℝ is any local defining function for C in H , we get a local defining function varPhi for S _C by Phi (x, y, z) = φ (sqrt x ^2 + y ^2, z ), | — |
| `FC08-C05-U028` | **Definition/convention — codimension of S in M to be dim M - dim S** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L2994 | So as to have a convenient language for talking about examples like these, we introduce the following definition. Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. An immersed submanifold of M is a subset S ⊆ M endowed with a topology (not necessarily the subspace topology) with respect to which it is a topological manifold (without boundary), and a smooth structure with respect to which the inclusion map S ↪ M is a smooth immersion. As for embedded submanifolds, we define the codimension of S in M to be dim M - dim S . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U029` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L2996 | Every embedded submanifold is also an immersed submanifold. Because immersed submanifolds are the more general of the two types of submanifolds, we adopt the convention that the term smooth submanifold without further qualification means an immersed one, which includes an embedded submanifold as a special case. Similarly, the term smooth hypersurface without qualification means an immersed submanifold of codimension 1. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U030` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L2998 | You should be aware that there are variations in how smooth submanifolds are defined in the literature. Some authors reserve the unqualified term “submanifold” to mean what we call an embedded submanifold. If there is room for confusion, it is safest to specify explicitly which type of submanifold—embedded or immersed—is meant. Even though both terms “smooth submanifold” and “immersed submanifold” encompass embedded ones as well, when we are considering general submanifolds we sometimes use the phrase immersed or embedded submanifold as a reminder that the discussion applies equally to the embedded case. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U031` | **Definition/convention — immersed topological submanifold of M to be a subset S** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L3002 | There are also various notions of submanifolds in the topological category. For example, if M is a topological manifold, one could define an immersed topological submanifold of M to be a subset S ⊆ M endowed with a topology such that it is a topological manifold and such that the inclusion map is a topological immersion. It is an embedded topological submanifold if the inclusion is a topological embedding. To be entirely consistent with our convention of assuming by default only continuity rather than smoothness, we would have to distinguish the types of submanifolds we have defined in this chapter by calling them smooth embedded submanifolds and smooth immersed submanifolds, respectively; but since we have no reason to treat topological submanifolds in this book, for the sake of simplicity let us agree that the terms embedded submanifold and immersed submanifold always refer to the smooth kind. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U032` | **Proposition 5.18** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L3006 | (Images of Immersions as Submanifolds). Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, N is a smooth manifold, and F N M is an injective smooth immersion. Let S = F ( N ) . Then S has a unique topology and smooth structure such that it is a smooth submanifold of M and such that F : N S is a diffeomorphism onto its image. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U033` | **Example 5.19** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L3018 | (The Figure-Eight and the Dense Curve on the Torus). Look back at the two examples we introduced in Chapter 4 of injective smooth immersions that are not embeddings: because the figure-eight of Example 4.19 and the dense curve of Example 4.20 are images of injective smooth immersions, they are immersed submanifolds when given appropriate topologies and smooth structures. They are not embedded submanifolds, because neither one has the subspace topology. In fact, their image sets cannot be made into embedded submanifolds even if we are allowed to change their topologies and smooth structures (see Problems 5-4 and 5-5). // | `FC08-C04-U019`, `FC08-C04-U020` |
| `FC08-C05-U034` | **Proposition 5.21** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L3026 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and S ⊆ M is an immersed submanifold. If any of the following holds, then S is embedded. (a) S has codimension 0 in M . (b) The inclusion map S M is proper. (c) S is compact. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U035` | **Proposition 5.22** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L3038 | (Immersed Submanifolds Are Locally Embedded). If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and S ⊆ M is an immersed submanifold, then for each p ∈ S there exists a neighborhood U of p in S that is an embedded submanifold of M . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U036` | **Definition/convention — smooth local parametrization** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L3047 | Suppose S M is an immersed k-dimensional submanifold. A local parametrization of S is a continuous map X : U → M whose domain is an open subset U ⊆ ℝ ^k , whose image is an open subset of S, and which, considered as a map into S , is a homeomorphism onto its image. It is called a smooth local parametrization if it is a diffeomorphism onto its image (with respect to S ^prime bf s smooth manifold structure). If the image of X is all of S , it is called a global parametrization. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U037` | **Proposition 5.23** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L3049 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, S ⊆ M is an immersed k-submanifold, iota : S ↪ M is the inclusion map, and U is an open subset of ℝ ^k . A map X : U → M is a smooth local parametrization of S if and only if there is a smooth coordinate chart ( V , φ ) for S such that X = iota ∘ φ ^- 1 Therefore, every point of S is in the image of some local parametrization. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U038` | **Example 5.25** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L3053 | (Graph Parametrizations). Suppose U ⊆ ℝ ^n is an open subset and f : U → ℝ ^k is a smooth function. The map γ _f : U → ℝ ^n × ℝ ^k given by γ _f ( u ) = ( u , f ( u ) ) is a smooth global parametrization of .f / called a graph parametrization. Its inverse is the graph coordinate map constructed in Example 1.3. | `FC08-C05-U039` | **Example 5.26** | Ch. 5, § Immersed Submanifolds; source L3061 | (Parametrization of the Figure-Eight Curve). The map β : ( - π , π ) → ℝ ^2 of Example 4.19 is a smooth global parametrization of S. | `FC08-C05-U033`, `FC08-C04-U019` |
| `FC08-C05-U040` | **Theorem 5.27** | Ch. 5, § Restricting Maps to Submanifolds; source L3073 | (Restricting the Domain of a Smooth Map). If M and N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, F : M → N is a smooth map, and S ⊆ M is an immersed or embedded submanifold (Fig. 5.9), then F ∣ _S : S → N is smooth. | — |

| `FC08-C05-U042` | **Theorem 5.29** | Ch. 5, § Restricting Maps to Submanifolds; source L3089 | (Restricting the Codomain of a Smooth Map). Suppose M is a smooth manifold (without boundary), S ⊆ M is an immersed submanifold, and F : N → M is a smooth map whose image is contained in S ( F i g . 5 . 1 0 ) If F is continuous as a map from N to S, then F : N S is smooth. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U043` | **Corollary 5.30** | Ch. 5, § Restricting Maps to Submanifolds; source L3108 | (Embedded Case). Let M be a smooth manifold and S ⊆ M be an embedded submanifold. Then every smooth map F : N → M whose image is contained in S is also smooth as a map from N to S . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U044` | **Definition/convention — initial submanifolds by some authors** | Ch. 5, § Restricting Maps to Submanifolds; source L3112 | Although the conclusion of the preceding corollary fails for some immersed submanifolds such as the figure-eight curve (see Example 5.28), it turns out that there are certain immersed but nonembedded submanifolds for which it holds. To distinguish them, we introduce the following definition. If M is a smooth manifold and S ⊆ M is an immersed submanifold, then S is said to be weakly embedded in M if every smooth map F N M whose image lies in S is smooth as a map from N to S. (Weakly embedded submanifolds are called initial submanifolds by some authors.) Corollary 5.30 shows that every embedded submanifold is weakly embedded. It follows from Example 5.28 that the figure-eight curve is not weakly embedded. However, the dense curve on the torus is weakly embedded; see Problem 5-13. In Chapter 19, we will encounter some classes of submanifolds that are automatically weakly embedded (see Theorems 19.17 and 19.25). | `FC08-C05-U041`, `FC08-C05-U043` |
| `FC08-C05-U045` | **Theorem 5.31** | Ch. 5, § Uniqueness of Smooth Structures on Submanifolds; source L3120 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold and S ⊆ M is an embedded submanifold. The subspace topology on S and the smooth structure described in Theorem 5.8 are the only topology and smooth structure with respect to which S is an embedded or immersed submanifold. | `FC08-C05-U014` |
| `FC08-C05-U046` | **Theorem 5.32** | Ch. 5, § Uniqueness of Smooth Structures on Submanifolds; source L3134 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold and S ⊆ M is an immersed submanifold. For the given topology on S, there is only one smooth structure making S into an immersed submanifold. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U047` | **Theorem 5.33** | Ch. 5, § Uniqueness of Smooth Structures on Submanifolds; source L3140 | If M is a smooth manifold and S ⊆ M is a weakly embedded submanifold, then S has only one topology and smooth structure with respect to which it is an immersed submanifold. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U048` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 5, § Extending Functions from Submanifolds; source L3146 | Complementary to the restriction problem is the problem of extending smooth functions from a submanifold to the ambient manifold. Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and let S ⊆ M be a smooth submanifold. If f : S → ℝ is a function, there are two ways we might interpret the statement “f is smooth”: it might mean that f is smooth as a function on the smooth manifold S (i.e., each coordinate representation is smooth), or it might mean that it is smooth as a function on the subset S ⊆ M (i.e., it admits a smooth extension to a neighborhood of each point). We adopt the convention that the notation f ∈ C ^∞ ( S ) always means that f is smooth in the former sense (as a function on the manifold S ). | — |
| `FC08-C05-U049` | **Lemma 5.34** | Ch. 5, § Extending Functions from Submanifolds; source L3148 | (Extension Lemma for Functions on Submanifolds). | `FC08-C05-U050` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 5, § The Tangent Space to a Submanifold; source L3165 | Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and let S ⊆ M be an immersed or embedded submanifold. | `FC08-C05-U055` | **Definition/convention — inwardpointing** | Ch. 5, § The Tangent Space to a Submanifold; source L3234 | If M is a smooth manifold with boundary and p ∈ ∂ M ; it is intuitively evident that the vectors in T _p M can be separated into three classes: those tangent to the boundary, those pointing inward, and those pointing outward. If p ∈ ∂ M , a vector v ∈ T _p M setminus T _p ∂ M is said to be inwardpointing if for some ε > 0 there exists a smooth curve γ : [ 0 , ε ) → M such that γ ( 0 ) = p and γ ^prime ( 0 ) = v , and it is outward-pointing if there exists such a curve whose domain is ( - ε , 0 ] . | `FC08-C05-U056` | **Proposition 5.41** | Ch. 5, § The Tangent Space to a Submanifold; source L3236 | Suppose M is a smooth n-dimensional manifold with boundary, p ∈ ∂ M . ; and ( x ^i ) are any smooth boundary coordinates defined on a neighborhood of p. The inward-pointing vectors in T _p M are precisely those with positive x ^n -component, the outward-pointing ones are those with negative x ^n -component, and the ones tangent to @M are those with zero x ^n -component. Thus, T _p M is the disjoint union of T _p ∂ M , ; the set of inward-pointing vectors, and the set of outwardpointing vectors, and v ∈ T _p M is inward-pointing if and only i f - v is outwardpointing. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U057` | **Definition/convention — boundary defining function for M** | Ch. 5, § The Tangent Space to a Submanifold; source L3240 | If M is a smooth manifold with boundary, a boundary defining function for M is a smooth function f : M → [ 0 , ∞ ) such that f ^- 1 ( 0 ) = ∂ M and d f _p ≠ 0 for all p ∈ ∂ M . For example, f ( x ) = 1 - ∣ x ∣ ^2 is a boundary defining function for the closed unit ball bar(B) ^n . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U058` | **Proposition 5.43** | Ch. 5, § The Tangent Space to a Submanifold; source L3242 | Every smooth manifold with boundary admits a boundary defining function. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U059` | **Example 5.45** | Ch. 5, § The Tangent Space to a Submanifold; source L3268 | Consider the subset S = ( x , y ) : y = ∣ x ∣ ⊆ ℝ ^2 . It is easy to check that S setminus ( 0 , 0 ) is an embedded 1-dimensional submanifold of ℝ ^2 , so if S itself is a smooth submanifold at all, it must be 1-dimensional. Differentiating twice and setting t = 0 , we conclude that 2 x ^prime ( 0 ) ^2 = 2 y ^prime ( 0 ) ^2 = 0 , which is a contradiction. Thus, there is no such smooth manifold structure. | `FC08-C05-U051` |
| `FC08-C05-U060` | **Definition/convention — embedded submanifold** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3275 | So far in this chapter, all of our submanifolds have been manifolds without boundary. For some purposes (notably in the theory of integration), it is important also to consider submanifolds that have boundaries. The definitions are straightforward generalizations of the ones for ordinary submanifolds. If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, a smooth submanifold with boundary in M is a subset S ⊆ M endowed with a topology and smooth structure making it into a smooth manifold with boundary such that the inclusion map is a smooth immersion. If the inclusion map is an embedding, then it is called an embedded submanifold with boundary; in the general case, it is an immersed submanifold with boundary. The terms codimension and properly embedded are defined just as in the submanifold case. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U061` | **Definition/convention — regular domain in M** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3279 | One particular type of submanifold with boundary is especially important. If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, a regular domain in M is a properly embedded codimension-0 submanifold with boundary. Familiar examples are the closed upper half space ℍ ^n ⊆ ℝ ^n , the closed unit ball overline mathbb B ^n ⊆ ℝ ^n , and the closed upper hemisphere in overline mathbb S ^n . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U062` | **Proposition 5.46** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3281 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold without boundary and D ⊆ M is a regular domain. The topological interior and boundary of D are equal to its manifold interior and boundary, respectively. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U063` | **Proposition 5.47** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3297 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold and f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) . . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U064` | **Definition/convention — regular sublevel set of f** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3299 | A set of the form f ^- 1 ( ( - ∞ , b ] ) for b a regular value of f is called a regular sublevel set of f . Part (a) of the preceding theorem shows that every regular sublevel set of a smooth real-valued function is a regular domain. If D ⊆ M is a regular domain and f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) is a smooth function such that D is a regular sublevel set of f , then f is called a defining function for D. | — |
| `FC08-C05-U065` | **Theorem 5.48** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3301 | If M is a smooth manifold and D ⊆ M is a regular domain, then there exists a defining function for D. If D is compact, then f can be taken to be a smooth exhaustion function for M . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U066` | **Proposition 5.49** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3307 | (Properties of Submanifolds with Boundary). Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary. (a) Every open subset of M is an embedded codimension-0 submanifold with (possibly empty) boundary. (b) If N is a smooth manifold with boundary and F : N → M is a smooth embedding, then with the subspace topology F .N / is a topological manifold with boundary, and it has a smooth structure making it into an embedded submanifold with boundary in M . (c) An embedded submanifold with boundary in M is properly embedded if and only if it is closed. (d) If S ⊆ M is an immersed submanifold with boundary, then for each p ∈ S there exists a neighborhood U of p in S that is embedded in M . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U067` | **Definition/convention — k-dimensional halfslice of U** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3319 | In order to adapt the results that depended on the existence of local slice charts, we have to generalize the local k-slice condition as follows. Suppose M is a smooth manifold (without boundary). If ( U , ( x ^i ) ) is a chart for M; a k-dimensional halfslice of U is a subset of the following form for some constants c ^k + 1 , … , c ^n | — |
| `FC08-C05-U068` | **Definition/convention — interior slice chart for S in M** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3325 | We say that a subset S ⊆ M satisfies the local k - s l i c e condition for submanifolds with boundary if each point of S is contained in the domain of a smooth chart ( U , ( x ^i ) ) such that S ∩ U is either an ordinary k-dimensional slice or a k-dimensional half-slice. In the former case, the chart is called an interior slice chart for S in M , and in the latter, it is a boundary slice chart for S in M . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U069` | **Theorem 5.51** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3327 | Let M be a smooth n-manifold without boundary. If S ⊆ M is an embedded k-dimensional submanifold with boundary, then S satisfies the local k-slice condition for submanifolds with boundary. Conversely, if S ⊆ M is a subset that satisfies the local k-slice condition for submanifolds with boundary, then with the subspace topology, S is a topological k-manifold with boundary, and it has a smooth structure making it into an embedded submanifold with boundary in M . | — |
| `FC08-C05-U070` | **Theorem 5.53** | Ch. 5, § Submanifolds with Boundary; source L3333 | (Restricting Maps to Submanifolds with Boundary). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds with boundary and S ⊆ M is an embedded submanifold with boundary. (a) RESTRICTING THE DOMAIN: If F : M → N is a smooth map, then F ∣ _S : S → N is smooth. (b) RESTRICTING THE CODOMAIN: If @M ¿ and F N M is a smooth map whose image is contained in S, then F is smooth as a map from N to S. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U001` | **Lemma 6.2** | Ch. 6, § Sets of Measure Zero; source L3446 | Suppose A ⊆ ℝ^n is a compact subset whose intersection with c × ℝ^n-1 has (n-1) -dimensional measure zero for every c ∈ ℝ . Then A has n -dimensional measure zero. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U002` | **Proposition 6.3** | Ch. 6, § Sets of Measure Zero; source L3457 | Suppose A is an open or closed subset of ℝ ^n - 1 ~ o r ~ ℍ ^n - 1 , and f : A → ℝ is a continuous function. Then the graph of f has measure zero in ℝ ^n . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U003` | **Corollary 6.4** | Ch. 6, § Sets of Measure Zero; source L3463 | Every proper affine subspace of ℝ ^n has measure zero in ℝ ^n . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U004` | **Proposition 6.5** | Ch. 6, § Sets of Measure Zero; source L3469 | Suppose A ⊆ ℝ ^n has measure zero and F : A → ℝ ^n is a smooth map. Then F .A/ has measure zero. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U005` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 6, § Sets of Measure Zero; source L3486 | If M is a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary, we say that a subset A ⊆ M has measure zero in M if for every smooth chart ( U , φ ) for M; the subset φ ( A ∩ U ) ⊆ ℝ ^n has n-dimensional measure zero. The following lemma shows that we need only check this condition for a single collection of smooth charts whose domains cover A. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U006` | **Lemma 6.6** | Ch. 6, § Sets of Measure Zero; source L3488 | Let M be a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary and A ⊆ M Suppose that for some collection ( U _α , φ _α ) of smooth charts whose domains cover A, φ _α ( A ∩ U _α ) has measure zero in ℝ ^n for each ˛. Then A has measure zero in M . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U007` | **Proposition 6.8** | Ch. 6, § Sets of Measure Zero; source L3500 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary and A ⊆ M has measure zero in M . Then M A is dense in M . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U008` | **Theorem 6.9** | Ch. 6, § Sets of Measure Zero; source L3504 | Suppose M and N are smooth n-manifolds with or without boundary, F : M → N is a smooth map, and A ⊆ M is a subset of measure zero. Then F ( A ) has measure zero in N . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U009` | **Theorem 6.10** | Ch. 6, § Sard’s Theorem; source L3516 | (Sard's Theorem). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary and F: M → N is a smooth map. Then the set of critical values of F has measure zero in N. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U010` | **Corollary 6.11** | Ch. 6, § Sard’s Theorem; source L3583 | Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, and F : M → N is a smooth map. If dim M < dim N , then F .M / has measure zero in N . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U011` | **Definition/convention — space-filling curve** | Ch. 6, § Sard’s Theorem; source L3589 | It is important to be aware that Corollary 6.11 is false if F is merely assumed to be continuous. For example, there is a continuous map F : [ 0 , 1 ] → ℝ ^2 whose image is the entire unit square [ 0 , 1 ] × [ 0 , 1 ] . (Such a map is called a space-filling curve. See [Rud76, p. 168] for an example.) | `FC08-C06-U010` |
| `FC08-C06-U012` | **Corollary 6.12** | Ch. 6, § Sard’s Theorem; source L3591 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and S ⊆ M is an immersed submanifold with or without boundary. If dim S < dim M; then S has measure zero in M . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U013` | **Lemma 6.13** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Embedding Theorem; source L3601 | Suppose M ⊆ ℝ ^N is a smooth n-dimensional submanifold with or without boundary. For any v ∈ ℝ ^N setminus ℝ ^N - 1 , let π _v : ℝ ^N → ℝ ^N - 1 be the projection with kernel Rv (where we identify ℝ ^N - 1 with the subspace of ℝ ^N consisting of points with last coordinate zero). If N > 2 n + 1 , then there is a dense set of vectors Phi _v ∈ ℝ ^N setminus ℝ ^N - 1 for which π _v ∣ _M is an injective immersion of M into ℝ ^N - 1 . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U014` | **Lemma 6.14** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Embedding Theorem; source L3615 | Let M be a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary. If M admits a smooth embedding into ℝ ^N for some N , then it admits a proper smooth embedding into ℝ ^2 n + 1 | — |
| `FC08-C06-U015` | **Theorem 6.15** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Embedding Theorem; source L3634 | (Whitney Embedding Theorem). Every smooth n-manifold with or without boundary admits a proper smooth embedding into ℝ ^2 n + 1 | — |
| `FC08-C06-U016` | **Corollary 6.16** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Embedding Theorem; source L3678 | Every smooth n-dimensional manifold with or without boundary is diffeomorphic to a properly embedded submanifold (with or without boundary) of ℝ ^2 n + 1 | — |
| `FC08-C06-U017` | **Corollary 6.17** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Embedding Theorem; source L3680 | Suppose M is a compact smooth n-manifold with or withou boundary. If N ≥ 2 n + 1 , then every smooth map from M to ℝ ^N can be uniformly approximated by embeddings. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U018` | **Theorem 6.18** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Embedding Theorem; source L3686 | (Whitney Immersion Theorem). Every smooth n-manifold with or without boundary admits a smooth immersion into ℝ ^2 n | — |
| `FC08-C06-U019` | **Theorem 6.19** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Embedding Theorem; source L3694 | (Strong Whitney Embedding Theorem). If n > 0 , every smooth n-manifold admits a smooth embedding into ℝ ^2 n . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U020` | **Theorem 6.20** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Embedding Theorem; source L3696 | (Strong Whitney Immersion Theorem). If n > 1 , every smooth n-manifold admits a smooth immersion into ℝ ^2 n - 1 . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U021` | **Convention/notation — weak/easy Whitney theorems** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Embedding Theorem; source L3698 | Because of these results, Theorems 6.15 and 6.18 are sometimes called the easy or weak Whitney embedding and immersion theorems. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U022` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Approximation Theorems; source L3706 | We begin with a theorem about smoothly approximating functions into Euclidean spaces. Our first theorem shows, in particular, that any continuous function from a smooth manifold M into ℝ ^k can be uniformly approximated by a smooth function. In fact, we will prove something stronger. I f ~ delta : M ℝ is a positive continuous function, we say that two functions F; tilde(cal F) : cal M → mathbb R ^k are ı-close if ∣ F ( x ) - widetilde F ( x ) ∣ < ı.x/ for all x ∈ M . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U023` | **Theorem 6.21** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Approximation Theorems; source L3708 | (Whitney Approximation Theorem for Functions). Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and F : M → ℝ ^k is a continuous function. Given any positive continuous function ı M R, there exists a smooth function tilde(F) : M → overline ℝ ^k that is ı-close to F . If F is smooth on a closed subset A ⊆ M . then tilde(F) can be chosen to be equal to F on A. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U024` | **Corollary 6.22** | Ch. 6, § The Whitney Approximation Theorems; source L3750 | If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary and delta : M → R is a positive continuous function, there is a smooth function e M R such that 0 < e ( x ) < delta ( x ) f o r a l l x ∈ M . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U025` | **Construction/definition — normal space to M at x to be the ( n - m ) -dimensional subsp** | Ch. 6, § Tubular Neighborhoods; source L3758 | For each x ∈ ℝ ^n , the tangent space T _x ℝ ^n is canonically identified with ℝ ^n itself, and the tangent bundle T ℝ ^n is canonically diffeomorphic to ℝ ^n × ℝ ^n . By virtue of this identification, each tangent space T _x ℝ ^n inherits a Euclidean dot product. Suppose M ⊆ ℝ ^n is an embedded m-dimensional submanifold. For each x ∈ M , we define the normal space to M at x to be the ( n - m ) -dimensional subspace N _x M ⊆ T _x ℝ ^n consisting of all vectors that are orthogonal to T _x M with respect to the Euclidean dot product. The normal bundle of M , denoted by NM; is the subset of T ℝ ^n approx ℝ ^n × ℝ ^n consisting of vectors that are normal to M : N M = (x, v) ∈ ℝ ^n × ℝ ^n: x ∈ M, v ∈ N _x M . There is a natural projection π _N M : N M → M defined as the restriction to NM of π : T ℝ ^n → ℝ ^n | — |
| `FC08-C06-U026` | **Theorem 6.23** | Ch. 6, § Tubular Neighborhoods; source L3766 | If M ⊆ ℝ ^n is an embedded m-dimensional submanifold, then NM is an embedded n-dimensional submanifold of T ℝ ^n approx ℝ ^n × ℝ ^n | — |
| `FC08-C06-U027` | **Theorem 6.24** | Ch. 6, § Tubular Neighborhoods; source L3815 | (Tubular Neighborhood Theorem). Every embedded submanifold of ℝ ^n has a tubular neighborhood. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U028` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 6, § Tubular Neighborhoods; source L3843 | One of the most useful features of tubular neighborhoods is expressed in the next proposition. A retraction of a topological space X onto a subspace M ⊆ X is a continuous map r X M such that r ∣ _M is the identity map of M . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U029` | **Proposition 6.25** | Ch. 6, § Tubular Neighborhoods; source L3845 | Let M ⊆ ℝ ^n be an embedded submanifold. If U is any tubular neighborhood of M; there exists a smooth map r : U → M that is both a retraction and a smooth submersion. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U030` | **Theorem 6.26** | Ch. 6, § Smooth Approximation of Maps Between Manifolds; source L3855 | (Whitney Approximation Theorem). Suppose N is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, M is a smooth manifold (without boundary), and F : N → M is a continuous map. Then F is homotopic to a smooth map. If F is already smooth on a closed subset A ⊆ N , then the homotopy can be taken to be relative to A. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U031` | **Corollary 6.27** | Ch. 6, § Smooth Approximation of Maps Between Manifolds; source L3873 | (Extension Lemma for Smooth Maps). Suppose N is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, M is a smooth manifold, A ⊆ N is a closed subset, and f : A → M is a smooth map. Then f has a smooth extension to N if and only if it has a continuous extension to N . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U032` | **Definition/convention — smooth homotopy** | Ch. 6, § Smooth Approximation of Maps Between Manifolds; source L3880 | If N and M are two smooth manifolds with or without boundary, a homotopy H : N × I → M is called a smooth homotopy if it is also a smooth map, in the sense that it extends to a smooth map on some neighborhood of N × I ~ i n ~ N × ℝ . . Two maps are said to be smoothly homotopic if there is a smooth homotopy between them. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U033` | **Lemma 6.28** | Ch. 6, § Smooth Approximation of Maps Between Manifolds; source L3882 | If N and M are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, smooth homotopy is an equivalence relation on the set of all smooth maps from N to M . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U034` | **Theorem 6.29** | Ch. 6, § Smooth Approximation of Maps Between Manifolds; source L3892 | Suppose N is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, M is a smooth manifold, and F; G : N → M are smooth maps. If F and G are homotopic, then they are smoothly homotopic. If F and G are homotopic relative to some closed subset A ⊆ N , then they are smoothly homotopic relative to A. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U035` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 6, § Transversality; source L3914 | For many purposes, it is more convenient to work with the following more general definition. If F : N → M is a smooth map and S ⊆ M is an embedded submanifold, we say that F is transverse to S if for every x ∈ F ^- 1 ( S ) the spaces T _F ( x ) S and d F _x ( T _x N ) together span T _F ( x ) M . One special case is worth noting: if F is a smooth submersion, then it is automatically transverse to every embedded submanifold of M . Two embedded submanifolds intersect transversely if and only if the inclusion of either one is transverse to the other. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U036` | **Theorem 6.30** | Ch. 6, § Transversality; source L3918 | Suppose N and M are smooth manifolds and S ⊆ M is an embedded submanifold. (a) If F : N → M is a smooth map that is transverse to S , then F ^- 1 ( S ) is an embedded submanifold of N whose codimension is equal to the codimension of S in M . (b) If S ^prime ⊆ M is an embedded submanifold that intersects S transversely, then S ∩ S ^prime is an embedded submanifold of M whose codimension is equal to the sum of the codimensions of S and S ^prime . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U037` | **Corollary 6.31** | Ch. 6, § Transversality; source L3940 | Suppose N and M are smooth manifolds, S ⊆ M is an embedded submanifold of codimension k, and F : N → M is a submersion. Then F ^- 1 ( S ) is an embedded codimension-k submanifold of N . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U038` | **Theorem 6.32** | Ch. 6, § Transversality; source L3944 | (Global Characterization of Graphs). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds and S ⊆ M × N is an immersed submanifold. Let π and π denote the projections from M × N onto M and N , respectively. The following are equivalent. (a) S is the graph of a smooth map f : M → N . (b) π _M ∣ _S is a diffeomorphism from S onto M . (c) For each p ∈ M , the submanifolds S and p × N intersect transversely in exactly one point. If these conditions hold, then S is the graph of the map f : M → N defined by dot f = π _N ∘ ( π _M ∣ _S ) ^- 1 | — |
| `FC08-C06-U039` | **Corollary 6.33** | Ch. 6, § Transversality; source L3956 | (Local Characterization of Graphs). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds, S ⊆ M × N is an immersed submanifold, and ( p , q ) ∈ S . If S intersects the submanifold p × N transversely at ( p , q ) , then there exist a neighborhood U of p in M and a neighborhood V of ( p , q ) in S such that V is the graph of a smooth map f : U → N . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U040` | **Definition/convention — smooth family vertof maps** | Ch. 6, § Transversality; source L3962 | Suppose N , M; and S are smooth manifolds, and for each s ∈ S we are given a map F _s : N → M The collection F _s : s ∈ S is called a smooth family vertof maps if the map F : M × S N defined by F ( x , s ) = F _s ( x ) is smooth. You should think of such a family as a higher-dimensional analogue of a homotopy. The next proposition shows how such families are related to ordinary homotopies. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U041` | **Proposition 6.34** | Ch. 6, § Transversality; source L3964 | If F _s : s ∈ S is a smooth family of maps from N to M and S is connected, then for any s _1 , s _2 ∈ S , the maps F _ s _1 , F _ s _2 : N M are homotopic. | — |
| `FC08-C06-U042` | **Definition/convention — family of smooth maps to contain at least one map that** | Ch. 6, § Transversality; source L3968 | The key to finding transverse maps is the following application of Sard’s theorem, which gives a simple sufficient condition for a family of smooth maps to contain at least one map that is transverse to a given submanifold. If S is a smooth manifold and B ⊆ S is a subset whose complement has measure zero in S, we say that B contains almost every element of s . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U043` | **Theorem 6.35** | Ch. 6, § Transversality; source L3970 | (Parametric Transversality Theorem). Suppose N and M are smooth manifolds, X ⊆ M is an embedded submanifold, and F _s : s ∈ S is a smooth family of maps from N to M . If the map F : N × S → M is transverse to X, then for almost every s ∈ S , the map F _s : N → M is transverse to X . | — |
| `FC08-C06-U044` | **Theorem 6.36** | Ch. 6, § Transversality; source L4014 | (Transversality Homotopy Theorem). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds and X ⊆ M is an embedded submanifold. Every smooth map f : N → M is homotopic to a smooth map g N M that is transverse to X . | — |
| `FC08-C07-U001` | **Definition/convention — Lie group** | Ch. 7, § Basic Definitions; source L4100 | A Lie group is a smooth manifold G (without boundary) that is also a group in the algebraic sense, with the property that the multiplication map m G × G → G and inversion map i G → G , given by m (g, h) = g h, i (g) = g ^- 1, | — |
| `FC08-C07-U002` | **Convention/notation — arbitrary Lie group** | Ch. 7, § Basic Definitions; source L4108 | The group operation in an arbitrary Lie group is denoted by juxtaposition, except in certain abelian groups such as ℝ ^n in which the operation is usually written additively. It is traditional to denote the identity element of an arbitrary Lie group by the symbol e (for German Einselement, “unit element”), and we follow this convention, except in specific examples in which there are more common notations (such as I _n for the identity matrix in a matrix group, or 0 for the identity element in R n ). | — |
| `FC08-C07-U003` | **Proposition 7.1** | Ch. 7, § Basic Definitions; source L4112 | If G is a smooth manifold with a group structure such that the map G × G → G given by ( g , h ) ↦ g h ^- 1 is smooth, then G is a Lie group. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U004` | **Example 7.3** | Ch. 7, § Basic Definitions; source L4130 | (Lie Groups). Each of the following manifolds is a Lie group with the indicated group operation. (a) The general linear group GL ( n , ℝ ) is the set of invertible n × n matrices with real entries. Multiplication is smooth because the matrix entries of a product matrix AB are polynomials in the entries of A and B. (b) Let GL ^+ ( n , ℝ ) denote the subset of GL ( n , ℝ ) consisting of matrices with positive determinant. Because det ( A B ) = ( operatorname* d e t A ) ( operatorname* d e t B ) and det ( A ^- 1 ) = 1= det A, it is a subgroup of GL ( n , ℝ ) ; ; and because it is the preimage of ( 0 , ∞ ) under the continuous determinant function, it is an open subset of GL ( n , ℝ ) and therefore an n ^2 . -dimensional manifold. (c) Suppose G is an arbitrary Lie group and H ⊆ G is an open subgroup (a subgroup that is also an open subset). By the same argument as in part (b), H is a Lie group with the inherited group structure and smooth manifold structure. (d) The complex general linear group GL ( n , ℂ ) is the group of invertible complex n × n matrices under matrix multiplication. It is an open submanifold of M ( n , ℂ ) and thus a 2 n ^2 -dimensional smooth manifold, and it is a Lie group because matrix products and inverses are smooth functions of the real and imaginary parts of the matrix entries. (e) If V is any real or complex vector space, GL.V / denotes the set of invertible linear maps from V to itself. Thus, the smooth manifold structure on GL.V / is independent of the choice of basis. (f) The real number field R and Euclidean space ℝ ^n are Lie groups under addition, because the coordinates of x - y are smooth (linear!) functions of ( x , y ) (g) Similarly, C and ℂ ^n are Lie groups under addition. (h) The set ℝ * of nonzero real numbers is a 1-dimensional Lie group under multiplication. (In fact, it is exactly GL.1; R/ if we identify a 1 × 1 matrix with the corresponding real number.) The subset ℝ ^+ of positive real numbers is an open subgroup, and is thus itself a 1-dimensional Lie group. (i) The set ℂ * of nonzero complex numbers is a 2-dimensional Lie group under complex multiplication, which can be identified with GL ( 1 , ℂ ) (j) The circle mathbb S ^1 ⊆ ℂ * is a smooth manifold and a group under complex multiplication. With appropriate angle functions as local coordinates on open subsets of mathbb S ^1 (see Problem 1-8), multiplication and inversion have the smooth coordinate expressions ( θ _1 , θ _2 ) ↦ θ _1 + θ _2 and θ ↦ - θ and therefore mathbb S ^1 is a Lie group, called the circle group. (k) Given Lie groups G _1 , … , G _k , their direct product is the product manifold G _1 × ·s × G _k with the group structure given by componentwise multiplication: (g _1, … , g _k ) (g _1 ^prime, … , g _k ^prime ) = (g _1 g _1 ^prime, … , g _k g _k ^prime ). It is a Lie group, as you can easily check. (l) The n-torus mathbb T ^n = mathbb S ^1 × ·s × mathbb S ^1 is an n-dimensional abelian Lie group. (m) Any group with the discrete topology is a topological group, called a discrete group. If in addition the group is finite or countably infinite, then it is a zerodimensional Lie group, called a discrete Lie group. | `FC08-C01-U038` |
| `FC08-C07-U005` | **Definition/convention — Lie group isomorphism** | Ch. 7, § Lie Group Homomorphisms; source L4166 | If G and H are Lie groups, a Lie group homomorphism from G to H is a smooth map F : G → H that is also a group homomorphism. It is called a Lie group isomorphism if it is also a diffeomorphism, which implies that it has an inverse that is also a Lie group homomorphism. In this case we say that G and H are isomorphic Lie groups. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U006` | **Example 7.4** | Ch. 7, § Lie Group Homomorphisms; source L4168 | (Lie Group Homomorphisms). (a) The inclusion map mathbb S ^1 ↪ ℂ * is a Lie group homomorphism. (b) Considering R as a Lie group under addition, and ℝ * as a Lie group under multiplication, the map exp ℝ → ℝ * given by exp ( t ) = e ^t is smooth, and is a Lie group homomorphism because overline e ^( s + t ) = e ^s e ^t The image of exp is the open subgroup ℝ ^+ consisting of positive real numbers, and exp ℝ → ℝ ^+ is a Lie group isomorphism with inverse log ℝ ^+ → ℝ (c) Similarly, exp ℂ → ℂ * given by exp ( z ) = e ^z is a Lie group homomorphism. It is surjective but not injective, because its kernel consists of the complex numbers of the form 2 π i k , where k is an integer. (d) The map ε : ℝ → ∣ mathbb S ^1 defined by ε ( t ) = e ^2 π i t is a Lie group homomorphism whose kernel is the set Z of integers. Similarly, the map ε ^n : ℝ ^n → mathbb T ^n defined by ε ^n ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) = ( e ^ 2 π i x ^1 , … , e ^ 2 π i x ^n ) is a Lie group homomorphism whose kernel is ℤ ^n (e) The determinant function det GL ( n , ℝ ) → ℝ * is smooth because det A is a polynomial in the matrix entries of A. It is a Lie group homomorphism because det ( A B ) = ( operatorname* d e t A ) .det B/. (f) If G is a Lie group and g ∈ G conjugation by g is the map C _g : G → G given by C _g ( h ) = g h g ^- 1 . Because group multiplication and inversion are smooth, C _g is smooth, and a simple computation shows that it is a group homomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U007` | **Definition/convention — normal subgroup** | Ch. 7, § Lie Group Homomorphisms; source L4180 | (f) If G is a Lie group and g ∈ G conjugation by g is the map C _g : G → G given by C _g ( h ) = g h g ^- 1 . Because group multiplication and inversion are smooth, C _g is smooth, and a simple computation shows that it is a group homomorphism. In fact, it is an isomorphism, because it has C _g ^- 1 as an inverse. A subgroup H ⊆ G is said to be normal if C _g ( H ) = H for every g ∈ G | — |
| `FC08-C07-U008` | **Theorem 7.5** | Ch. 7, § Lie Group Homomorphisms; source L4184 | Every Lie group homomorphism has constant rank. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U009` | **Corollary 7.6** | Ch. 7, § Lie Group Homomorphisms; source L4200 | A Lie group homomorphism is a Lie group isomorphism if and only if it is bijective. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U010` | **Theorem 7.7** | Ch. 7, § The Universal Covering Group; source L4208 | (Existence of a Universal Covering Group). Let G be a connected Lie group. There exists a simply connected Lie group tilde(G) , called the universal covering group of G , that admits a smooth covering map π : tilde(G) → G that is also a Lie group homomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U011` | **Theorem 7.9** | Ch. 7, § The Universal Covering Group; source L4256 | (Uniqueness of the Universal Covering Group). For any connected Lie group G , the universal covering group is unique in the following sense: i f tilde(G) and tilde(G) ^prime are simply connected Lie groups that admit smooth covering maps π : tilde(G) → G and π ^prime : tilde(G) ^prime → G that are also Lie group homomorphisms, then there exists a Lie group isomorphism φ : tilde(G) tilde(G) ^prime such that π ^prime ∘ φ = π | — |
| `FC08-C07-U012` | **Example 7.10** | Ch. 7, § The Universal Covering Group; source L4260 | (Universal Covering Groups). (a) For each n, the map ε ^n : ℝ ^n → mathbb T ^n given by ε^n big (x ^1, … , x ^n big) = big (e ^ 2 π i x ^1, … , e ^ 2 π i x ^n big) is a Lie group homomorphism and a smooth covering map (see Example 7.4(d)). Since ℝ ^n is simply connected, this shows that the universal covering group of mathbb T ^n is the additive Lie group ℝ ^n (b) The Lie group homomorphism exp ℂ → ℂ * described in Example 7 . 4 ( c ) is also a smooth covering map, s o ℂ is the universal covering group of ℂ * . | `FC08-C07-U006` |
| `FC08-C07-U013` | **Definition/convention — Lie subgroup of G** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4274 | Suppose G is a Lie group.
