A Lie subgroup of G is a subgroup of G endowed with a topology and smooth structure making it into a Lie group and an immersed submanifold of G. The following proposition shows that embedded subgroups are automatically Lie subgroups. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U014` | **Proposition 7.11** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4276 | Let G be a Lie group, and suppose H ⊆ G is a subgroup that is also an embedded submanifold. Then H is a Lie subgroup. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U015` | **Lemma 7.12** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4282 | Suppose G is a Lie group and H ⊆ G is an open subgroup. Then H is an embedded Lie subgroup. In addition, H is closed, so it is a union of connected components of G. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U016` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4286 | If G is a group and S ⊆ G , the subgroup generated by S is the smallest subgroup containing S (i.e., the intersection of all subgroups containing S). | — |
| `FC08-C07-U017` | **Proposition 7.14** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4290 | Suppose G is a Lie group, and W ⊆ G is any neighborhood of the identity. (a) W generates an open subgroup of G . (b) If W is connected, it generates a connected open subgroup of G . (c) If G is connected, then W generates G . | — |
| `FC08-C07-U018` | **Definition/convention — identity component of G** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4316 | If G is a Lie group, the connected component of G containing the identity is called the identity component of G . | — |
| `FC08-C07-U019` | **Proposition 7.15** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4318 | Let G be a Lie group and let G _0 be its identity component. Then G 0 is a normal subgroup of G, and is the only connected open subgroup. Every connected component of G is diffeomorphic to G _0 | — |
| `FC08-C07-U020` | **Proposition 7.16** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4324 | Let F G H be a Lie group homomorphism. The kernel of F is a properly embedded Lie subgroup of G, whose codimension is equal to the rank of F . | — |
| `FC08-C07-U021` | **Proposition 7.17** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4330 | If F : G → H is an injective Lie group homomorphism, the image of F has a unique smooth manifold structure such that F .G/ is a Lie subgroup of H and F : G → F ( G ) is a Lie group isomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U022` | **Example 7.18** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4336 | (Embedded Lie Subgroups). (a) The subgroup GL ^+ ( n , ℝ ) ⊆ GL ( n , ℝ ) described in Example 7.3(b) is an open subgroup and thus an embedded Lie subgroup. (b) The circle mathbb S ^1 is an embedded Lie subgroup of ℂ * because it is a subgroup and an embedded submanifold. (c) The set SL ( n , ℝ ) of n × n real matrices with determinant equal to 1 is called the special linear group of degree n. Because the determinant function is surjective, it is a smooth submersion by the global rank theorem, so SL ( n , ℝ ) has dimension n ^2 - 1 (d) Let n be a positive integer, and define a map β : GL ( n , ℂ ) → GL ( 2 n , ℝ ) / by replacing each complex matrix entry a + i b with the 2 × 2 block binom a b ^- b _a ) β ( beginarrayc a _1 ^1 + i b _1 ^1 … a _1 ^n + i b _1 ^n vdots & vdots a _n ^1 + i b _n ^1 … a _n ^n + i b _n ^n endarray ) = ( beginarrayc c c c a _1 ^1 - b _1 ^1 & a _1 ^n - b _1 ^n b _1 ^1 & a _1 ^1 … & b _1 ^n & a _1 ^n vdots & & vdots a _n ^1 - b _n ^1 & a _n ^n - b _n ^n b _n ^1 & a _n ^1 … & b _n ^n & a _n ^n endarray ). It is straightforward to verify that β is an injective Lie group homomorphism whose image is a properly embedded Lie subgroup of GL ( 2 n , ℝ ) . (e) The subgroup SL ( n , ℂ ) ⊆ GL ( n , ℂ ) consisting of complex matrices of determinant 1 is called the complex special linear group of degree n. Therefore, SL ( n , ℂ ) = Ker ( d e t ) is a properly embedded Lie subgroup whose codimension is equal to dim ℂ * = 2 and whose dimension is therefore 2 n ^2 - 2 Finally, here is an example of a Lie subgroup that is not embedded. | `FC08-C07-U004` |
| `FC08-C07-U023` | **Example 7.19** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4356 | (A Dense Lie Subgroup of the Torus). Let H ⊆ mathbb T ^2 be the dense submanifold of the torus that is the image of the immersion γ : ℝ → mathbb T ^2 defined in Example 4.20. It is easy to check that γ is an injective Lie group homomorphism, and thus H is an immersed Lie subgroup of mathbb T ^2 by Proposition 7.17. // | `FC08-C04-U020`, `FC08-C07-U021` |
| `FC08-C07-U024` | **Theorem 7.21** | Ch. 7, § Lie Subgroups; source L4367 | Suppose G is a Lie group and H ⊆ G is a Lie subgroup. Then H is closed in G if and only if it is embedded. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U025` | **Definition/convention — group** | Ch. 7, § Group Actions and Equivariant Maps; source L4410 | The most important applications of Lie groups to smooth manifold theory involve actions by Lie groups on other manifolds. If G is a group and M is a set, a left action of G on M is a map G × M → M ; often written as ( g , p ) ↦ g · p , that satisfies beginarrayc c g _1 · (g _2 · p) = (g _1 g _2) · p & text for all g _1, g _2 ∈ G text and p ∈ M; e · p = p & text for all p ∈ M. endarraytag7.6 | — |
| `FC08-C07-U026` | **Definition/convention — a continuous action** | Ch. 7, § Group Actions and Equivariant Maps; source L4422 | If M is a topological space and G is a topological group, an action of G on M is said to be a continuous action if the defining map G × M → M ~ o r ~ M × G → M is continuous. In this case, M is said to be a (left or right) G -space. If in addition M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, G is a Lie group, and the defining map is smooth, then the action is said to be a smooth action. We are primarily interested in smooth actions of Lie groups on smooth manifolds. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U027` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 7, § Group Actions and Equivariant Maps; source L4438 | For left actions, we generally use the notations g · p and θ _g ( p ) interchangeably. The latter notation contains a bit more information, and is useful when it is important to specify the particular action under consideration, while the former is often more convenient when the action is understood. For right actions, the notation p · g is generally preferred because of the way composition works. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U028` | **Definition/convention — symmetry group of the structure) frequently turns out to be a Lie** | Ch. 7, § Group Actions and Equivariant Maps; source L4442 | Lie group actions typically arise in situations involving some kind of symmetry. For example, if M is a vector space or smooth manifold endowed with a metric or other geometric structure, the set of diffeomorphisms of M that preserve the structure (called the symmetry group of the structure) frequently turns out to be a Lie group acting smoothly on M . | — |
| `FC08-C07-U029` | **Definition/convention — transitive** | Ch. 7, § Group Actions and Equivariant Maps; source L4460 | The action is said to be transitive if for every pair of points p , q ∈ M ; there exists g ∈ G such that g · p = q , or equivalently if the only orbit is all of M . | — |
| `FC08-C07-U030` | **Definition/convention — free** | Ch. 7, § Group Actions and Equivariant Maps; source L4462 | The action is said to be free if the only element of G that fixes any element of M is the identity: g · p = p for some p ∈ M implies g = e , or equivalently if every isotropy group is trivial. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U031` | **Example 7.22** | Ch. 7, § Group Actions and Equivariant Maps; source L4466 | (Lie Group Actions). (a) If G is any Lie group and M is any smooth manifold, the trivial action of G on M is defined by g · p = p for all g ∈ G and p ∈ M . It is a smooth action, for which each orbit is a single point and each isotropy group is all of G (b) The natural action of GL ( n , ℝ ) o n ℝ ^n is the left action given by matrix multiplication: ( A , x ) ↦ A x , considering x ∈ ℝ ^n as a column matrix. Because any nonzero vector can be taken to any other by some invertible linear transformation, there are exactly two orbits: 0 and ℝ ^n setminus 0 (c) Every Lie group G acts smoothly on itself by left translation. More generally, if H is a Lie subgroup of G, then the restriction of the multiplication map to H × G → G defines a smooth and free (but generally not transitive) left action of H on G. (d) Every Lie group acts smoothly on itself by conjugation: g · h = g h g ^- 1 . (e) An action of a discrete group η on a manifold M is smooth if and only if for each g ∈ T , the map p ↦ g · p is a smooth map from M to itself. An automorphism of π (also called a deck transformation or covering transformation) is a homeomorphism φ : E → E such that π ∘ φ = π ; (7.8) The set A u t _π ( E ) of all automorphisms of π , called the automorphism group of π , is a group under composition, acting on E on the left. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U032` | **Definition/convention — normal covering map** | Ch. 7, § Group Actions and Equivariant Maps; source L4488 | The set A u t _π ( E ) of all automorphisms of π , called the automorphism group of π , is a group under composition, acting on E on the left. It can be shown that A u t _π ( E ) acts transitively on each fiber of π if and only if π is a normal covering map, which means that π _* ( π _1 ( E , q ) ) is a normal subgroup of π _1 ( M , π ( q ) ) for every q ∈ E (see, for example, [LeeTM, Cor. 12.5]). | — |
| `FC08-C07-U033` | **Proposition 7.23** | Ch. 7, § Group Actions and Equivariant Maps; source L4490 | Suppose E and M are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, and π : E → M is a smooth covering map. With the discrete topology, the automorphism group A u t _π ( E ) is a zero-dimensional Lie group acting smoothly and freely on E. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U034` | **Definition/convention — equivariant** | Ch. 7, § Equivariant Maps; source L4506 | For some manifolds with group actions, there is an easily verified sufficient condition for a smooth map to have constant rank. Suppose G is a Lie group, and M and N are both smooth manifolds endowed with smooth left or right G-actions. A map F : M → N is said to be equivariant with respect to the given G-actions if for each g ∈ G , beginarrayl l F (g · p) = g · F (p) & (text for left actions ), F (p · g) = F (p) · g & (text for right actions ). endarray Equivalently, if θ and ' are the given actions on M and N _: , respectively, F is equivariant if the following diagram commutes for each g ∈ G : beginarrayc M xrightarrow F N theta_g Big downarrow Big downarrow varphi_g M xrightarrow F N. endarray This condition is also expressed by saying that F intertwines θ and φ . | — |
| `FC08-C07-U035` | **Example 7.24** | Ch. 7, § Equivariant Maps; source L4520 | Let v = ( v ^1 , … , v ^n ) ∈ ℝ ^n be any fixed nonzero vector. Define smooth left actions of R on ℝ ^n and vec mathbb T ^n by t · (x ^1, … , x ^n) = (x ^1 + t v ^1, … , x ^n + t v ^n), t · (z ^1, … , z ^n) = (e ^ 2 π i t v ^1 z ^1, … , e ^ 2 π i t v ^n z ^n), for t ∈ ℝ , ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) ∈ ℝ ^n , and ( z ^1 , … , z ^n ) ∈ mathbb T ^n The smooth map ε ^n : ℝ ^n → T n given by ε ^n ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) = ( e ^ 2 π i x ^1 , … , e ^ 2 π i x ^n ) is equivariant with respect to these actions. The following generalization of Theorem 7.5 is an extremely useful tool for proving that certain maps have constant rank. | `FC08-C07-U008` |
| `FC08-C07-U036` | **Theorem 7.25** | Ch. 7, § Equivariant Maps; source L4537 | (Equivariant Rank Theorem). Let M and N be smooth manifolds and let G be a Lie group. Suppose F : M → N is a smooth map that is equivariant with respect to a transitive smooth G-action on M and any smooth G-action on N . Then F has constant rank. Thus, if F is surjective, it is a smooth submersion; if it is injective, it is a smooth immersion; and if it is bijective, it is a diffeomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U037` | **Definition/convention — orbit map** | Ch. 7, § Equivariant Maps; source L4547 | Here is an important application of the equivariant rank theorem. Suppose G is a Lie group, M is a smooth manifold, and θ : G × M → M is a smooth left action. (The definitions for right actions are analogous.) For each p ∈ M , ; define a map θ .p/ G M by θ^(p) (g) = g · p.tag7.9 This is often called the orbit map, because its image is the orbit G · p . In addition, the preimage ( θ ^( p ) ) ^- 1 ( p ) is the isotropy group G _p | — |
| `FC08-C07-U038` | **Proposition 7.26** | Ch. 7, § Equivariant Maps; source L4557 | (Properties of the Orbit Map). Suppose θ is a smooth left action of a Lie group G on a smooth manifold M . For each p ∈ M , ; the o r - bit map θ ^( p ) : G → M is smooth and has constant rank, so the isotropy group G _p = ( θ ^( p ) ) ^- 1 ( p ) is a properly embedded Lie subgroup of G. If G _p = e , then θ ^( p ) is an injective smooth immersion, so the orbit G p is an immersed submanifold of M . | — |
| `FC08-C07-U039` | **Example 7.27** | Ch. 7, § Equivariant Maps; source L4585 | (The Orthogonal Group). A real n × n matrix A is said to be orthogonal if as a linear map A : ℝ ^n → ℝ ^n it preserves the Euclidean dot product: (A x) · (A y) = x · y text for all x, y ∈ ℝ ^n. It is compact because it is closed and bounded in M ( n , ℝ ) ≅ ℝ ^ n ^2 : closed because it is a level set of varPhi and bounded because every A ∈ O ( n ) has columns of norm 1, and therefore satisfies ∣ A ∣ = sqrt n To determine the dimension of O ( n ) , we need to compute the rank of varPhi . The set O.n/ of all orthogonal n × n matrices is a subgroup of GL.n; R/, called the orthogonal group of degree n. It is easy to check that a matrix A is orthogonal if and only if it takes the standard basis of ℝ ^n to an orthonormal basis, which is equivalent to the columns of A being orthonormal. To show that varPhi has constant rank and therefore that O ( n ) is an embedded Lie subgroup, we show that varPhi is equivariant with respect to suitable right actions of GL.n; R/. Thus for any B ∈ T _ I _n bf GL ( n , ℝ ) = bf M ( n , ℝ ) , let γ : ( - ε , ε ) → GL ( n , ℝ ) be the curve γ ( t ) = I _n + t B , and compute d Phi_ I _n (B) = . It follows that the image of d Phi _ I _n is exactly the space of symmetric matrices. It follows that O ( n ) is an embedded Lie subgroup of dimension n ^2 - n ( n + 1 ) / 2 = n ( n - 1 ) / 2 This is a linear subspace of M ( n , ℝ ) of dimension n ( n + 1 ) / 2 , because each symmetric matrix is uniquely determined by its values on and above the main diagonal. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U040` | **Example 7.28** | Ch. 7, § Equivariant Maps; source L4615 | (The Special Orthogonal Group). The special orthogonal group of degree n is defined as SO ( n ) = O ( n ) ∩ SL ( n , ℝ ) ⊆ GL ( n , ℝ ) . Because every matrix A ∈ O ( n ) satisfies 1 = det I _n = det (A ^T A ) = (det A) (det A ^T ) = (det A) ^2, it follows that det A = ± 1 for all A ∈ O ( n ) . Therefore, SO.n/ is the open subgroup of O ( n ) consisting of matrices of positive determinant, and is therefore also an embedded Lie subgroup of dimension n ( n - 1 ) / 2 in GL.n; R/. It is a compact group because it is a closed subset of O ( n ) | — |
| `FC08-C07-U041` | **Example 7.29** | Ch. 7, § Equivariant Maps; source L4623 | (The Unitary Group). For any complex matrix A , , the adjoint of A is the matrix A * formed by conjugating the entries of A and taking the transpose: A * = overline A ^T Observe that ( A B ) * = ( bar(A) bar(B) ) ^T = bar(B) ^T bar(A) ^T = B * A * . For any positive integer n, the unitary group of degree n is the subgroup U ( n ) ⊆ GL ( n , ℂ ) consisting of complex n × n matrices whose columns form an orthonormal basis for ℂ ^n with respect to the Hermitian dot product beginarray r z · w = sum _i z ^i overline w ^i endarray . It is straightforward to check that U.n/ consists of those matrices A such that overline A * A = I _n . Problem 7-13 shows that it is a properly embedded Lie subgroup of GL ( n , ℂ ) of dimension n ^2 // | — |
| `FC08-C07-U042` | **Example 7.30** | Ch. 7, § Equivariant Maps; source L4625 | (The Special Unitary Group). The group S U ( n ) = U ( n ) ∩ SL.n; C/ is called the complex special unitary group of degree n. Problem 7-14 shows that it is a properly embedded ( n ^2 - 1 ) /-dimensional Lie subgroup of U.n/. Since the composition of smooth embeddings S U ( n ) ↪ U ( n ) ↪ GL ( n , ℂ ) is again a smooth embedding, this implies that SU.n/ is also embedded in GL.n; C/. // | — |
| `FC08-C07-U043` | **Construction/definition — semidirect product of H** | Ch. 7, § Semidirect Products; source L4629 | Group actions give us a powerful new way to construct Lie groups. Suppose H and N are Lie groups, and θ : H × N → N is a smooth left action of H on N . It is said to be an action by automorphisms if for each h ∈ H , the map θ _h : N → N is a group automorphism of N (i.e., an isomorphism from N to itself). Given such an action, we define a new Lie group N rtimes _θ H , called a semidirect product of H and N , as follows. As a smooth manifold, N Ì θ H is just the Cartesian product N × H ; but the group multiplication is defined by (n, h) (n ^prime, h ^prime) = (n theta_h (n ^prime), h h ^prime ).tag7.10 | — |
| `FC08-C07-U044` | **Example 7.32** | Ch. 7, § Semidirect Products; source L4639 | (The Euclidean Group). If we consider ℝ ^n as a Lie group under addition, then the natural action of O.n/ on ℝ ^n is an action by automorphisms. The resulting semidirect product E ( n ) = ℝ ^n rtimes O ( n ) is called the Euclidean group; its multiplication is given by ( b , A ) ( b ^prime , A ^prime ) = ( b + A b ^prime , A A ^prime ) . It acts on ℝ ^n via (b, A) · x = b + A x. This action preserves lines, distances, and angle measures, and thus all of the relationships of Euclidean geometry. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U045` | **Proposition 7.33** | Ch. 7, § Semidirect Products; source L4649 | (Properties of Semidirect Products). Suppose N and H are Lie groups, and θ is a smooth action of H on N by automorphisms. Let G = N rtimes _θ H (a) The subsets tilde(N) = N × e and tilde(H) = e × H are closed Lie subgroups of G isomorphic to N and H , respectively. (b) tilde(N) is a normal subgroup of Gz. (c) tilde(N) ∩ tilde(H) = ( e , e ) and Nz Hz G. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U046` | **Theorem 7.35** | Ch. 7, § Semidirect Products; source L4661 | (Characterization of Semidirect Products). Suppose G is a Lie group, and N , H ⊆ G are closed Lie subgroups such that N is normal, N H e , and N H = G . Then the map .n; h/ nh is a Lie group isomorphism between N Ì H and G, where θ : H × N → N is the action by conjugation: θ _h ( n ) = hnh -1 . | — |
| `FC08-C07-U047` | **Construction/definition — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 7, § Semidirect Products; source L4665 | Under the hypotheses of Theorem (7.35), we say that G is the internal semidirect product of N and H . Some examples are described in Problem 7-20. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U048` | **Definition/convention — faithful** | Ch. 7, § Representations; source L4673 | If a representation ρ : G → GL ( V ) is injective, it is said to be faithful. In that case, it follows from Proposition 7.17 that the image of ρ is a Lie subgroup of GL.V /, and ρ gives a Lie group isomorphism between G and ρ ( G ) ⊆ GL ( V ) ≅ GL.n; R/ or GL.n; C/. Thus, a Lie group admits a faithful representation if and only if it is isomorphic to a Lie subgroup of GL.n; R/ or GL.n; C/ for some n. Not every Lie group admits such a representation. We do not yet have the technology to construct a counterexample, but Problem 21-26 asks you to prove that the universal covering group of SL.2; R/ has no faithful representation and therefore is not isomorphic to any matrix group. | `FC08-C07-U021` |
| `FC08-C07-U049` | **Example 7.36** | Ch. 7, § Representations; source L4677 | (Lie Group Representations). (a) If G is any Lie subgroup of GL.n; R/, the inclusion map cal G ↪ GL ( n , ℝ ) = GL ( ℝ ^n ) is a faithful representation, called the defining representation of G . The defining representation of a Lie subgroup of GL ( n , ℂ ) is defined similarly. (b) The inclusion map mathbb S ^1 ↪ ℂ * ≅ GL ( 1 , ℂ ) is a faithful representation of the circle group. More generally, the map ρ : mathbb T ^n → GL ( n , ℂ ) given by ρ (z ^1, … , z ^n ) = ( beginarrayc c c c z ^1 & 0 & … & 0 0 & z ^2 & … & 0 vdots & vdots & ddots & vdots 0 & 0 & … & z ^n endarray ) is a faithful representation of mathbb T ^n (c) Let σ : ℝ ^n → GL ( n + 1 , ℝ ) be the map that sends x ∈ ℝ ^n to the matrix σ ( x ) defined in block form by σ (x) = ( beginarrayc c I _n & x 0 & 1 endarray ), where I _n is the n × n identity matrix and x is regarded as an n × 1 column matrix. A straightforward computation shows that σ is a faithful representation of the additive Lie g r o u p ℝ ^n (d) Another faithful representation of ℝ ^n is the map ℝ ^n → GL ( n , ℝ ) that sends ( overline x ^1 , … , x ^n ) to the diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are ( e ^ x ^1 , … , e ^ x ^n ) (e) Yet another representation of ℝ ^n is the map ℝ ^n → GL ( n , ℂ ) sending x to the diagonal matrix with diagonal entries ( e ^ 2 π i x ^1 , … , e ^ 2 π i x ^n ) This one is not faithful, because its kernel is the subgroup ℤ ^n ⊆ ℝ ^n . (f) Let E.n/ be the Euclidean group (Example 7.32). A faithful representation of E.n/ is given by the map ρ : E ( n ) GL ( n + 1 , ℝ ) defined in block form by ρ (b, A) = ( beginarrayc c A & b 0 & 1 endarray ), where b is considered as a column matrix. (g) For positive integers n and d . , let mathcal P _d ^n denote the vector space of real-valued polynomial functions p : ℝ ^n → ℝ of degree at most d . For any matrix A ∈ GL ( n , ℝ ) define a linear map τ _d ^n ( A ) : P _d ^n → P _d ^n by tau_d ^n (A) p = p ∘ A ^- 1. | `FC08-C07-U044` |
| `FC08-C07-U050` | **Definition/convention — a linear action** | Ch. 7, § Representations; source L4717 | There is a close connection between representations and group actions. Let G be a Lie group and V be a finite-dimensional vector space. An action of G on V is said to be a linear action if for each g ∈ G , the map from V to itself given by x ↦ g · x is linear. For example, if ρ : G → GL ( V ) is a representation of G, there is an associated smooth linear action of G on V given by g · x = ρ ( g ) x . The next proposition shows that every linear action is of this type. | — |
| `FC08-C07-U051` | **Proposition 7.37** | Ch. 7, § Representations; source L4719 | Let G be a Lie group and V be a finite-dimensional vector space. A smooth left action of G on V is linear if and only if it is of the form g · x = ρ ( g ) x for some representation ρ of G. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U001` | **Convention/notation — vector field** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4877 | If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, a vector field on M is a section of the map π : T M → M . More concretely, a vector field is a continuous map X : M → T M ; usually written p ↦ X _p , with the property that π ∘ X = Id _M,tag8.1 or equivalently, X _p ∈ T _p M for each p ∈ M . (We write the value of X at p as X _p instead of X ( p ) to be consistent with our notation for elements of the tangent bundle, as well as to avoid conflict with the notation v ( f ) for the action of a vector on a function.) You should visualize a vector field on M in the same way as you visualize vector fields in Euclidean space: as an arrow attached to each point of M; chosen to be tangent to M and to vary continuously from point to point (Fig. 8.1). | — |
| `FC08-C08-U002` | **Definition/convention — compactly supported** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4888 | We are primarily interested in smooth vector fields, the ones that are smooth as maps from M to TM; when TM is given the smooth manifold structure described in Proposition 3.18. In addition, for some purposes it is useful to consider maps from M to TM that would be vector fields except that they might not be continuous. A rough vector field on M is a (not necessarily continuous) map X : M → T M satisfying (8.1). Just as for functions, if X is a vector field on M; the support of X is defined to be the closure of the set p ∈ M : X _p ≠ 0 . A vector field is said to be compactly supported if its support is a compact set. | `FC08-C03-U031` |
| `FC08-C08-U003` | **Definition/convention — component functions of X in the given chart** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4890 | Suppose M is a smooth n-manifold (with or without boundary). If X : M → T M is a rough vector field and ( U , ( x ^i ) ) is any smooth coordinate chart for M; we can write the value of X at any point p ∈ U in terms of the coordinate basis vectors: X _p = X ^i (p) . (∂)/(∂ x ^i) ∣ _p.tag8.2 This defines n functions X ^i : U ℝ , called the component functions of X in the given chart. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U004` | **Proposition 8.1** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4898 | (Smoothness Criterion for Vector Fields). Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and let X : M → T M be a rough vector field. I f ( U , ( x ^i ) ) is any smooth coordinate chart on M; then the restriction of X to U is smooth if and only if its component functions with respect to this chart are smooth. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U005` | **Example 8.2** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4908 | (Coordinate Vector Fields). If ( U , ( x ^i ) ) is any smooth chart on M , the assignment p ↦ . (∂)/(∂ x ^i) ∣ _p determines a vector field on U , called the i th coordinate vector field and denoted by ∂ / ∂ x ^i . It is smooth because its component functions are constants. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U006` | **Example 8.3** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4916 | (The Euler Vector Field). The vector field V on ℝ ^n whose value at x ∈ ℝ ^n is V _x = x ^1 . (∂)/(∂ x ^1) ∣ _x + … + x ^n . (∂)/(∂ x ^n) ∣ _x is smooth because its coordinate functions are linear. It vanishes at the origin, and points radially outward everywhere else. It is called the Euler vector field because of its appearance in Euler’s homogeneous function theorem (see Problem 8-2). | — |
| `FC08-C08-U007` | **Example 8.4** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4924 | (The Angle Coordinate Vector Field on the Circle). Let θ be any angle coordinate on a proper open subset U ⊆ mathbb S ^1 (see Problem 1-8), and let d / d θ denote the corresponding coordinate vector field. It is a smooth vector field because its component function is constant in any such chart. We denote this global vector field by d / d θ , even though, strictly speaking, it cannot be considered as a coordinate vector field on the entire circle at once. // | — |
| `FC08-C08-U008` | **Example 8.5** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4926 | (Angle Coordinate Vector Fields on Tori). On the n-dimensional torus mathbb T ^n , choosing an angle function θ ^i for the i th circle factor, i = 1 , … , n , yields local coordinates ( θ ^1 , … , θ ^n ) for mathbb T ^n . An analysis similar to that of the previous example shows that the coordinate vector fields ∂ / ∂ θ ^1 , … , ∂ / ∂ θ ^n are smooth and globally defined on mathbb T ^n // | — |
| `FC08-C08-U009` | **Definition/convention — vector field along a subset** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4930 | The next lemma is a generalization of Lemma 2.26 to vector fields, and is proved in much the same way. If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary and A ⊆ M is an arbitrary subset, a vector field along A is a continuous map X : A → TM satisfying π ∘ X = I d _A (or in other words X _p ∈ T _p M for each p ∈ A ) . We call it a smooth vector field along A if for each p ∈ A , there is a neighborhood V of p in M and a smooth vector field tilde(X) on V that agrees with X on V ∩ A | `FC08-C02-U033` |
| `FC08-C08-U010` | **Lemma 8.6** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4932 | (Extension Lemma for Vector Fields). Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and let A ⊆ M be a closed subset. Suppose X is a smooth vector field along A. Given any open subset U containing A, there exists a smooth global vector field tilde(X) on M such that tilde(X) ∣ _A = X and supp tilde(X) ⊆ U | — |
| `FC08-C08-U011` | **Proposition 8.7** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4938 | Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. Given p ∈ M and v ∈ T _p M ; there is a smooth global vector field X on M such tha X _p = v | — |
| `FC08-C08-U012` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4942 | If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, it is standard to use the notation X ( M ) to denote the set of all smooth vector fields on M . It is a vector space under pointwise addition and scalar multiplication: (a X + b Y) _p = a X _p + b Y _p. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U013` | **Definition/convention — f X** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4948 | The zero element of this vector space is the zero vector field, whose value at each p ∈ M is 0 ∈ T _p M . In addition, smooth vector fields can be multiplied by smooth real-valued functions: if f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) and X ∈ X ( M ) , we define f X : M → T M by (f X) _p = f (p) X _p. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U014` | **Proposition 8.8** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields on Manifolds; source L4956 | Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. (a) If X and Y are smooth vector fields on M and f; g ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) , then f X + g Y is a smooth vector field. (b) X.M / is a module over the ring C ^∞ ( M ) . | — |
| `FC08-C08-U015` | **Convention/notation — global frame** | Ch. 8, § Local and Global Frames; source L4976 | Suppose M is a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary. An ordered k - tuple ( X _1 , … , X _k ) of vector fields defined on some subset A ⊆ M is said to be linearly independent if ( X _1 ∣ _p , … , X _k ∣ _p ) is a linearly independent k-tuple in T _p M for each p ∈ A , and is said to span the tangent bundle if the k-tuple ( X _1 ∣ _p , … , X _k ∣ _p ) spans T _p M at each p ∈ A . A local frame for M is an ordered n-tuple of vector fields ( E _1 , … , E _n ) defined on an open subset U ⊆ M that is linearly independen and spans the tangent bundle; thus the vectors ( E _1 ∣ _p , … , E _n ∣ _p ) form a basis for T _p M at each p ∈ U . It is called a global frame if U = M ; and a smooth frame if each of the vector fields E _i is smooth. We often use the shorthand notation ( E _i ) to denote a frame ( E _1 , … , E _n ) . If M has dimension n, then to check that an ordered n-tuple of vector fields ( E _1 , … , E _n ) is a local frame, it suffices to check either that it is linearly independent or that it spans the tangent bundle. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U016` | **Example 8.10** | Ch. 8, § Local and Global Frames; source L4978 | (Local and Global Frames). (a) The standard coordinate vector fields form a smooth global frame for ℝ ^n (b) If ( U , ( x ^i ) ) is any smooth coordinate chart for a smooth manifold M (possibly with boundary), then the coordinate vector fields form a smooth local frame ( ∂ / ∂ x ^i ) on U , called a coordinate frame. Every point of M is in the domain of such a local frame. (c) The vector field d / d θ defined in Example 8.4 constitutes a smooth global frame for the circle. (d) The n-tuple of vector fields ( ∂ / ∂ θ ^1 , … , ∂ / ∂ θ ^n ) on the n-torus, defined in Example 8.4, is a smooth global frame for mathbb T ^n | `FC08-C08-U007` |
| `FC08-C08-U017` | **Proposition 8.11** | Ch. 8, § Local and Global Frames; source L4990 | (Completion of Local Frames). Let M be a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary. (a) If ( X _1 , … , X _k ) is a linearly independent k-tuple of smooth vector fields on an open subset U ⊆ M ; with 1 ≤ k < n , then for each p ∈ U there exist smooth vector fields X _k + 1 , … , X _n in a neighborhood V of p such that ( X _1 , … , X _n ) is a smooth local frame for M on U ∩ V (b) H ( v _1 , … , v _k ) is a linearly independent k-tuple of vectors in T _p M for some p ∈ M ; with 1 ≤ k ≤ n , then there exists a smooth local frame ( X _i ) on a neighborhood of p such that X _i ∣ _p = v _i f o r i = 1 , . . . , k (c) H f ( X _1 , … , X _n ) is a linearly independent n-tuple of smooth vector fields along a closed subset A ⊆ M ; then there exists a smooth local frame ( tilde(X) _1 , … , tilde(X) _n ) on some neighborhood of A such that tilde(X) _i ∣ _A = X _i f o r i = 1 , . . . , n | — |
| `FC08-C08-U018` | **Definition/convention — orthonormal frame** | Ch. 8, § Local and Global Frames; source L5000 | For subsets of ℝ ^n , there is a special type of frame that is often more useful for geometric problems than arbitrary frames. A k-tuple of vector fields ( E _1 , … , E _k ) defined on some subset A ⊆ ℝ ^n is said to be orthonormal if for each p ∈ A , the vectors ( E _1 ∣ _p , … , E _k ∣ _p ) are orthonormal with respect to the Euclidean dot product (where we identify T _p ℝ ^n with ℝ ^n in the usual way). A (local or global) frame consisting of orthonormal vector fields is called an orthonormal frame. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U019` | **Example 8.12** | Ch. 8, § Local and Global Frames; source L5002 | The standard coordinate frame is a global orthonormal frame on ℝ ^n For a less obvious example, consider the smooth vector fields defined on ℝ ^2 setminus 0 by E _1 = (x)/(r) (∂)/(∂ x) + (y)/(r) (∂)/(∂ y), E _2 = - (y)/(r) (∂)/(∂ x) + (x)/(r) (∂)/(∂ y),tag8.3 where r = sqrt x ^2 + y ^2 . A straightforward computation shows that ( E _1 , E _2 ) is an orthonormal frame for ℝ ^2 over the open subset ℝ ^2 setminus 0 . Geometrically, E _1 and E _2 are unit vector fields tangent to radial lines and circles centered at the origin, respectively. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U020` | **Lemma 8.13** | Ch. 8, § Local and Global Frames; source L5012 | (Gram–Schmidt Algorithm for Frames). Suppose ( X _j ) is a smooth local frame for T ℝ ^n over an open subset U ⊆ ℝ ^n . Then there is a smooth orthonormal frame ( E _j ) over U such that span ( E _1 ∣ _p , … , E _j ∣ _p ) = s p a n ( X _1 ∣ _p , … , X _j ∣ _p ) for each j = 1 , …c , n and each p ∈ U | — |
| `FC08-C08-U021` | **Definition/convention — parallelizable** | Ch. 8, § Local and Global Frames; source L5022 | Although smooth local frames are plentiful, global ones are not. A smooth manifold with or without boundary is said to be parallelizable if it admits a smooth global frame. Example 8.10 shows that ℝ ^n , mathbb S ^1 , and mathbb T ^n are all parallelizable. Problems 8-6 and 8-7 show that mathbb S ^3 and mathbb S ^7 are parallelizable. Later in this chapter, we will see that all Lie groups are parallelizable (see Corollary 8.39 below). However, despite the evidence of these examples, most smooth manifolds are not parallelizable. (As we will see in Chapter 10, parallelizability of M is intimately connected to the question of whether its tangent bundle is diffeomorphic to the product M × ℝ ^n . , ) | `FC08-C08-U016` |
| `FC08-C08-U022` | **Proposition 8.14** | Ch. 8, § Local and Global Frames; source L5042 | Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and le X M TM be a rough vector field. The following are equivalent: (a) X is smooth. (b) For every f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) , the function Xf is smooth on M . (c) For every open subset U ⊆ M and every f ∈ C ^∞ ( U ) , the function X f is smooth on U | — |
| `FC08-C08-U023` | **Definition/convention — derivation (as distinct** | Ch. 8, § Local and Global Frames; source L5076 | as you can easily check by evaluating both sides at an arbitrary point p ∈ M . In general, a map X : C ^∞ ( M ) → C ^∞ ( M ) is called a derivation (as distinct from a derivation at p , defined in Chapter 3) if it is linear over R and satisfies (8.5) for all f , g ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) | — |
| `FC08-C08-U024` | **Proposition 8.15** | Ch. 8, § Local and Global Frames; source L5080 | Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. A map D : C ^∞ ( M ) → C ^∞ ( M ) is a derivation if and only if it is of the form D f = X f f o r some smooth vector field X ∈ X ( M ) . | — |
| `FC08-C08-U025` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields and Smooth Maps; source L5099 | Suppose F : M → N is smooth and X is a vector field on M; and suppose there happens to be a vector field Y on N with the property that for each p ∈ M , d F _p ( X _p ) = Y _F ( p ) . In this case, we say the vector fields X and Y are F -related (see F i g . 8 . 3 ) . The next proposition shows how F -related vector fields act on smooth functions. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U026` | **Proposition 8.16** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields and Smooth Maps; source L5101 | Suppose F : M → N is a smooth map between manifolds with or without boundary, X ∈ X ( M ) , and Y ∈ X ( N ) . Then X and Y are F -related if and only if for every smooth real-valued function f defined on an open subset of N , X (f ∘ F) = (Y f) ∘ F.tag8.6 | — |
| `FC08-C08-U027` | **Example 8.17** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields and Smooth Maps; source L5121 | Let F : ℝ → ℝ ^2 be the smooth map F ( t ) = ( cos t , sin t ) . Then d / d t ∈ X ( ℝ ) is F -related to the vector field Y ∈ X ( ℝ ^2 ) defined by Y = x (∂)/(∂ y) - y (∂)/(∂ x).tag | — |
| `FC08-C08-U028` | **Proposition 8.19** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields and Smooth Maps; source L5134 | Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, and F : M → N is a diffeomorphism. For every X ∈ X ( M ) , there is a unique smooth vector field on N that is F -related to X. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U029` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields and Smooth Maps; source L5150 | In the situation of the preceding proposition we denote the unique vector field that is F -related to X by F _* X , and call it the pushforward of X by F . Remember, it is only when F is a diffeomorphism that F _* X is defined. The proof of Proposition 8.19 shows that F _* X is defined explicitly by the formula (F _* X) _q = d F _F ^- 1 (q) (X _F ^- 1 (q) ).tag8.7 | `FC08-C08-U028` |
| `FC08-C08-U030` | **Example 8.20** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields and Smooth Maps; source L5158 | (Computing the Pushforward of a Vector Field). Let M and N be the following open submanifolds of ℝ ^2 : beginarrayl M = big (x, y): y > 0 text and x + y > 0 big , N = big (u, v): u > 0 text and v > 0 big , endarray and define F : M → N by F ( x , y ) = ( x + y , x / y + 1 ) . Then F is a diffeomorphism because its inverse is easily computed: just solve ( u , v ) = ( x + y , x / y + 1 ) for x and y to obtain the formula ( x , y ) = F ^- 1 ( u , v ) = ( u - u / v , ~ u / v ) . Let us compute the pushforward F _* X , where X is the following smooth vector field on M : X _(x, y) = y ^2 (∂)/(∂ x) Big ∣ _(x, y). The differential of F at a point ( x , y ) ∈ M is represented by its Jacobian matrix, D F (x, y) = ( beginarrayc c 1 & 1 (1)/(y) & - (x)/(y ^2) endarray ), and thus d F _F ^- 1 ( u , v ) is represented by the matrix D F (u - (u)/(v), (u)/(v) ) = ( beginarrayc c 1 & 1 (v)/(u) & (v - v ^2)/(u) endarray ). For any ( u , v ) ∈ N X _F ^- 1 (u, v) = (u ^2)/(v ^2) . (∂)/(∂ x) ∣ _F ^- 1 (u, v). Therefore, applying (8.7) with p = ( u , v ) yields the formula for F _* X (F _* X) _(u, v) = . (u ^2)/(v ^2) (∂)/(∂ u) ∣ _(u, v) + . (u)/(v) (∂)/(∂ v) ∣ _(u, v). The next corollary follows directly from Proposition 8.16. | `FC08-C08-U026` |
| `FC08-C08-U031` | **Corollary 8.21** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields and Smooth Maps; source L5196 | Suppose F : M → N is a diffeomorphism and X ∈ X ( M ) . For any f ∈ C ^∞ ( N ) ( (F _* X ) f ) ∘ F = X (f ∘ F). | — |
| `FC08-C08-U032` | **Definition/convention — tangent to S at p** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields and Submanifolds; source L5204 | If S ⊆ M is an immersed or embedded submanifold (with or without boundary), a vector field X on M does not necessarily restrict to a vector field on S, because X _p may not lie in the subspace T _p S ⊆ T _p M at a point p ∈ S Given a point p ∈ S a vector field X on M is said to be tangent to S at p if X _p ∈ T _p S ⊆ T _p M . It is tangent to S if it is tangent to S at every point of S (Fig. 8.4). | — |
| `FC08-C08-U033` | **Proposition 8.22** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields and Submanifolds; source L5206 | Let M be a smooth manifold, S ⊆ M be an embedded submanifold with or without boundary, and X be a smooth vector field on M . Then X is tangent to S if and only i f ( X f ) ∣ _S = 0 for every f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) such that f ∣ _S equiv 0 | — |
| `FC08-C08-U034` | **Proposition 8.23** | Ch. 8, § Vector Fields and Submanifolds; source L5217 | (Restricting Vector Fields to Submanifolds). Let M be a smooth manifold, let S ⊆ M be an immersed submanifold with or without boundary, and let ι S ↪ M denote the inclusion map. If Y ∈ X ( M ) is tangent to S, then there is a unique smooth vector field on S, denoted by Y ∣ _S , that is ι-related to Y . | — |
| `FC08-C08-U035` | **Example 8.24** | Ch. 8, § Lie Brackets; source L5231 | Define vector fields X = ∂ / ∂ x and Y = x ∂ / ∂ y on ℝ ^2 , and let f ( x , y ) = x , g ( x , y ) = y . Then direct computation shows that X Y ( f g ) = 2 x , while f X Y g + g X Y f = x , so X Y is not a derivation of C ^∞ ( ℝ ^2 ) // | — |
| `FC08-C08-U036` | **Lemma 8.25** | Ch. 8, § Lie Brackets; source L5241 | The Lie bracket of any pair of smooth vector fields is a smooth vector field. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U037` | **Proposition 8.26** | Ch. 8, § Lie Brackets; source L5259 | (Coordinate Formula for the Lie Bracket). Let X; Y be smooth vector fields on a smooth manifold M with or without boundary, and let X = X ^i ∂ / ∂ x ^i and Y = Y ^j ∂ / ∂ x ^j be the coordinate expressions for X and Y in terms of some smooth local coordinates ( x ^i ) for M . Then [ X , Y ] has the following coordinate expression: [ X, Y ] = (X ^i (∂ Y ^j)/(∂ x ^i) - Y ^i (∂ X ^j)/(∂ x ^i) ) (∂)/(∂ x ^j),tag8.8 or more concisely, [ X, Y ] = big (X Y ^j - Y X ^j big) (∂)/(∂ x ^j).tag8.9 | — |
| `FC08-C08-U038` | **Example 8.27** | Ch. 8, § Lie Brackets; source L5287 | Define smooth vector fields X; Y ∈ X ( ℝ ^3 ) by X = x (∂)/(∂ x) + (∂)/(∂ y) + x (y + 1) (∂)/(∂ z), Y = (∂)/(∂ x) + y (∂)/(∂ z). Then (8.9) yields beginarrayl [ X, Y ] = X (1) (∂)/(∂ x) + X (y) (∂)/(∂ z) - Y (x) (∂)/(∂ x) - Y (1) (∂)/(∂ y) - Y big (x (y + 1) big) (∂)/(∂ z) = 0 (∂)/(∂ x) + 1 (∂)/(∂ z) - 1 (∂)/(∂ x) - 0 (∂)/(∂ y) - (y + 1) (∂)/(∂ z) = - (∂)/(∂ x) - y (∂)/(∂ z). endarray | — |
| `FC08-C08-U039` | **Proposition 8.28** | Ch. 8, § Lie Brackets; source L5303 | (Properties of the Lie Bracket). The Lie bracket satisfies the following identities for all X , Y , Z ∈ X ( M ) : (a) BILINEARITY: F o r a , b ∈ R, [ a X + b Y, Z ] = a [ X, Z ] + b [ Y, Z ], [ Z, a X + b Y ] = a [ Z, X ] + b [ Z, Y ]. (b) ANTISYMMETRY: [ X, Y ] = - [ Y, X ]. (c) JACOBI IDENTITY: [ X, [ Y, Z ] ] + [ Y, [ Z, X ] ] + [ Z, [ X, Y ] ] = 0. (d) For f , g ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) /, [ f X, g Y ] = f g [ X, Y ] + (f X g) Y - (g Y f) X.tag8.11 | — |
| `FC08-C08-U040` | **Proposition 8.30** | Ch. 8, § Lie Brackets; source L5345 | (Naturality of the Lie Bracket). Let F : M → N be a smooth map between manifolds with or without boundary, and let X _1 , X _2 ∈ X ( M ) and Y _1 , Y _2 ∈ X ( N ) be vector fields such that X _i is F -related to Y _i f o r i = 1 , 2 . Then [ X _1 , X _2 ] is F -related to [ Y _1 , Y _2 ] | — |
| `FC08-C08-U041` | **Corollary 8.31** | Ch. 8, § Lie Brackets; source L5371 | (Pushforwards of Lie Brackets). Suppose F : M → N is a diffeomorphism and X _1 , X _2 ∈ X ( M ) . Then F _* [ X _1 , X _2 ] = [ F _* X _1 , F _* X _2 ] . | — |
| `FC08-C08-U042` | **Corollary 8.32** | Ch. 8, § Lie Brackets; source L5377 | (Brackets of Vector Fields Tangent to Submanifolds). Let M be a smooth manifold and let S be an immersed submanifold with or without boundary in M . If Y _1 and Y _2 are smooth vector fields on M that are tangent to S, then [ Y _1 , Y _2 ] is also tangent to S . | — |
| `FC08-C08-U043` | **Definition/convention — left-invariant** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5383 | One of the most important applications of Lie brackets occurs in the context of Lie groups. Suppose G is a Lie group. Recall that G acts smoothly and transitively on itself by left translation: L _g ( h ) = g h . (See Example 7.22(c).) A vector field X on G is said to be left-invariant if it is invariant under all left translations, in the sense that it is L _g - r e l a t e d to itself for every g ∈ G . More explicitly, this means d (L _g) _g ^prime (X _g ^prime) = X _g g ^prime, forall g, g ^prime ∈ G.tag8.12 Since L _g is a diffeomorphism, this can be abbreviated by writing ( L _g ) _* X = X for every g ∈ G | `FC08-C07-U031` |
| `FC08-C08-U044` | **Proposition 8.33** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5393 | Let G be a Lie group, and suppose X and Y are smooth leftinvariant vector fields on G. Then ŒX; Y  is also left-invariant. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U045` | **Definition/convention — bracket** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5403 | A Lie algebra (over R) is a real vector space g endowed with a map called the bracket from g × g to g, usually denoted by ( X , Y ) ↦ [ X , Y ] , that satisfies the following properties for all X; Y; Z ∈ g (i) BILINEARITY: For a; b ∈ ℝ [ a X + b Y, Z ] = a [ X, Z ] + b [ Y, Z ], [ Z, a X + b Y ] = a [ Z, X ] + b [ Z, Y ]. (ii) ANTISYMMETRY: [ X, Y ] = - [ Y, X ]. (iii) JACOBI IDENTITY: [ X, [ Y, Z ] ] + [ Y, [ Z, X ] ] + [ Z, [ X, Y ] ] = 0. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U046` | **Definition/convention — Lie algebras** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5427 | Notice that the Jacobi identity is a substitute for associativity, which does not hold in general for brackets in a Lie algebra. It is useful in some circumstances to define Lie algebras over C or other fields, but we do not have any reason to consider such Lie algebras; thus all of our Lie algebras are assumed without further comment to be real. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U047` | **Definition/convention — Lie subalgebra of g** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5429 | If g is a Lie algebra, a linear subspace h ⊆ g is called a Lie subalgebra of g if it is closed under brackets. In this case h is itself a Lie algebra with the restriction of the same bracket. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U048` | **Definition/convention — Lie algebra homomorphism** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5431 | If g and h are Lie algebras, a linear map A : g h is called a Lie algebra homomorphism if it preserves brackets: A [ X , Y ] = [ A X , A Y ] . An invertible Lie algebra homomorphism is called a Lie algebra isomorphism. If there exists a Lie algebra isomorphism from g to h, we say that they are isomorphic as Lie algebras. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U049` | **Example 8.36** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5437 | (Lie Algebras). (a) The space X.M / of all smooth vector fields on a smooth manifold M is a Lie algebra under the Lie bracket by Proposition 8.28. (b) If G is a Lie group, the set of all smooth left-invariant vector fields on G is a Lie subalgebra of X.G/ and is therefore a Lie algebra. (c) The vector space M ( n , ℝ ) of n × n real matrices becomes an n ^2 -dimensional Lie algebra under the commutator bracket: [ A, B ] = A B - B A. When we are regarding M.n; R/ as a Lie algebra with this bracket, we denote it by gl.n; R/. (d) Similarly, g l ( n , ℂ ) is the 2 n ^2 -dimensional (real) Lie algebra obtained by endowing M ( n , ℂ ) with the commutator bracket. (e) If V is a vector space, the vector space of all linear maps from V to itself becomes a Lie algebra, which we denote by g l ( V ) , with the commutator bracket: [ A, B ] = A ∘ B - B ∘ A. Under our usual identification of n × n matrices with linear maps from ℝ ^n to itself, g l ( ℝ ^n ) is the same as g l ( n , ℝ ) (f) Any vector space V becomes a Lie algebra if we define all brackets to be zero. (The name reflects the fact that brackets in most Lie algebras, as in the preceding examples, are defined as commutators in terms of underlying associative products, so all brackets are zero precisely when the underlying product is commutative; it also reflects the connection between abelian Lie algebras and abelian Lie groups, which you will explore in Problems 8-25 and 20-7.) | `FC08-C08-U039` |
| `FC08-C08-U050` | **Definition/convention — abelian Lie algebra** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5464 | (f) Any vector space V becomes a Lie algebra if we define all brackets to be zero. Such a Lie algebra is said to be abelian. (The name reflects the fact that brackets in most Lie algebras, as in the preceding examples, are defined as commutators in terms of underlying associative products, so all brackets are zero precisely when the underlying product is commutative; it also reflects the connection between abelian Lie algebras and abelian Lie groups, which you will explore in Problems 8-25 and 20-7.) // | — |
| `FC08-C08-U051` | **Definition/convention — Lie algebra of G** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5466 | Example (b) is the most important one. The Lie algebra of all smooth leftinvariant vector fields on a Lie group G is called the Lie algebra of G , and is denoted by Lie.G/. (We will see below that the assumption of smoothness is redundant; see Corollary 8.38.) The fundamental fact is that Lie.G/ is finite-dimensional, and in fact has the same dimension as G itself, as the following theorem shows. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U052` | **Theorem 8.37** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5468 | Let G be a Lie group. The evaluation map " Lie.G/ T G, given by ".X / X e , is a vector space isomorphism. Thus, Lie.G/ is finite-dimensional, with dimension equal to dim G . | — |
| `FC08-C08-U053` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5496 | Given any vector v ∈ T _e G , we continue to use the notation v ^L to denote the smooth left-invariant vector field defined by (8.13). | — |
| `FC08-C08-U054` | **Corollary 8.38** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5500 | Every left-invariant rough vector field on a Lie group is smooth. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U055` | **Definition/convention — left-invariant frame** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5504 | The existence of global left-invariant vector fields also yields the following important property of Lie groups. Recall that a smooth manifold is said to be parallelizable if it admits a smooth global frame. If G is a Lie group, a local or global frame consisting of left-invariant vector fields is called a left-invariant frame. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U056` | **Corollary 8.39** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5506 | Every Lie group admits a left-invariant smooth global frame, and therefore every Lie group is parallelizable. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U057` | **Example 8.40** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5510 | Let us determine the Lie algebras of some familiar Lie groups. (a) EUCLIDEAN SPACE ℝ ^n : If we consider ℝ ^n as a Lie group under addition, left translation by an element b ∈ ℝ ^n is given by the affine map L _b ( x ) = b + x whose differential d ( L _b ) is represented by the identity matrix in standard coordinates. Thus a vector field bar(X) ^i ∂ / ∂ x ^i is left-invariant if and only if its coefficients X ^i are constants. (b) THE CIRCLE GROUP mathbb S ^1 : In terms of appropriate angle coordinates, each left translation has a local coordinate representation of the form θ ↦ θ + c . Since the differential of this map is the 1 × 1 identity matrix, it follows that the vector field d / d θ defined in Example 8.4 is left-invariant, and is therefore a basis for the Lie algebra of mathbb S ^1 This Lie algebra is 1-dimensional and abelian, and therefore Lie ( mathbb S ^1 ) ≅ ℝ (c) THE n-TORUS mathbb T ^n = mathbb S ^1 × ·s × mathbb S ^1 : An analysis similar to the one above shows that ( ∂ / ∂ θ ^1 , … , ∂ / ∂ θ ^n ) is a basis for Lie ( mathbb T ^n ) /, where ∂ / ∂ θ ^i is the angle coordinate vector field on the ith mathbb S ^1 factor. Since the Lie brackets of these coordinate vector fields are all zero, Lie ( mathbb T ^n ) ≅ ℝ ^n | `FC08-C08-U007` |
| `FC08-C08-U058` | **Proposition 8.41** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5526 | (Lie Algebra of the General Linear Group). The composition of the natural maps Lie (GL (n, ℝ) ) → T _ I _n GL (n, ℝ) → g l (n, ℝ)tag8.14 gives a Lie algebra isomorphism between Lie big ( GL ( n , ℝ ) big ) and the matrix algebra g l ( n , ℝ ) | — |
| `FC08-C08-U059` | **Corollary 8.42** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Group; source L5580 | If V is any finite-dimensional real vector space, the composition of the canonical isomorphisms in (8.16) yields a Lie algebra isomorphism between Lie - GL.V / and gl.V /. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U060` | **Theorem 8.44** | Ch. 8, § Induced Lie Algebra Homomorphisms; source L5588 | (Induced Lie Algebra Homomorphisms). Let G and H be Lie groups, and let g and h be their Lie algebras. Suppose F : G → H is a Lie group homomorphism. For every X ∈ g , there is a unique vector field in h that is F -related to X. With this vector field denoted by F _* X , the map F _* : g → h so defined is a Lie algebra homomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U061` | **Definition/convention — induced Lie algebra homomorphism** | Ch. 8, § Induced Lie Algebra Homomorphisms; source L5615 | The map F _* : g → h whose existence is asserted in this theorem is called the induced Lie algebra homomorphism. Note that the theorem implies that for any left-invariant vector field X ∈ g , F _* X is a well-defined smooth vector field on H , even though F may not be a diffeomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U062` | **Proposition 8.45** | Ch. 8, § Induced Lie Algebra Homomorphisms; source L5617 | (Properties of Induced Homomorphisms). (a) The homomorphism ( I d _G ) _* : Lie ( G ) → Lie ( G ) induced by the identity map of G is the identity of Lie.G/. (b) If F _1 : G → H and F _2 : H → K are Lie group homomorphisms, then (F _2 ∘ F _1) _* = (F _2) _* ∘ (F _1) _* : Lie (G) → Lie (K). (c) Isomorphic Lie groups have isomorphic Lie algebras. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U063` | **Definition/convention — covariant functor** | Ch. 8, § Induced Lie Algebra Homomorphisms; source L5631 | The preceding proposition has a categorical interpretation, as you might have guessed. Let Lie denote the category whose objects are Lie groups and whose morphisms are Lie group homomorphisms, and lie the one whose objects are finitedimensional Lie algebras and whose morphisms are Lie algebra homomorphisms. Proposition 8.45 can be interpreted as showing that the assignments G ↦ Lie ( G ) , F ↦ F _* define a covariant functor from Lie to lie. | `FC08-C08-U062` |
| `FC08-C08-U064` | **Theorem 8.46** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Subgroup; source L5637 | (The Lie Algebra of a Lie Subgroup). Suppose H ⊆ G is a Lie subgroup, and iota : H ↪ G is the inclusion map. There is a Lie subalgebra h ⊆ Lie.G/ that is canonically isomorphic to Lie.H /, characterized by either of the following descriptions: beginarrayl h = iota_* big (Lie (H) big) = big X ∈ Lie (G): X _e ∈ T _e H big . endarraytag8.17 | — |
| `FC08-C08-U065` | **Example 8.47** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Subgroup; source L5649 | (The Lie Algebra of O.n/). The orthogonal group O.n/ is a Lie subgroup of GL.n; R/. By the result of Exercise 5.40, T _ I _n O ( n ) is equal to the kernel of d Phi _ I _n : T _ I _n GL ( n , ℝ ) → T _ I _n M ( n , ℝ ) By the computation in Example 7.27, this differential is d Phi _ I _n ( B ) = B ^T + B , so beginarrayc T _ I _n O (n) = big B ∈ g l (n, ℝ): B ^T + B = 0 big = text skew - symmetric n× n matrices . endarray We denote this subspace of gl.n; R/ by o.n/. Theorem 8.46 then implies that o.n/ is a Lie subalgebra of g l ( n , ℝ ) that is canonically isomorphic to Lie ( O ( n ) ) . Notice that we did not even have to verify directly that o.n/ is a subalgebra. | `FC08-C07-U039`, `FC08-C08-U064` |
| `FC08-C08-U066` | **Proposition 8.48** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Subgroup; source L5665 | (The Lie Algebra of GL ( n , ℂ ) ) . The composition of the maps in (8.18) yields a Lie algebra isomorphism between Lie ( GL ( n , ℂ ) ) and the matrix algebra gl ( n , ℂ ) | — |
| `FC08-C08-U067` | **Definition/convention — faithful** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Subgroup; source L5695 | Parallel to the notion of representations of Lie groups, there is also a notion of representations of Lie algebras. If g is a finite-dimensional Lie algebra, a (finitedimensional) representation of g is a Lie algebra homomorphism φ : g → g l ( V ) for some finite-dimensional vector space V , where gl.V / denotes the Lie algebra of linear maps from V to itself. If ' is injective, it is said to be faithful, in which case g is isomorphic to the Lie subalgebra φ ( g ) ⊆ g l ( V ) ≅ g l ( n , ℝ ) | — |
| `FC08-C08-U068` | **Theorem 8.49** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Subgroup; source L5701 | (Ado’s Theorem). Every finite-dimensional real Lie algebra admits a faithful finite-dimensional representation. | — |
| `FC08-C08-U069` | **Corollary 8.50** | Ch. 8, § The Lie Algebra of a Lie Subgroup; source L5703 | Every finite-dimensional real Lie algebra is isomorphic to a Lie subalgebra of some matrix algebra gl.n; R/ with the commutator bracket. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U001` | **Definition/convention — integral curve of V** | Ch. 9, § Integral Curves; source L5902 | If V is a vector field on M; an integral curve of V is a differentiable curve γ : J → M whose velocity at each point is equal to the value of V at that point: γ^prime (t) = V _γ (t) text for all t ∈ J. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U002` | **Definition/convention — starting point of γ** | Ch. 9, § Integral Curves; source L5908 | (See Fig. 9.1.) I f 0 ∈ J , the point γ ( 0 ) is called the starting point of γ . . (The reason for the term “integral curve” will be explained shortly. Note that this is one definition that requires some differentiability hypothesis, because the definition of an integra curve would make no sense for a curve that is merely continuous.) | — |
| `FC08-C09-U003` | **Example 9.1** | Ch. 9, § Integral Curves; source L5910 | (Integral Curves). (a) Let ( x , y ) be standard coordinates on ℝ ^2 , and let V = ∂ / ∂ x be the first coordinate vector field. Thus, there is a unique integral curve starting at each point of the plane, and the images of different integral curves are either identical or disjoint. (b) Let W = x ∂ / ∂ y - y ∂ / ∂ x on ℝ ^2 ( F i g . 9 . 2 ( b ) ) . Since γ ( 0 ) = ( a , b ) , we see once again that there is a unique integral curve starting at each point ( a , b ) ∈ ℝ ^2 , and the images of the various integral curves are either identical or disjoint. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U004` | **Convention/notation — system of ODEs** | Ch. 9, § Integral Curves; source L5953 | (We use a dot to denote an ordinary derivative with respect to t when there are superscripts that would make primes hard to read.) The fundamental fact about such systems is the existence, uniqueness, and smoothness theorem, Theorem D.1. (This is the reason for the terminology “integral curves,” because solving a system of ODEs is often referred to as “integrating” the system.) We will derive detailed consequences of that theorem later; for now, we just note the following simple result. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U005` | **Proposition 9.2** | Ch. 9, § Integral Curves; source L5955 | Let V be a smooth vector field on a smooth manifold M . For each point p ∈ M ; there exist ε > 0 and a smooth curve γ : ( - ε , ε ) → M that is an integral curve of V starting at p. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U006` | **Lemma 9.3** | Ch. 9, § Integral Curves; source L5964 | (Rescaling Lemma). Let V be a smooth vector field on a smooth manifold M; let J ⊆ ℝ be an interval, and let J → M be an integral curve of V . For any a ∈ ℝ , the curve widetilde γ : widetilde J M defined by tilde(γ) ( t ) = γ ( a t ) is an integral curve of the vector field aV , where tilde(J) = t : a t ∈ J | — |
| `FC08-C09-U007` | **Lemma 9.4** | Ch. 9, § Integral Curves; source L5972 | (Translation Lemma). Let V , M; J , and be as in the preceding lemma. For any b ∈ ℝ , the curve hat(γ) : hat(J) → M defined by hat(γ) ( t ) = γ ( t + b ) is also an integral curve of V , where hat(J) = t : t + b ∈ J | — |
| `FC08-C09-U008` | **Proposition 9.6** | Ch. 9, § Integral Curves; source L5976 | (Naturality of Integral Curves). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds and F : M → N is a smooth map. Then X ∈ X ( M ) and Y ∈ X ( N ) are F -related if and only if F takes integral curves of X to integral curves of Y , meaning that for each integral curve of X , F ∘ γ is an integral curve of Y . | — |
| `FC08-C09-U009` | **Convention/notation — map** | Ch. 9, § Flows; source L6000 | Here is another way to visualize the family of integral curves associated with a vector field. Let M be a smooth manifold and V ∈ X ( M ) , and suppose that for each point p ∈ M . V has a unique integral curve starting at p and defined for all t ∈ ℝ , which we denote by θ ^( p ) : ℝ → M . (It may not always be the case that every integral curve is defined for all t , but for purposes of illustration let us assume so for the time being.) For each t ∈ ℝ , we can define a map θ _t : M → M by sending each p ∈ M to the point obtained by following for time t the integral curve starting at p: theta_t (p) = θ^(p) (t). | — |
| `FC08-C09-U010` | **Definition/convention — one-parameter group action) to be a continuous left R-action** | Ch. 9, § Flows; source L6014 | Motivated by these observations, we define a global flow on M (also called a one-parameter group action) to be a continuous left R-action on M ; that is, a continuous map θ : ℝ × M → M satisfying the following properties for all s , t ∈ ℝ and p ∈ M θ (t, θ (s, p)) = θ (t + s, p), θ (0, p) = p.tag9.2 | — |
| `FC08-C09-U011` | **Definition/convention — two collections of maps as follows** | Ch. 9, § Flows; source L6020 | Given a global flow θ on M; we define two collections of maps as follows: For each t ∈ ℝ , define a continuous map θ _t : M → M by theta_t (p) = θ (t, p). The defining properties (9.2) are equivalent to the group laws theta_t ∘ theta_s = theta_t + s, theta_0 = Id _M.tag9.3 As is the case for any continuous group action, each map θ _t : M → M is a homeomorphism, and if the flow is smooth, θ _t is a diffeomorphism For each p ∈ M ; define a curve θ ^( p ) : ℝ → M by θ^(p) (t) = θ (t, p). The image of this curve is the orbit of p under the group action. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U012` | **Definition/convention — infinitesimal generator of θ** | Ch. 9, § Flows; source L6047 | The next proposition shows that every smooth global flow is derived from the integral curves of some smooth vector field in precisely the way we described above. If θ : ℝ × M → M is a smooth global flow, for each p ∈ M we define a tangent vector V _p ∈ T _p M by V _p = θ^(p) prime (0). The assignment p ↦ V _p is a (rough) vector field on M; which is called the infinitesimal generator of θ, for reasons we will explain below. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U013` | **Proposition 9.7** | Ch. 9, § Flows; source L6055 | Let θ : ℝ × M → M be a smooth global flow on a smooth manifold M . The infinitesimal generator V of θ is a smooth vector field on M; and each curve θ ^( p ) is an integral curve of V . | — |
| `FC08-C09-U014` | **Example 9.8** | Ch. 9, § Flows; source L6079 | (Global Flows). The two vector fields on the plane described in Example 9.1 both had integral curves defined for all t ∈ ℝ , so they generate global flows. Using the results of that example, we can write down the flows explicitly. | `FC08-C09-U003` |
| `FC08-C09-U015` | **Example 9.9** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6103 | Let M = ℝ ^2 setminus 0 with standard coordinates ( x , y ) , and let V be the vector field ∂ / ∂ x on M . The unique integral curve of V starting at ( - 1 , 0 ) ∈ M is γ ( t ) = ( t - 1 , 0 ) . But we can also consider γ as a map into ℝ ^2 by composing with the inclusion M ↪ ℝ ^2 , and it is obvious from the formula that γ ( t ) ( 0 , 0 ) as t nearrow 1 . Since limits in ℝ ^2 are unique, this is a contradiction. // | — |
| `FC08-C09-U016` | **Example 9.10** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6105 | For a more subtle example, let M be all of ℝ ^2 and let W = x ^2 ∂ / ∂ x You can check easily that the unique integral curve of W starting at .1; 0/ is γ (t) = ((1)/(1 - t), 0 ). This curve also cannot be extended past t = 1 , because its x-coordinate is unbounded as t nearrow 1 | — |
| `FC08-C09-U017` | **Definition/convention — flow domain for M** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6113 | For this reason, we make the following definitions. If M is a manifold, a flow domain for M is an open subset D ⊆ ℝ × M with the property that for each p ∈ M , the set D ^( p ) = t ∈ ℝ : ( t , p ) ∈ D is an open interval containing 0 (Fig. 9.5). A flow on M is a continuous map θ : D M ; where D ⊆ ℝ × M is a flow domain, that satisfies the following group laws: for all p ∈ M , | — |
| `FC08-C09-U018` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6130 | If θ is a flow, we define θ _t ( p ) = θ ^( p ) ( t ) = θ ( t , p ) whenever ( t , p ) ∈ D , just as for a global flow. For each t ∈ ℝ , we also define | — |
| `FC08-C09-U019` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6142 | If θ is smooth, the infinitesimal generator of ±b θ is defined by V _p = θ ^( p ) prime ( 0 ) | — |
| `FC08-C09-U020` | **Proposition 9.11** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6144 | If θ : D M is a smooth flow, then the infinitesimal generator V of θ is a smooth vector field, and each curve θ ^( p ) is an integral curve of V . | — |
| `FC08-C09-U021` | **Definition/convention — maximal integral curve** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6148 | The next theorem is the main result of this section. A maximal integral curve is one that cannot be extended to an integral curve on any larger open interval, and a maximal flow is a flow that admits no extension to a flow on a larger flow domain. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U022` | **Theorem 9.12** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6150 | (Fundamental Theorem on Flows). Let V be a smooth vector field on a smooth manifold M . There is a unique smooth maximal flow θ : D M whose infinitesimal generator is V . This flow has the following properties: (a) For each p ∈ M , ; the curve boldsymbol θ ^( p ) : D ^( p ) → M is the unique maximal integral curve of V starting at p. (b) I f s ∈ D ^( p ) , then bar D ^( θ ( s , p ) ) is the interval D ^( p ) - s = t - s : t ∈ D ^( p ) (c) For each t ∈ ℝ , the set M _t is open in M; and θ _t : M _t → M _- t is a diffeomorphism with inverse θ _- t . | — |
| `FC08-C09-U023` | **Definition/convention — flow generated by V** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6185 | The flow whose existence and uniqueness are asserted in the fundamental theorem is called the flow generated by V , or just the flow of V . The term “infinitesimal generator” comes from the following picture: in a smooth chart, a good approximation to an integral curve can be obtained by composing many small straight-line motions, with the direction and length of each motion determined by the value of the vector field at the point arrived at in the previous step. Intuitively, one can think of a flow as a sequence of infinitely many infinitesimally small linear steps. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U024` | **Proposition 9.13** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6189 | (Naturality of Flows). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds, F : M → N is a smooth map, X ∈ X ( M ) , and Y ∈ X ( N ) . Let θ be the flow of X and η the flow of Y . If X and Y are F -related, then for each t ∈ ℝ , F ( M _t ) ⊆ N _t and η _t ∘ F = F ∘ θ _t on M _t : beginarrayc M _t xrightarrow F N _t theta_t Big downarrow Big downarrow eta_t M _- t xrightarrow F N _- t. endarray | — |
| `FC08-C09-U025` | **Corollary 9.14** | Ch. 9, § The Fundamental Theorem on Flows; source L6211 | (Diffeomorphism Invariance of Flows). Let F : M → N be a diffeomorphism. If X ∈ X ( M ) and θ is the flow of X, then the flow of F _* X i s η _t = F ∘ θ _t ∘ F ^- 1 , with domain N _t = F ( M _t ) for each t ∈ ℝ | — |
| `FC08-C09-U026` | **Definition/convention — smooth vector field** | Ch. 9, § Complete Vector Fields; source L6215 | As we observed earlier in this chapter, not every smooth vector field generates a global flow. The ones that do are important enough to deserve a name. We say that a smooth vector field is complete if it generates a global flow, or equivalently if each of its maximal integral curves is defined for all t R. For example, both of the vector fields on the plane whose flows we computed in Example 9.8 are complete, whereas those of Examples 9.9 and 9.10 are not. | `FC08-C09-U014` |
| `FC08-C09-U027` | **Lemma 9.15** | Ch. 9, § Complete Vector Fields; source L6221 | (Uniform Time Lemma). Let V be a smooth vector field on a smooth manifold M; and let θ be its flow. | `FC08-C09-U028` | **Theorem 9.16** | Ch. 9, § Complete Vector Fields; source L6231 | Every compactly supported smooth vector field on a smooth manifold is complete. | — |

| `FC08-C09-U039` | **Theorem 9.24** | Ch. 9, § Flows and Flowouts on Manifolds with Boundary; source L6406 | (Boundary Flowout Theorem). | `FC08-C09-U042` | **Theorem 9.26** | Ch. 9, § Flows and Flowouts on Manifolds with Boundary; source L6418 | Let M be a smooth manifold with nonempty boundary, and let ι Int M ↪ M denote inclusion. | `FC08-C09-U047` | **Example 9.32** | Ch. 9, § Flows and Flowouts on Manifolds with Boundary; source L6494 | (The Double of a Smooth Manifold with Boundary). | `FC08-C09-U057` | **Definition/convention — invariant under** | Ch. 9, § Commuting Vector Fields; source L6628 | Let M be a smooth manifold and V; W ∈ X ( M ) We say that V and W commute if V W f = W V f for every smooth function f , or equivalently if [ V , W ] equiv 0 . | `FC08-C09-U058` | **Theorem 9.42** | Ch. 9, § Commuting Vector Fields; source L6630 | For smooth vector fields V and W on a smooth manifold M, the following are equivalent: (a) V and W commute. (b) W is invariant under the flow of V. (c) V is invariant under the flow of W. | — |
