If θ and are flows on M; we say that θ and commute if the following condition holds for every p ∈ M : whenever J and K are open intervals containing 0 such that one of the expressions θ _t ∘ ψ _s ( p ) or ψ _s ∘ θ _t ( p ) is defined for all ( s , t ) ∈ J × K , both are defined and they are equal. For global flows, this is the same as saying that θ _t ∘ ψ _s = ψ _s ∘ θ _t for all s and t . | — |
| `FC08-C09-U061` | **Theorem 9.44** | Ch. 9, § Commuting Vector Fields; source L6651 | Smooth vector fields commute if and only if their flows commute. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U062` | **Definition/convention — commuting frame** | Ch. 9, § Commuting Frames; source L6689 | Suppose M is a smooth n-manifold. Recall that a local frame for M is an n-tuple ( E _i ) of vector fields defined on an open subset U ⊆ M such that ( E _i ∣ _p ) forms a basis for T _p M at each p ∈ U . A smooth local frame ( E _i ) for M is called a commuting frame if [ E _i , E _j ] = 0 for all i and j . (Commuting frames are called holonomic frames by some authors.) | — |
| `FC08-C09-U063` | **Example 9.45** | Ch. 9, § Commuting Frames; source L6691 | (Commuting and Noncommuting Frames). (a) The simplest examples of commuting frames are the coordinate frames. Given any smooth coordinate chart ( U , ( x ^i ) ) for a smooth manifold M; (8.10) shows that the coordinate frame ( ∂ / ∂ x ^i ) is a commuting frame. (b) The frame ( E _1 , E _2 ) for ℝ ^2 over ℝ ^2 setminus 0 defined by (8.3) is not a commuting frame, because a straightforward computation shows that [ E _1, E _2 ] = (y)/(r ^2) (∂)/(∂ x) - (x)/(r ^2) (∂)/(∂ y) ≠ 0.tag Because every coordinate frame is a commuting frame, and because Lie brackets are invariantly defined, it follows that a necessary condition for a smooth frame to be expressible as a coordinate frame in some smooth chart is that it be a commuting frame. Thus, the computation above shows that ( E _1 , E _2 ) cannot be expressed as a coordinate frame for ℝ ^2 with respect to any choice of smooth local coordinates. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U064` | **Theorem 9.46** | Ch. 9, § Commuting Frames; source L6705 | (Canonical Form for Commuting Vector Fields). Let M be a smooth n-manifold, and let ( V _1 , … , V _k ) be a linearly independent k-tuple of smooth commuting vector fields on an open subset W ⊆ M . For each p ∈ W , there exists a smooth coordinate chart ( U , ( s ^i ) ) centered at p such that V _i = ∂ / ∂ s ^i for i = 1 , … , k . I f S ⊆ W is an embedded codimension-k submanifold and p is a point of S such that T _p S is complementary to the span of ( V _1 ∣ _p , … , V _k ∣ _p ) , then the coordinates can also be chosen such that S ∩ U is the slice defined by s ^1 = ·s = s ^k = 0 | — |
| `FC08-C09-U065` | **Example 9.47** | Ch. 9, § Commuting Frames; source L6749 | Consider the following two vector fields on ℝ ^2 : V = x (∂)/(∂ y) - y (∂)/(∂ x), W = x (∂)/(∂ x) + y (∂)/(∂ y). A computation shows that [ V , W ] = 0 . Example 9.8 showed that the flow of V is theta_t (x, y) = (x cos t - y sin t, x sin t + y cos t), and an easy verification shows that the flow of W is eta_t (x, y) = (e ^t x, e ^t y ). Because k = n = 2 in this case, we can take the subset S to be the single point ( 1 , 0 ) , and define φ : ℝ ^2 ℝ ^2 by Phi (s, t) = eta_t ∘ theta_s (1, 0) = (e ^t cos s, e ^t sin s ). In this case, we can solve for ( s , t ) = varPhi ^- 1 ( x , y ) explicitly in a neighborhood of ( 1 , 0 ) to obtain the coordinate map (s, t) = (tan^- 1 (y / x), log sqrt x ^2 + y ^2 ).tag | `FC08-C09-U014` |
| `FC08-C09-U066` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 9, § Time-Dependent Vector Fields; source L6781 | All of the systems of differential equations we have encountered so far have been autonomous ones, meaning that when they are written in the form (9.1), the functions V ^i on the right-hand sides do not depend explicitly on the independent variable t (see Appendix D). However, nonautonomous ODEs do arise in manifold theory, so it is worth exploring how the results of this chapter can be extended to cover this case. We will use this theory only in Chapter 22. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U067` | **Definition/convention — time-dependent vector field** | Ch. 9, § Time-Dependent Vector Fields; source L6783 | Let M be a smooth manifold. A time-dependent vector field on M is a continuous map V : J × M → T M ; where J ⊆ ℝ is an interval, such that V ( t , p ) ∈ T _p M for each ( t , p ) ∈ J × M . This means that for each t ∈ J , the map V _t : M → T M defined by V _t ( p ) = V ( t , p ) is a vector field on M . If V is a time-dependent vector field on M; an integral curve of V is a differentiable curve γ : J _0 M ; where J _0 is an interval contained in J , such that γ^prime (t) = V (t, γ (t) ) text for all t ∈ J _0. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U068` | **Theorem 9.48** | Ch. 9, § Time-Dependent Vector Fields; source L6793 | (Fundamental Theorem on Time-Dependent Flows). Let M be a smooth manifold, let J ⊆ ℝ be an open interval, and let V J × M → T M be a smooth time-dependent vector field on M . There exist an open subset E ⊆ J × J × M and a smooth map ψ : E → M called the time-dependent flow of V , with the following properties: (a) For each t _0 ∈ J and p ∈ M ; the set E ^ ( t _0 , p ) = t ∈ J : ( t , t _0 , p ) ∈ E is an open interval containing t _0 , and the smooth curve ψ ^ ( t _0 , p ) : E ^ ( t _0 , p ) → M defined by ψ ^ ( t _0 , p ) ( t ) = ψ ( t , t _0 , p ) is the unique maximal integral curve of V with initial condition ψ ^ ( t _0 , p ) ( t _0 ) = p (b) I f t _1 ∈ E ^ ( t _0 , p ) and q = ψ ^ ( t _0 , p ) ( t _1 ) , then E ^ ( t _1 , q ) = E ^ ( t _0 , p ) and ψ ^ ( t _1 , q ) = ψ ^ ( t _0 , p ) (c) For each ( t _1 , t _0 ) ∈ J × J , the set M _ t _1 , t _0 = p ∈ M : ( t _1 , t _0 , p ) ∈ E is open in M; and the map ψ _ t _1 , t _0 : M _ t _1 , t _0 → M defined by ψ _ t _1 , t _0 ( p ) = ψ ( t _1 , t _0 , p ) is a diffeomorphism from M _ t _1 , t _0 onto M _ t _0 , t _1 with inverse ψ _ t _0 , t _1 (d) If p ∈ M _ t _1 , t _0 and ψ _ t _1 , t _0 ( p ) ∈ M _ t _2 , t _1 , then p ∈ M _ t _2 , t _0 and psi_ t _2, t _1 ∘ psi_ t _1, t _0 (p) = psi_ t _2, t _0 (p).tag9.18 | — |
| `FC08-C09-U069` | **Example 9.50** | Ch. 9, § Time-Dependent Vector Fields; source L6861 | Define a time-dependent vector field V on ℝ ^n by V (t, x) = (1)/(t) x ^i . (∂)/(∂ x ^i) ∣ _x, (t, x) ∈ (0, ∞) × ℝ ^n. Therefore, the time-dependent flow of V is given by ψ ( t , t _0 , x ) = t x / t _0 for ( t , t _0 , x ) ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) × ( 0 , ∞ ) × ℝ ^n The maximal solution to this system, as you can easily check, is x ^i ( t ) = t x _0 ^i / t _0 , defined for all t > 0 . | — |
| `FC08-C09-U070` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 9, § First-Order Partial Differential Equations; source L6881 | One of the most powerful applications of the theory of flows is to partial differential equations. In its most general form, a partial differential equation (PDE) is any equation that relates an unknown function of two or more variables with its partial derivatives up to some order and with the independent variables. The order of the PDE is the highest-order derivative of the unknown function that appears. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U071` | **Definition/convention — fully nonlinear** | Ch. 9, § First-Order Partial Differential Equations; source L6883 | The number of specialized techniques that have been developed to solve partial differential equations is staggering. (For an introduction to the general theory, you can consult one of the many excellent introductory books on the subject, such as [Eva98, Fol95, Joh91].) However, it is a remarkable fact that real-valued first-order PDEs can be reduced to ordinary differential equations by means of the theory of flows, and thus can be solved using only ODEs and a little differential-geometric insight but no specialized PDE theory. In this section, we describe how this is done for two special classes of first-order equations: first, linear equations; and then, somewhat more generally, quasilinear equations (which we define below). A PDE that is not quasilinear is said to be fully nonlinear; we will show how to treat fully nonlinear first-order equations in Chapter 22. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U072` | **Definition/convention — Cauchy problem** | Ch. 9, § First-Order Partial Differential Equations; source L6899 | The problem of finding a solution to (9.20) in a neighborhood of S subject to the initial condition (9.21) is called a Cauchy problem. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U073` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 9, § Linear Equations; source L6905 | The first type of equation we will treat is a first-order linear PDE, which is one that depends linearly or affinely on the unknown function and its derivatives. In coordinate form, the most general such equation can be written | — |
| `FC08-C09-U074` | **Definition/convention — noncharacteristic** | Ch. 9, § Linear Equations; source L6919 | In terms of A , , we can rewrite (9.22) in the simple form A u + b u = f . In this form, it makes sense on any smooth manifold, and is no more difficult to solve in that generality, so we state our first theorem in that context. The Cauchy problem for A u + b u = f with initial hypersurface S is said to be noncharacteristic if A is nowhere tangent to S. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U075` | **Theorem 9.51** | Ch. 9, § Linear Equations; source L6921 | (The Linear First-Order Cauchy Problem). Let M be a smooth manifold. Suppose we are given an embedded hypersurface S ⊆ M ; a smooth vector field A ∈ X ( M ) that is nowhere tangent to S , and functions b , f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) and φ ∈ C ^∞ ( S ) . Then for some neighborhood U of S in M; there exists a unique solution u ∈ C ^∞ ( U ) to the noncharacteristic Cauchy problem A u + b u = f,tag9.24 u ∣ _S = φ .tag9.25 | — |
| `FC08-C09-U076` | **Example 9.52** | Ch. 9, § Linear Equations; source L6947 | (A Linear Cauchy Problem). Suppose we wish to solve the following Cauchy problem for a smooth function u ( x , y ) in the plane: x (∂ u)/(∂ y) - y (∂ u)/(∂ x) = x,tag9.27 u (x, 0) = x text when x > 0.tag9.28 The vector field acting on u on the left-hand side of (9.27) is the vector field W of Example 9.23. Substituting for ( t , s ) in terms of ( x , y ) using (9.13), we obtain the solution u ( x , y ) = y + sqrt x ^2 + y ^2 to the original problem. (Notice that this would not be the case if we took S to be the entire x-axis.) Using the computations of Example 9.23, we find that the transformation ( x , y ) = varPsi ( t , s ) = ( s cos t , s sin t ) pushes ∂ / ∂ t forward to W , and thus transforms (9.27)–(9.28) to the ODE initial value problem beginarrayc (∂ hat(u))/(∂ t) (t, s) = s cos t, hat(u) (0, s) = s. endarray This is solved by hat(u) ( t , s ) = s sin t + s . | `FC08-C09-U038` |
| `FC08-C09-U077` | **Definition/convention — quasilinear** | Ch. 9, § Quasilinear Equations; source L6967 | The preceding results extend easily to certain nonlinear partial differential equations. A PDE is called quasilinear if it can be written as an affine equation in the highest-order derivatives of the unknown function, with coefficients that may depend on the function itself and its derivatives of lower order. Thus, in coordinates, a quasilinear first-order PDE is a differential equation of the form a ^1 (x, u (x)) (∂ u)/(∂ x ^1) + … + a ^n (x, u (x)) (∂ u)/(∂ x ^n) = f (x, u (x))tag9.29 for an unknown real-valued function u ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) , where a ^1 , … , a ^n and f are smooth real-valued functions defined on some open subset W ⊆ ℝ ^n + 1 . (For simplicity, this time we concentrate only on the local problem, and restrict our attention to open subsets of Euclidean space.) | — |
| `FC08-C09-U078` | **Definition/convention — noncharacteristic** | Ch. 9, § Quasilinear Equations; source L6975 | We wish to solve a Cauchy problem for this equation with initial condition u ∣ _S = φ ,tag9.30 where S ⊆ ℝ ^n is a smooth, embedded hypersurface, and φ : S ℝ is a smooth function whose graph is contained in W . A quasilinear Cauchy problem is said to be noncharacteristic if the vector field A ^φ along S defined by . A ^φ ∣ _x = a ^1 (x, φ (x)) . (∂)/(∂ x ^1) ∣ _x + … + a ^n (x, φ (x)) . (∂)/(∂ x ^n) ∣ _xtag9.31 is nowhere tangent to S . (Notice that in this case the noncharacteristic condition depends on the initial value φ , not just on the initial hypersurface.) We will show that a noncharacteristic Cauchy problem always has local solutions. (As we will see below, finding global solutions can be problematic because of the lack of uniqueness.) | — |
| `FC08-C09-U079` | **Theorem 9.53** | Ch. 9, § Quasilinear Equations; source L6989 | (The Quasilinear Cauchy Problem). If the Cauchy problem (9.29)–(9.30) is noncharacteristic, then for each p ∈ S there exists a neighborhood U of p in M on which there exists a unique solution u to (9.29)–(9.30). | — |
| `FC08-C09-U080` | **Example 9.54** | Ch. 9, § Quasilinear Equations; source L7021 | (A Quasilinear Cauchy Problem). Suppose we wish to solve the following quasilinear Cauchy problem in the plane: beginarrayc (u + 1) (∂ u)/(∂ x) + (∂ u)/(∂ y) = 0, u (x, 0) = x. endarray The initial hypersurface S is the x - mathbf vec a X mathbf dot 1 S , , and the initial value is φ ( x , 0 ) = x . To reparametrize the graph in terms of x and y _: we invert the map π ∘ ψ ; that is, we solve ( x , y ) = ( s + t + s t , t ) (locally) for t and s, yielding (t, s) = (y, (x - y)/(1 + y) ), and therefore on the flowout manifold we have z = s = ( x - y ) / ( 1 + y ) . The solution to our Cauchy problem is u ( x , y ) = ( x - y ) / ( 1 + y ) . Note that it is defined only in a neighborhood of S (the set where y > - 1 ) , not on the whole plane. | — |
| `FC08-C09-U081` | **Definition/convention — characteristic curves (** | Ch. 9, § Quasilinear Equations; source L7049 | The integral curves of ξ in ℝ ^n + 1 are called the characteristic curves (or characteristics) of the PDE (9.29). This solution technique, which boils down to constructing the graph of u as a union of characteristic curves, is called the method of characteristics. (For linear equations, the term characteristic curves is also sometimes applied to the integral curves of the vector field A defined by (9.23). Of course, the method described above for quasilinear equations can be applied to linear ones as well, but the technique of Example 9.52 is usually easier in the linear case.) | `FC08-C09-U076` |
| `FC08-C10-U001` | **Construction/definition — local trivialization of E** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7195 | Let M be a topological space. A (real) vector bundle of rank k over M is a topological space E together with a surjective continuous map π : E → M satisfying the following conditions: (i) For each p ∈ M ; the fiber E _p = π ^- 1 ( p ) over p is endowed with the structure of a k-dimensional real vector space. (ii) For each p ∈ M ; there exist a neighborhood U of p in M and a homeomorphism φ : π ^- 1 ( U ) → U × ℝ ^k (called a local trivialization of E over U ) , satisfying the following conditions (Fig. 10.1): π U ∘ φ = π (where π U U × ℝ ^k → U is the projection); for each q ∈ U , the restriction of varPhi to E _q is a vector space isomorphism from E _q ~ to ~ q × ℝ ^k ≅ ℝ ^k | — |
| `FC08-C10-U002` | **Definition/convention — smooth vector bundle** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7208 | If M and E are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, π is a smooth map, and the local trivializations can be chosen to be diffeomorphisms, then E is called a smooth vector bundle. In this case, we call any local trivialization that is a diffeomorphism onto its image a smooth local trivialization. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U003` | **Convention/notation — a (real) line bundle** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7210 | A rank-1 vector bundle is often called a (real) line bundle. Complex vector bundles are defined similarly, with “real vector space” replaced by “complex vector space” and ℝ ^k replaced by ℂ ^k in the definition. We have no need to treat complex vector bundles in this book, so all of our vector bundles are understood without further comment to be real. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U004` | **Convention/notation — total space of the bundle** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7212 | The space E is called the total space of the bundle, M is called its base, and π is its projection. Depending on what we wish to emphasize, we sometimes omit some of the ingredients from the notation, and write “E is a vector bundle over M;” o r ^·s E → M is a vector bundle,” or * π : E → M is a vector bundle.” | — |
| `FC08-C10-U005` | **Definition/convention — global trivialization of E )** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7216 | If there exists a local trivialization of E over all of M (called a global trivialization of E ), then E is said to be a trivial bundle. In this case, E itself is homeomorphic to the product space M × ℝ ^k . If E → M is a smooth bundle that admits a smooth global trivialization, then we say that E is smoothly trivial. In this case E is diffeomorphic to M × ℝ ^k , not just homeomorphic. For brevity, when we say that a smooth bundle is trivial, we always understand this to mean smoothly trivial, not just trivial in the topological sense. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U006` | **Example 10.2** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7221 | (Product Bundles). One particularly simple example of a rankk vector bundle over any space M is the product space E = M × ℝ ^k with π = π _1 : M × ℝ ^k → M as its projection. Any such bundle, called a product bundle, is trivial (with the identity map as a global trivialization). If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, then M × ℝ ^k is smoothly trivial. // | — |
| `FC08-C10-U007` | **Example 10.3** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7225 | (The Möbius Bundle). Define an equivalence relation on ℝ ^2 by declaring that ( x , y ) ∼ ( x ^prime , y ^prime ) if and only if ( x ^prime , y ^prime ) = ( x + n , ( - 1 ) ^n y ) for some n ∈ ℤ . Let E = ℝ ^2 / ∼ denote the quotient space, and let dot q : ℝ ^2 → E be the quotient map. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U008` | **Proposition 10.4** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7239 | (The Tangent Bundle as a Vector Bundle). Let M be a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary, and let TM be its tangent bundle. With its standard projection map, its natural vector space structure on each fiber, and the topology and smooth structure constructed in Proposition 3.18, TM is a smooth vector bundle of rank n over M . | `FC08-C03-U031` |
| `FC08-C10-U009` | **Lemma 10.5** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7257 | Let π E M be a smooth vector bundle of rank k over M . Suppose ˚ π ^- 1 ( U ) → U × ℝ ^k and ψ : π ^- 1 ( V ) → V × ℝ ^k are two smooth local trivializations of E with U ∩ V ≠ emptyset . There exists a smooth map τ : U ∩ V → GL ( k , ℝ ) 1 such that the composition dot φ ∘ ψ ^- 1 : ( U ∩ V ) × ℝ ^k → ( U ∩ V ) × ℝ ^k has the form varPhi ∘ Psi^- 1 (p, v) = (p, τ (p) v ), where τ ( p ) v denotes the usual action of the k × k matrix τ ( p ) on the vector v ∈ ℝ ^k | — |
| `FC08-C10-U010` | **Definition/convention — transition function** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7277 | The smooth map τ : U ∩ V → GL ( k , ℝ ) described in this lemma is called the transition function between the local trivializations varPhi and ψ . (This is one of the few situations in smooth manifold theory in which it is traditional to use the word “function” even though the codomain is not R or ℝ ^k . ) For example, if M is a smooth manifold and varPhi and ψ are the local trivializations of TM associated with two different smooth charts, then (3.12) shows that the transition function between them is the Jacobian matrix of the coordinate transition map. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U011` | **Lemma 10.6** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7281 | (Vector Bundle Chart Lemma). Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and suppose that for each p ∈ M we are given a real vector space E _p of some fixed dimension k. Let beginarray r E = coprod _p ∈ M E _p endarray , and let π E → M be the map that takes each element of E _p to the point p. Suppose furthermore that we are given the following data: (i) an open cover U _α _α ∈ A of M (ii) for each α ∈ A , a bijective map φ _α : π ^- 1 ( U _α ) → U _α × ℝ ^k whose restriction to each E _p is a vector space isomorphism from E _p to p × ℝ ^k ≅ ℝ ^k (iii) for each α , β ∈ A with U _α ∩ U _β ≠ emptyset , a smooth map τ _α β : U _α ∩ U _β → GL.k; R/ such that the map varPhi _α ∘ varPhi _β ^- 1 from ( U _α ∩ U _β ) × ℝ ^k to itself has the form Phi_α ∘ Phi_β ^- 1 (p, v) = (p, tau_α β (p) v )tag10.3 Then E has a unique topology and smooth structure making it into a smooth manifold with or without boundary and a smooth rank-k vector bundle over M; with π as projection and ( U _α , Phi _α ) as smooth local trivializations. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U012` | **Example 10.7** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7327 | (Whitney Sums). Given a smooth manifold M and smooth vector bundles E ^prime → M and E ^prime prime → M of ranks k ^prime and k ^prime prime , respectively, we will construct a new vector bundle over M called the Whitney sum of E ^prime and E ^prime prime , whose fiber at each p ∈ M is the direct sum E _p ^prime oplus E _p ^prime prime . Because this depends smoothly on p , it follows from the chart lemma that E ^prime oplus E ^prime prime is a smooth vector bundle over M . The total space is defined as E ^prime oplus E ^prime prime = textstyle L _p ∈ M ( E _p ^prime oplus E _p ^prime prime ) , with the obvious projection π E ^prime oplus E ^prime prime → M . Then the transition function for E ^prime oplus E ^prime prime has the form tilde(Phi) ∘ Phi^- 1 big (p, (v ^prime, v ^prime prime) big) = big (p, τ (p) (v ^prime, v ^prime prime) big), where τ ( p ) = τ ^prime ( p ) oplus τ ^prime prime ( p ) ∈ GL ( k ^prime + k ^prime prime ; R/ is the block diagonal matrix ( beginarrayc c τ^prime (p) & 0 0 & τ^prime prime (p) endarray ). | — |
| `FC08-C10-U013` | **Example 10.8** | Ch. 10, § Vector Bundles; source L7347 | (Restriction of a Vector Bundle). Suppose π : E → M is a rank-k vector bundle and S ⊆ M is any subset. If E is a smooth vector bundle and S ⊆ M is an immersed or embedded submanifold, it follows easily from the chart lemma that E ∣ _S is a smooth vector bundle. We define the restriction of E to S to be the set textstyle E ∣ _S = bigcup _p ∈ S E _p , with the projection E ∣ _S S obtained by restricting π. In particular, if S ⊆ M is a smooth (embedded or immersed) submanifold, then the restricted bundle T M ∣ _S is called the ambient tangent bundle over M . // | — |
| `FC08-C10-U014` | **Definition/convention — cross section) is a section of the map π** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Sections of Vector Bundles; source L7351 | Let π : E → M be a vector bundle. A section of E (sometimes called a cross section) is a section of the map π, that is, a continuous map σ : M → E satisfying π ∘ σ = I d _M . This means that σ ( p ) is an element of the fiber E _p for each p ∈ M | — |
| `FC08-C10-U015` | **Definition/convention — global section** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Sections of Vector Bundles; source L7353 | More generally, a local section of E is a continuous map σ : U → E defined on some open subset U ⊆ M and satisfying π ∘ σ = I d _U (see Fig. 10.3). To emphasize the distinction, a section defined on all of M is sometimes called a global section. Note that a local section of E over U ⊆ M is the same as a global section of the restricted bundle E ∣ _U . If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary and E is a smooth vector bundle, a smooth (local or global) section of E is one that is a smooth map from its domain to E | — |
| `FC08-C10-U016` | **Definition/convention — rough (local** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Sections of Vector Bundles; source L7355 | Just as with vector fields, for some purposes it is useful also to consider maps that would be sections except that they might not be continuous. Thus, we define a rough (local or global) section of E over a set U ⊆ M to be a map σ : U → E (not necessarily continuous) such that π ∘ σ = I d _U . A “section” without further qualification always means a continuous section. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U017` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Sections of Vector Bundles; source L7360 | The zero section of E is the global section zeta : M → E defined by zeta (p) = 0 ∈ E _p text for each p ∈ M. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U018` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Sections of Vector Bundles; source L7366 | As in the case of vector fields, the support of a section σ is the closure of the set p ∈ M : σ ( p ) ≠ 0 | — |
| `FC08-C10-U019` | **Example 10.10** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Sections of Vector Bundles; source L7370 | (Sections of Vector Bundles). Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary. (a) Sections of TM are vector fields on M . (b) Given an immersed submanifold S ⊆ M with or without boundary, a section of the ambient tangent bundle T M ∣ _S S is called a vector field along S . This is different from a vector field on S, which satisfies X _p ∈ T _p S at each point. (c) If E = M × ℝ ^k is a product bundle, there is a natural one-to-one correspondence between sections of E and continuous functions from M to ℝ ^k : a continuous function F : M → ℝ ^k determines a section tilde(F) : M → M × ℝ ^k by tilde(F) ( x ) = ( x , F ( x ) ) , and vice versa. If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, then the section tilde(F) is smooth if and only if F is. (d) The correspondence in the preceding paragraph yields a natural identification between the space C ^∞ ( M ) / and the space of smooth sections of the trivial line bundle M × ℝ → M | — |
| `FC08-C10-U020` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Sections of Vector Bundles; source L7380 | If E → M is a smooth vector bundle, the set of all smooth global sections of E is a vector space under pointwise addition and scalar multiplication: (c _1 sigma_1 + c _2 sigma_2) (p) = c _1 sigma_1 (p) + c _2 sigma_2 (p). | — |
| `FC08-C10-U021` | **Convention/notation — Gamma(E), the space of smooth sections** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Sections of Vector Bundles; source L7386 | This vector space is usually denoted by Gamma ( E ) . (For particular vector bundles, we will often introduce specialized notations for their spaces of sections, such as the notation X ( M ) introduced in Chapter 8 for the space of smooth sections of T M . ) | — |
| `FC08-C10-U022` | **Lemma 10.12** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Sections of Vector Bundles; source L7400 | (Extension Lemma for Vector Bundles). Let π : E → M be a smooth vector bundle over a smooth manifold M with or without boundary. Suppose A is a closed subset of M; and σ A E is a section of E ∣ _A that is smooth in the sense that σ extends to a smooth local section of E in a neighborhood of each point. For each open subset U ⊆ M containing A, there exists a global smooth section tilde(σ) ∈ Gamma ( E ) such that tilde(σ) ∣ _A = σ and supp tilde(σ) ⊆ U | — |
| `FC08-C10-U023` | **Definition/convention — global frame** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7408 | The concept of local frames that we introduced in Chapter 8 extends readily to vector bundles. Let E → M be a vector bundle. If U ⊆ M is an open subset, a k - tuple of local sections ( σ _1 , … , σ _k ) of E over U is said to be linearly independent if their values big ( σ _1 ( p ) , … , σ _k ( p ) big ) form a linearly independent k-tuple in E _p for each p ∈ U . Similarly, they are said to span E if their values span E _p for each p ∈ U . A local frame for E over U is an ordered k-tuple ( σ _1 , … , σ _k ) of linearly independent local sections over U that span E ; ; thus big ( σ _1 ( p ) , … , σ _k ( p ) big ) is a basis for the fiber E _p for each p ∈ U . It is called a global frame if U = M . If E → M is a smooth vector bundle, a local or global frame is a smooth frame if each σ _i is a smooth section. We often denote a frame ( σ _1 , … , σ _k ) by ( σ _i ) | — |
| `FC08-C10-U024` | **Proposition 10.15** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7414 | (Completion of Local Frames for Vector Bundles). Suppose π : E → M is a smooth vector bundle of rank k. (a) If ( σ _1 , hdots , σ _m ) is a linearly independent m-tuple of smooth local sections of E over an open subset U ⊆ M ; with 1 ≤ m < k , then for each p ∈ U there exist smooth sections σ _m + 1 , … , σ _k defined on some neighborhood V of p such that ( σ _1 , … , σ _k ) is a smooth local frame for E over U ∩ V (b) I f ( v _1 , … , v _m ) is a linearly independent m-tuple of elements of E _p for some p ∈ M ; with 1 ≤ m ≤ k , then there exists a smooth local frame ( σ _i ) for E over some neighborhood of p such that σ _i ( p ) = v _i f o r i = 1 , … , m (c) If A ⊆ M is a closed subset and ( τ _1 , … , τ _k ) is a linearly independent k-tuple of sections of E ∣ _A that are smooth in the sense described in Lemma 10.12, then there exists a smooth local frame ( σ _1 , … , σ _k ) for E over some neighborhood of A such that σ _i ∣ _A = τ _i f o r i = 1 , … , k | `FC08-C10-U022` |
| `FC08-C10-U025` | **Example 10.17** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7426 | (A Global Frame for a Product Bundle). If E = M × ℝ ^k → M is a product bundle, the standard basis ( e _1 , … , e _k ) for ℝ ^k yields a global frame ( tilde(e) _i ) for E, defined by tilde(e) _i ( p ) = ( p , e _i ) . If M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, then this global frame is smooth. // | — |
| `FC08-C10-U026` | **Example 10.18** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7428 | (Local Frames Associated with Local Trivializations). Suppose π : E → M is a smooth vector bundle. If φ : π ^- 1 ( U ) → U × ℝ ^k is a smooth local trivialization of E , we can use the same idea as in the preceding example to construct a local frame for E over U . Define maps σ _1 , … , σ _k : U → E by σ _i ( p ) = φ ^- 1 ( p , e _i ) = φ ^- 1 ∘ tilde(e) _i ( p ) | — |
| `FC08-C10-U027` | **Convention/notation — frame associated with a local trivialization** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7438 | so σ _i is a section. To see that ( σ _i ( p ) ) forms a basis for E _p , just note that varPhi restricts to an isomorphism from E _p to p × ℝ ^k , and varPhi ( σ _i ( p ) ) = ( p , e _i ) , so varPhi takes ( σ _i ( p ) ) to the standard basis for p × ℝ ^k ≅ ℝ ^k . We say that this local frame ( σ _i ) is associated with φ // | — |
| `FC08-C10-U028` | **Proposition 10.19** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7440 | Every smooth local frame for a smooth vector bundle is associated with a smooth local trivialization as in Example 10.18. | `FC08-C10-U026` |
| `FC08-C10-U029` | **Corollary 10.20** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7478 | A smooth vector bundle is smoothly trivial if and only if it admits a smooth global frame. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U030` | **Corollary 10.21** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7484 | Let π : E → M be a smooth vector bundle of rank k, let ( V , φ ) be a smooth chart on M with coordinate functions ( x ^i ) , and suppose there exists a smooth local frame ( σ _i ) for E over V . Define widetilde φ : π ^- 1 ( V ) → φ ( V ) × mathbb R ^k by tilde(φ) big (v ^i sigma_i (p) big) = big (x ^1 (p), … , x ^n (p), v ^1, … , v ^k big). Then ( π ^- 1 ( V ) , tilde(φ) ) is a smooth coordinate chart for E | — |
| `FC08-C10-U031` | **Definition/convention — component functions of τ** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7494 | Just as smoothness of vector fields can be characterized in terms of their component functions in any smooth chart, smoothness of sections of vector bundles can be characterized in terms of local frames. Suppose ( σ _i ) is a smooth local frame for E over some open subset U ⊆ M . If τ : M → E is a rough section, the value of τ at an arbitrary point p ∈ U can be written τ ( p ) = τ ^i ( p ) σ _i ( p ) for some uniquely determined numbers ( τ ^1 ( p ) , … , τ ^n ( p ) ) . This defines k functions τ ^i : U → ℝ , called the component functions of τ with respect to the given local frame. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U032` | **Proposition 10.22** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7496 | (Local Frame Criterion for Smoothness). Let π : E → M be a smooth vector bundle, and let τ : M → E be a rough section. I f ( σ _i ) is a smooth local frame for E over an open subset U ⊆ M ; then τ is smooth on U if and only if its component functions with respect to ( σ _i ) are smooth. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U033` | **Proposition 10.24** | Ch. 10, § Local and Global Frames; source L7506 | (Uniqueness of the Smooth Structure on TM ). Let M be a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary. The topology and smooth structure on TM constructed in Proposition 3.18 are the unique ones with respect to which π : T M → M is a smooth vector bundle with the given vector space structure on the fibers, and such that all coordinate vector fields are smooth local sections. | `FC08-C03-U031` |
| `FC08-C10-U034` | **Definition/convention — bundle homomorphism** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7512 | If π E → M and π ^prime : E ^prime → M ^prime are vector bundles, a continuous map F : E → E ^prime is called a bundle homomorphism if there exists a map f : M → M ^prime satisfying π ^prime ∘ F = f ∘ π | — |
| `FC08-C10-U035` | **Proposition 10.25** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7520 | Suppose π : E → M and π ^prime : E → M ^prime are vector bundles and F : E → E ^prime is a bundle homomorphism covering f : M → M ^prime . Then f is continuous and is uniquely determined by F . If the bundles and F are all smooth, then f is smooth as well. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U036` | **Definition/convention — bundle isomorphism** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7524 | A bijective bundle homomorphism F : E → E ^prime whose inverse is also a bundle homomorphism is called a bundle isomorphism; if F is also a diffeomorphism, it is called a smooth bundle isomorphism. If there exists a (smooth) bundle isomorphism between E and E ^prime , the two bundles are said to be (smoothly) isomorphic. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U037` | **Definition/convention — slightly more restrictive notion of bundle homomorphism** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7526 | In the special case in which both E and E ^prime are vector bundles over the same base space M; a slightly more restrictive notion of bundle homomorphism is usually more useful. A bundle homomorphism over M is a bundle homomorphism covering the identity map of M; or in other words, a continuous map F : E → E ^prime such that π ^prime ∘ F = π beginarrayc E xrightarrow F E ^prime π Big downarrow Big downarrow π^prime M, endarray and whose restriction to each fiber is linear. If there exists a bundle homomorphism F : E → E ^prime over M that is also a (smooth) bundle isomorphism, then we say that E and E ^prime are (smoothly) isomorphic over M . The next proposition shows that it is not necessary to check smoothness of the inverse. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U038` | **Proposition 10.26** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7536 | Suppose E and E ^prime are smooth vector bundles over a smooth manifold M with or without boundary, and F E E ^prime is a bijective smooth bundle homomorphism over M . Then F is a smooth bundle isomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U039` | **Example 10.28** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7542 | (Bundle Homomorphisms). (a) If F : M → N is a smooth map, the global differential d F : T M → T N is a smooth bundle homomorphism covering F . (b) If E → M is a smooth vector bundle and S ⊆ M is an immersed submanifold with or without boundary, then the inclusion map E ∣ _S ↪ E is a smooth bundle homomorphism covering the inclusion of S into M . | — |
| `FC08-C10-U040` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7548 | Suppose E → M and E ^prime → M are smooth vector bundles over a smooth manifold M with or without boundary, and let Gamma ( E ) , Gamma ( E ^prime ) denote their spaces of smooth global sections. If F : E → E ^prime is a smooth bundle homomorphism over M; then composition with F induces a map tilde(F) : Gamma ( E ) → Gamma ( E ^prime ) as follows: tilde(F) (σ) (p) = (F ∘ σ) (p) = F (σ (p) ).tag10.5 | — |
| `FC08-C10-U041` | **Definition/convention — linear** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7556 | Because a bundle homomorphism is linear on fibers, the resulting map tilde(F) on sections is linear over R. In fact, it satisfies a stronger linearity property. A map F : Gamma ( E ) Gamma ( E ^prime ) is said to be linear over C ^∞ ( M ) if for any smooth functions u _1 , u _2 ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) and smooth sections σ _1 , σ _2 ∈ Gamma ( E ) F (u _1 sigma_1 + u _2 sigma_2) = u _1 F (sigma_1) + u _2 F (sigma_2). | — |
| `FC08-C10-U042` | **Lemma 10.29** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7564 | (Bundle Homomorphism Characterization Lemma). Let π : E → M and π ^prime : E ^prime → M be smooth vector bundles over a smooth manifold M with or without boundary, and let Gamma ( E ) , Gamma ( E ^prime ) denote their spaces of smooth sections. A map F : Gamma ( E ) Gamma ( E ^prime ) is linear over C ^∞ ( M ) if and only if there is a smooth bundle homomorphism F : E → E ^prime over M such that F ( σ ) = F σ for all σ ∈ Gamma ( E ) | — |
| `FC08-C10-U043` | **Example 10.30** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7600 | (Bundle Homomorphisms Over Manifolds). (a) If M is a smooth manifold and f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) , the map from X ( M ) to itself defined by X ↦ f X is linear over C ^∞ ( M ) because f ( u _1 X _1 + u _2 X _2 ) = u _1 f X _1 + u _2 f X _2 , and thus defines a smooth bundle homomorphism over M from T M to itself. (b) If Z is a smooth vector field on ℝ ^3 , the cross product with Z defines a map from X ( ℝ ^3 ) to itself: X ↦ X × Z . Since it is linear over C ^∞ ( ℝ ^3 ) in X , it determines a smooth bundle homomorphism over ℝ ^3 from T ℝ ^3 Delta to T ℝ ^3 (c) Given Z ∈ X ( ℝ ^n ) , the Euclidean dot product defines a map X ↦ X · Z from X ( ℝ ^n ) to C ^∞ ( ℝ ^n ) , which is linear over C ^∞ ( ℝ ^n ) and thus determines a smooth bundle homomorphism over ℝ ^n from T ℝ ^n to the trivial line bundle I ℝ ^n × ℝ | — |
| `FC08-C10-U044` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 10, § Bundle Homomorphisms; source L7608 | Because of Lemma 10.29, we usually dispense with the notation tilde(F) and use the same symbol for both a bundle homomorphism F : E → E ^prime over M and the linear map F : Gamma ( E ) → Gamma ( E ^prime ) that it induces on sections, and we refer to a map of either of these types as a bundle homomorphism. Because the action on sections is obtained simply by applying the bundle homomorphism pointwise, this should cause no confusion. In fact, we have been doing the same thing all along in certain circumstances. For example, if a ∈ ℝ , we use the same notation X ↦ a X to denote both the operation of multiplying vectors in each tangent space T _p M by ^a . , and the operation of multiplying vector fields by a. Because multiplying by a is a bundle homomorphism from TM to itself, there is no ambiguity about what is meant. | `FC08-C10-U042` |
| `FC08-C10-U045` | **Definition/convention — smooth subbundle** | Ch. 10, § Subbundles; source L7614 | Given a vector bundle π _E : E → M ; a subbundle of E (see Fig. 10.4) is a vector bundle π _D : D → M ; in which D is a topological subspace of E and π _D is the restriction of π _E to D, such that for each p ∈ M ; the subset D _p = D ∩ E _p is a linear subspace of E _p , and the vector space structure on D _p is the one inherited from E _p . Note that the condition that D be a vector bundle over M implies that all of the fibers D _p must be nonempty and have the same dimension. If E → M is a smooth bundle, then a subbundle of E is called a smooth subbundle if it is a smooth vector bundle and an embedded submanifold with or without boundary in E. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U046` | **Lemma 10.32** | Ch. 10, § Subbundles; source L7623 | (Local Frame Criterion for Subbundles). Let π : E → M be a smooth vector bundle, and suppose that for each p ∈ M we are given an mdimensional linear subspace D _p ⊆ E _p . Then beginarray r D = bigcup _p ∈ M D _p ⊆ E endarray is a smooth subbundle of E if and only if the following condition is satisfied: Each point of M has a neighborhood U on which there exist smooth local sections σ _1 , . . . , σ _m : U → E with the property that σ _1 ( q ) , … , σ _m ( q ) form a basis for D _q at each q ∈ U (10.6) | — |
| `FC08-C10-U047` | **Example 10.33** | Ch. 10, § Subbundles; source L7647 | (Subbundles). (a) If M is a smooth manifold and V is a nowhere-vanishing smooth vector field on M; then the set D ⊆ T M whose fiber at each p ∈ M is the linear span of V _p is a smooth 1-dimensional subbundle of T M (b) Suppose E → M is any trivial bundle, and let ( E _1 , … , E _k ) be a smooth global frame for E. If 0 ≤ m ≤ k , the subset D ⊆ E defined by D _p = span ( E _1 ∣ _p , … , E _m ∣ _p ) for each p ∈ M is a smooth subbundle of E. (c) Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary and S ⊆ M is an immersed k-submanifold with or without boundary. Problem 10-14 asks you to prove that TS is a smooth rank-k subbundle of the ambient tangent bundle T M ∣ _S | — |
| `FC08-C10-U048` | **Definition/convention — rank of F at p** | Ch. 10, § Subbundles; source L7655 | The next theorem shows how to obtain many more subbundles. Suppose E → M and E ^prime → M are vector bundles and F : E → E ^prime is a bundle homomorphism over M . For each p ∈ M ; the rank of the linear map F big vert _ E _p is called the rank of F at p. We say that F has constant rank if its rank is the same for all p ∈ M | — |
| `FC08-C10-U049` | **Theorem 10.34** | Ch. 10, § Subbundles; source L7657 | Let E and E 0 be smooth vector bundles over a smooth manifold M; and let F : E → E ^prime be a smooth bundle homomorphism over M . Define subsets Ker F ⊆ E and Im F ⊆ E ^prime b y Ker F = bigcup_p ∈ M Ker (F ∣ _ E _p ), Im F = bigcup_p ∈ M Im (F ∣ _ E _p ). Then Ker F and Im F are smooth subbundles of E and E ^prime , respectively, if and only if F has constant rank. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U050` | **Lemma 10.35** | Ch. 10, § Subbundles; source L7671 | (Orthogonal Complement Bundles). Let M be an immersed submanifold with or without boundary in ℝ ^n , and D be a smooth rank-k subbundle of T ℝ ^n ∣ _M . For each p ∈ M ; let D _p ^perp denote the orthogonal complement of D _p in T _p ℝ ^n with respect to the Euclidean dot product, and let D ^perp ⊆ T ℝ ^n ∣ _M be the subset D ^perp = (p, v) ∈ T ℝ ^n: p ∈ M, v ∈ D _p ^perp . Then D ^perp is a smooth ran k mathord - ( n - k ) subbundle of T ℝ ^n ∣ _M . For each p ∈ M ; there is a smooth orthonormal frame for D ^perp on a neighborhood of p . | — |
| `FC08-C10-U051` | **Corollary 10.36** | Ch. 10, § Subbundles; source L7681 | (The Normal Bundle to a Submanifold of ℝ ^n ) I f M ⊆ ℝ ^n is an immersed m-dimensional submanifold with or without boundary, its normal bundle NM is a smooth rank- ( n - m ) subbundle of T ℝ ^n ∣ _M . For each p ∈ M ; there exists a smooth orthonormal frame for NM on a neighborhood of p . | — |
| `FC08-C10-U052` | **Definition/convention — local trivialization of E** | Ch. 10, § Fiber Bundles; source L7689 | Let M and F be topological spaces. A fiber bundle over M with model fiber F is a topological space E together with a surjective continuous map π : E → M with the property that for each x ∈ M ; there exist a neighborhood U of x in M and a homeomorphism φ : π ^- 1 ( U ) → U × F , called a local trivialization of E over U , such that the following diagram commutes: beginarrayc π^- 1 (U) xrightarrow varPhi U × F big backslash big backslash big backslash big backslash U. endarray | — |
| `FC08-C10-U053` | **Construction/definition — total space of the bundle** | Ch. 10, § Fiber Bundles; source L7695 | The space E is called the total space of the bundle, M is its base, and π is its projection. If E, M; and F are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, π is a smooth map, and the local trivializations can be chosen to be diffeomorphisms, then it is called a smooth fiber bundle. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U054` | **Definition/convention — smoothly trivial** | Ch. 10, § Fiber Bundles; source L7697 | A trivial fiber bundle is one that admits a local trivialization over the entire base space (a global trivialization). It is said to be smoothly trivial if it is a smooth bundle and the global trivialization is a diffeomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C10-U055` | **Example 10.37** | Ch. 10, § Fiber Bundles; source L7699 | (Fiber Bundles). (a) Every product space M × F is a fiber bundle with projection π _1 : M × F → M . called a product fiber bundle. It has a global trivialization given by the identity map M × F M × F , so every product bundle is trivial. (b) Every rank-k vector bundle is a fiber bundle with model fiber ℝ ^k (c) If E → mathbb S ^1 is the Möbius bundle of Example 10.3, then the image of ℝ × [ - 1 , 1 ] under the quotient map q : ℝ ^2 → E is a fiber bundle over mathbb S ^1 with model fiber Œ 1; 1. It is not a trivial bundle. (d) Every covering map π : E → M is a fiber bundle whose model fiber is discrete. For each evenly covered open subset U ⊆ M ; define a map φ : π ^- 1 ( U ) → U × S by choosing a bijection between the set of components of π ^- 1 ( U ) and S, and letting φ ( x ) = ( π ( x ) , c ( x ) ) , where c ( x ) is the element of S corresponding to the component containing x. | `FC08-C10-U007` |
| `FC08-C11-U001` | **Definition/convention — dual space of V** | Ch. 11, § Covectors; source L7805 | Let V be a finite-dimensional vector space. (As usual, all of our vector spaces are assumed to be real.) We define a covector on V to be a real-valued linear functional on V , that is, a linear map ! V ℝ . The space of all covectors on V is itself a real vector space under the obvious operations of pointwise addition and scalar multiplication. It is denoted by V * and called the dual space of V . | — |
| `FC08-C11-U002` | **Proposition 11.1** | Ch. 11, § Covectors; source L7809 | Let V be a finite-dimensional vector space. Given any basis ( E _1 , … , E _n ) for V , let ε ^1 , … , ε ^n ∈ V * be the covectors defined by ε^i (E _j) = delta_j ^i, where delta _j ^i is the Kronecker delta symbol defined by (4.4). Then ( ε ^1 , … , ε ^n ) is a basis for V * , called the dual basis to ( E _j ) . Therefore, dim V * = dim V | — |
| `FC08-C11-U003` | **Definition/convention — standard dual basis** | Ch. 11, § Covectors; source L7819 | For example, we can apply this to the standard basis ( e _1 , … , e _n ) for ℝ ^n . These basis covectors are the linear functionals on ℝ ^n given by e ^i (v) = e ^i (v ^1, … , v ^n ) = v ^i. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U004` | **Definition/convention — dual map** | Ch. 11, § Covectors; source L7851 | Suppose V and W are vector spaces and A V W is a linear map. We define a linear map A * : W * → V * , called the dual map or transpose of A, by (A * ω) (v) = ω (A v) text for ω ∈ W *, v ∈ V. | — |

| `FC08-C11-U006` | **Corollary 11.6** | Ch. 11, § Covectors; source L7867 | The assignment that sends a vector space to its dual space and a linear map to its dual map is a contravariant functor from the category of real vector spaces to itself. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U007` | **Proposition 11.8** | Ch. 11, § Covectors; source L7881 | For any finite-dimensional vector space V , the map ξ : V → V ^* * is an isomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U008` | **Convention/notation — vector v** | Ch. 11, § Covectors; source L7891 | Because of Proposition 11.8, the real number ω ( v ) obtained by applying a covector ! to a vector v is sometimes denoted by either of the more symmetric-looking notations langle ω , v rangle and langle v , ω rangle ; both expressions can be thought of either as the action of the covector ω ∈ V * on the vector v ∈ V , or as the action of the linear functional ξ ( v ) ∈ V ^* * on the element ω ∈ V * . There should be no cause for confusion with the use of the same angle bracket notation for inner products: whenever one of the arguments is a vector and the other a covector, the notation langle ω , v rangle is always to be interpreted as the natural pairing between vectors and covectors, not as an inner product. We typically omit any mention of the map ξ , and think of v ∈ V either as a vector or as a linear functional on V * , depending on the context. | `FC08-C11-U007` |
| `FC08-C11-U009` | **Definition/convention — cotangent space at p** | Ch. 11, § Tangent Covectors on Manifolds; source L7897 | Now let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. For each p ∈ M , ; we define the cotangent space at p , denoted by T _p * M ; to be the dual space to T _p M | — |
| `FC08-C11-U010` | **Definition/convention — tangent covectors at p** | Ch. 11, § Tangent Covectors on Manifolds; source L7903 | Elements of T _p * M are called tangent covectors at p, or just covectors at p . | — |
| `FC08-C11-U011` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 11, § Tangent Covectors on Manifolds; source L7905 | Given smooth local coordinates ( x ^i ) on an open subset U ⊆ M ; for each p ∈ U the coordinate basis ( ∂ / ∂ x ^i ∣ _p ) gives rise to a dual basis for T _p * M , ; which we denote for the moment by ( λ ^i ∣ _p ) . (In a short while, we will come up with a better notation.) Any covector ω ∈ T _p * M can thus be written uniquely as ω = ω _i λ ^i ∣ _p , where omega_i = ω ((∂)/(∂ x ^i) Big ∣ _p ). | — |
| `FC08-C11-U012` | **Definition/convention — contravariant vectors** | Ch. 11, § Tangent Covectors on Manifolds; source L7937 | Since the transformation law (11.4) for the coordinate partial derivatives follows directly from the chain rule, it can be thought of as fundamental. Thus it became customary to call tangent covectors covariant vectors because their components transform in the same way as (“vary with”) the coordinate partial derivatives, with the Jacobian matrix ( ∂ tilde(x) ^j / ∂ x ^i ) multiplying the objects associated with the “new” coordinates ( tilde(x) ^j ) to obtain those associated with the “old” coordinates ( x ^i ) . Analogously, tangent vectors were called contravariant vectors, because their components transform in the opposite way. (Remember, it was the component n-tuples that were thought of as the objects of interest.) Admittedly, these terms do not make a lot of sense, but by now they are well entrenched, and we will see them again in Chapter 12. Note that this use of the terms covariant and contravariant has nothing to do with the covariant and contravariant functors of category theory! | — |
| `FC08-C11-U013` | **Convention/notation — cotangent bundle of M** | Ch. 11, § Covector Fields; source L7941 | For any smooth manifold M with or without boundary, the disjoint union T * M = coprod_p ∈ M T _p * M is called the cotangent bundle of M . It has a natural projection map π : T * M → M sending ω ∈ T _p * M to p ∈ M . As above, given any smooth local coordinates ( x ^i ) on an open subset U ⊆ M ; for each p ∈ U we denote the basis for T _p * M dual to ( ∂ / ∂ x ^i ∣ _p ) by ( λ ^i ∣ _p ) . This defines n maps λ ^1 , … , λ ^n : U → T * M ; called coordinate covector fields. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U014` | **Proposition 11.9** | Ch. 11, § Covector Fields; source L7949 | (The Cotangent Bundle as a Vector Bundle). Let M be a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary. With its standard projection map and the natural vector space structure on each fiber, the cotangent bundle T * M has a unique topology and smooth structure making it into a smooth rank-n vector bundle over M for which all coordinate covector fields are smooth local sections. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U015` | **Comparison/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 11, § Covector Fields; source L7967 | As in the case of the tangent bundle, smooth local coordinates for M yield smooth local coordinates for its cotangent bundle. If ( x ^i ) are smooth coordinates on an open subset U ⊆ M ; Corollary 10.21 shows that the map from π ^- 1 ( U ) to ℝ ^2 n given by . xi_i λ^i ∣ _p ↦ (x ^1 (p), … , x ^n (p), xi_1, … , xi_n ) is a smooth coordinate chart for T * M . We call ( x ^i , ξ _i ) the natural coordinates for T * M associated with ( x ^i ) . (In this situation, we must forgo our insistence that coordinate functions have upper indices, because the fiber coordinates ξ _i are already required by our index conventions to have lower indices. Nonetheless, the convention still holds that each index to be summed over in a given term appears once as a superscript and once as a subscript.) | `FC08-C10-U030` |
| `FC08-C11-U016` | **Convention/notation — covector field** | Ch. 11, § Covector Fields; source L7975 | A (local or global) section of T * M is called a covector field or a (differential) I ±b form . (The reason for the latter terminology will become clear in Chapter 14, when we define differential k - form s for k > 1 . ) Like sections of other bundles, covector fields without further qualification are assumed to be merely continuous; when we make different assumptions, we use the terms rough covector field and smooth covector field with the obvious meanings. As we did with vector fields, we write the value of a covector field ω at a point p ∈ M as ω _p instead of ω ( p ) to avoid conflict with the notation for the action of a covector on a vector. If ! itself has subscripts or superscripts, we usually use the notation ω ∣ _p instead. In any smooth local coordinates on an open subset U ⊆ M ; a (rough) covector field ω can be written in terms of the coordinate covector fields ( λ ^i ) as ω = ω _i λ ^i for n functions ω _i : U ℝ called the component functions of !. They are characterized by omega_i (p) = omega_p ((∂)/(∂ x ^i) Big ∣ _p ). | — |

| `FC08-C11-U018` | **Proposition 11.11** | Ch. 11, § Covector Fields; source L7991 | (Smoothness Criteria for Covector Fields). Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and let ! M → T * M be a rough covector field. The following are equivalent: (a) ! is smooth. (b) In every smooth coordinate chart, the component functions of ! are smooth. (e) For every open subset U ⊆ M and every smooth vector field X on U , the function ω ( X ) : U → ℝ is smooth on U . | — |

| `FC08-C11-U031` | **Proposition 11.20** | Ch. 11, § The Differential of a Function; source L8130 | (Properties of the Differential).
