Dualizing this leads to a linear map on covectors going in the opposite direction. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U035` | **Construction/definition — the (pointwise) pullback by F at p** | Ch. 11, § Pullbacks of Covector Fields; source L8190 | Let F : M → N be a smooth map between smooth manifolds with or without boundary, and let p ∈ M be arbitrary. The differential d F _p : T _p M → T _F ( p ) N yields a dual linear map d F _p * : T _F (p) * N → T _p * M, called the (pointwise) pullback by F at p, or the cotangent map of F . Unraveling the definitions, we see that d F _p * is characterized by d F _p * (ω) (v) = ω (d F _p (v) ), text for ω ∈ T _F (p) * N, v ∈ T _p M. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U036` | **Construction/definition — pullback of** | Ch. 11, § Pullbacks of Covector Fields; source L8204 | When we discussed vector fields, we made a point of noting that pushforwards of vector fields under smooth maps are defined only in the special cases of diffeomorphisms or Lie group homomorphisms. The surprising thing about covectors is that covector fields always pull back to covector fields. Given a smooth map F : M → N and a covector field ω on N , define a rough covector field F * ω on M; called the pullback of ! by F , by (F * ω) _p = d F _p * (omega_F (p)).tag11.13 | — |
| `FC08-C11-U037` | **Proposition 11.25** | Ch. 11, § Pullbacks of Covector Fields; source L8218 | Let F : M → N be a smooth map between smooth manifolds with or without boundary. Suppose u is a continuous real-valued function on N , and ! is a covector field on N . Then F * (u ω) = (u ∘ F) F * ω .tag11.14 If in addition u is smooth, then F * d u = d (u ∘ F).tag11.15 | — |
| `FC08-C11-U038` | **Proposition 11.26** | Ch. 11, § Pullbacks of Covector Fields; source L8242 | Suppose F : M → N is a smooth map between smooth manifolds with or without boundary, and let ! be a covector field on N . Then F * ω is a (continuous) covector field on M . If ! is smooth, then so is F * ω | — |
| `FC08-C11-U039` | **Example 11.27** | Ch. 11, § Pullbacks of Covector Fields; source L8262 | Let F : ℝ ^3 ℝ ^2 be the map given by (u, v) = F (x, y, z) = (x ^2 y, y sin z ), and let ω ∈ X * ( ℝ ^2 ) be the covector field ω = u d v + v d u. According to (11.16), the pullback F * ω is given by beginarrayr l & F * ω = (u ∘ F) d (v ∘ F) + (v ∘ F) d (u ∘ F) & = (x ^2 y ) d (y sin z) + (y sin z) d (x ^2 y ) & = x ^2 y (sin z d y + y cos z d z) + y sin z (2 x y d x + x ^2 d y ) & = 2 x y ^2 sin z d x + 2 x ^2 y sin z d y + x ^2 y ^2 cos z d z. endarray In other words, to compute F * ω , all you need to do is substitute the component functions of F for the coordinate functions of N everywhere they appear in !! This also yields an easy way to remember the transformation law for a covector field under a change of coordinates. Again, an example will convey the idea better than a general formula. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U040` | **Example 11.28** | Ch. 11, § Pullbacks of Covector Fields; source L8284 | Let ( r , θ ) be polar coordinates on, say, the right half-plane H = ( x , y ) : x > 0 . We can think of the change of coordinates ( x , y ) = ( r cos θ , r sin θ ) as the coordinate expression for the identity map of H , but using ( r , θ ) as coordinates for the domain and ( x , y ) for the codomain. Then the pullback formula (11.17) tells us that we can compute the polar coordinate expression for a covector field simply by substituting x = r cos θ, y = r sin θ and expanding. For example, beginarrayr l x d y - y d x & = Id * (x d y - y d x) & = (r cos θ) d (r sin θ) - (r sin θ) d (r cos θ) endarray beginarrayl = (r cos θ) (sin θ d r + r cos θ d θ) - (r sin θ) (cos θ d r - r sin θ d θ) = (r cos θ sin θ - r sin θ cos θ) d r + (r ^2 cos^2 θ + r ^2 sin^2 θ ) d θ = r ^2 d θ . endarraytag | — |
| `FC08-C11-U041` | **Definition/convention — restriction of** | Ch. 11, § Restricting Covector Fields to Submanifolds; source L8304 | since d iota _p : T _p S → T _p M is just the inclusion map, under our usual identification of T _p S with a subspace of T _p M . Thus, iota * ω is just the restriction of ! to vectors tangent to S . For this reason, iota * ω is often called the restriction of ! to S . Be warned, however, that iota * ω might equal zero at a given point of S, even though considered as a covector field on M , ! might not vanish there. An example will help to clarify this distinction. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U042` | **Example 11.29** | Ch. 11, § Restricting Covector Fields to Submanifolds; source L8306 | Let ω = d y on ℝ ^2 , and let S be the x-axis, considered as an embedded submanifold of ℝ ^2 . As a covector field on ℝ ^2 , ! is nonzero everywhere, because one of its component functions is always 1. The weaker condition that iota * ω = 0 is expressed by saying that the restriction of ! to S vanishes, or the pullback of ! to S vanishes. However, the restriction iota * ω is identically zero, because y vanishes identically on S: iota* ω = iota* d y = d (y ∘ iota) = 0.tag To distinguish the two ways in which we might interpret the statement “! vanishes on S _Psi ^~ tiny ~ > ~ one usually says that ! vanishes along S or vanishes at points of S if ω _p = 0 for every point p ∈ S . | — |
| `FC08-C11-U043` | **Convention/notation — integral of** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8320 | We begin with the simplest case: an interval in the real line. Suppose [ a , b ] ⊆ ℝ is a compact interval, and ω is a smooth covector field on Œa; b. (This means that the component function of ω admits a smooth extension to some neighborhood of [ a , b ] . ) If we let t denote the standard coordinate on R, then ω can be written ω _t = f ( t ) d t for some smooth function f : [ a , b ] ℝ . The similarity between this and the usual notation textstyle int f ( t ) d t for an integral suggests that there might be a connection between covector fields and integrals, and indeed there is. We define the integral of ! over [ a , b ] to be int_[ a, b ] ω = int_a ^b f (t) d t. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U044` | **Proposition 11.31** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8328 | (Diffeomorphism Invariance of the Integral). Let ! be a smooth covector field on the compact interval [ a , b ] ⊆ ℝ . If φ : [ c , d ] [ a , b ] is an increasing diffeomorphism (meaning that t _1 < t _2 implies φ ( t _1 ) < φ ( t _2 ) ) , then int_[ c, d ] φ* ω = int_[ a, b ] ω . | — |
| `FC08-C11-U045` | **Definition/convention — smooth curve segment** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8346 | Now let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. By a curve segment in M we mean a continuous curve γ : [ a , b ] → M whose domain is a compact interval. It is a smooth curve segment if it is smooth when [ a , b ] is considered as a manifold with boundary (or, equivalently, if γ has an extension to a smooth curve defined in a neighborhood of each endpoint). It is a piecewise smooth curve segment if there exists a finite partition a = a _0 < a _1 < ·s < a _k = b of [ a , b ] such that γ vert _ [ a _i - 1 , a _i ] is smooth for each i (Fig. 11.4). Continuity of γ means that γ ( t ) approaches the same value as t approaches any of the points a _i (other than a _0 or a _k ) from the left or the right. Smoothness of γ on each subinterval means that γ has one-sided velocity vectors at each such a _i when approaching from the left or the right, but these one-sided velocities need not be equal. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U046` | **Proposition 11.33** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8348 | If M is a connected smooth manifold with or without boundary, any two points of M can be joined by a piecewise smooth curve segment. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U047` | **Definition/convention — line integral of** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8361 | If γ : [ a , b ] → M is a smooth curve segment and ω is a smooth covector field on M; we define the line integral of ! over γ to be the real numbe int_γ ω = int_[ a, b ] γ* ω . Because γ * ω is a smooth covector field on [ a , b ] , this definition makes sense. More generally, if γ is piecewise smooth, we define int_γ ω = sum_i = 1 ^k int_ [ a _i - 1, a _i ] γ* ω , | — |
| `FC08-C11-U048` | **Proposition 11.34** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8375 | (Properties of Line Integrals). Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. Suppose γ : [ a , b ] → M is a piecewise smooth curve segment, and !; ω _1 , ω _2 ∈ X * ( M ) (a) For any c _1 , c _2 ∈ ℝ int_γ (c _1 omega_1 + c _2 omega_2) = c _1 int_γ omega_1 + c _2 int_γ omega_2. I f γ is a constant map, then beginarray r int _γ ω = 0 endarray (c) I f γ _1 = γ ∣ _[ a , c ] and γ _2 = γ ∣ _[ c , b ] with a < c < b , then int_γ ω = int_ gamma_1 ω + int_ gamma_2 ω . (d) If F : M → N is any smooth map and η ∈ X * ( N ) , then int_γ F * η = int_F ∘ γ η . | — |
| `FC08-C11-U049` | **Example 11.36** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8399 | Let M = ℝ ^2 setminus 0 , let ! be the covector field on M given by ω = (x d y - y d x)/(x ^2 + y ^2), and let γ : [ 0 , 2 π ] → M be the curve segment defined by γ ( t ) = ( cos t , sin t ) . Since γ * ω can be computed by substituting x = cos t and y = sin t everywhere in the formula for !, we find that int_γ ω = int_[ 0, 2 π ] (cos t (cos t d t) - sin t (- sin t d t))/(sin^2 t + cos^2 t) = int_0 ^2 π d t = 2 π .tag One of the most significant features of line integrals is that they are independent of parametrization, in a sense we now make precise. If γ : [ a , b ] → M and tilde(γ) : [ c , d ] → M are piecewise smooth curve segments, we say that tilde(γ) is a reparametrization of if widetilde γ = γ ∘ φ for some diffeomorphism φ : [ c , d ] → [ a , b ] . If φ is an increasing function, we say that tilde(γ) is a forward reparametrization, and if φ is decreasing, it is a backward reparametrization. (More generally, with obvious modifications one can allow φ to be piecewise smooth.) | — |
| `FC08-C11-U050` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8411 | One of the most significant features of line integrals is that they are independent of parametrization, in a sense we now make precise. If γ : [ a , b ] → M and tilde(γ) : [ c , d ] → M are piecewise smooth curve segments, we say that tilde(γ) is a reparametrization of if widetilde γ = γ ∘ φ for some diffeomorphism φ : [ c , d ] → [ a , b ] . If φ is an increasing function, we say that tilde(γ) is a forward reparametrization, and if φ is decreasing, it is a backward reparametrization. (More generally, with obvious modifications one can allow φ to be piecewise smooth.) | — |
| `FC08-C11-U051` | **Proposition 11.37** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8413 | (Parameter Independence of Line Integrals). Suppose M is a smooth manifold with o r without boundary, ω ∈ X * ( M ) , and is a piecewise smooth curve segment in M . For any reparametrization tilde(γ) of γ , we have int_tilde(γ) ω = beginarrayl l int_γ ω & text if tilde(γ) text is a forward reparametrization , - int_γ ω & text if tilde(γ) text is a backward reparametrization . endarray . | — |
| `FC08-C11-U052` | **Proposition 11.38** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8429 | I f γ : [ a , b ] → M is a piecewise smooth curve segment, the line integral of ! over can also be expressed as the ordinary integral int_γ ω = int_a ^b omega_γ (t) (γ^prime (t)) d t.tag11.18 | — |
| `FC08-C11-U053` | **Theorem 11.39** | Ch. 11, § Line Integrals; source L8457 | (Fundamental Theorem for Line Integrals). Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. Suppose f is a smooth real-valued function on M and γ : [ a , b ] → M is a piecewise smooth curve segment in M . Then int_γ d f = f (γ (b)) - f (γ (a)). | — |
| `FC08-C11-U054` | **Definition/convention — potential for** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8481 | Theorem 11.39 shows that the line integral of any covector field that can be written as the differential of a smooth function can be computed easily once the smooth function is known. For this reason, there is a special term for covector fields with this property. A smooth covector field ω on a smooth manifold M with or without boundary is said to be exact (or an exact differential) on M if there is a function f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) such that ω = d f . In this case, the function f is called a potential for !. The potential is not uniquely determined, but by Proposition 11.22, the difference between any two potentials for ! must be constant on each component of M . | `FC08-C11-U053`, `FC08-C11-U032` |
| `FC08-C11-U055` | **Definition/convention — conservative** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8485 | We say that a smooth covector field ω is conservative if the line integral of ! over every piecewise smooth closed curve segment is zero. This terminology comes from physics, where a force field is called conservative if the change in energy caused by the force acting along any closed path is zero (“energy is conserved”). (In elementary physics, force fields are usually thought of as vector fields rather than covector fields; see Problem 11-15 for the connection.) | — |
| `FC08-C11-U056` | **Proposition 11.40** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8489 | A smooth covector field ! is conservative if and only if its line integrals are path-independent, in the sense that textstyle int _γ ω = int _tilde(γ) ! whenever and tilde(γ) are piecewise smooth curve segments with the same starting and ending points. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U057` | **Theorem 11.42** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8493 | Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. A smooth covector field on M is conservative if and only if it is exact. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U058` | **Example 11.43** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8546 | The covector field ω of Example 11.36 cannot be exact on ℝ ^2 setminus bar 0 , because it is not conservative: the computation in that example showed that textstyle int _γ ω = 2 π ≠ 0 , where γ is the unit circle traversed counterclockwise. // | `FC08-C11-U049` |
| `FC08-C11-U059` | **Definition/convention — closed covector field** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8562 | We say that a smooth covector field ω is closed if its components in every smooth chart satisfy (11.21). The following proposition summarizes the computation above. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U060` | **Proposition 11.44** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8564 | Every exact covector field is closed. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U061` | **Proposition 11.45** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8568 | Let ! be a smooth covector field on a smooth manifold M with or without boundary. The following are equivalent: (a) ! is closed. (b) ! satisfies (11.21) in some smooth chart around every point. (c) For any open subset U ⊆ M and smooth vector fields X , Y ∈ X ( U ) X (ω (Y)) - Y (ω (X)) = ω ([ X, Y ]).tag11.22 | — |
| `FC08-C11-U062` | **Corollary 11.46** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8600 | Suppose F : M → N is a local diffeomorphism. Then the pullback F * : X * ( N ) → X * ( M ) takes closed covector fields to closed covector fields, and exact ones to exact ones. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U063` | **Example 11.47** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8604 | Consider the following covector field on ℝ ^2 : ω = y cos x y d x + x cos x y d y. It is easy to check that (∂ (y cos x y))/(∂ y) = (∂ (x cos x y))/(∂ x) = cos x y - x y sin x y, so ! is closed. Thus η is not exact. The answer is almost yes, but there is an important restriction. It turns out that the answer to the question depends in a subtle way on the shape of the domain, as the next example illustrates. | `FC08-C11-U060` |
| `FC08-C11-U064` | **Example 11.48** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8632 | Look once again at the covector field ω of Example 11.36. A straightforward computation shows that ! is closed; but as we observed above, it is not exact on ℝ ^2 setminus 0 . On the other hand, if we restrict the domain to the right half-plane U = ( x , y ) : x > 0 , a computation shows that ω = d ( tan ^- 1 y / x ) there. This can be seen more clearly in polar coordinates, where ω = d θ . The problem, of course, is that there is no smooth (or even continuous) angle function on all of ℝ ^2 setminus 0 , which is a consequence of the “hole” in the center. // | `FC08-C11-U049` |
| `FC08-C11-U065` | **Definition/convention — star-shaped subset** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8634 | This last example illustrates a key principle: the question of whether a particular closed covector field is exact is a global one, depending on the shape of the domain in question. This observation is the starting point for de Rham cohomology, which expresses a deep relationship between smooth structures and topology. We will define de Rham cohomology and study this relationship in Chapter 17, but for now we can prove the following result. If dot V is a finite-dimensional vector space, a subset U ⊆ V is said to be star-shaped if there is a point c ∈ U such that for every x ∈ U the line segment from c to x is entirely contained in U (Fig. 11.6). For example, every convex subset is star-shaped. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U066` | **Theorem 11.49** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8636 | (Poincaré Lemma for Covector Fields). If U is a star-shaped open subset of ℝ ^n o r ℍ ^n , then every closed covector field on U is exact. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U067` | **Corollary 11.50** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8667 | (Local Exactness of Closed Covector Fields). Let ! be a closed covector field on a smooth manifold M with or without boundary. Then every point of M has a neighborhood on which ! is exact. | — |
| `FC08-C11-U068` | **Example 11.51** | Ch. 11, § Conservative Covector Fields; source L8675 | Let ω be the following covector field on ℝ ^3 : ω = e ^ y ^2 d x + 2 x y e ^ y ^2 d y - 2 z d z. You can check that ! is closed. Thus a potential function for ω is given by f ( x , y , z ) = x e ^ y ^2 - z ^2 . For f to be a potential for ω . , it must satisfy (∂ f)/(∂ x) = e ^ y ^2, (∂ f)/(∂ y) = 2 x y e ^ y ^2, (∂ f)/(∂ z) = - 2 z.tag11.25 Holding y and z fixed and integrating the first equation with respect to x, we obtain f (x, y, z) = int e ^ y ^2 d x = x e ^ y ^2 + C _1 (y, z), where the “constant” of integration C _1 ( y , z ) may depend on the choice of ( y , z ) Now the second equation of (11.25) implies 2 x y e ^ y ^2 = (∂)/(∂ y) (x e ^ y ^2 + C _1 (y, z) ) = 2 x y e ^ y ^2 + (∂ C _1)/(∂ y), which forces ∂ C _1 / ∂ y = 0 , so C _1 is actually a function of z only. Any other potential differs from this one by a constant. | — |
| `FC08-C12-U001` | **Definition/convention — multilinear** | Ch. 12, § Multilinear Algebra; source L8889 | Suppose V _1 , … , V _k , and W are vector spaces. A map F : V _1 × ·s × V _k → W is said to be multilinear if it is linear as a function of each variable separately when the others are held fixed: for each i, | — |
| `FC08-C12-U002` | **Example 12.1** | Ch. 12, § Multilinear Algebra; source L8903 | (Some Familiar Multilinear Functions). (a) The dot product in ℝ ^n is a scalar-valued bilinear function of two vectors, used to compute lengths of vectors and angles between them. (b) The cross product in ℝ ^3 is a vector-valued bilinear function of two vectors, used to compute areas of parallelograms and to find a third vector orthogonal to two given ones. (c) The determinant is a real-valued multilinear function of n vectors in ℝ ^n , used to detect linear independence and to compute the volume of the parallelepiped spanned by the vectors. (d) The bracket in a Lie algebra g is a g-valued bilinear function of two elements of g . . | — |
| `FC08-C12-U003` | **Example 12.2** | Ch. 12, § Multilinear Algebra; source L8915 | (Tensor Products of Covectors). Suppose V is a vector space, and ω , η ∈ V * . Define a function ω ⊗ η : V × V → ℝ by ω ⊗ η (v _1, v _2) = ω (v _1) η (v _2), where the product on the right is just ordinary multiplication of real numbers. The linearity of ! and η guarantees that ω ⊗ η is a bilinear function of v _1 and v _2 , so it is an element of L ( V , V ; ℝ ) . For example, if ( e ^1 , e ^2 ) denotes the standard dual basis for ( ℝ ^2 ) * , then e ^1 ⊗ e ^2 : ℝ ^2 × ℝ ^2 → ℝ is the bilinear function e ^1 ⊗ e ^2 ((w, x), (y, z)) = w z. The last example can be generalized to arbitrary real-valued multilinear functions as follows: let V _1 , … , V _k , W _1 , … , W _l be real vector spaces, and suppose F ∈ L ( V _1 , … , V _k ; ℝ ) and G ∈ L ( W _1 , … , W _l ; ℝ ) . Define a function F ⊗ G : V _1 × … × V _k × W _1 × … × W _l → ℝ by F ⊗ G (v _1, … , v _k, w _1, … , w _l) = F (v _1, … , v _k) G (w _1, … , w _l).tag12.1 It follows from the multilinearity of F and G that F ⊗ G ( v _1 , … , v _k , w _1 , … , w _l ) depends linearly on each argument v _i or w _j separately, so F ⊗ G is an element of L ( V _1 , … , V _k , W _1 , … , W _l ; ℝ ) , called the tensor product of F and G . | — |
| `FC08-C12-U004` | **Proposition 12.4** | Ch. 12, § Multilinear Algebra; source L8957 | (A Basis for the Space of Multilinear Functions). Let V _1 , … , V _k be real vector spaces of dimensions n _1 , … , n _k , respectively. For each j ∈ 1 , … , k let big ( E _1 ^( j ) , … , bar(E) _ n _j ^( j ) big ) be a basis for V _j , and let big ( ε _( j ) ^1 , … , ε _( j ) ^ n _j big ) be the corresponding dual basis for V _j * . Then the set B = varepsilon_(1) ^ i _1 ⊗ … ⊗ varepsilon_(k) ^ i _k: 1 ≤ i _1 ≤ n _1, … , 1 ≤ i _k ≤ n _k is a basis for L ( V _1 , … , V _k ; ℝ ) , which therefore has dimension equal to n _1 ·s n _k | — |
| `FC08-C12-U005` | **Definition/convention — formal linear combination of elements of S** | Ch. 12, § Abstract Tensor Products of Vector Spaces; source L8999 | To begin, we need to make sense of “formal linear combinations.” Let S be a set. Roughly speaking, a formal linear combination of elements of S is an expression of the form scriptstyle sum _i = 1 ^m a _i x _i , where a _1 , … , a _m are real numbers and x _1 , … , x _m are elements of S. Of course, since we are not assuming that S has any algebraic structure, we cannot literally add elements of S together or multiply them by numbers. But the essential feature of such an expression is that it is completely determined by which elements of S appear in the sum, and what coefficients appear with them. Thus, we make the following definition: for any set S, a formal linear combination of elements of S is a function f : S → ℝ such that f ( s ) = 0 for all but finitely many s ∈ S . The free (real) vector space on S , denoted by F ( S ) , is the set of all formal linear combinations of elements of S. Under pointwise addition and scalar multiplication, F ( S ) becomes a vector space over R. | — |
| `FC08-C12-U006` | **Proposition 12.5** | Ch. 12, § Abstract Tensor Products of Vector Spaces; source L9003 | (Characteristic Property of the Free Vector Space). For any set S and any vector space W , every map A : S W has a unique extension to a linear map bar(A) : F ( S ) → W | — |
| `FC08-C12-U007` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 12, § Abstract Tensor Products of Vector Spaces; source L9007 | Now let V _1 , … , V _k be real vector spaces. We begin by forming the free vector space F ( V _1 × ·s × V _k ) , which is the set of all finite formal linear combinations of k-tuples ( v _1 , … , v _k ) with v _i ∈ V _i for i = 1 , … , k . Let R be the subspace of F ( V _1 × ·s × V _k ) spanned by all elements of the following forms: (v _1, … , a v _i, … , v _k) - a (v _1, … , v _i, … , v _k), | — |
| `FC08-C12-U008` | **Construction/definition — the (abstract) tensor product of v** | Ch. 12, § Abstract Tensor Products of Vector Spaces; source L9019 | Define the tensor product of the spaces V _1 , … , V _k , denoted by V _1 ⊗ ·s ⊗ V _k to be the following quotient vector space: V _1 ⊗ … ⊗ V _k = F (V _1 × … × V _k) / R, and let π : F ( V _1 × ·s × V _k ) → V _1 ⊗ ·s ⊗ V _k be the natural projection. The equivalence class of an element ( v _1 , … , v _k ) in V _1 ⊗ ·s ⊗ V _k is denoted by v _1 ⊗ … ⊗ v _k = Pi (v _1, … , v _k),tag12.5 and is called the (abstract) tensor product of v _1 , … , v _k . It follows from the definition that abstract tensor products satisfy v _1 ⊗ … ⊗ a v _i ⊗ … ⊗ v _k = a (v _1 ⊗ … ⊗ v _i ⊗ … ⊗ v _k), beginarrayr l & v _1 ⊗ … ⊗ (v _i + v _i ^prime) ⊗ … ⊗ v _k = (v _1 ⊗ … ⊗ v _i ⊗ … ⊗ v _k) & + (v _1 ⊗ … ⊗ v _i ^prime ⊗ … ⊗ v _k). endarray Note that the definition implies that every element of V _1 ⊗ ·s ⊗ V _k can be expressed as a linear combination of elements of the form v _1 ⊗ ·s ⊗ v _k for v _i ∈ V _i ; but it is not true in general that every element of the tensor product space is of the form v _1 ⊗ ·s ⊗ v _k (see Problem 12-1). | — |
| `FC08-C12-U009` | **Proposition 12.7** | Ch. 12, § Abstract Tensor Products of Vector Spaces; source L9043 | (Characteristic Property of the Tensor Product Space). Let V _1 , … , V _k be finite-dimensional real vector spaces. If A V _1 × ·s × V _k → X is any multilinear map into a vector space X, then there is a unique linear map tilde(A) : V _1 ⊗ ·s ⊗ V _k → X such that the following diagram commutes: beginarrayc V _1 × … × V _k xrightarrow A X, Biggl downarrow π tilde(tilde(A)) V _1 ⊗ … ⊗ V _k endarraytag12.6 where π is the map π ( v _1 , … , v _k ) = v _1 ⊗ ·s ⊗ v _k . | — |
| `FC08-C12-U010` | **Proposition 12.8** | Ch. 12, § Abstract Tensor Products of Vector Spaces; source L9067 | (A Basis for the Tensor Product Space). Suppose V _1 , … , V _k are real vector spaces of dimensions n _1 , … , n _k , respectively. For each j = 1 , … , k suppose big ( E _1 ^( j ) , … , E _ n _j ^( j ) big ) is a basis for V _j . Then the set C = E _ i _1 ^(1) ⊗ … ⊗ E _ i _k ^(k): 1 ≤ i _1 ≤ n _1, … , 1 ≤ i _k ≤ n _k is a basis for V _1 ⊗ ·s ⊗ V _k , which therefore has dimension equal to n _1 ·s n _k | — |
| `FC08-C12-U011` | **Proposition 12.9** | Ch. 12, § Abstract Tensor Products of Vector Spaces; source L9097 | (Associativity of Tensor Product Spaces). Let V _1 , V _2 , V _3 be finite-dimensional real vector spaces. There are unique isomorphisms V _1 ⊗ (V _2 ⊗ V _3) ≅ V _1 ⊗ V _2 ⊗ V _3 ≅ (V _1 ⊗ V _2) ⊗ V _3, under which elements of the forms v _1 ⊗ ( v _2 ⊗ v _3 ) , v _1 ⊗ v _2 ⊗ v _3 , and ( v _1 ⊗ v _2 ) ⊗ v _3 all correspond. | — |
| `FC08-C12-U012` | **Proposition 12.10** | Ch. 12, § Abstract Tensor Products of Vector Spaces; source L9115 | (Abstract vs. Concrete Tensor Products). Thus, T ^1 ( V * ) is equal to V * (b) A covariant 2-tensor on V is a real-valued bilinear function of two vectors, also called a bilinear form. | `FC08-C12-U020` | **Corollary 12.12** | Ch. 12, § Covariant and Contravariant Tensors on a Vector Space; source L9199 | Let V be an n-dimensional real vector space. Suppose ( E _i ) is any basis for V and ( ε ^j ) is the dual basis for V * . Then the following sets constitute bases for the tensor spaces over V : ε^ i _1 ⊗ … ⊗ ε^ i _k: 1 ≤ i _1, … , i _k ≤ n f o r T ^k (V *); E _ i _1 ⊗ … ⊗ E _ i _k: 1 ≤ i _1, … , i _k ≤ n f o r T ^k (V); E _ i _1 ⊗ … ⊗ E _ i _k ⊗ ε^ j _1 ⊗ … ⊗ ε^ j _l: 1 ≤ i _1, … , i _k, j _1, … , j _l ≤ n f o r T ^(k, l) (V). Therefore, dim T ^k ( V * ) = dim T ^k ( V ) = n ^k and dim T ^( k , l ) ( V ) = n ^k + l In particular, once a basis is chosen for V , every covariant k-tensor α ∈ T ^k ( V * ) can be written uniquely in the form α = alpha_ i _1 … i _k ε^ i _1 ⊗ … ⊗ ε^ i _k, where the n ^k coefficients α _ i _1 … i _k are determined by alpha_ i _1 … i _k = α (E _ i _1, … , E _ i _k ). For example, T ^2 ( V * ) is the space of bilinear forms on V , and every bilinear form can be written as β = β _i j ε ^i ⊗ ε ^j for some uniquely determined n × n matrix ( β _i j ) /. In this book we are concerned primarily with covariant tensors. Thus tensors wil always be understood to be covariant unless we explicitly specify otherwise. However, it is important to be aware that contravariant and mixed tensors play important roles in more advanced parts of differential geometry, especially Riemannian geometry. | — |
| `FC08-C12-U021` | **Definition/convention — symmetric** | Ch. 12, § Symmetric Tensors; source L9237 | Let V be a finite-dimensional vector space. | `FC08-C12-U022` | **Convention/notation — new k - t e n s o r** | Ch. 12, § Symmetric Tensors; source L9253 | The set of symmetric covariant k-tensors is a linear subspace of the space T ^k ( V * ) of all covariant k-tensors on V : ; we denote this subspace by Sigma ^k ( dot V * ) There is a natural projection from T ^k ( V * ) to Sigma ^k ( V * ) defined as follows. First, let S _k denote the symmetric group on k elements, that is, the group of permutations of the set 1 , … , k . Given a k-tensor ˛ and a permutation σ ∈ S _k , we define a new k - t e n s o r ^σ α by ^σ α ( v _1 , … , v _k ) = α ( v _σ ( 1 ) , … , v _σ ( k ) ) . | — |
| `FC08-C12-U023` | **Convention/notation — symmetrization by Sym α** | Ch. 12, § Symmetric Tensors; source L9259 | Note that τ ( ^σ α ) = ^τ σ α , where τσ represents the composition of τ and σ _: , that is, τ σ ( i ) = τ bigl ( σ ( i ) ) . | `FC08-C12-U024` | **Proposition 12.14** | Ch. 12, § Symmetric Tensors; source L9271 | (Properties of Symmetrization). Let ˛ be a covariant tensor on a finite-dimensional vector space. (a) Sym ˛ is symmetric. (b) Sym α = α if and only if ˛ is symmetric. | — |

| `FC08-C12-U026` | **Proposition 12.15** | Ch. 12, § Symmetric Tensors; source L9303 | (Properties of the Symmetric Product). (a) The symmetric product is symmetric and bilinear: for all symmetric tensors ˛, β , γ and all a , b ∈ ℝ beginarrayc α β = β α , (a α + b β) γ = a α γ + b β γ = γ (a α + b β). endarray (b) If ˛ and β are covectors, then α β = (1)/(2) (α ⊗ β + β ⊗ α). | — |
| `FC08-C12-U027` | **Definition/convention — exterior forms** | Ch. 12, § Alternating Tensors; source L9321 | We continue to assume that V is a finite-dimensional real vector space. A covariant k-tensor ˛ on V is said to be alternating (or antisymmetric or skew-symmetric) if it changes sign whenever two of its arguments are interchanged. This means that for all vectors v _1 , …c , v _k ∈ V and every pair of distinct indices i , j it satisfies α (v _1, … , v _i, … , v _j, … , v _k) = - α (v _1, … , v _j, … , v _i, … , v _k). Alternating covariant k-tensors are also variously called exterior forms, multicovectors, or k-covectors. The subspace of all alternating covariant k-tensors on V is denoted by Lambda ^k ( V * ) ⊆ T ^k ( V * ) . | — |
| `FC08-C12-U028` | **Definition/convention — alternating 2-tensor** | Ch. 12, § Alternating Tensors; source L9343 | Every 0-tensor (which is just a real number) is both symmetric and alternating, because there are no arguments to interchange. Similarly, every 1-tensor is both symmetric and alternating. An alternating 2-tensor on V is a skew-symmetric bilinear form. It is interesting to note that every covariant 2-tensor β can be expressed as a sum of an alternating tensor and a symmetric one, because | — |

| `FC08-C12-U031` | **Definition/convention — tensor bundle** | Ch. 12, § Tensors and Tensor Fields on Manifolds; source L9397 | Any one of these bundles is called a tensor bundle over M . (Thus, the tangent and cotangent bundles are special cases of tensor bundles.) A section of a tensor bundle is called a (covariant, contravariant, or mixed) tensor field on M . A smooth tensor field is a section that is smooth in the usual sense of smooth sections of vector bundles. Using the identifications above, we see that contravariant 1-tensor fields are the same as vector fields, and covariant 1-tensor fields are covector fields. Because a 0-tensor is just a real number, a 0-tensor field is the same as a continuous real-valued function. | — |

Because smooth covariant tensor fields occupy most of our attention, we adopt the following shorthand notation for the space of all smooth covariant k-tensor fields: T ^k (M) = Gamma (T ^k T * M ). | — |
| `FC08-C12-U033` | **Proposition 12.19** | Ch. 12, § Tensors and Tensor Fields on Manifolds; source L9411 | (Smoothness Criteria for Tensor Fields). Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and let A M → T ^k T * M be a rough section. The following are equivalent. (a) A is smooth. (b) In every smooth coordinate chart, the component functions of A are smooth. (c) Each point of M is contained in some coordinate chart in which A has smooth component functions. (d) I f X _1 , … , X _k ∈ X ( M ) , then the function A ( X _1 , … , X _k ) : M → ℝ , defined by A (X _1, … , X _k) (p) = A _p (X _1 ∣ _p, … , X _k ∣ _p ), is smooth. (e) Whenever X _1 , … , X _k are smooth vector fields defined on some open subset U ⊆ M ; the function A ( X _1 , … , X _k ) is smooth on U . | — |
| `FC08-C12-U034` | **Proposition 12.22** | Ch. 12, § Tensors and Tensor Fields on Manifolds; source L9433 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, A ∈ mathcal T ^k ( M ) , B ∈ mathcal T ^l ( M ) , and f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) . Then fA and A B are also smooth tensor fields, whose components in any smooth local coordinate chart are beginarrayc (f A) _ i _1 … i _k = f A _ i _1 … i _k, (A ⊗ B) _ i _1 … i _k + l = A _ i _1 … i _k B _ i _k + 1 … i _k + l. endarray | — |

A map A : underbrace X (M) × ·s × X (M) _ k text copies → C ^∞ (M),tag12.7 is induced by a smooth covariant k-tensor field as above if and only if it is multilinear over C ^∞ ( M ) | — |
| `FC08-C12-U036` | **Definition/convention — covariant tensor field** | Ch. 12, § Tensors and Tensor Fields on Manifolds; source L9489 | A symmetric tensor field on a manifold (with or without boundary) is simply a covariant tensor field whose value at each point is a symmetric tensor. The symmetric product of two or more tensor fields is defined pointwise, just like the tensor product. Thus, for example, if A and B are smooth covector fields, their symmetric product is the smooth 2-tensor field AB, which by Proposition 12.15(b) is given by A B = (1)/(2) (A ⊗ B + B ⊗ A). | `FC08-C12-U026` |

If A is a covariant k-tensor field on N , we define a rough k-tensor field F * A on M; called the pullback of A by F , by (F * A) _p = d F _p * big (A _F (p) big). | — |
| `FC08-C12-U039` | **Proposition 12.25** | Ch. 12, § Pullbacks of Tensor Fields; source L9519 | (Properties of Tensor Pullbacks). Suppose F : M → N and G : N → P are smooth maps, A and B are covariant tensor fields on N , and f is a real-valued function on N . (a) F * ( f B ) = ( f ∘ F ) F * B (b) F * ( A ⊗ B ) = F * A ⊗ F * B . (c) F * ( A + B ) = F * A + F * B (d) F * B is a (continuous) tensor field, and is smooth if B is smooth. (e) ( G ∘ F ) * B = F * ( G * B ) (f) ( I d _N ) * B = B | — |
| `FC08-C12-U040` | **Corollary 12.28** | Ch. 12, § Pullbacks of Tensor Fields; source L9541 | Let F : M → N be smooth, and let B be a covariant k-tensor field on N , I f p ∈ M and ( y ^i ) are smooth coordinates for N on a neighborhood of F ( p ) , then F * B has the following expression in a neighborhood of p: beginarrayl F * (B _ i _1 … i _k d y ^ i _1 ⊗ … ⊗ d y ^ i _k ) = (B _ i _1 … i _k ∘ F ) d (y ^ i _1 ∘ F ) ⊗ … ⊗ d (y ^ i _k ∘ F ). endarray In words, this corollary just says that F * B is computed by the same technique we described in Chapter 11 for computing the pullback of a covector field: wherever you see y ^i in the expression for B, just substitute the i th component function of F and expand. | — |
| `FC08-C12-U041` | **Example 12.29** | Ch. 12, § Pullbacks of Tensor Fields; source L9549 | (Pullback of a Tensor Field). Let M = ( r , θ ) : r > 0 , ∣ θ ∣ < π / 2 and N = ( x , y ) : x > 0 , and let F : M → ℝ ^2 be the smooth map F ( r , θ ) = .r cos θ; r sin θ /. The pullback of the tensor field A = x ^- 2 d y ⊗ d y by F can be computed easily by substituting x = r cos θ , y = r sin θ and simplifying: beginarrayl F * A = (r cos θ) ^- 2 d (r sin θ) ⊗ d (r sin θ) = (r cos θ) ^- 2 (sin θ d r + r cos θ d θ) ⊗ (sin θ d r + r cos θ d θ) = r ^- 2 tan^2 θ d r ⊗ d r + r ^- 1 tan θ (d θ ⊗ d r + d r ⊗ d θ) + d θ ⊗ d θ . endarray In general, there is neither a pushforward nor a pullback operation for mixed tensor fields. However, in the special case of a diffeomorphism, tensor fields of any variance can be pushed forward and pulled back at will (see Problem 12-10). | — |

| `FC08-C12-U047` | **Example 12.35** | Ch. 12, § Lie Derivatives of Tensor Fields; source L9636 | Suppose A is an arbitrary smooth covariant 2-tensor field, and V is a smooth vector field. We compute the Lie derivative L _V A in smooth local coordinates ( x ^i ) . First, we observe that L _V d x ^i = d ( L _V x ^i ) = d ( V x ^i ) = d V ^i Therefore, beginarrayl L _V A = L _V (A _i j d x ^i ⊗ d x ^j ) = L _V (A _i j) d x ^i ⊗ d x ^j + A _i j (L _V d x ^i ) ⊗ d x ^j + A _i j d x ^i ⊗ (L _V d x ^j ) = V A _i j d x ^i ⊗ d x ^j + A _i j d V ^i ⊗ d x ^j + A _i j d x ^i ⊗ d V ^j = (V A _i j + A _k j (∂ V ^k)/(∂ x ^i) + A _i k (∂ V ^k)/(∂ x ^j) ) d x ^i ⊗ d x ^j. endarray Recall that the Lie derivative of a vector field W with respect to V is zero if and only if W is invariant under the flow of V (see Theorem 9.42). It turns out that the Lie derivative of a covariant tensor field has exactly the same interpretation. If A is a smooth tensor field on M and θ is a flow on M; we say that A is invariant under θ if for each t , the map θ _t pulls A back to itself wherever it is defined; more precisely, this means d (theta_t) _p * (A _ theta_t (p)) = A _ptag12.11 for all ( t , p ) in the domain of θ . If θ is a global flow, this is equivalent to θ _t * A = A for all t ∈ ℝ In order to prove the connection between Lie derivatives and invariance under flows, we need the following proposition, which shows how the Lie derivative can be used to compute t-derivatives at times other than t = 0 . It is a generalization to tensor fields of Proposition 9.41. | `FC08-C09-U058`, `FC08-C09-U056` |

| `FC08-C13-U001` | **Definition/convention — Riemannian metric** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Manifolds; source L9788 | Transferring these ideas to manifolds, we obtain one of the most important applications of tensors to differential geometry. Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. A Riemannian metric on M is a smooth symmetric covariant 2-tensor field on M that is positive definite at each point. A Riemannian manifold is a pair ( M , g ) , where M is a smooth manifold and g is a Riemannian metric on M . One sometimes simply says “M is a Riemannian manifold” if M is understood to be endowed with a specific Riemannian metric. A Riemannian manifold with boundary is defined similarly. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U002` | **Definition/convention — Riemannian metric** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Manifolds; source L9792 | Note that a Riemannian metric is not the same thing as a metric in the sense of metric spaces, although the two concepts are related, as we will see below. Because of this ambiguity, we usually use the term “distance function” for a metric in the metric space sense, and reserve “metric” for a Riemannian metric. In any event, which type of metric is being considered should always be clear from the context. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U003` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Manifolds; source L9794 | If g is a Riemannian metric on M; then for each p ∈ M ; the 2-tensor g _p is an inner product on T _p M . Because of this, we often use the notation langle v , w rangle _g to denote the real number g _p ( v , w ) for v , w ∈ T _p M | — |
| `FC08-C13-U004` | **Example 13.1** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Manifolds; source L9808 | (The Euclidean Metric). The simplest example of a Riemannian metric is the Euclidean metric bar(g) on ℝ ^n , given in standard coordinates by bar(g) = delta_i j d x ^i d x ^j, where delta _i j is the Kronecker delta. It is common to abbreviate the symmetric product of a tensor ˛ with itself by α ^2 , so the Euclidean metric can also be written bar(g) = (d x ^1 ) ^2 + … + (d x ^n ) ^2. Applied to vectors v; w ∈ T _p ℝ ^n , this yields bar(g) _p (v, w) = delta_i j v ^i w ^j = sum_i = 1 ^n v ^i w ^i = v · w. In other words, bar(g) is the 2-tensor field whose value at each point is the Euclidean dot product. (As you may recall, we warned in Chapter 1 that expressions involving the Euclidean dot product are likely to violate our index conventions and therefore to require explicit summation signs. This can usually be avoided by writing the metric coefficients delta _i j explicitly, as in delta _i j v ^i w ^j . ) | — |
| `FC08-C13-U005` | **Example 13.2** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Manifolds; source L9828 | (Product Metrics). If ( M , g ) and ( widetilde M , widetilde g ) are Riemannian manifolds, we can define a Riemannian metric widehat g = g oplus widetilde g on the product manifold M × widetilde M , called the product metric, as follows: hat(g) big ((v, tilde(v)), (w, tilde(w)) big) = g (v, w) + tilde(g) (tilde(v), tilde(w))tag13.1 for any ( v , tilde(v) ) , ( w , tilde(w) ) ∈ T _p M oplus T _q tilde(M) ≅ T _( p , q ) ( M × tilde(M) ) . Given any local coordinates ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) for M and ( y ^1 , … , y ^m ) for tilde(M) , we obtain local coordinates ( x ^1 , … , x ^n , y ^1 , … , y ^m ) for M × tilde(M) , and you can check that the product metric is represented locally by the block diagonal matrix (hat(g) _i j) = ( beginarrayc c g _i j & 0 0 & tilde(g) _i j endarray ). For example, it is easy to verify that the Euclidean metric on ℝ ^n + m is the same as the product metric determined by the Euclidean metrics on ℝ ^n and ℝ ^m | — |
| `FC08-C13-U006` | **Proposition 13.3** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Manifolds; source L9844 | (Existence of Riemannian Metrics). Every smooth manifold with or without boundary admits a Riemannian metric. | — |

| `FC08-C13-U010` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Manifolds; source L9878 | One highly useful tool for the study of Riemannian manifolds is orthonormal frames. Just as we did for the case of R n in Chapter 8, we say that a local frame ( E _1 , … , E _n ) for M on an open subset U ⊆ M is an orthonormal frame if the vectors ( E _1 ∣ _p , … , E _n ∣ _p ) form an orthonormal basis for T _p M at each point p ∈ U , or equivalently if langle bar E _i , bar E _j rangle _g = delta _i j | — |
| `FC08-C13-U011` | **Example 13.4** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Manifolds; source L9880 | The coordinate frame ( ∂ / ∂ x ^i ) is a global orthonormal frame for ℝ ^n with the Euclidean metric. // | — |

| `FC08-C13-U013` | **Proposition 13.6** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Manifolds; source L9886 | Suppose ( M , g ) is a Riemannian manifold with or without boundary, and ( X _j ) is a smooth local frame for M over an open subset U ⊆ M . Then there is a smooth orthonormal frame ( E _j ) over U such that span ( E _1 ∣ _p , … , E _j ∣ _p ) = s p a n ( X _1 ∣ _p , … , X _j ∣ _p ) for each j = 1 , …c , n and each p ∈ U | — |

| `FC08-C13-U015` | **Construction/definition — pullback metric determined by F** | Ch. 13, § Pullback Metrics; source L9898 | Suppose M; N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary, g is a Riemannian metric on N , and F : M → N is smooth. The pullback F * g is a smooth 2-tensor field on M . If it is positive definite, it is a Riemannian metric on M; called the pullback metric determined by F . The next proposition shows when this is the case. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U016` | **Proposition 13.9** | Ch. 13, § Pullback Metrics; source L9903 | (Pullback Metric Criterion). Suppose F : M → N is a smooth map and g is a Riemannian metric on N . Then F * g is a Riemannian metric on M i f and only if F is a smooth immersion. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U017` | **Example 13.11** | Ch. 13, § Pullback Metrics; source L9909 | Consider the smooth map F : ℝ ^2 → ℝ ^3 given by F (u, v) = (u cos v, u sin v, v). It is a proper injective smooth immersion, and thus it is an embedding by Proposition 4.22. Its image is a surface called a helicoid; it looks like an infinitely wide spiral-shaped ramp (Fig. 13.1).The pullback metric F * bar(g) can be computed by substituting the coordinate functions for F in place of x , y , z in the formula for bar(g) : beginarrayl F * bar(g) = d (u cos v) ^2 + d (u sin v) ^2 + d (v) ^2 = (cos v d u - u sin v d v) ^2 + (sin v d u + u cos v d v) ^2 + d v ^2 = cos^2 v d u ^2 - 2 u sin v cos v d u d v + u ^2 sin^2 v d v ^2 + sin^2 v d u ^2 + 2 u sin v cos v d u d v + u ^2 cos^2 v d v ^2 + d v ^2 = d u ^2 + (u ^2 + 1) d v ^2. endarray (By convention, when u is a real-valued function, the notation d u ^2 means the symmetric product du du, not scriptstyle d ( u ^2 ) ) . | `FC08-C04-U022` |
| `FC08-C13-U018` | **Example 13.12** | Ch. 13, § Pullback Metrics; source L9925 | To illustrate, we compute the coordinate expression for the Euclidean metric bar(g) = d x ^2 + d y ^2 on ℝ ^2 in polar coordinates. Substituting x = r cos θ and y = r sin θ and expanding, we obtain beginarrayl bar(g) = d x ^2 + d y ^2 = d (r cos θ) ^2 + d (r sin θ) ^2 = (cos θ d r - r sin θ d θ) ^2 + (sin θ d r + r cos θ d θ) ^2 = (cos^2 θ + sin^2 θ ) d r ^2 + (r ^2 sin^2 θ + r ^2 cos^2 θ ) d θ^2 + (- 2 r cos θ sin θ + 2 r sin θ cos θ) d r d θ = d r ^2 + r ^2 d θ^2. endarray If ( M , g ) and ( tilde(M) , tilde(g) ) are both Riemannian manifolds, a smooth map F : M → tilde(M) is called a (Riemannian) isometry if it is a diffeomorphism that satisfies F * widetilde g = g . More generally, F is called a local isometry if every point p ∈ M has a neighborhood U such that F ∣ _U is an isometry of U onto an open subset of tilde(M) ; or equivalently, if F is a local diffeomorphism satisfying F * widetilde g = g If there exists a Riemannian isometry between ( M , g ) and ( widetilde M , widetilde g ) , we say that they are isometric as Riemannian manifolds. The study of properties of Riemannian manifolds that are invariant under (local or global) isometries is called Riemannian geometry. One such property is flatness. A Riemannian n-manifold ( M , g ) is said to be a flat Riemannian manifold, and g is a flat metric, if ( M , g ) is locally isometric to ( ℝ ^n , bar(g) ) /. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U019` | **Definition/convention — isometric and locally isometric Riemannian manifolds** | Ch. 13, § Pullback Metrics; source L9933 | If there exists a Riemannian isometry between ( M , g ) and ( widetilde M , widetilde g ) , we say that they are isometric as Riemannian manifolds. If each point of M has a neighborhood that is isometric to an open subset of ( tilde(M) , tilde(g) ) , then we say that ( M , g ) is locally isometric to ( tilde(M) , tilde(g) ) . The study of properties of Riemannian manifolds that are invariant under (local or global) isometries is called Riemannian geometry. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U020` | **Definition/convention — flat Riemannian manifold and flat metric** | Ch. 13, § Pullback Metrics; source L9935 | One such property is flatness. A Riemannian n-manifold ( M , g ) is said to be a flat Riemannian manifold, and g is a flat metric, if ( M , g ) is locally isometric to ( ℝ ^n , bar(g) ) /. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U021` | **Theorem 13.14** | Ch. 13, § Pullback Metrics; source L9941 | For a Riemannian manifold ( M , g ) , the following are equivalent: (a) g is flat. (b) Each point of M is contained in the domain of a smooth coordinate chart in which g has the coordinate representation g = delta _i j d x ^i d x ^j (c) Each point of M is contained in the domain of a smooth coordinate chart in which the coordinate frame is orthonormal. (d) Each point of M is contained in the domain of a commuting orthonormal frame. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U022` | **Construction/definition — induced metric** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Submanifolds; source L9965 | Pullback metrics are especially important for submanifolds. If ( M , g ) is a Riemannian manifold with or without boundary, every submanifold S ⊆ M (immersed or embedded, with or without boundary) automatically inherits a pullback metric iota * g where iota : S ↪ M is inclusion. In this setting, the pullback metric is also called the induced metric on S. By definition, this means for v; w ∈ T _p S that (iota* g ) (v, w) = g (d iota_p (v), d iota_p (w) ) = g (v, w), because d iota _p : T _p S → T _p M is our usual identification of T _p S as a subspace of T _p M Thus iota * g is just the restriction of g to pairs of vectors tangent to S . With this metric, S is called a Riemannian submanifold (with or without boundary) of M . | — |
| `FC08-C13-U023` | **Example 13.16** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Submanifolds; source L9973 | The metric stackrel ∘ g = iota * bar(g) induced on mathbb S ^n by the usual inclusion iota : mathbb S ^n ↪ ℝ ^n + 1 is called the round metric (or the standard metric) on the sphere. // | — |
| `FC08-C13-U024` | **Example 13.17** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Submanifolds; source L9977 | (Induced Metrics in Graph Coordinates). Let U ⊆ ℝ ^n be an open subset, and let S ⊆ ℝ ^n + 1 be the graph of a smooth function f : U → ℝ The map X : U → ℝ ^n + 1 given by X big ( u ^1 , … , u ^n big ) = big ( u ^1 , … , u ^n , f ( u ) big ) is a smooth global parametrization of S and the induced metric on S is given in graph coordinates by X * bar(g) = X * ( (d x ^1 ) ^2 + … + (d x ^n + 1 ) ^2 ) = (d u ^1 ) ^2 + … + (d u ^n ) ^2 + d f ^2. For example, the upper hemisphere of mathbb S ^2 is parametrized by the map X : mathbb B ^2 → ℝ ^3 given by X (u, v) = (u, v, sqrt 1 - u ^2 - v ^2 ). In these coordinates, the round metric can be written beginarrayc stackrel ∘ g = X * bar(g) = d u ^2 + d v ^2 + (frac u d u + v d vsqrt 1 - u ^2 - v ^2 ) ^2 = ((1 - v ^2) d u ^2 + (1 - u ^2) d v ^2 + 2 u v d u d v)/(1 - u ^2 - v ^2). endarray | — |
| `FC08-C13-U025` | **Example 13.18** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Submanifolds; source L9995 | (Induced Metrics on Surfaces of Revolution). Let C be an embedded 1-dimensional submanifold of the half-plane ( r , z ) : r > 0 , and let S _C be the surface of revolution generated by C as described in Example 5.17. To compute the induced metric on S _C , choose any smooth local parametrization γ ( t ) = ( a ( t ) , b ( t ) ) for C , and note that the map X ( t , θ ) = big ( a ( t ) cos θ , a ( t ) sin θ , b ( t ) big ) yields a smooth local parametrization of S _C , provided that ( t , θ ) is restricted to a sufficiently small open subset of the plane. Thus we can compute beginarrayr l X * bar(g) & = d (a (t) cos θ) ^2 + d (a (t) sin θ) ^2 + d (b (t)) ^2 & = (a ^prime (t) cos θ d t - a (t) sin θ d θ) ^2 & + (a ^prime (t) sin θ d t + a (t) cos θ d θ) ^2 + (b ^prime (t) d t) ^2 & = (a ^prime (t) ^2 + b ^prime (t) ^2) d t ^2 + a (t) ^2 d θ^2. endarray In particular, i f γ is a unit-speed curve, meaning that ∣ γ ^prime ( t ) ∣ ^2 = a ^prime ( t ) ^2 + b ^prime ( t ) ^2 = 1 this reduces to the simple formula d t ^2 + a ( t ) ^2 d θ ^2 Here are some familiar examples of surfaces of revolution. (a) The embedded torus described in Example 5.17 is the surface of revolution generated by the circle ( r - 2 ) ^2 + z ^2 = 1 . Using the unit-speed parametrization γ ( t ) = ( 2 + cos t , sin t ) for the circle, we obtain the formula d t ^2 + ( 2 + cos t ) ^2 d θ ^2 for the induced metric. (b) The unit sphere (minus the north and south poles) is a surface of revolution whose generating curve is the semicircle parametrized by γ ( t ) = ( sin t , cos t ) for 0 < t < π . The induced metric is d t ^2 + sin ^2 t d θ ^2 (c) The unit cylinder x ^2 + y ^2 = 1 is a surface of revolution whose generating curve is the vertical line parametrized by γ ( t ) = ( 1 , t ) for t ∈ ℝ . The induced metric is d t ^2 + d θ ^2 | `FC08-C05-U026` |
| `FC08-C13-U026` | **Proposition 13.19** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Submanifolds; source L10017 | (Flatness Criterion for Surfaces of Revolution). Let C ⊆ H be a connected embedded 1-dimensional submanifold of the half-plane H = ( r , z ) r > 0 , and let S _C be the surface of revolution generated by C . The induced metric on S _C is flat if and only i f C is part of a straight line. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U027` | **Corollary 13.20** | Ch. 13, § Riemannian Submanifolds; source L10063 | The round metric on mathbb S ^2 is not flat. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U028` | **Construction/definition — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 13, § The Normal Bundle; source L10067 | Suppose ( M , g ) is an n-dimensional Riemannian manifold with or without boundary, and S ⊆ M is a k-dimensional Riemannian submanifold (also with or without boundary). Just as we did for submanifolds of R n , for any p ∈ S we say that a vector v ∈ T _p M is normal to S if v is orthogonal to every vector in T _p S with respect to the inner product langle · , · rangle _g . The normal space to S at p is the subspace N _p S ⊆ T _p M consisting of all vectors that are normal to S at p , and the normal bundle of S is the subset N S ⊆ T M consisting of the union of all the normal spaces at points of S . The projection π _N S : N S → S is defined as the restriction to NS of π : T M → M The following proposition is proved in the same way as Corollary 10.36. | `FC08-C10-U051` |
| `FC08-C13-U029` | **Proposition 13.21** | Ch. 13, § The Normal Bundle; source L10069 | (The Normal Bundle to a Riemannian Submanifold). Let ( M , g ) be a Riemannian n-manifold with or without boundary. For any immersed k-dimensional submanifold S ⊆ M with or without boundary, the normal bundle NS is a smooth rank-.n k/ subbundle of T M vert _S . For each p ∈ S , there is a smooth frame for NS on a neighborhood of p that is orthonormal with respect to g . | — |
| `FC08-C13-U030` | **Definition/convention — lengths of curves** | Ch. 13, § The Riemannian Distance Function; source L10075 | One of the most important tools that a Riemannian metric gives us is the ability to define lengths of curves. Suppose ( M , g ) is a Riemannian manifold with or without boundary. If γ : [ a , b ] → M is a piecewise smooth curve segment, the length of is L _g (γ) = int_a ^b ∣ γ^prime (t) ∣ _g d t. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U031` | **Proposition 13.25** | Ch. 13, § The Riemannian Distance Function; source L10095 | (Parameter Independence of Length). Let ( M , g ) be a R i e - mannian manifold with or without boundary, and let γ : [ a , b ] → M be a piecewise smooth curve segment. H tilde(γ) is a reparametrization of γ _: , then L _g ( tilde(γ) ) = L _g ( γ ) | — |
| `FC08-C13-U032` | **Definition/convention — distances between points** | Ch. 13, § The Riemannian Distance Function; source L10109 | Using curve segments as “measuring tapes,” we can define distances between points on a Riemannian manifold. Suppose ( M , g ) is a connected Riemannian manifold. (The theory is most straightforward when ∂ M = emptyset , so we assume that for the rest of this section.) For any p , q ∈ M ; the (Riemannian) distance from p to ±b q , denoted by d _g ( p , q ) , is defined to be the infimum of L _g ( γ ) over all piecewise smooth curve segments γ from p to q . Because any pair of points in M can be joined by a piecewise smooth curve segment (Proposition 11.33), this is well defined. | `FC08-C11-U046` |
| `FC08-C13-U033` | **Example 13.26** | Ch. 13, § The Riemannian Distance Function; source L10111 | In ( ℝ ^n , bar(g) ) , Problem 13-10 shows that any straight line segment is the shortest piecewise smooth curve segment between its endpoints. Therefore, the distance function d _bar(g) is equal to the usual Euclidean distance: d _bar(g) (x, y) = ∣ x - y ∣. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U034` | **Lemma 13.28** | Ch. 13, § The Riemannian Distance Function; source L10121 | Let g be a Riemannian metric on an open subset U ⊆ ℝ ^n . Given a compact subset K ⊆ U , there exist positive constants c , C such that for all x ∈ K and all v ∈ T _x ℝ ^n c ∣ v ∣ _bar(g) ≤ ∣ v ∣ _g ≤ C ∣ v ∣ _bar(g).tag13.4 | — |
| `FC08-C13-U035` | **Theorem 13.29** | Ch. 13, § The Riemannian Distance Function; source L10141 | (Riemannian Manifolds as Metric Spaces). Let ( M , g ) be a connected Riemannian manifold. With the Riemannian distance function, M is a metric space whose metric topology is the same as the original manifold topology. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U036` | **Definition/convention — complete** | Ch. 13, § The Riemannian Distance Function; source L10181 | As a consequence of this theorem, all of the terminology of metric spaces can be carried over to connected Riemannian manifolds. Thus, a connected Riemannian manifold ( M , g ) is said to be complete, and g is said to be a complete Riemannian metric, if ( M , d _g ) is a complete metric space (i.e., if every Cauchy sequence in M converges to a point in M ) ; and a subset B ⊆ M bounded there exists a constant K such that d _g ( x , y ) ≤ K for all x , y ∈ B . Problems 13-17 and 13-18 outline two different proofs that every connected smooth manifold admits a complete Riemannian metric. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U037` | **Definition/convention — metrizable** | Ch. 13, § The Riemannian Distance Function; source L10183 | Recall that a topological space is said to be metrizable if it admits a distance function whose metric topology is the same as the given topology. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U038` | **Corollary 13.30** | Ch. 13, § The Riemannian Distance Function; source L10185 | Every smooth manifold with or without boundary is metrizable. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U039` | **Definition/convention — bundle homomorphism hat(g)** | Ch. 13, § The Tangent–Cotangent Isomorphism; source L10197 | Another convenient feature of every Riemannian metric is that it provides a natural isomorphism between the tangent and cotangent bundles. Given a Riemannian metric g on a smooth manifold M with or without boundary, we define a bundle homomorphism hat(g) : T M → T * M as follows. For each p ∈ M and each v ∈ T _p M we let widehat g ( v ) ∈ T _p * M be the covector defined by hat(g) (v) (w) = g _p (v, w) text for all w ∈ T _p M. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U040` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 13, § The Tangent–Cotangent Isomorphism; source L10233 | It is customary to denote the components of the covector field hat(g) ( X ) by hat(g) (X) = X _j d x ^j, text where X _j = g _i j X ^i. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U041` | **Convention/notation — tone** | Ch. 13, § The Tangent–Cotangent Isomorphism; source L10239 | Because of this, one says that hat(g) ( X ) is obtained from X by lowering an index. The notation X ^flat is frequently used for hat(g) ( X ) , because the symbol [ ( ^6 6 f l a t ^prime 3 ) is used in musical notation to indicate that a tone is to be lowered. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U042` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 13, § The Tangent–Cotangent Isomorphism; source L10241 | The matrix of the inverse map hat(g) ^- 1 : T _p * M → T _p M is thus the inverse of ( g _i j ) (Because ( g _i j ) is the matrix of the isomorphism widehat g , it is invertible at each point.) We ( g ^i j ) denote the matrix-valued function whose value at p ∈ M is the inverse of the matrix ( g _i j ( p ) ) , so that | — |
| `FC08-C13-U043` | **Convention/notation — musical isomorphisms** | Ch. 13, § The Tangent–Cotangent Isomorphism; source L10253 | We use the notation ω ^sharp ( ^ast ω · s h a r p ^ast ) for widehat g ^- 1 ( ω ) , and say that ω ^sharp is obtained from ! by raising an index. Because the symbols [ and ] are borrowed from musical notation, these two inverse isomorphisms are frequently called the musical isomorphisms. A handy mnemonic device for keeping the flat and sharp operations straight is to remember that the value of ω ^sharp at each point is a vector, which we visualize as a (sharp) arrow; while the value of X ^flat is a covector, which we visualize by means of its (flat) level sets. | — |
| `FC08-C13-U044` | **Definition/convention — gradient of f by grad f** | Ch. 13, § The Tangent–Cotangent Isomorphism; source L10255 | The most important use of the sharp operation is to reinstate the gradient as a vector field on Riemannian manifolds. For any smooth real-valued function f on a Riemannian manifold ( M , g ) with or without boundary, we define a vector field called the gradient of f by grad f = (d f) ^sharp = hat(g) ^- 1 (d f). | — |
| `FC08-C13-U045` | **Example 13.31** | Ch. 13, § The Tangent–Cotangent Isomorphism; source L10293 | Let us compute the gradient of a function f ∈ C ^∞ ( ℝ ^2 ) with respect to the Euclidean metric in polar coordinates. From Example 13.12 we see that the matrix of bar(g) in polar coordinates is binom 1 0 binom 0 r ^2 , so its inverse matrix is binom 1 0 _1 / r ^2 Inserting this into the formula for the gradient, we obtain grad f = (∂ f)/(∂ r) (∂)/(∂ r) + (1)/(r ^2) (∂ f)/(∂ θ) (∂)/(∂ θ).tag Problem 13-21 shows that the gradient of a function f on a Riemannian manifold has the same geometric interpretation as it has in Euclidean space: its direction is the direction in which f is increasing fastest, and is orthogonal to the level sets of f ; and its length is the maximum directional derivative of f in any direction. | `FC08-C13-U018` |
| `FC08-C13-U046` | **Definition/convention — signature of g** | Ch. 13, § Pseudo-Riemannian Metrics; source L10303 | An important generalization of Riemannian metrics is obtained by relaxing the positivity requirement. A symmetric 2-tensor g on a vector space V is said to be nondegenerate if the linear map hat(g) : V → V * defined by widehat g ( v ) ( w ) = g ( v , w ) is an isomorphism, or equivalently if for every nonzero v ∈ V there exists w ∈ V such that g ( v , w ) ≠ 0 . Just as any inner product can be transformed to the Euclidean one by switching to an orthonormal basis, every nondegenerate symmetric 2-tensor can be transformed by a change of basis to one whose matrix is diagonal with all entries equal to ± 1 (the proof is an adaptation of the Gram–Schmidt algorithm). The numbers r and s of positive and negative diagonal entries, respectively, are independent of the choice of basis ( a fact known as Sylvester’s law of inertia; see [FIS03] for a proof). Thus the ordered pair ( r , s ) , called the signature of g , is an invariant of g . | — |
| `FC08-C13-U047` | **Definition/convention — Lorentz metrics** | Ch. 13, § Pseudo-Riemannian Metrics; source L10305 | A pseudo-Riemannian metric on a smooth manifold M is a smooth symmetric 2-tensor field whose value is nondegenerate at each point, with the same signature everywhere on M . Pseudo-Riemannian metrics with signature ( n - 1 , 1 ) (or ( 1 , n - 1 ) , depending on the convention used) are called Lorentz metrics; they play a central role in physics, where they are used to model gravitation in Einstein’s general theory of relativity. | — |
| `FC08-C14-U001` | **Definition/convention — exterior forms** | Ch. 14, § The Algebra of Alternating Tensors; source L10456 | Let V be a finite-dimensional (real) vector space. Recall that a covariant k-tensor on V is said to be alternating if its value changes sign whenever two arguments are interchanged, or equivalently if any permutation of the arguments causes its value to be multiplied by the sign of the permutation (see Exercise 12.17). Alternating covariant k-tensors are also called exterior forms, multicovectors, or k-covectors. The vector space of all k-covectors on V is denoted by Lambda ^k ( V * ) . As we noted in Chapter 12, all 0-tensors and 1-tensors are alternating. | — |
| `FC08-C14-U002` | **Lemma 14.1** | Ch. 14, § The Algebra of Alternating Tensors; source L10460 | Let ˛ be a covariant k-tensor on a finite-dimensional vector space V . (c) ˛ gives the value zero whenever two of its arguments are equal: α (v _1, … , w, … , w, … , v _k) = 0. | — |

If β is a 2-tensor, then (Alt β) (v, w) = (1)/(2) (β (v, w) - β (w, v)). For a 3-tensor γ . , beginarrayc (text Alt γ) (v, w, x) = (1)/(6) big (γ (v, w, x) + γ (w, x, v) + γ (x, v, w) - γ (w, v, x) - γ (v, x, w) - γ (x, w, v) big). endarray The next proposition is the analogue for alternating tensors of Proposition 12.14. | `FC08-C12-U024` |
| `FC08-C14-U005` | **Proposition 14.3** | Ch. 14, § The Algebra of Alternating Tensors; source L10518 | (Properties of Alternation). Let ˛ be a covariant tensor on a finite-dimensional vector space. (a) Alt ˛ is alternating. (b) Alt α = α if and only if ˛ is alternating. | — |
| `FC08-C14-U006` | **Convention/notation — multi-index of length k** | Ch. 14, § Elementary Alternating Tensors; source L10528 | For computations with alternating tensors, the following notation is exceedingly useful. | `FC08-C14-U007` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 14, § Elementary Alternating Tensors; source L10542 | In other words, if v denotes the n × k matrix whose columns are the components of the vectors v _1 , … , v _k with respect to the basis ( E _i ) dual to ( ε ^i ) , then ε ^I ( v _1 , … , v _k ) is the determinant of the k × k submatrix consisting of rows i _1 , … , i _k of v. | `FC08-C14-U013` | **Definition/convention — similar product operation for alternating tensors** | Ch. 14, § The Wedge Product; source L10652 | In this section we define a similar product operation for alternating tensors. | `FC08-C14-U014` | **Definition/convention — their wedge product** | Ch. 14, § The Wedge Product; source L10654 | We continue with the assumption that V is a finite-dimensional real vector space. Alt (ω ⊗ η).tag14.3 | — |
| `FC08-C14-U015` | **Lemma 14.10** | Ch. 14, § The Wedge Product; source L10662 | Let V be an n-dimensional vector space and let ( ε ^1 , … , ε ^n ) be a basis for V * . | `FC08-C14-U016` | **Proposition 14.11** | Ch. 14, § The Wedge Product; source L10698 | (Properties of the Wedge Product). Suppose !, ω ^prime , η , η ^prime , and ξ are multicovectors on a finite-dimensional vector space V . (a) BILINEARITY: For a , a ^prime ∈ ℝ beginarrayl (a ω + a ^prime ω^prime) ∧ η = a (ω ∧ η) + a ^prime (ω^prime ∧ η), η ∧ (a ω + a ^prime ω^prime) = a (η ∧ ω) + a ^prime (η ∧ ω^prime). endarray (b) ASSOCIATIVITY: ω ∧ (η ∧ ξ) = (ω ∧ η) ∧ ξ . (c) ANTICOMMUTATIVITY: For ω ∈ Lambda ^k ( V * ) and η ∈ Lambda ^l ( V * ) , ω ∧ η = (- 1) ^k l η ∧ ω .tag14.6 (d) H ( ε ^i ) is any basis for V * and I = ( i _1 , … , i _k ) is any multi-index, then ε^ i _1 ∧ … ∧ ε^ i _k = ε^I.tag14.7 (e) For any covectors ω ^1 , … , ω ^k and vectors v _1 , … , v _k ω^1 ∧ … ∧ ω^k (v _1, … , v _k) = det (ω^j (v _i) ).tag14.8 | — |
| `FC08-C14-U017` | **Definition/convention — decomposable** | Ch. 14, § The Wedge Product; source L10748 | A k-covector η is said to be decomposable if it can be expressed in the form η = ω ^1 ∧ ·s ∧ ω ^k , where ω ^1 , … , ω ^k are covectors. It is important to be aware that not every k-covector is decomposable when k > 1 (see Problem 14-2); however, it follows from Propositions 14.8 and 14.11(d) that every k-covector can be written as a linear combination of decomposable ones. | — |
| `FC08-C14-U018` | **Construction/definition — exterior algebra (** | Ch. 14, § The Wedge Product; source L10760 | It follows from Proposition 14.8 that dim Lambda ( V * ) = 2 ^n . Proposition 14.11 shows that the wedge product turns Lambda ( V * ) into an associative algebra, called the exterior algebra (or Grassmann algebra) of V . This algebra is not commutative, but it has a closely related property. An algebra A is said to be graded if it has a direct sum decomposition overset · A = overset · oplus _k ∈ ℤ overset · A ^k such that the product satisfies ( A ^k ) ( A ^l ) ⊆ A ^k + l for each k and l. A graded algebra is anticommutative if the product satisfies a b = ( - 1 ) ^k l b a for a overset · ∈ A ^k , b ∈ A ^l . Proposition 14.11(c) shows that Lambda ( V * ) is an anticommutative graded algebra (where we interpret A ^k = Lambda ^k ( V * ) for 0 ≤ k ≤ n and A ^k = 0 otherwise). | `FC08-C14-U011`, `FC08-C14-U016` |
| `FC08-C14-U019` | **Convention/notation — wedge product without the unwieldy coefficient of (14** | Ch. 14, § The Wedge Product; source L10762 | As we observed at the beginning of this section, one could also define the wedge product without the unwieldy coefficient of (14.3). Some authors choose this alternative definition of the wedge product. To avoid confusion, we denote it by Z : ω ∧ η = Alt (ω ⊗ η).tag14.9 | — |
| `FC08-C14-U020` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 14, § The Wedge Product; source L10782 | Because of (14.8), we call definition (14.3) the determinant convention for the wedge product, and (14.9) the Alt convention. The choice of which definition to use is largely a matter of taste. Although the definition of the Alt convention is perhaps a bit more natural, the computational advantages of the determinant convention make it preferable for most applications, and we use it exclusively in this book. (But see Problem 14-3 for an argument in favor of the Alt convention.) The determinant convention is most common in introductory differential geometry texts, and is used, for example, in [Boo86, Cha06, dC92, LeeJeff09, Pet06, Spi99]. The Alt convention is used in [KN69] and is more common in complex differential geometry. | — |

| `FC08-C14-U028` | **Example 14.18** | Ch. 14, § Differential Forms on Manifolds; source L10920 | Define F : ℝ ^2 ℝ ^3 by F ( u , v ) = ( u , v , u ^2 - v ^2 ) , and let ! be the 2-form y d x ∧ d z + x d y ∧ d z o n ℝ ^3 . The pullback F * ω is computed as follows: beginarrayr l F * (y d x ∧ d z + x d y ∧ d z) & = v d u ∧ d (u ^2 - v ^2 ) + u d v ∧ d (u ^2 - v ^2 ) & = v d u ∧ (2 u d u - 2 v d v) + u d v ∧ (2 u d u - 2 v d v) & = - 2 v ^2 d u ∧ d v + 2 u ^2 d v ∧ d u, endarray where we have used the fact d u ∧ d u = d v ∧ d v = 0 by anticommutativity. Because dv ∧ d u = - d u ∧ d v , this simplifies to F * ω = - 2 (u ^2 + v ^2) d u ∧ d v.tag The same technique can also be used to compute the expression for a differentia form in another smooth chart. | — |
| `FC08-C14-U029` | **Example 14.19** | Ch. 14, § Differential Forms on Manifolds; source L10934 | Let ω = d x ∧ d y on ℝ ^2 . Thinking of the transformation to polar coordinates x = r cos θ , y = r sin θ as an expression for the identity map with respect to different coordinates on the domain and codomain, we obtain beginarrayr l d x ∧ d y & = d (r cos θ) ∧ d (r sin θ) & = (cos θ d r - r sin θ d θ) ∧ (sin θ d r + r cos θ d θ) & = r d r ∧ d θ . endarray The similarity between this formula and the formula for changing a double integral from Cartesian to polar coordinates is striking. | `FC08-C14-U033` | **Definition/convention — its exterior derivative d** | Ch. 14, § Exterior Derivatives; source L11004 | The definition of d on Euclidean space is straightforward: if beginarray r ω = sum _J ^prime ω _J d x ^J endarray is a smooth k-form on an open subset U ⊆ ℝ ^n or ℍ ^n , we define its exterior derivative d! to be the following ( k + 1 ) /-form: d (sum_J ^prime omega_J d x ^J ) = sum_J ^prime d omega_J ∧ d x ^J,tag14.19 | — |

| `FC08-C14-U035` | **Theorem 14.24** | Ch. 14, § Exterior Derivatives; source L11092 | (Existence and Uniqueness of Exterior Differentiation). Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary. There are unique operators d : Omega ^k ( M ) Omega ^k + 1 ( M ) for all k, called exterior differentiation, satisfying the following four properties: (i) d is linear over R. (ii) If ω ∈ Omega ^k ( M ) and η ∈ Omega ^l ( M ) , then d (ω ∧ η) = d ω ∧ η + (- 1) ^k ω ∧ d η . (iii) d ∘ d equiv 0 (iv) For f ∈ Omega ^0 ( M ) = C ^∞ ( M ) , df is the differential of f , given by d f ( X ) = X f In any smooth coordinate chart, d is given by (14.19). | — |

It is said to be an antiderivation if it satisfies T ( x y ) = ( T x ) y + ( - 1 ) ^k x ( T y ) whenever x ∈ A ^k and y ∈ A ^l . The preceding theorem can be summarized by saying that the differential on functions extends uniquely to an antiderivation of Omega * ( M ) of degree 1 whose square is zero. | — |
| `FC08-C14-U037` | **Proposition 14.26** | Ch. 14, § Exterior Derivatives; source L11132 | (Naturality of the Exterior Derivative). If F : M → N is a smooth map, then for each k the pullback map F * : Omega ^k ( N ) → Omega ^k ( M ) commutes with d : for all ω ∈ Omega ^k ( N ) , F * (d ω) = d (F * ω).tag14.23 | — |
