Because the exterior derivative of a 1-form satisfies (14.18), this definition of closed 1-forms agrees with the one we gave in Chapter 11. | — |
| `FC08-C14-U039` | **Example 14.27** | Ch. 14, § Exterior Derivatives and Vector Calculus in ℝ ^3; source L11154 | Let us work out the exterior derivatives of arbitrary 1-forms and 2-forms on ℝ ^3 . Any smooth 1-form can be written ω = P d x + Q d y + R d z for some smooth functions P; Q; R. Using (14.19) and the fact that the wedge product of any 1-form with itself is zero, we compute beginarrayl d ω = d P ∧ d x + d Q ∧ d y + d R ∧ d z = ((∂ P)/(∂ x) d x + (∂ P)/(∂ y) d y + (∂ P)/(∂ z) d z ) ∧ d x + ((∂ Q)/(∂ x) d x + (∂ Q)/(∂ y) d y + (∂ Q)/(∂ z) d z ) ∧ d y + ((∂ R)/(∂ x) d x + (∂ R)/(∂ y) d y + (∂ R)/(∂ z) d z ) ∧ d z = ((∂ Q)/(∂ x) - (∂ P)/(∂ y) ) d x ∧ d y + ((∂ R)/(∂ x) - (∂ P)/(∂ z) ) d x ∧ d z + ((∂ R)/(∂ y) - (∂ Q)/(∂ z) ) d y ∧ d z. endarray An arbitrary 2-form on ℝ ^3 can be written η = u d x ∧ d y + v d x ∧ d z + w d y ∧ d z. A similar computation shows that d η = ((∂ u)/(∂ z) - (∂ v)/(∂ y) + (∂ w)/(∂ x) ) d x ∧ d y ∧ d z. Recall the classical vector calculus operators on ℝ ^n : the (Euclidean) gradient of a function f ∈ C ^∞ ( ℝ ^n ) and the divergence of a vector field X ∈ X ( ℝ ^n ) are defined by grad f = sum_i = 1 ^n (∂ f)/(∂ x ^i) (∂)/(∂ x ^i), div X = sum_i = 1 ^n (∂ X ^i)/(∂ x ^i).tag14.24 In addition, in the case n = 3 , the curl of a vector field X ∈ X ( ℝ ^3 ) is defined by (11.26). It is interesting to note that the components of the 2-form d! in the preceding example are exactly the components of the curl of the vector field with components ( P , Q , R ) (except perhaps in a different order and with different signs). Similarly, but for signs and ordering of terms, there is a strong analogy between the formula for d η and the divergence of a vector field. These analogies can be made precise in the following way. The Euclidean metric on ℝ ^3 yields an index-lowering isomorphism [ X ( ℝ ^3 ) Omega ^1 ( ℝ ^3 ) . Interior multiplication yields another map β : X ( ℝ ^3 ) Omega ^2 ( ℝ ^3 ) as follows: β (X) = X lrcorner (d x ∧ d y ∧ d z).tag14.25 It is easy to check that β is linear over C ^∞ ( ℝ ^3 ) , so it corresponds to a smooth bundle homomorphism from T M to Lambda ^2 T * ℝ ^3 . It is a bundle isomorphism because it is injective and both T M and Lambda ^2 T * ℝ ^3 are bundles of rank 3. Similarly, we define a smooth bundle isomorphism C ^∞ ( ℝ ^3 ) Omega ^3 ( ℝ ^3 ) by * (f) = f d x ∧ d y ∧ d z.tag14.26 The relationships among all of these operators are summarized in the following diagram: beginarrayc C ^∞ (ℝ ^3 ) xrightarrow text grad X (ℝ ^3 ) xrightarrow text curl X (ℝ ^3 ) xrightarrow text div C ^∞ (ℝ ^3 ) Biggl downarrow text Id Biggl downarrow_flat Biggl downarrow_β Biggl downarrow_* Omega^0 (ℝ ^3 ) xrightarrow [ d ] Omega^1 (ℝ ^3 ) xrightarrow [ d ] Omega^2 (ℝ ^3 ) xrightarrow [ d ] Omega^3 (ℝ ^3 ). endarraytag14.27 | — |
| `FC08-C14-U040` | **Proposition 14.29** | Ch. 14, § An Invariant Formula for the Exterior Derivative; source L11212 | (Exterior Derivative of a 1-Form). For any smooth 1-form ! and smooth vector fields X and Y , d ω (X, Y) = X (ω (Y)) - Y (ω (X)) - ω ([ X, Y ]).tag14.28 | — |
| `FC08-C14-U041` | **Proposition 14.30** | Ch. 14, § An Invariant Formula for the Exterior Derivative; source L11234 | Let M be a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary, let ( E _i ) be a smooth local frame for M; and let ( ε ^i ) be the dual coframe. For each i, let b _j k ^i denote the component functions of the exterior derivative of ε ^i in this frame, and for each j; k, let c _j k ^i be the component functions of the Lie bracket [ E _j , E _k ] d ε^i = sum_j < k b _j k ^i ε^j ∧ ε^k; [ E _j, E _k ] = c _j k ^i E _i. Then b _j k ^i = - c _j k ^i | — |
| `FC08-C14-U042` | **Proposition 14.32** | Ch. 14, § An Invariant Formula for the Exterior Derivative; source L11246 | (Invariant Formula for the Exterior Derivative). Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and ω ∈ Omega ^k ( M ) . For any smooth vector fields X _1 , … , X _k + 1 on M; beginarrayl d ω (X _1, … , X _k + 1) = sum_1 ≤ i ≤ k + 1 (- 1) ^i - 1 X _i big (ω (X _1, … , widehat X _i, … , X _k + 1) big) + sum_1 ≤ i < j ≤ k + 1 (- 1) ^i + j ω big ([ X _i, X _j ], X _1, … , widehat X _i, … , widehat X _j, … , X _k + 1 big), endarraytag14.29 where the hats indicate omitted arguments. | — |
| `FC08-C14-U043` | **Proposition 14.33** | Ch. 14, § Lie Derivatives of Differential Forms; source L11320 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold, V ∈ X ( M ) , and ω , η ∈ Omega * ( M ) . Then L _V (ω ∧ η) = (L _V ω) ∧ η + ω ∧ (L _V η). | — |
| `FC08-C14-U044` | **Theorem 14.35** | Ch. 14, § Lie Derivatives of Differential Forms; source L11330 | (Cartan’s Magic Formula). On a smooth manifold M; for any smooth vector field V and any smooth differential form !, L _V ω = V lrcorner (d ω) + d (V lrcorner ω).tag14.32 | — |
| `FC08-C14-U045` | **Corollary 14.36** | Ch. 14, § Lie Derivatives of Differential Forms; source L11364 | (The Lie Derivative Commutes with d). I f V is a smooth vector field and ! is a smooth differential form, then L _V (d ω) = d (L _V ω). | — |
| `FC08-C15-U001` | **Definition/convention — orientable** | Ch. 15; source L11474 | As we will see in this chapter, a vector space always has exactly two choices of orientation. In R n , there is a standard orientation that we can all agree on; but in other vector spaces, an arbitrary choice has to be made. For manifolds, the situation is much more complicated. On a sphere, it is possible to decide unambiguously which rotational direction is counterclockwise, by looking at the surface from the outside (Fig. 15.1). On the other hand, a Möbius band (Fig. 15.2) has the curious property that a figure moving around on the surface can come back to its starting point transformed into its mirror image, so it is impossible to decide consistently which of the two possible rotational directions on the surface to call “clockwise” and which “counterclockwise,” or which is the “front” side and which is the “back” side. The analogous phenomenon in a 3-manifold would be a right-handed person who takes a long trip and comes back left-handed. Manifolds like the sphere, in which it is possible to choose a consistent orientation, are said to be orientable; those like the Möbius band in which it is not possible are said to be nonorientable. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U002` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Vector Spaces; source L11492 | Thus we are led to introduce the following definition. Let V be a real vector space of dimension n ≥ 1 . We say that two ordered bases ( E _1 , … , E _n ) and ( tilde(E) _1 , … , tilde(E) _n ) for V are consistently oriented if the transition matrix ( B _i ^j ) , defined by E _i = B _i ^j tilde(E) _j,tag15.1 | — |
| `FC08-C15-U003` | **Convention/notation — oriented vector space** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Vector Spaces; source L11502 | If dim V = n ≥ 1 , we define an orientation for V as an equivalence class of ordered bases. If ( E _1 , … , E _n ) is any ordered basis for V , we denote the orientation that it determines by [ E _1 , … , E _n ] , and the opposite orientation by - [ E _1 , … , E _n ] A vector space together with a choice of orientation is called an oriented vector space. If V is oriented, then any ordered basis ( E _1 , … , E _n ) that is in the given orientation is said to be oriented or positively oriented. Any basis that is not in the given orientation is said to be negatively oriented. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U004` | **Definition/convention — orientation of V to be simply a choice of one of the numbers** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Vector Spaces; source L11504 | For the special case of a zero-dimensional vector space V , we define an orientation of V to be simply a choice of one of the numbers ± 1 | — |
| `FC08-C15-U005` | **Example 15.2** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Vector Spaces; source L11506 | The orientation [ e _1 , … , e _n ] of ℝ ^n determined by the standard basis is called the standard orientation. You should convince yourself that, in our usual way of representing the axes graphically, an oriented basis for R is one that points to the right; an oriented basis for bar ℝ ^2 is one for which the rotation from the first basis vector to the second is counterclockwise; and an oriented basis for ℝ ^3 is a righthanded one. (These can be taken as mathematical definitions for the words “right,” “counterclockwise,” and “right-handed.”) The standard orientation for ℝ ^0 is defined to be + 1 // | — |
| `FC08-C15-U006` | **Proposition 15.3** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Vector Spaces; source L11510 | Let V be a vector space of dimension n. Each nonzero element ω ∈ Lambda ^n ( V * ) determines an orientation O _ω of V as follows: i f n ≥ 1 , then O _ω is the set of ordered bases ( E _1 , … , E _n ) such that ! ( E _1 , … , E _n ) > 0 ; while if dot n = 0 , then O _ω i s + 1 i f ω > 0 . , and 1 if ! < 0. Two nonzero n-covectors determine the same orientation if and only if each is a positive multiple of the other. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U007` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Vector Spaces; source L11522 | If V is an oriented n-dimensional vector space and ω is an n-covector that determines the orientation of V as described in this proposition, we say that ω is a (positively) oriented n-covector. For example, the n-covector e ^1 ∧ ·s ∧ e ^n is positively oriented for the standard orientation on ℝ ^n | — |
| `FC08-C15-U008` | **Definition/convention — pointwise orientation** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11531 | Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary. We define a pointwise orientation on M to be a choice of orientation of each tangent space. By itself, this is not a very useful concept, because the orientations of nearby points may have no relation to each other. For example, a pointwise orientation on ℝ ^n might switch randomly from point to point between the standard orientation and its opposite. In order for orientations to have some relationship with the smooth structure, we need an extra condition to ensure that the orientations of nearby tangent spaces are consistent with each other. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U009` | **Definition/convention — negatively oriented frame** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11533 | Let M be a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary, endowed with a pointwise orientation. If ( E _i ) is a local frame for TM; we say that ( E _i ) is (positively) oriented if ( E _1 ∣ _p , … , E _n ∣ _p ) is a positively oriented basis for T _p M at each point p ∈ U . A negatively oriented frame is defined analogously. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U010` | **Definition/convention — continuous** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11535 | A pointwise orientation is said to be continuous if every point of M is in the domain of an oriented local frame. (Recall that by definition the vector fields that make up a local frame are continuous.) An orientation of M is a continuous pointwise orientation. We say that M is orientable if there exists an orientation for it, and nonorientable if not. An oriented manifold is an ordered pair ( M , O ) , where M is an orientable smooth manifold and O is a choice of orientation for M ; an oriented manifold with boundary is defined similarly. For each p ∈ M ; the orientation of T _p M determined by O is denoted by O _p . When it is not important to name the orientation explicitly, we use the usual shorthand expression “M is an oriented smooth manifold” (or “manifold with boundary”). | — |
| `FC08-C15-U011` | **Definition/convention — orientation of M** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11537 | If M is zero-dimensional, this definition just means that an orientation of M is a choice of ± 1 attached to each of its points. The continuity condition is vacuous in this case, and the notion of oriented frames is not useful. Clearly, every 0-manifold is orientable. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U012` | **Proposition 15.5** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11543 | (The Orientation Determined by an n-Form). Let M be a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary. Any nonvanishing n-form ! on M determines a unique orientation of M for which ! is positively oriented at each point. Conversely, if M is given an orientation, then there is a smooth nonvanishing n-form on M that is positively oriented at each point. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U013` | **Definition/convention — orientation form** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11545 | Remark. Because of this proposition, if M is a smooth n-manifold with or without boundary, any nonvanishing n-form on M is called an orientation form. If M is oriented and ω is an orientation form determining the given orientation, we also say that ω is (positively) oriented. It is easy to check that if ω and tilde(ω) are two positively oriented smooth forms on M , ; then tilde(ω) = f ω for some strictly positive smooth realvalued function f . If M is a 0-manifold, a nonvanishing 0-form (i.e., real-valued function) assigns the orientation 1 to points where it is positive and 1 to points where it is negative. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U014` | **Definition/convention — consistently oriented** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11557 | A smooth coordinate chart on an oriented smooth manifold with or without boundary is said to be (positively) oriented if the coordinate frame ( ∂ / ∂ x ^i ) is positively oriented, and negatively oriented if the coordinate frame is negatively oriented. A smooth atlas ( U _α , φ _α ) is said to be consistently oriented if for each ˛, β , the transition map φ _β ∘ φ _α ^- 1 has positive Jacobian determinant everywhere on φ _α ( U _α ∩ U _β ) | — |
| `FC08-C15-U015` | **Proposition 15.6** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11559 | (The Orientation Determined by a Coordinate Atlas). Let M be a smooth positive-dimensional manifold with or without boundary. Given any consistently oriented smooth atlas for M; there is a unique orientation for M with the property that each chart in the given atlas is positively oriented. Conversely, if M is oriented and either ∂ M = emptyset or dim M > 1 , then the collection of all oriented smooth charts is a consistently oriented atlas for M . | — |
| `FC08-C15-U016` | **Proposition 15.7** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11565 | (Product Orientations). Suppose M _1 , … , M _k are orientable smooth manifolds. There is a unique orientation on M _1 × ·s × M _k , called the product orientation, with the following property: if for each i = 1 , … , k , ω _i is an orientation form for the given orientation on M _i , then π _1 * ω _1 ∧ ·s ∧ π _k * ω _k is an orientation form for the product orientation. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U017` | **Proposition 15.9** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11569 | Let M be a connected, orientable, smooth manifold with or without boundary. Then M has exactly two orientations. If two orientations of M agree at one point, they are equal. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U018` | **Proposition 15.11** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11573 | (Orientations of Codimension-0 Submanifolds). Suppose M is an oriented smooth manifold with or without boundary, and D ⊆ M is a smooth codimension-0 submanifold with or without boundary. Then the orientation of M restricts to an orientation of D. If ! is an orientation form for M; then iota _D * ω is an orientation form for D. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U019` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11577 | Let M and N be oriented smooth manifolds with or without boundary, and suppose F : M → N is a local diffeomorphism. If M and N are positive-dimensional, we say that F is orientation-preserving if for each p ∈ M , ; the isomorphism d F _p takes oriented bases of T _p M to oriented bases of T _F ( p ) N , and orientationreversing if it takes oriented bases of T _p M to negatively oriented bases of T _F ( p ) N If M and N are 0-manifolds, then F is orientation-preserving if for every p ∈ M the points p and F ( p ) have the same orientation; and it is orientation-reversing if they have the opposite orientation. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U020` | **Proposition 15.15** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11591 | (The Pullback Orientation). Suppose M and N are smooth manifolds with or without boundary. If F : M → N is a local diffeomorphism and N is oriented, then M has a unique orientation, called the pullback orientation induced by F , such that F is orientation-preserving. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U021` | **Construction/definition — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11595 | In the situation of the preceding proposition, if O denotes the given orientation on N , the pullback orientation on M is denoted by F * O | — |
| `FC08-C15-U022` | **Proposition 15.17** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11601 | Every parallelizable smooth manifold is orientable. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U023` | **Example 15.18** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11608 | The preceding proposition implies that Euclidean space ℝ ^n , the n-torus mathbb T ^n , the spheres S ^1 , S ^bar(3) , and mathbb S ^7 , and products of them are all orientable, because they are all parallelizable. Therefore, any codimension-0 submanifold of one of these manifolds is also orientable. Likewise, every Lie group is orientable because it is parallelizable. // | — |
| `FC08-C15-U024` | **Definition/convention — left-invariant orientation** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11610 | In the case of Lie groups, we can say more. If G is a Lie group, an orientation of G is said to be left-invariant if L _g is orientation-preserving for every g ∈ G | — |
| `FC08-C15-U025` | **Proposition 15.19** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Manifolds; source L11612 | Every Lie group has precisely two left-invariant orientations, corresponding to the two orientations of its Lie algebra. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U026` | **Definition/convention — oriented smooth manifold** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Hypersurfaces; source L11618 | If M is an oriented smooth manifold and S is a smooth submanifold of M (with or without boundary), S might not inherit an orientation from M; even if S is embedded. A useful example to consider is the Möbius band, which is not orientable (see Example 15.38 below), even though it can be embedded in ℝ ^3 | — |
| `FC08-C15-U027` | **Proposition 15.21** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Hypersurfaces; source L11624 | Suppose M is an oriented smooth n-manifold with or without boundary, S is an immersed hypersurface with or without boundary in M; and N is a vector field along S that is nowhere tangent to S. Then S has a unique orientation such that for each p ∈ S , ( E _1 , … , E _n - 1 ) is an oriented basis for T _p S if and only if ( N _p , E _1 , … , E _n - 1 ) is an oriented basis for T _p M . If ! is an orientation form for M; then iota _S * ( N lrcorner ω ) is an orientation form for S with respect to this orientation, where iota _S : S ↪ M is inclusion. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U028` | **Example 15.22** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Hypersurfaces; source L11639 | The sphere mathbb S ^n is a hypersurface in ℝ ^n + 1 , to which the vector field N = x ^i ∂ / ∂ x ^i is nowhere tangent, so this vector field induces an orientation on mathbb S ^n This shows that all spheres are orientable. We define the standard orientation of mathbb S ^n to be the orientation determined by N . Unless otherwise specified, we always use this orientation. (The standard orientation on mathbb S ^0 is the one that assigns the orientation 1 to the point + 1 ∈ mathbb S ^0 and - 1 ~ to ~ - 1 ∈ mathbb S ^0 . ) // | — |
| `FC08-C15-U029` | **Proposition 15.23** | Ch. 15, § Orientations of Hypersurfaces; source L11645 | Let M be an oriented smooth manifold, and suppose S ⊆ M is a regular level set of a smooth function f M → ℝ . Then S is orientable | — |
| `FC08-C15-U030` | **Definition/convention — outward-pointing vector field** | Ch. 15, § Boundary Orientations; source L11651 | If M is a smooth manifold with boundary, then @M is an embedded hypersurface in M (see Theorem 5.11), and Problem 8-4 showed that there is always a smooth outward-pointing vector field along @M . | `FC08-C15-U031` | **Proposition 15.24** | Ch. 15, § Boundary Orientations; source L11653 | (The Induced Orientation on a Boundary). Let M be an oriented smooth n-manifold with boundary, n ≥ 1 . Then @M is orientable, and all outward-pointing vector fields along @M determine the same orientation on @M . | — |
| `FC08-C15-U032` | **Definition/convention — induced orientation** | Ch. 15, § Boundary Orientations; source L11655 | Remark. The orientation on @M determined by any outward-pointing vector field is called the induced orientation or the Stokes orientation on @M . | `FC08-C15-U033` | **Example 15.25** | Ch. 15, § Boundary Orientations; source L11661 | This proposition gives a simpler proof that mathbb S ^n is orientable, because it is the boundary of the closed unit ball. | `FC08-C15-U034` | **Example 15.26** | Ch. 15, § Boundary Orientations; source L11663 | Let us determine the induced orientation on ∂ ℍ ^n when ℍ ^n itself has the standard orientation inherited from ℝ ^n . We can identify ∂ ℍ ^n with ℝ ^n - 1 under the correspondence ( x ^1 , … , x ^n - 1 , 0 ) ( x ^1 , … , x ^n - 1 ) . This orientation satisfies beginarrayc [ - ∂ / ∂ x ^n, ∂ / ∂ x ^1, … , ∂ / ∂ x ^n - 1 ] = - [ ∂ / ∂ x ^n, ∂ / ∂ x ^1, … , ∂ / ∂ x ^n - 1 ] = (- 1) ^n [ ∂ / ∂ x ^1, … , ∂ / ∂ x ^n - 1, ∂ / ∂ x ^n ]. endarray Thus, the induced orientation on ∂ ℍ ^n is equal to the standard orientation on ℝ ^n - 1 when n is even, but it is opposite to the standard orientation when n is odd. In particular, the standard coordinates on ∂ ℍ ^n approx ℝ ^n - 1 are positively oriented if and only if n is even. (This fact will be important in the proof of Stokes’s theorem in Chapter 16.) | — |
| `FC08-C15-U035` | **Lemma 15.27** | Ch. 15, § Boundary Orientations; source L11676 | Let M be an oriented smooth n-manifold with boundary. Suppose U ⊆ ℝ ^n - 1 is open, a; b are real numbers with a < b , and F ( a , b ] × U → M is a smooth embedding that restricts to an embedding of b × U into @M . Then the parametrization f : U → ∂ M given by f ( x ) = F ( b , x ) is orientation-preserving for @M if and only if F is orientation-preserving for M . | — |
| `FC08-C15-U036` | **Example 15.28** | Ch. 15, § Boundary Orientations; source L11705 | Spherical coordinates (Example C.38) yield a smooth local parametrization of mathbb S ^2 as follows. Because F ( 1 , φ , θ ) = X ( φ , θ ) , the hypotheses of Lemma 15.27 are satisfied. By direct computation, the Jacobian determinant of F is ρ ^2 sin φ _: , which is positive on ( 0 , 1 ] × U . By virtue of Lemma 15.27, X is orientation-preserving. | `FC08-C15-U035` |
| `FC08-C15-U037` | **Proposition 15.29** | Ch. 15, § The Riemannian Volume Form; source L11723 | Suppose ( M , g ) is an oriented Riemannian n-manifold with or without boundary, and n ≥ 1 . There is a unique smooth orientation form ω _g ∈ Omega ^n ( M ) , called the Riemannian volume form, that satisfies omega_g (E _1, … , E _n) = 1tag15.2 for every local oriented orthonormal frame ( E _i ) for M . | — |
| `FC08-C15-U038` | **Proposition 15.31** | Ch. 15, § The Riemannian Volume Form; source L11763 | Let ( M , g ) be an oriented Riemannian n-manifold with or without boundary, n ≥ 1 . | `FC08-C15-U039` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 15, § Hypersurfaces in Riemannian Manifolds; source L11799 | Let ( M , g ) be an oriented Riemannian manifold with or without boundary, and suppose S ⊆ M is an immersed hypersurface with or without boundary. | `FC08-C15-U040` | **Proposition 15.32** | Ch. 15, § Hypersurfaces in Riemannian Manifolds; source L11801 | Let ( M , g ) be an oriented Riemannian manifold with or without boundary, let S ⊆ M be an immersed hypersurface with or without boundary, and let widetilde g denote the induced metric on S. | `FC08-C15-U042` | **Corollary 15.34** | Ch. 15, § Hypersurfaces in Riemannian Manifolds; source L11828 | H ( M , g ) is an oriented Riemannian manifold with boundary and widetilde g is the induced Riemannian metric on @M; then the volume form of widetilde g is omega_tilde(g) = iota_∂ M * (N lrcorner omega_g), where N is the outward unit normal vector field along @M . | — |
| `FC08-C15-U043` | **Proposition 15.35** | Ch. 15, § Orientations and Covering Maps; source L11842 | If π E → M is a smooth covering map and M is orientable, then E is also orientable. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U044` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 15, § Orientations and Covering Maps; source L11846 | The next theorem is more interesting. If G is a Lie group acting smoothly on a smooth manifold E (on the left, say), we say the action is an orientation-preserving action if for each g ∈ G , the diffeomorphism x ↦ g · x is orientation-preserving. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U045` | **Theorem 15.36** | Ch. 15, § Orientations and Covering Maps; source L11848 | Suppose E is a connected, oriented, smooth manifold with o r without boundary, and π : E → M is a smooth normal covering map. Then M is orientable if and only if the action of A u t _π ( E ) on E is orientation-preserving. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U046` | **Example 15.37** | Ch. 15, § Orientations and Covering Maps; source L11862 | (Orientability of Projective Spaces). For n ≥ 1 , consider the smooth covering map q : mathbb S ^n → mathbb R P ^n of Example 4.35. The only nontrivial covering automorphism of q is the antipodal map α ( x ) = - x . Problem 15-3 shows that ˛ is orientation-preserving if and only if n is odd, so it follows that R mathbb P ^n is orientable if and only if n is odd. // | `FC08-C04-U036` |
| `FC08-C15-U047` | **Example 15.38** | Ch. 15, § Orientations and Covering Maps; source L11864 | (The Möbius Bundle and the Möbius Band). Let E be the total space of the Möbius bundle (Example 10.3). The quotient map q : ℝ ^2 → E used to define E is a smooth normal covering map, and the covering automorphism group is isomorphic to ℤ , , acting on ℝ ^2 by n · ( x , y ) = ( x + n , ( - 1 ) ^n y ) . (You can check this directly from the definitions, or you can accept this for now and wait until we have developed more machinery in Chapter 21, where a simpler proof is available; see Problem 21-9.) For n odd, the diffeomorphism ( x , y ) ↦ n · ( x , y ) of ℝ ^2 pulls back the orientation form dx ∧ d y to - d x ∧ d y , so the action of A u t _π ( E ) is not orientation-preserving. Thus, Theorem 15.36 shows that E is not orientable. | `FC08-C10-U007`, `FC08-C15-U045` |
| `FC08-C15-U048` | **Definition/convention — neighborhood that** | Ch. 15, § The Orientation Covering; source L11872 | In order to handle the orientable and nonorientable cases in a uniform way, it is useful to expand our definition of covering maps slightly, by allowing “covering spaces” that are not connected. If N and M are topological spaces, let us say that a map π : N → M is a generalized covering map if it satisfies all of the requirements for a covering map except that N might not be connected: this means that N is locally path-connected, π is surjective and continuous, and each point p ∈ M has a neighborhood that is evenly covered by π. If in addition N and M are smooth manifolds with or without boundary and π is a local diffeomorphism, we say it is a generalized smooth covering map. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U049` | **Lemma 15.39** | Ch. 15, § The Orientation Covering; source L11874 | Suppose N and M are topological spaces and π N M is a generalized covering map. If M is connected, then the restriction of π to each component of N is a covering map. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U050` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 15, § The Orientation Covering; source L11880 | Let M be a connected, smooth, positive-dimensional manifold with or without boundary, and let hat(M) denote the set of orientations of all tangent spaces to M : hat(M) = (p, O _p): p ∈ M text and O _p text is an orientation of T _p M . | — |
| `FC08-C15-U051` | **Construction/definition — orientation covering of M** | Ch. 15, § The Orientation Covering; source L11886 | Define the projection hat(π) : hat(M) → M by sending an orientation of T _p M to the point p itself: hat(π) ( p , O _p ) = p . Since each tangent space has exactly two orientations, each fiber of this map has cardinality 2. The map hat(π) : hat(M) → M is called the orientation covering of M . | — |
| `FC08-C15-U052` | **Proposition 15.40** | Ch. 15, § The Orientation Covering; source L11888 | (Properties of the Orientation Covering). Suppose M is a connected, smooth, positive-dimensional manifold with or without boundary, and let hat(π) : hat(M) → M be its orientation covering. Then hat(M) can be given the structure of a smooth, oriented manifold with or without boundary, with the following properties: (a) hat(π) : hat(M) → M is a generalized smooth covering map. (b) A connected open subset U ⊆ M is evenly covered by hat(π) if and only if U is orientable. (c) If U ⊆ M is an evenly covered open subset, then every orientation of U is the pullback orientation induced by a local section of hat(π) over U | — |
| `FC08-C15-U053` | **Theorem 15.41** | Ch. 15, § The Orientation Covering; source L11912 | (Orientation Covering Theorem). Suppose M is a connected smooth manifold with or without boundary, and let hat(π) : hat(M) → M be its orientation covering. (a) If M is orientable, then hat(M) has exactly two components, and the restriction of hat(π) to each component is a diffeomorphism onto M . (b) If M is nonorientable, then hat(M) is connected, and hat(π) is a two-sheeted smooth covering map. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U054` | **Definition/convention — oriented double covering of M** | Ch. 15, § The Orientation Covering; source L11925 | The orientation covering is sometimes called the oriented double covering of M . There are other ways of constructing it besides the one we have given here, but as the next theorem shows, the specific details of the construction do not matter, because they all yield isomorphic covering manifolds. | — |
| `FC08-C15-U055` | **Theorem 15.42** | Ch. 15, § The Orientation Covering; source L11927 | (Uniqueness of the Orientation Covering). Let M be a nonorientable connected smooth manifold with or without boundary, and let hat(π) : hat(M) → M be its orientation covering. If tilde(M) is an oriented smooth manifold with or without boundary that admits a two-sheeted smooth covering map widetilde π : tilde(M) → M ; then there exists a unique orientation-preserving diffeomorphism ' widetilde M → widehat M such that hat(π) ∘ φ = tilde(π) | — |
| `FC08-C15-U056` | **Theorem 15.43** | Ch. 15, § The Orientation Covering; source L11933 | Let M be a connected smooth manifold with or without boundary, and suppose the fundamental group of M has no subgroup of index 2. Then M is orientable. In particular, if M is simply connected then it is orientable. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U001` | **Definition/convention — integral of** | Ch. 16, § Integration of Differential Forms; source L12064 | Recall that a domain of integration in ℝ ^n is a bounded subset whose boundary has measure zero. Let D ⊆ ℝ ^n be a domain of integration, and let ω be a (continuous) n-form on bar(D) . Any such form can be written as ω = f d x ^1 ∧ ·s ∧ d x ^n for some continuous function f : bar(D) ℝ . We define the integral of ! over D to be int_D ω = int_D f d V. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U002` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 16, § Integration of Differential Forms; source L12078 | Somewhat more generally, let U be an open subset of ℝ ^n or ℍ ^n , and suppose ! is a compactly supported n-form on U . We define | — |
| `FC08-C16-U003` | **Proposition 16.1** | Ch. 16, § Integration of Differential Forms; source L12088 | Suppose D and E are open domains of integration in ℝ ^n or ℍ ^n and G : bar(D) bar(E) is a smooth map that restricts to an orientation-preserving o r orientation-reversing diffeomorphism from D to E. If ! is an n-form on bar(E) , then int_D G * ω = beginarrayl l int_E ω & text if G is orientation - preserving, - int_E ω & text if G is orientation - reversing. endarray . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U004` | **Lemma 16.2** | Ch. 16, § Integration of Differential Forms; source L12104 | Suppose U is an open subset of ℝ ^n ~ o r ~ ℍ ^n , and K is a compact subset of U . Then there is an open domain of integration D such that K ⊆ D ⊆ bar(D) ⊆ U | — |
| `FC08-C16-U005` | **Proposition 16.3** | Ch. 16, § Integration of Differential Forms; source L12111 | Suppose U , V are open subsets of ℝ ^n or H n , and G : U → V is an orientation-preserving or orientation-reversing diffeomorphism. If ! is a compactly supported n-form on V , then int_V ω = ± int_U G * ω , with the positive sign if G is orientation-preserving, and the negative sign otherwise. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U006` | **Definition/convention — integral of** | Ch. 16, § Integration on Manifolds; source L12123 | Using the results of the previous section, we can now make sense of the integral of a differential form over an oriented manifold. Let M be an oriented smooth nmanifold with or without boundary, and let ! be an n-form on M . Suppose first that ! is compactly supported in the domain of a single smooth chart ( U , φ ) that is either positively or negatively oriented. We define the integral of ! over M to be int_M ω = ± int_φ (U) (φ^- 1 ) * ω ,tag16.1 with the positive sign for a positively oriented chart, and the negative sign otherwise. (See Fig. 16.6.) Since ( φ ^- 1 ) * ω is a compactly supported n-form on the open subset φ ( U ) ⊆ ℝ ^n or H n , its integral is defined as discussed above. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U007` | **Proposition 16.4** | Ch. 16, § Integration on Manifolds; source L12131 | With ! as above, textstyle int _M ! does not depend on the choice of smooth chart whose domain contains supp !. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U008` | **Proposition 16.5** | Ch. 16, § Integration on Manifolds; source L12152 | The definition of textstyle int _M ! given above does not depend on the choice of open cover or partition of unity. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U009` | **Definition/convention — oriented 0-manifold M** | Ch. 16, § Integration on Manifolds; source L12179 | As usual, we have a special definition in the zero-dimensional case. The integral of a compactly supported 0-form (i.e., a real-valued function) f over an oriented 0-manifold M is defined to be the sum | — |
| `FC08-C16-U010` | **Proposition 16.6** | Ch. 16, § Integration on Manifolds; source L12194 | (Properties of Integrals of Forms). Suppose M and N are nonempty oriented smooth n-manifolds with or without boundary, and !; η are compactly supported n-forms on M . (a) LINEARITY: If a; b ∈ ℝ , then int_M a ω + b η = a int_M ω + b int_M η . (b) ORIENTATION REVERSAL: I f - M denotes M with the opposite orientation, then int_- M ω = - int_M ω . (c) POSITIVITY: If ! is a positively oriented orientation form, then textstyle int _M ω > 0 (d) DIFFEOMORPHISM INVARIANCE: If F : N → M is an orientation-preserving or orientation-reversing diffeomorphism, then int_M ω = beginarrayl l int_N F * ω & text if F is orientation - preserving, - int_N F * ω & text if F is orientation - reversing. endarray . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U011` | **Proposition 16.8** | Ch. 16, § Integration on Manifolds; source L12226 | (Integration Over Parametrizations). Let M be an oriented smooth n-manifold with or without boundary, and let ! be a compactly supported n - form on M . Suppose D _1 , … , D _k are open domains of integration in ℝ ^n , and for i = 1 , … , k , we are given smooth maps F _i : bar(D) _i → M satisfying (i) F _i restricts to an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism from D _i onto an open subset W _i ⊆ M ; (ii) W _i ∩ W _j = emptyset when i ≠ j (iii) supp ω ⊆ bar(W) _1 ∪ ·s ∪ bar(W) _k Then int_M ω = sum_i = 1 ^k int_ D _i F _i * ω .tag16.3 | — |
| `FC08-C16-U012` | **Example 16.9** | Ch. 16, § Integration on Manifolds; source L12267 | Let us use this technique to compute the integral of a 2-form over mathbb S ^2 , oriented as the boundary of overline mathbb B ^3 . Let ω be the following 2-form on ℝ ^3 : ω = x d y ∧ d z + y d z ∧ d x + z d x ∧ d y. Let D be the open rectangle ( 0 , π ) × ( 0 , 2 π ) , and let F : bar(D) mathbb S ^2 be the spherical coordinate parametrization F ( φ , θ ) = ( sin φ cos θ ; sin φ sin θ . ; cos '/. Example 15.28 showed that F ∣ _D is orientation-preserving, so it satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition 16.8. Note that F * d x = cos φ cos θ d φ - sin φ sin θ d θ , F * d y = cos φ sin θ d φ + sin φ cos θ d θ , F * d z = - sin φ d φ . Therefore, beginarrayl int_ mathbb S ^2 ω = int_D (- sin^3 φ cos^2 θ d θ ∧ d φ + sin^3 φ sin^2 θ d φ ∧ d θ . . + cos^2 φ sin φ cos^2 θ d φ ∧ d θ - cos^2 φ sin φ sin^2 θ d θ ∧ d φ ) = int_D sin φ d φ ∧ d θ = int_0 ^2 π int_0 ^π sin φ d φ d θ = 4 π . endarraytag It is worth remarking that the hypotheses of Proposition 16.8 can be relaxed somewhat. The requirement that each map F _i be smooth on bar(D) _i is included to ensure that the boundaries of the image sets W _i have measure zero and that the pullback forms F _i * ω are continuous on bar(D) _i . Provided the open subsets W _i together fill up all of M except for a set of measure zero, we can allow maps F _i that do not extend smoothly to the boundary, by interpreting the resulting integrals of unbounded forms either as improper Riemann integrals or as Lebesgue integrals. For example, if the closed upper hemisphere of mathbb S ^2 is parametrized by the map mathbf bar F : mathbf overline mathbb B ^2 → mathbb S ^2 given by F ( u , v ) = ( u , v , sqrt 1 - u ^2 - v ^2 ) , then F is continuous but not smooth up to the boundary, but the conclusion of the proposition still holds. We leave it to the interested reader to work out reasonable conditions under which such a generalization of Proposition 16.8 holds. | `FC08-C15-U036`, `FC08-C16-U011` |
| `FC08-C16-U013` | **Definition/convention — left-invariant** | Ch. 16, § Integration on Lie Groups; source L12297 | Let G be a Lie group. A covariant tensor field A on G is said to be left-invariant if L _g * A = A for all g ∈ G | — |
| `FC08-C16-U014` | **Proposition 16.10** | Ch. 16, § Integration on Lie Groups; source L12299 | Let G be a compact Lie group endowed with a left-invariant orientation. Then G has a unique positively oriented left-invariant n-form ! with the property that textstyle int _G ω _G = 1 | — |
| `FC08-C16-U015` | **Definition/convention — Haar volume form** | Ch. 16, § Integration on Lie Groups; source L12328 | Remark. The orientation form whose existence is asserted in this proposition is called the Haar volume form on G . Similarly, the map f ↦ textstyle int _G f ! is called the Haar integral. Observe that the proof above did not use the fact that G was compact until the last paragraph; thus every Lie group has a left-invariant orientation form that is uniquely defined up to a constant multiple. It is only in the compact case, however, that we can use the volume normalization to single out a unique one. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U016` | **Theorem 16.11** | Ch. 16, § Stokes’s Theorem; source L12336 | (Stokes’s Theorem). Let M be an oriented smooth n-manifold with boundary, and let ! be a compactly supported smooth .n 1/-form on M . Then int_M d ω = int_∂ M ω .tag16.4 | — |
| `FC08-C16-U017` | **Example 16.12** | Ch. 16, § Stokes’s Theorem; source L12416 | Let M be a smooth manifold and suppose γ : [ a , b ] → M is a smooth embedding, so that S = γ ( [ a , b ] ) is an embedded 1-submanifold with boundary in M . If we give S the orientation such that γ is orientation-preserving, then for any smooth function f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) , Stokes’s theorem says that int_γ d f = int_[ a, b ] γ* d f = int_S d f = int_∂ S f = f (γ (b)) - f (γ (a)). Thus Stokes’s theorem reduces to the fundamental theorem for line integrals (Theorem 11.39) in this case. In particular, when γ : [ a , b ] ℝ is the inclusion map, then Stokes’s theorem is just the ordinary fundamental theorem of calculus. | `FC08-C11-U053` |
| `FC08-C16-U018` | **Corollary 16.13** | Ch. 16, § Stokes’s Theorem; source L12426 | (Integrals of Exact Forms). If M is a compact oriented smooth manifold without boundary, then the integral of every exact form over M is zero: int_M d ω = 0 i f ∂ M = varnothing . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U019` | **Corollary 16.14** | Ch. 16, § Stokes’s Theorem; source L12432 | (Integrals of Closed Forms over Boundaries). Suppose M is a compact oriented smooth manifold with boundary. If ! is a closed form on M; then the integral of ! over @M is zero: int_∂ M ω = 0 i f d ω = 0 o n M. These results have the following extremely useful applications to submanifolds. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U020` | **Corollary 16.15** | Ch. 16, § Stokes’s Theorem; source L12440 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold with or without boundary, S ⊆ M is an oriented compact smooth k-dimensional submanifold (without boundary), and ! is a closed k-form on M . If beginarray r int _S ω ≠ 0 . endarray , then both of the following are true: (a) ! is not exact on M (b) S is not the boundary of an oriented compact smooth submanifold with boundary in M . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U021` | **Example 16.16** | Ch. 16, § Stokes’s Theorem; source L12446 | It follows from the computation of Example 11.36 that the closed 1-form ω = ( x d y - y d x ) / big ( x ^2 + y ^2 big ) has nonzero integral over mathbb S ^1 . We already observed that ! is not exact on ℝ ^2 setminus 0 . The preceding corollary tells us in addition that mathbb S ^1 is not the boundary of a compact regular domain in mathbb R ^2 setminus 0 // | `FC08-C11-U049` |
| `FC08-C16-U022` | **Theorem 16.17** | Ch. 16, § Stokes’s Theorem; source L12450 | (Green’s Theorem). Suppose D is a compact regular domain in ℝ ^2 , and P , Q are smooth real-valued functions on D. Then int_D ((∂ Q)/(∂ x) - (∂ P)/(∂ y) ) d x d y = int_∂ D P d x + Q d y. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U023` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12462 | Let overline ℝ _+ ^n denote the subset of ℝ ^n where all of the coordinates are nonnegative: bar ℝ _+ ^n = (x ^1, … , x ^n ) ∈ ℝ ^n: x ^1 ≥ 0, … , x ^n ≥ 0 . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U024` | **Definition/convention — chart** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12472 | Suppose M is a topological n-manifold with boundary. A chart with corners for M is a pair ( U , φ ) , where U ⊆ M is open and φ is a homeomorphism from U to a (relatively) open subset hat(U) ⊆ overline ℝ _+ ^n (Fig. 16.10). Two charts with corners ( U , φ ) ( V , ψ ) are smoothly compatible if the composite map φ ∘ ψ ^- 1 : ψ ( U ∩ V ) → φ ( U ∩ V ) is smooth. (As usual, this means that it admits a smooth extension in an open neighborhood of each point.) | — |
| `FC08-C16-U025` | **Definition/convention — smooth manifold** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12474 | A smooth structure with corners on a topological manifold with boundary is a maximal collection of smoothly compatible interior charts and charts with corners whose domains cover M . A topological manifold with boundary together with a smooth structure with corners is called a smooth manifold with corners. Any chart with corners in the given smooth structure with corners is called a smooth chart with corners for M . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U026` | **Example 16.19** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12476 | Any closed rectangle in ℝ ^n is a smooth n-manifold with corners. // | — |
| `FC08-C16-U027` | **Definition/convention — corner points of the model orthant** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12483 | The boundary of overline ℝ _+ ^n in ℝ ^n is the set of points at which at least one coordinate vanishes. The points in overline ℝ _+ ^n at which more than one coordinate vanishes are called its corner points. For example, the corner points of overline ℝ _+ ^3 are the origin together with all the points on the positive x - , y - . , and z-axes. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U028` | **Proposition 16.20** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12485 | (Invariance of Corner Points). Let M be a smooth n-manifold with corners, n ≥ 2 , and let p ∈ M I f φ ( p ) is a corner point for some smooth chart with corners ( U , φ ) , then the same is true for every such chart whose domain contains p | — |
| `FC08-C16-U029` | **Definition/convention — corner point** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12496 | If M is a smooth manifold with corners, a point p ∈ M is called a corner point if φ ( p ) is a corner point in overline ℝ _+ ^n with respect to some (and hence every) smooth chart with corners ( U , φ ) . Similarly, p is called a boundary point if φ ( p ) ∈ ∂ overline ℝ _+ ^n with respect to some (hence every) such chart. For example, the set of corner points of the unit cube [ 0 , 1 ] ^3 ⊆ ℝ ^3 is the union of its eight vertices and twelve edges. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U030` | **Definition/convention — smooth manifold** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12498 | Every smooth manifold with or without boundary is also a smooth manifold with corners (but with no corner points). Conversely, a smooth manifold with corners is a smooth manifold with boundary if and only if it has no corner points. The boundary of a smooth manifold with corners, however, is in general not a smooth manifold with corners (e.g., think of the boundary of a cube). In fact, even the boundary of overline ℝ _+ ^n itself is not a smooth manifold with corners. It is, however, a union of finitely many such: ∂ mathbb bar(R) _+ ^n = H _1 ∪ ·s ∪ H _n , where H _i = (x ^1, … , x ^n ) ∈ overline ℝ _+ ^n: x ^i = 0 tag16.7 is an ( n - 1 ) -dimensional smooth manifold with corners contained in the subspace defined by x ^i = 0 | — |
| `FC08-C16-U031` | **Definition/convention — integral of** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12508 | In addition, for Stokes’s theorem we need to integrate a differential form over the boundary of a smooth manifold with corners. Since the boundary is not itself a smooth manifold with corners, this requires a separate (albeit routine) definition. Let M be an oriented smooth n-manifold with corners, and suppose ω is an .n 1/- form on @M that is compactly supported in the domain of a single oriented smooth chart with corners ( U , φ ) . We define the integral of ! over @M by int_∂ M ω = sum_i = 1 ^n int_ H _i (φ^- 1 ) * ω , where H _i , defined by (16.7), is given the induced orientation as part of the boundary of the set where x ^i ≥ 0 . In other words, we simply integrate ! in coordinates over the codimension-1 portion of the boundary. Finally, if ! is an arbitrary compactly supported .n 1/-form on M; we define the integral of ω over @M by piecing together with a partition of unity just as in the case of a manifold with boundary. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U032` | **Proposition 16.21** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12521 | The statement of Proposition 16.8 is true if M is replaced by the boundary of a compact, oriented, smooth n-manifold with corners. | `FC08-C16-U011` |
| `FC08-C16-U033` | **Example 16.23** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12525 | Let I × I = [ 0 , 1 ] × [ 0 , 1 ] be the unit square in ℝ ^2 , and suppose ! is a 1-form on ∂ ( I × I ) . Then it is not hard to check that the maps F _i : I → I × I given by beginarrayl l F _1 (t) = (t, 0), & F _2 (t) = (1, t), F _3 (t) = (1 - t, 1), & F _4 (t) = (0, 1 - t), endarraytag16.8 satisfy the hypotheses of Proposition 16.21. (These four curve segments in sequence traverse the boundary of I × I in the counterclockwise direction; see Fig. 16.12.) Therefore, int_∂ (I × I) ω = int_ F _1 ω + int_ F _2 ω + int_ F _3 ω + int_ F _4 ω .tag | `FC08-C16-U032` |
| `FC08-C16-U034` | **Theorem 16.25** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12544 | (Stokes’s Theorem on Manifolds with Corners). Let M be an oriented smooth n-manifold with corners, and let ! be a compactly supported smooth .n 1/-form on M . Then int_M d ω = int_∂ M ω . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U035` | **Theorem 16.26** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12560 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold and 0 ; γ _1 : [ a , b ] → M are path-homotopic piecewise smooth curve segments. For every closed 1-form ! on M; int_ gamma_0 ω = int_ gamma_1 ω . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U036` | **Corollary 16.27** | Ch. 16, § Manifolds with Corners; source L12611 | On a simply connected smooth manifold, every closed 1-form is exact. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U037` | **Definition/convention — volume of M by Vol beginarray r ( M )** | Ch. 16, § Integration of Functions on Riemannian Manifolds; source L12623 | Suppose ( M , g ) is an oriented Riemannian manifold with or without boundary, and let ω _g denote its Riemannian volume form. If f is a compactly supported continuous real-valued function on M; then f ω _g is a compactly supported n-form, so we can define the integral of f over M to be textstyle int _M f ω _g . If M itself is compact, we define the volume of M by Vol beginarray r ( M ) = int _M ω _g endarray | — |
| `FC08-C16-U038` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 16, § Integration of Functions on Riemannian Manifolds; source L12625 | Because of these definitions, the Riemannian volume form is often denoted by d V _g (or d A _g or d s _g in the 2-dimensional or 1-dimensional case, respectively). Then the integral of f over M is written textstyle int _M f d V _g , and the volume of M as textstyle int _M d V _g Be warned, however, that this notation is not meant to imply that the volume form is the exterior derivative of an .n 1/-form; in fact, as we will see when we study de Rham cohomology, this is never the case on a compact manifold. You should just interpret d V _g as a notational convenience. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U039` | **Proposition 16.28** | Ch. 16, § Integration of Functions on Riemannian Manifolds; source L12627 | Let ( M , g ) be a nonempty oriented Riemannian manifold with or without boundary, and suppose f is a compactly supported continuous realvalued function on M satisfying f ≥ 0 . Then textstyle int _M f d V _g ≥ 0 , with equality if and only if f equiv 0 | — |
| `FC08-C16-U040` | **Definition/convention — smooth bundle isomorphism** | Ch. 16, § The Divergence Theorem; source L12653 | In addition, as we did in Chapter 14 in the case of ℝ ^3 , we define a smooth bundle isomorphism β : X ( M ) → Omega ^n - 1 ( M ) as follows: β (X) = X lrcorner d V _g.tag16.11 | — |
| `FC08-C16-U041` | **Lemma 16.30** | Ch. 16, § The Divergence Theorem; source L12661 | Let ( M , g ) be an oriented Riemannian manifold with or without boundary. Suppose S ⊆ M is an immersed hypersurface with the orientation determined by a unit normal vector field N , and widetilde g is the induced metric on S. If X is any vector field along S, then iota_S * (β (X)) = langle X, N rangle_g d V _tilde(g).tag16.12 | — |
| `FC08-C16-U042` | **Theorem 16.32** | Ch. 16, § The Divergence Theorem; source L12712 | (The Divergence Theorem). Let ( M , g ) be an oriented Riemannian manifold with boundary. For any compactly supported smooth vector field X on M; int_M (div X) d V _g = int_∂ M langle X, N rangle_g d V _tilde(g), where N is the outward-pointing unit normal vector field along @M and widetilde g is the induced Riemannian metric on @M . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U043` | **Definition/convention — volume-increasing** | Ch. 16, § The Divergence Theorem; source L12728 | The term “divergence” is used because of the following geometric interpretation. A smooth flow θ on M is said to be volume-preserving if for every compact regular domain D, we have Vol ( θ _t ( D ) ) = V o l ( D ) whenever the domain of θ _t contains D. It is called volume-increasing, volume-decreasing, volume-nonincreasing, or volume-nondecreasing if for every such D, Vol ( θ _t ( D ) ) is strictly increasing, strictly decreasing, nonincreasing, or nondecreasing, respectively, as a function of t. Note that the properties of flow domains ensure that if D is contained in the domain of θ _t for some t , then the same is true for all times between 0 and t . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U044` | **Proposition 16.33** | Ch. 16, § The Divergence Theorem; source L12732 | (Geometric Interpretation of the Divergence). Let M be an oriented Riemannian manifold, let X ∈ X ( M ) , and let θ be the flow of X. Then θ is (a) volume-preserving if and only if div X = 0 everywhere on M . (b) volume-nondecreasing if and only if div X ≥ 0 everywhere on M . (c) volume-nonincreasing if and only if div X le 0 everywhere on M . (d) volume-increasing if and only if div X > 0 on a dense subset of M . (e) volume-decreasing if and only if div X < 0 on a dense subset of M . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U045` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 16, § Surface Integrals; source L12794 | Now suppose S ⊆ M is a compact 2-dimensional submanifold with or without boundary, and N is a smooth unit normal vector field along S. Let dA denote the Riemannian volume form on S with respect to the induced metric iota _S * g and the orientation determined by N , so that d A = iota _S * ( N lrcorner d V _g ) by Proposition 15.32. (See Fig. 16.16.) For any smooth vector field X defined on M; the surface integral of X over S (with respect to the given choice of unit normal field) is defined as | `FC08-C15-U040` |
| `FC08-C16-U046` | **Theorem 16.34** | Ch. 16, § Surface Integrals; source L12805 | (Stokes’s Theorem for Surface Integrals). Suppose M is an oriented Riemannian 3-manifold with or without boundary, and S is a compact oriented 2-dimensional smooth submanifold with boundary in M . For any smooth vector field X on M; int_S langle curl X, N rangle_g d A = int_∂ S langle X, T rangle_g d s, where N is the smooth unit normal vector field along S that determines its orientation, ds is the Riemannian volume form for @S (with respect to the metric and orientation induced from S), and T is the unique positively oriented unit tangent vector field on @S. | — |
| `FC08-C16-U047` | **Definition/convention — objects** | Ch. 16, § Densities; source L12841 | In the theory of integration of differential forms, the crucial place where orientations entered the picture was in our proof of the diffeomorphism-invariance of the integral (Proposition 16.1), because the transformation law for an n-form on an n-manifold under a change of coordinates involves the Jacobian determinant of the transition map, while the transformation law for integrals involves the absolute value of the determinant. We had to restrict attention to orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms so that we could freely remove the absolute value signs. In this section we define objects whose transformation law involves the absolute value of the determinant, so that we no longer have this sign problem. | `FC08-C16-U003` |
| `FC08-C16-U048` | **Definition/convention — density** | Ch. 16, § Densities; source L12843 | We begin, as always, in the linear-algebraic setting. Let V be an n-dimensional vector space. A density on V is a function | — |
| `FC08-C16-U049` | **Definition/convention — density** | Ch. 16, § Densities; source L12855 | (Compare this with the corresponding formula (14.2) for n-forms.) Observe that a density is not a tensor, because it is not linear over R in any of its arguments. Let D ( V ) denote the set of all densities on V . | — |
| `FC08-C16-U050` | **Proposition 16.35** | Ch. 16, § Densities; source L12857 | (Properties of Densities). Let V be a vector space of dimension n ≥ 1 (a) D .V / is a vector space under the obvious vector operations: (c _1 mu_1 + c _2 mu_2) (v _1, … , v _n) = c _1 mu_1 (v _1, … , v _n) + c _2 mu_2 (v _1, … , v _n). | `FC08-C16-U051` | **Definition/convention — positive density** | Ch. 16, § Densities; source L12903 | A positive density on V is a density μ satisfying μ ( v _1 , … , v _n ) > 0 whenever ( v _1 , … , v _n ) is a linearly independent n-tuple. A negative density is defined similarly. If ! is a nonzero element of Lambda ^n ( V * ) , then it is clear that ! is a positive density; more generally, a density c ∣ ω ∣ is positive, negative, or zero if and only if c has the same property. Thus, each density on V is either positive, negative, or zero, and the set of positive densities is a convex subset of D ( V ) (namely, a half-line). | — |

| `FC08-C16-U065` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 16, § The Riemannian Density; source L13069 | Because of Exercise 16.46(b), it is customary to denote the Riemannian density simply by d V _g , and to specify when necessary whether the notation refers to a density or a form. If f : M → ℝ is a compactly supported continuous function, the integral of f over M is defined to be textstyle int _M f d V _g . Exercise 16.46 shows that when M is oriented, it does not matter whether we interpret d V _g as the Riemannian volume form or the Riemannian density. When M is not orientable, however, we have no choice but to interpret it as a density. | — |

| `FC08-C17-U001` | **Definition/convention — de Rham cohomology groups** | Ch. 17; source L13307 | In Chapter 14 we defined closed and exact forms: a smooth differential form ! is closed if d ω = 0 , and exact if it can be written ω = d η . Because d ∘ d = 0 . every exact form is closed. In this chapter, we explore the implications of the converse question: Is every closed form exact? The answer, in general, is no: in Example 11.48, for instance, we saw a 1-form on the punctured plane that is closed but not exact; the failure of exactness seemed to be a consequence of the “hole” in the center of the domain. For higher-degree forms, the question of which closed forms are exact depends on subtle topological properties of the manifold, connected with the existence of “holes” of higher dimensions. Making this dependence quantitative leads to a new set of invariants of smooth manifolds, called the de Rham cohomology groups, which are the subject of this chapter. | `FC08-C11-U064` |
| `FC08-C17-U002` | **Convention/notation — de Rham cohomology group in degree p (** | Ch. 17, § The de Rham Cohomology Groups; source L13327 | Let M be a smooth manifold with or without boundary, and let p be a nonnegative integer. Because d Omega ^p ( M ) → Omega ^p + 1 ( M ) is linear, its kernel and image are linear subspaces. We define Z ^p (M) = Ker (d: Omega^p (M) arrow Omega^p + 1 (M) ) = text closed p text -forms on M , B ^p (M) = Im (d: Omega^p - 1 (M) arrow Omega^p (M) ) = text exact p text -forms on M . By convention, we consider Omega ^p ( M ) to be the zero vector space when p < 0 or p > n = dim M ; so that, for example, B ^0 ( M ) = 0 and Z ^n ( M ) = Omega ^n ( M ) The fact that every exact form is closed implies that B ^p ( M ) ⊆ Z ^p ( M ) . Thus, it makes sense to define the de Rham cohomology group in degree p (or the pth de Rham group) of M to be the quotient vector space H _dR ^p (M) = (Z ^p (M))/(B ^p (M)). (It is a real vector space, and thus in particular a group under vector addition. Perhaps “de Rham cohomology space” would be a more appropriate term, but because most other cohomology theories produce only groups it is traditional to use the term group in this context as well, bearing in mind that these “groups” are actually real vector spaces.) It is clear that H _d R ^p ( M ) = 0 for p < 0 or p > dim M; because Omega ^p ( M ) = 0 in those cases. For 0 ≤ p ≤ n , the definition implies that H _d R ^p ( M ) = 0 if and only if every closed p-form on M is exact. | — |
| `FC08-C17-U003` | **Example 17.1** | Ch. 17, § The de Rham Cohomology Groups; source L13347 | The fact that there is a closed 1-form on ℝ ^2 setminus 0 that is not exact means that H _d R ^1 ( ℝ ^2 setminus 0 ) ≠ 0 (see Example 11.48). On the other hand, the Poincaré lemma for 1-forms (Theorem 11.49) implies that H _d R ^1 ( U ) = 0 for any star-shaped open subset U ⊆ ℝ ^n // | `FC08-C11-U064`, `FC08-C11-U066` |
| `FC08-C17-U004` | **Definition/convention — cohomology class of** | Ch. 17, § The de Rham Cohomology Groups; source L13349 | The first order of business is to show that the de Rham groups are diffeomorphism invariants. For any closed p - form ω on M; we let Œ! denote the equivalence class of ! in H _d R ^p ( M ) , called the cohomology class of !. If [ ω ] = [ ω ^prime ] (that is, if ! and ω ^prime differ by an exact form), we say that ! and ω ^prime are cohomologous. | — |
| `FC08-C17-U005` | **Proposition 17.2** | Ch. 17, § The de Rham Cohomology Groups; source L13351 | (Induced Cohomology Maps). For any smooth map F : M → N between smooth manifolds with or without boundary, the pullback F * : Omega ^p ( N ) → Omega ^p ( M ) carries Z ^p ( N ) into Z ^p ( M ) and B ^p ( N ) into B ^p ( M ) . It thus descends to a linear map, still denoted by F * , from H _d R ^p ( N ) to H _d R ^p ( M ) , called the induced cohomology map. It has the following properties: (a) I f G : N → P is another smooth map, then (G ∘ F) * = F * ∘ G * : H _dR ^p (P) → H _dR ^p (M). (b) If Id denotes the identity map of M; then I d * is the identity map of H _d R ^p ( M ) | — |
| `FC08-C17-U006` | **Corollary 17.3** | Ch. 17, § The de Rham Cohomology Groups; source L13371 | (Functoriality). For any integer p, the assignment M ↦ H _d R ^p ( M ) F ↦ F * is a contravariant functor from the category of smooth manifolds with boundary to the category of real vector spaces. | — |
| `FC08-C17-U007` | **Corollary 17.4** | Ch. 17, § The de Rham Cohomology Groups; source L13373 | (Diffeomorphism Invariance of de Rham Cohomology). D i f f e o - morphic smooth manifolds (with or without boundary) have isomorphic de Rham cohomology groups. | — |
| `FC08-C17-U008` | **Proposition 17.5** | Ch. 17, § Elementary Computations; source L13379 | (Cohomology of Disjoint Unions). Let M _j be a countable collection of smooth n-manifolds with or without boundary, and let beginarray r M = coprod _j M _j endarray . For each p, the inclusion maps iota _j : M _j ↪ M induce an isomorphism from H _d R ^p ( M ) to the direct product space Q H _d R ^p ( M _j ) | — |
| `FC08-C17-U009` | **Proposition 17.6** | Ch. 17, § Elementary Computations; source L13393 | (Cohomology in Degree Zero). If M is a connected smooth manifold with or without boundary, then H _d R ^0 ( M ) is equal to the space of constant functions and is therefore 1-dimensional. | — |
| `FC08-C17-U010` | **Corollary 17.7** | Ch. 17, § Elementary Computations; source L13397 | (Cohomology of Zero-Manifolds). Suppose M is a manifold of dimension 0. Then H _d R ^0 ( M ) is a direct product of 1-dimensional vector spaces, one for each point of M; and all other de Rham cohomology groups of M are zero. | — |

| `FC08-C17-U019` | **Definition/convention — star-shaped** | Ch. 17, § Computations Using Homotopy Invariance; source L13518 | In Theorem 11.49, we showed that every closed 1-form on a star-shaped open subset of ℝ ^n is exact. | `FC08-C17-U042` | **Theorem 17.35** | Ch. 17, § Degree Theory; source L13822 | (Degree of a Smooth Map). Suppose M and N are compact, connected, oriented, smooth manifolds of dimension n, and F : M → N is a smooth map. There exists a unique integer k, called the degree of F , that satisfies both of the following conditions. (a) For every smooth n-form ! on N , int_M F * ω = k int_N ω . (b) H q ∈ N is a regular value of F , then k = sum_x ∈ F ^- 1 (q) sgn (x), where s g n ( x ) = + 1 i f d F _x is orientation-preserving, and 1 if it is orientationreversing. | — |
| `FC08-C17-U043` | **Proposition 17.36** | Ch. 17, § Degree Theory; source L13854 | (Properties of the Degree). Suppose M; N , and P are compact, connected, oriented, smooth n-manifolds. (a) If F : M → N and G : N → P are both smooth maps, then deg ( G ∘ F ) = ( deg G ) ( deg F ) (b) If F : M → N is a diffeomorphism, then deg F = + 1 i f F is orientationpreserving and 1 if it is orientation-reversing. (c) If two smooth maps F _0 , F _1 : M → N are homotopic, then they have the same degree. | — |
| `FC08-C17-U044` | **Definition/convention — degree of a continuous map F** | Ch. 17, § Degree Theory; source L13864 | This proposition allows us to define the degree of a continuous map F : M → N between compact, connected, oriented, smooth n-manifolds, by letting deg F be the degree of any smooth map that is homotopic to F . The Whitney approximation theorem guarantees that there is such a map, and the preceding proposition guarantees that the degree is the same for every map homotopic to F | — |

I f ~ f : ∂ X → N is a continuous map that has a continuous extension to X , then deg f = 0 | — |

In that case, the term homology is used in place of cohomology.) | — |
| `FC08-C17-U052` | **Definition/convention — cochain map** | Ch. 17, § Proof of the Mayer–Vietoris Theorem; source L13936 | If A * and B * are complexes, a cochain map from A * to B * , denoted by F : A * → B * , is a collection of linear maps F : A ^p → B ^p (it is easiest to use the same symbol for all of the maps) such that the following diagram commutes for each p : beginarrayc … → A ^p xrightarrow d A ^p + 1 → … F Big downarrow Big downarrow F … → B ^p xrightarrow d B ^p + 1 → … . endarray | — |
| `FC08-C17-U053` | **Definition/convention — chain map** | Ch. 17, § Proof of the Mayer–Vietoris Theorem; source L13942 | The fact that F ∘ d = d ∘ F means that any cochain map induces a linear map on cohomology F * : H ^p ( A * ) → H ^p ( B * ) for each p , , just as in the case of de Rham cohomology. | `FC08-C17-U054` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 17, § Proof of the Mayer–Vietoris Theorem; source L13944 | A short exact sequence of complexes consists of three complexes A * , B * , C * together with cochain maps 0 → A * stackrel F → B * stackrel G → C * → 0 such that each sequence 0 → A ^p xrightarrow F B ^p xrightarrow G C ^p → 0 is exact. H φ , ψ is a smooth partition of unity subordinate to U , V , we can take η = ψ ω and η ^prime = - φ ω , both extended by zero outside the supports of and φ | — |
| `FC08-C18-U001` | **Definition/convention — dimension of the simplex** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14127 | We begin with a brief summary of singular homology theory. Suppose v _0 , … , v _p are any p 1 points in some Euclidean space R n . They are said to be affinely independent (or in general position) if they are not contained in any .p 1/-dimensional affine subspace. A geometric p-simplex is a subset of ℝ ^n of the form sum_i = 0 ^p t _i v _i: 0 ≤ t _i ≤ 1 text and sum_i = 0 ^p t _i = 1 , for some p + 1 affinely independent points v _0 , … , v _p . The integer p (one less than the number of vertices) is called the dimension of the simplex. The points v _0 , … , v _p are called its vertices, and the geometric simplex with these vertices is denoted by [ v _0 , … , v _p ] . It is a compact convex set, in fact the smallest convex set containing v _0 , … , v _p . The simplices whose vertices are nonempty subsets of v _0 , … , v _p are called the faces of the simplex. The ( p - 1 ) -dimensional faces are called its boundary faces. There are precisely p + 1 boundary faces, obtained by omitting each of the vertices in turn; the ith boundary face [ v _0 , … , widehat v _i , … , v _p ] (with v _i omitted) is denoted by ∂ _i [ v _0 , … , v _p ] , and is called the face opposite ±b v _i | — |
| `FC08-C18-U002` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14140 | The standard p-simplex is the simplex Delta _p = [ e _0 , e _1 , … , e _p ] ⊆ ℝ ^p , where e _0 = 0 and e _i is the i th standard basis vector. For example, Delta _0 = 0 , Delta _1 = [ 0 , 1 ] ， Delta _2 is the triangle with vertices .0; 0/, .1; 0/, and .0; 1/ together with its interior, and Delta _3 is a solid tetrahedron (Fig. 18.1). | — |
| `FC08-C18-U003` | **Definition/convention — singular p-simplex in M** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14142 | Let M be a topological space. A continuous map σ : Delta _p M is called a singular p-simplex in M . The singular chain group of M in degree p, denoted by C _p ( M ) , is the free abelian group generated by all singular p-simplices in M . An element of this group, called a singular p-chain, is a finite formal linear combination of singular p-simplices in M with integer coefficients. | — |
| `FC08-C18-U004` | **Definition/convention — affine singular simplex in M** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14144 | One special case that arises frequently is that in which the space M is a convex subset of some Euclidean space ℝ ^m . In that case, for any ordered ( p + 1 ) /-tuple of points ( w _0 , … , w _p ) in M; not necessarily affinely independent, there is a unique affine map from R p to ℝ ^m that takes e _i to w _i for i = 0 , … , p . (The map is easily constructed by first finding a linear map that takes e _i to w _i - w _0 for i = 1 , … , p , and then translating by w _0 . ) The restriction of this affine map to Delta _p is denoted by A ( w _0 , … , w _p ) , and is called an affine singular simplex in M | — |
| `FC08-C18-U005` | **Definition/convention — i th face map in Delta** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14149 | For each i = 0 , … , p , we define the i th face map in Delta _p to be the affine singular ( p - 1 ) /-simplex F _i , p : Delta _p - 1 → Delta _p defined by F _i, p = A (e _0, … , widehat e _i, … , e _p ). | — |
| `FC08-C18-U006` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14157 | The boundary of a singular p-simplex σ Delta _p M is the singular ( p - 1 ) -chain @σ defined by ∂ σ = sum_i = 0 ^p (- 1) ^i σ ∘ F _i, p. | — |
| `FC08-C18-U007` | **Definition/convention — singular boundary operator** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14163 | For example, if σ is a singular 2-simplex, its boundary is a formal sum of three singular 1-simplices with coefficients 1, as indicated in Fig. 18.2. This extends uniquely to a group homomorphism @ C _p ( M ) → C _p - 1 ( M ) , called the singular boundary operator. The basic fact about the boundary operator is the next lemma. | — |
| `FC08-C18-U008` | **Lemma 18.2** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14165 | If c is any singular chain, then ∂ ( ∂ c ) = 0 | — |
| `FC08-C18-U009` | **Definition/convention — cycle** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14175 | A singular p-chain c is called a cycle if ∂ c = 0 , and a boundary if c = ∂ b for some singular ( p + 1 ) /-chain b. Let Z _p ( M ) denote the set of singular p-cycles in M; and B _p ( M ) the set of singular p-boundaries. Because @ is a homomorphism, Z _p ( M ) and B _p ( M ) are subgroups of C _p ( M ) , and because ∂ ∘ ∂ = 0 , they satisfy B _p ( M ) ⊆ Z _p ( M ) . The pth singular homology group of M is the quotient group H _p (M) = (Z _p (M))/(B _p (M)). To put it another way, the sequence of abelian groups and homomorphisms … → C _p + 1 (M) xrightarrow ∂ C _p (M) xrightarrow ∂ C _p - 1 (M) → … is a complex, called the singular chain complex, and H _p ( M ) is the pth homology group of this complex. The equivalence class in H _p ( M ) of a singular p-cycle c is called its homology class, and is denoted by Œc. We say that two p-cycles are homologous if they differ by a boundary. | — |
| `FC08-C18-U010` | **Proposition 18.3** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14196 | (Properties of Singular Homology Groups). (a) For any one-point space q , H _0 ( q ) is the infinite cyclic group generated by the homology class of the unique singular 0-simplex mapping Delta _0 to q _· , and H _p ( q ) = 0 f o r a l l p ≠ 0 (b) Let M _j be any collection of topological spaces, and let beginarray r M = coprod _i M _j endarray . The inclusion maps iota _j : M _j ↪ M induce an isomorphism bigoplus _j H _p ( M _j ) ≅ H _p ( M ) (c) Homotopy equivalent spaces have isomorphic singular homology groups. | — |
| `FC08-C18-U011` | **Theorem 18.4** | Ch. 18, § Singular Homology; source L14218 | (Mayer–Vietoris for Singular Homology). Let M be a topological space and let U; V be open subsets of M whose union is M . For each p there is a connecting homomorphism ∂ _* : H _p ( M ) → H _p - 1 ( U ∩ V ) such that the following sequence is exact: beginarrayc … stackrel partial_* → H _p (U ∩ V) stackrel α → H _p (U) oplus H _p (V) stackrel β → H _p (M) stackrel partial_* → H _p - 1 (U ∩ V) stackrel α → … , endarraytag18.4 where α [ c ] = (i _* [ c ], - j _* [ c ] ), β ([ c ], [ c ^prime ] ) = k _* [ c ] + l _* [ c ^prime ], and ∂ _* [ e ] = [ c ] , provided there exist f ∈ C _p ( U ) and f ^prime ∈ C _p ( V ) such that k _sharp f + l _sharp f ^prime is homologous to e and ( i _sharp c , - j _sharp c ) = ( ∂ f , ∂ f ^prime ) | — |
| `FC08-C18-U012` | **Definition/convention — singular cohomology groups** | Ch. 18, § Singular Cohomology; source L14244 | In addition to the singular homology groups, for any topological space M and any abelian group G one can define a closely related sequence of groups H ^p ( M ; G ) called the singular cohomology groups with coefficients in G . The precise definition is unimportant for our purposes; we are only concerned with the special case G = mathbb R , in which case it can be shown that H ^p ( M ; ℝ ) is a real vector space that is naturally isomorphic to the space Hom ( H _p ( M ) , ℝ ) of group homomorphisms from H _p ( M ) into R. (For simplicity, let us take this as our definition of H ^p ( M , ℝ ) . ) Any continuous map F : M → N induces a linear map F * : H ^p ( N ; ℝ ) → H ^p ( M ; ℝ ) defined by ( F * γ ) [ c ] = γ ( F _* [ c ] ) / for each γ ∈ H ^p ( N ; ℝ ) ≅ Hom ( H _p ( N ) , ℝ ) and each singular p-chain c in M . The functorial properties of F _* carry over to cohomology: ( G ∘ F ) * = F * ∘ G * and ( I d _M ) * = I d _H ^p ( M ; ℝ ) . It follows that pth singular cohomology with coefficients in R defines a contravariant functor from the topological category to the category of real vector spaces and linear maps. | — |
| `FC08-C18-U013` | **Proposition 18.5** | Ch. 18, § Singular Cohomology; source L14248 | (Properties of Singular Cohomology). (a) For any one-point space q , H ^p ( q ; ℝ ) is trivial except when p = 0 , in which case it is 1-dimensional. (b) If M _j is any collection of topological spaces and beginarray r M = coprod _i M _j endarray , then the inclusion maps iota _j : M _j ↪ M induce an isomorphism from H ^p ( M ; ℝ ) to beginarray r prod _i H ^p ( M _j ; ℝ ) endarray (c) Homotopy equivalent spaces have isomorphic singular cohomology groups. | — |
| `FC08-C18-U014` | **Theorem 18.6** | Ch. 18, § Singular Cohomology; source L14260 | (Mayer–Vietoris for Singular Cohomology). Suppose M; U , and V satisfy the hypotheses of Theorem 18.4. The following sequence is exact: beginarrayc … xrightarrow ∂* H ^p (M; ℝ) xrightarrow k * oplus l * H ^p (U; ℝ) oplus H ^p (V; ℝ) xrightarrow i * - j * H ^p (U ∩ V; ℝ) xrightarrow ∂* H ^p + 1 (M; ℝ) xrightarrow k * oplus l * … , endarraytag18.5 where the maps k * oplus l * and i * - j * are defined as in (17.8), and ∂ * is defined by ∂ * ( γ ) = γ ∘ ∂ _* , with ∂ _* as in Theorem 18.4. | `FC08-C18-U011` |
| `FC08-C18-U015` | **Definition/convention — smooth chain group in degree p** | Ch. 18, § Smooth Singular Homology; source L14274 | If M is a smooth manifold, a smooth p-simplex in M is a map σ : Delta _p M that is smooth in the sense that it has a smooth extension to a neighborhood of each point. The subgroup of C _p ( M ) generated by smooth simplices is denoted by C _p ^∞ ( M ) and called the smooth chain group in degree p. Elements of this group, which are finite formal linear combinations of smooth simplices, are called smooth chains. Because the boundary of a smooth simplex is a smooth chain, we can define the pth smooth singular homology group of M to be the quotient group | — |
| `FC08-C18-U016` | **Theorem 18.7** | Ch. 18, § Smooth Singular Homology; source L14285 | (Smooth Singular vs. Singular Homology). For any smooth manifold M; the map iota _* : H _p ^∞ ( M ) → H _p ( M ) induced by inclusion is an isomorphism. | — |

| `FC08-C18-U018` | **Lemma 18.9** | Ch. 18, § Smooth Singular Homology; source L14373 | Let M be a smooth manifold, let  be a geometric p-simplex in ℝ ^n and let f : ∂ Delta → M be a continuous map whose restriction to each individual boundary face of Delta is smooth. | `FC08-C18-U019` | **Lemma 18.10** | Ch. 18, § The de Rham Theorem; source L14495 | (The Five Lemma). Consider the following commutative diagram of modules and linear maps: beginarrayc A _1 xrightarrow alpha_1 A _2 xrightarrow alpha_2 A _3 xrightarrow alpha_3 A _4 xrightarrow alpha_4 A _5 Big downarrow f _1 Big downarrow f _2 Big downarrow f _3 Big downarrow f _4 Big downarrow f _5 B _1 xrightarrow beta_1 B _2 xrightarrow beta_2 B _3 xrightarrow beta_3 B _4 xrightarrow beta_4 B _5. endarray If the horizontal rows are exact and f _1 , f _2 , f _4 , and f _5 are isomorphisms, then f _3 is also an isomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C18-U020` | **Definition/convention — integral of** | Ch. 18, § The de Rham Theorem; source L14505 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold, ! is a closed p-form on M; and σ is a smooth p-simplex in M .
