| `FC08-C18-U023` | **Proposition 18.13** | Ch. 18, § The de Rham Theorem; source L14557 | (Naturality of the de Rham Homomorphism). For a smooth manifold M and nonnegative integer p _: , let J : H _d R ^p ( M ) → H ^p ( M ; ℝ ) denote the de Rham homomorphism. (a) If F : M → N is a smooth map, then the following diagram commutes: beginarrayc H _dR ^p (N) xrightarrow F * H _dR ^p (M) I Big downarrow Big downarrow I H ^p (N; ℝ) xrightarrow [ F * ] H ^p (M; ℝ). endarray (b) If M is a smooth manifold and U; V are open subsets of M whose union is M; then the following diagram commutes: beginarrayc H _dR ^p - 1 (U ∩ V) xrightarrow delta H _dR ^p (M) I Biggl downarrow Biggl downarrow I H ^p - 1 (U ∩ V; ℝ) xrightarrow [ ∂* ] H ^p (M; ℝ), endarraytag18.15 where delta and ∂ * are the connecting homomorphisms of the Mayer–Vietoris sequences for de Rham and singular cohomology, respectively. | — |
| `FC08-C18-U024` | **Theorem 18.14** | Ch. 18, § The de Rham Theorem; source L14612 | (de Rham). For every smooth manifold M and nonnegative integer p, the de Rham homomorphism I H _d R ^p ( M ) → H ^p ( M ; ℝ ) is an isomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C18-U025` | **Definition/convention — de Rham basis for M** | Ch. 18, § The de Rham Theorem; source L14616 | If M is any smooth manifold, let us call an open cover U _i of M a de Rham cover if each subset U _i is a de Rham manifold, and every finite intersection U _ i _1 ∩ ·s ∩ U _ i _k is de Rham. A de Rham cover that is also a basis for the topology of M is called a de Rham basis for M . | — |
| `FC08-C19-U001` | **Definition/convention — smooth distribution** | Ch. 19, § Distributions and Involutivity; source L14751 | Let M be a smooth manifold. A distribution on M of rank k is a rank-k subbundle of TM . It is called a smooth distribution if it is a smooth subbundle. Distributions are also sometimes called tangent distributions (especially if there is any opportunity for confusion with the use of the term distribution for generalized functions in analysis), k-plane fields, or tangent subbundles. | — |
| `FC08-C19-U002` | **Definition/convention — rank-k distribution** | Ch. 19, § Distributions and Involutivity; source L14753 | Often a rank-k distribution is described by specifying for each p ∈ M a linear subspace D _p ⊆ T _p M of dimension k, and letting textstyle D = bigcup _p ∈ M D _p . It then follows from the local frame criterion for subbundles (Lemma 10.32) that D is a smooth distribution if and only if each point of M has a neighborhood U on which there are smooth vector fields X _1 , … , X _k : U T M such that X _1 ∣ _q , … , X _k ∣ _q form a basis for D _q at each q ∈ U . In this case, we say that D is the distribution (locally) spanned by the vector fields X _1 , … , X _k | `FC08-C10-U046` |
| `FC08-C19-U003` | **Definition/convention — integral manifold of D** | Ch. 19, § Integral Manifolds and Involutivity; source L14757 | Suppose D ⊆ T M is a smooth distribution. A nonempty immersed submanifold N ⊆ M is called an integral manifold of D if T _p N = D _p at each point p ∈ N The main question we want to address in this chapter is that of the existence of integral manifolds. | — |
| `FC08-C19-U004` | **Example 19.1** | Ch. 19, § Integral Manifolds and Involutivity; source L14761 | (Distributions and Integral Manifolds). (a) If V is a nowhere-vanishing smooth vector field on a manifold M; then V spans a smooth rank-1 distribution on M (see Example 10.33(a)). The image of any integral curve of V is an integral manifold of D (b) In ℝ ^n , the vector fields ∂ / ∂ x ^1 , … , ∂ / ∂ x ^k span a smooth distribution of rank k. The k-dimensional affine subspaces parallel to ℝ ^k are integral manifolds. (c) Let R be the distribution on ℝ ^n setminus 0 spanned by the unit radial vector field x ^i ∂ / ∂ x ^i , and let R ^perp be its orthogonal complement bundle (see Lemma 10.35). Through each point x ∈ ℝ ^n setminus 0 , the sphere of radius ∣ x ∣ around 0 is an integral manifold of R ^perp (d) Let D be the smooth distribution on ℝ ^3 spanned by the following vector fields: X = (∂)/(∂ x) + y (∂)/(∂ z), Y = (∂)/(∂ y). Therefore, no such integral manifold exists. | `FC08-C10-U047`, `FC08-C10-U050` |
| `FC08-C19-U005` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 19, § Integral Manifolds and Involutivity; source L14780 | The last example shows that in general, integral manifolds may fail to exist. Suppose D is a smooth distribution on M . We say that D is involutive if given any pair of smooth local sections of D (i.e., smooth vector fields X, Y defined on an open subset of M such that X _p , Y _p ∈ D _p for each p ) , their Lie bracket is also a local section of D. The next proposition shows that the involutivity condition can be expressed concisely in terms of lie algebras. | — |
| `FC08-C19-U006` | **Proposition 19.2** | Ch. 19, § Integral Manifolds and Involutivity; source L14782 | Let D ⊆ T M be a smooth distribution, and let Gamma ( D ) ⊆ X ( M ) denote the space of smooth global sections of D . Then D is involutive if and only if Gamma ( D ) is a Lie subalgebra of X.M /. | — |
| `FC08-C19-U007` | **Definition/convention — integrable** | Ch. 19, § Integral Manifolds and Involutivity; source L14788 | A smooth distribution D on M is said to be integrable if each point of M is contained in an integral manifold of D. | — |
| `FC08-C19-U008` | **Proposition 19.3** | Ch. 19, § Integral Manifolds and Involutivity; source L14790 | Every integrable distribution is involutive. | — |
| `FC08-C19-U009` | **Lemma 19.4** | Ch. 19, § Integral Manifolds and Involutivity; source L14798 | (Local Frame Criterion for Involutivity). Let D ⊆ T M be a distribution. If in a neighborhood of every point of M there exists a smooth local frame ( V _1 , … , V _k ) for D such that [ V _i , V _j ] is a section of D for each i , j = 1 , … , k , then D is involutive. | — |
| `FC08-C19-U010` | **Lemma 19.5** | Ch. 19, § Involutivity and Differential Forms; source L14812 | (1-Form Criterion for Smooth Distributions). Suppose M is a smooth n-manifold and D ⊆ T M is a distribution of rank k. Then D is smooth if and only if each point p ∈ M has a neighborhood U on which there are smooth 1-forms ω ^1 , … , ω ^n - k such that for each q ∈ U D _q = Ker ω^1 big ∣ _q ∩ … ∩ Ker ω^n - k big ∣ _q.tag19.1 | — |
| `FC08-C19-U011` | **Definition/convention — local defining forms for D** | Ch. 19, § Involutivity and Differential Forms; source L14826 | If D is a rank-k distribution on a smooth n-manifold M; any n - k linearly independent 1-forms ω ^1 , … , ω ^n - k defined on an open subset U ⊆ M and satisfying (19.1) for each q ∈ U are called local defining forms for D. More generally, if 0 ≤ p ≤ n , we say that a p-form ω ∈ Omega ^p ( M ) annihilates D if ω ( X _1 , … , X _p ) = 0 whenever X _1 , … , X _p are local sections of D. (In the case p = 0 , only the zero function annihilates D.) | — |
| `FC08-C19-U012` | **Lemma 19.6** | Ch. 19, § Involutivity and Differential Forms; source L14828 | Suppose M is a smooth n-manifold and D is a smooth rank-k distribution on M . Let ω ^1 , … , ω ^n - k be smooth local defining forms for D over an open subset U ⊆ M . A smooth p-form η defined on U annihilates D if and only if it can be expressed in the form η = sum_i = 1 ^n - k ω^i ∧ β^itag19.2 for some smooth ( p - 1 ) -forms β ^1 , … , β ^n - k on U . | — |
| `FC08-C19-U013` | **Theorem 19.7** | Ch. 19, § Involutivity and Differential Forms; source L14854 | (1-Form Criterion for Involutivity). Suppose D ⊆ T M is a smooth distribution. Then D is involutive if and only if the following condition is satisfied: If η is any smooth 1-form that annihilates D on an open subset U ⊆ M ; then dη also annihilates D on U (19.3) | — |
| `FC08-C19-U014` | **Proposition 19.8** | Ch. 19, § Involutivity and Differential Forms; source L14880 | (Local Coframe Criterion for Involutivity). Let D be a smooth distribution of rank k on a smooth n-manifold M; and let ω ^1 , … , ω ^n - k be smooth defining forms for D on an open subset U ⊆ M . The following are equivalent: (a) D is involutive on U . (b) d ω ^1 , … , d ω ^n - k annihilate D. (c) There exist smooth 1-forms α _j ^i : i , j = 1 , … , n - k such that d ω^i = sum_j = 1 ^n - k ω^j ∧ alpha_j ^i, f o r e a c h i = 1, … , n - k. | — |

Let I ^p ( D ) ⊆ Omega ^p ( M ) denote the space of smooth p-forms that annihilate D , and let J ( D ) = J ^0 ( D ) oplus ·s oplus J ^n ( D ) ⊆ Omega * ( M ) | — |
| `FC08-C19-U016` | **Definition/convention — Pfaffian system** | Ch. 19, § Involutivity and Differential Forms; source L14903 | Any ideal of the form A ( D ) for some smooth distribution D is sometimes called a Pfaffian system. An ideal A ⊆ Omega * ( M ) is said to be a differential ideal if d ( A ) ⊆ A that is, if ω ∈ A implies d ω ∈ A | — |
| `FC08-C19-U017` | **Proposition 19.11** | Ch. 19, § Involutivity and Differential Forms; source L14905 | (Differential Ideal Criterion for Involutivity). Let M be a smooth manifold. A smooth distribution D ⊆ T M is involutive if and only i f vartheta ( D ) is a differential ideal in Omega * ( M ) . | — |
| `FC08-C19-U018` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 19, § The Frobenius Theorem; source L14911 | In Example 19.1, all of the distributions we defined except the last one had the property that there was an integral manifold through each point. Moreover, locally these submanifolds all “fit together” nicely like parallel affine subspaces of R n . Given a rank-k distribution D ⊆ T M , ; let us say that a smooth coordinate chart ( U , φ ) on M is flat for D if φ ( U ) is a cube in ℝ ^n , and at points of U , D is spanned by the first k coordinate vector fields ∂ / ∂ x ^1 , … , ∂ / ∂ x ^k ( F i g . 1 9 . 2 ) . In any such chart, each slice of the form x ^k + 1 = c ^k + 1 , . . . , x ^n = c ^n for constants c ^k + 1 , … , c ^n is an integral manifold of D. This is the nicest possible local situation for integral manifolds. We say that a distribution D ⊆ T M is completely integrable if there exists a flat chart for D in a neighborhood of each point of M . Obviously, every completely integrable distribution is integrable and therefore involutive. In summary, text completely integrable Rightarrow text integrable Rightarrow text involutive . | `FC08-C19-U004` |
| `FC08-C19-U019` | **Theorem 19.12** | Ch. 19, § The Frobenius Theorem; source L14923 | (Frobenius). Every involutive distribution is completely integrable. | — |
| `FC08-C19-U020` | **Corollary 19.13** | Ch. 19, § The Frobenius Theorem; source L14964 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold, D is an involutive rank-k distribution on M; and S ⊆ M is a codimension-k embedded submanifold. If p ∈ S is a point such that T _p S is complementary to D _p , then there is a flat chart ( U , ( s ^i ) ) for D centered at p in which S ∩ U is the slice s ^1 = ·s = s ^k = 0 | — |
| `FC08-C19-U021` | **Example 19.14** | Ch. 19, § The Frobenius Theorem; source L14970 | Let D ⊆ T ℝ ^3 be the distribution spanned by the vector fields X = x (∂)/(∂ x) + (∂)/(∂ y) + x (y + 1) (∂)/(∂ z), Y = (∂)/(∂ x) + y (∂)/(∂ z). The computation of Example 8.27 showed that [ X, Y ] = - (∂)/(∂ x) - y (∂)/(∂ z) = - Y, so D is involutive. It follows that the integral manifolds of D are the level sets of w ( x , y , z ) = z - x y (Since the flat chart we have constructed is actually a global chart in this case, this describes all of the integral manifolds, not just the ones near the origin.) The flat coordinates we seek are given by inverting the map ( x , y , z ) = φ ( u , v , w ) = ( u , v , w + u v ) , to yield (u, v, w) = varPhi^- 1 (x, y, z) = (x, y, z - x y). | `FC08-C08-U038`, `FC08-C09-U065` |
| `FC08-C19-U022` | **Proposition 19.16** | Ch. 19, § The Frobenius Theorem; source L15039 | (Local Structure of Integral Manifolds). Let D be an involutive distribution of rank k on a smooth manifold M; and let ( U , ( x ^i ) ) be a flat chart for D. If H is any integral manifold of D, then H U is a union of countably many disjoint open subsets of parallel k-dimensional slices of U , each of which is open in H and embedded in M . | — |
| `FC08-C19-U023` | **Theorem 19.17** | Ch. 19, § The Frobenius Theorem; source L15049 | Every integral manifold of an involutive distribution is weakly embedded. | — |
| `FC08-C19-U024` | **Definition/convention — leaves of the foliation)** | Ch. 19, § Foliations; source L15063 | To express more precisely what we mean by “fitting together,” we need to extend our notion of a flat chart slightly. Let M be a smooth n-manifold, and let F be any collection of k-dimensional submanifolds of M . A smooth chart ( U , φ ) for M is said to be flat for F if φ ( U ) is a cube in ℝ ^n , and each submanifold in F intersects U in either the empty set or a countable union of k-dimensional slices of the form x ^k + 1 = c ^k + 1 , . . . , x ^n = c ^n . We define a foliation of dimension k on M to be a collection F of disjoint, connected, nonempty, immersed k-dimensional submanifolds of M (called the leaves of the foliation), whose union is M; and such that in a neighborhood of each point p ∈ M there exists a flat chart for F | — |
| `FC08-C19-U025` | **Example 19.18** | Ch. 19, § Foliations; source L15065 | (Foliations). (a) The collection of all k-dimensional affine subspaces of ℝ ^n parallel to ℝ ^k × 0 is a k-dimensional foliation of ℝ ^n (b) The collection of open rays of the form λ x : λ > 0 as x ranges over ℝ ^n setminus 0 is a 1-dimensional foliation of ℝ ^n setminus 0 (c) The collection of all spheres centered at 0 is an .n 1/-dimensional foliation of ℝ ^n setminus 0 (d) If M and N are connected smooth manifolds, the collection of subsets of the form M × q as q ranges over points in N forms a foliation of M × N , each of whose leaves is diffeomorphic to M . A different foliation of mathbb T ^2 is given by the collection of circles of the form p × mathbb S ^1 ( F i g . ~ 1 9 . 5 ( b ) ) . (e) If ˛ is a fixed real number, the images of all curves of the form gamma_θ (t) = (e ^i t, e ^i (α t + θ) ) as θ ranges over R form a 1-dimensional foliation of the torus (Fig. 19.5(c)). If α is rational, each leaf is an embedded circle: whereas if α is irrational, each leaf is dense (see Example 4.20 and Problem 4-4). (f) The collection of connected components of the curves in the .y; z/-plane defined by the following equations is a foliation of ℝ ^2 (Fig. 19.6(a)): beginarrayl l z = sec y + c, & c ∈ ℝ; y = (k + (1)/(2)) π , & k ∈ ℤ. endarray (g) If we revolve the curves of the previous example around the z-axis, we obtain a 2-dimensional foliation of ℝ ^3 in which some of the leaves are diffeomorphic to disks and some are diffeomorphic to cylinders (Fig. 19.6(b)). | `FC08-C04-U020` |
| `FC08-C19-U026` | **Proposition 19.19** | Ch. 19, § Foliations; source L15102 | Let F be a foliation on a smooth manifold M . The collection of tangent spaces to the leaves of F forms an involutive distribution on M . | — |
| `FC08-C19-U027` | **Theorem 19.21** | Ch. 19, § Foliations; source L15108 | (Global Frobenius Theorem). Let D be an involutive distribution on a smooth manifold M . The collection of all maximal connected integral manifolds of D forms a foliation of M . | — |

| `FC08-C19-U031` | **Definition/convention — left-invariant** | Ch. 19, § Lie Subalgebras and Lie Subgroups; source L15159 | Foliations have profound applications to the theory of Lie groups. Here we present two such applications; we will see many more in the next two chapters. Both rely on the following simple relationship between Lie subalgebras and distributions. A distribution D on a Lie group G is said to be left-invariant if it is invariant under every left translation. (Recall that this means d ( L _g ) ( D ) = D for each g ∈ G . ) | — |

| `FC08-C19-U033` | **Theorem 19.25** | Ch. 19, § Lie Subalgebras and Lie Subgroups; source L15171 | (Lie Subgroups Are Weakly Embedded). Every Lie subgroup is an integral manifold of an involutive distribution, and therefore is a weakly embedded submanifold. | — |

Then for each p ∈ S there is a neighborhood U of p such that for every φ ∈ C ^∞ ( S ∩ U ) , there exists a unique solution u ∈ C ^∞ ( U ) to the following overdetermined Cauchy problem: A _i u = f _i f o r i = 1, … , m,tag19.11 u big ∣ _S ∩ U = φ .tag19.12 | — |

| `FC08-C20-U001` | **Definition/convention — one-parameter subgroup of G** | Ch. 20, § One-Parameter Subgroups; source L15479 | A one-parameter subgroup of G is defined to be a Lie group homomorphism γ : ℝ → G , with R considered as a Lie group under addition. | `FC08-C20-U014` | **Theorem 20.12** | Ch. 20, § The Closed Subgroup Theorem; source L15757 | (Closed Subgroup Theorem). Suppose G is a Lie group and H ⊆ G is a subgroup that is also a closed subset of G. Then H is an embedded Lie subgroup. | — |

(b) Conversely, if G is simply connected, every antihomomorphism hat(θ) : Lie ( G ) → X.M / such that hat(θ) ( X ) is complete for each X ∈ Lie ( G ) is the infinitesima generator of a unique left G-action. | — |
| `FC08-C20-U022` | **Definition/convention — left g-action** | Ch. 20, § Left Actions; source L15973 | Because of this theorem, for a finite-dimensional Lie algebra g and a smooth manifold M; a left g-action on M is defined as an antihomomorphism from g to X ( M ) | — |
| `FC08-C20-U023` | **Theorem 20.19** | Ch. 20, § The Lie Correspondence; source L15983 | Suppose G and H are Lie groups with G simply connected, and let g and h be their Lie algebras. For any Lie algebra homomorphism φ : g h there is a unique Lie group homomorphism φ : G → H such that Phi _* = φ | — |
| `FC08-C20-U024` | **Corollary 20.20** | Ch. 20, § The Lie Correspondence; source L16019 | If G and H are simply connected Lie groups with isomorphic Lie algebras, then G and H are isomorphic. | — |
| `FC08-C20-U025` | **Theorem 20.21** | Ch. 20, § The Lie Correspondence; source L16025 | (The Lie Correspondence). There is a one-to-one correspondence between isomorphism classes of finite-dimensional Lie algebras and isomorphism classes of simply connected Lie groups, given by associating each simply connected Lie group with its Lie algebra. | — |
| `FC08-C20-U026` | **Definition/convention — left-invariant** | Ch. 20, § Lie’s Fundamental Theorems; source L16043 | The left translation map L _g : U → V is defined just as for ordinary Lie groups, and a vector field X ∈ X ( U ) is said to be left-invariant if d ( L _g ) _g ^prime ( X _g ^prime ) = X _m ( g , g ^prime ) for all g , g ^prime ∈ U such that m ( g , g ^prime ) ∈ U . Two local Lie groups ( U , e , m , i ) and ( U ^prime , e ^prime , m ^prime , i ^prime ) are said to be locally isomorphic if there is a diffeomorphism from a neighborhood of e in U to a neighborhood of e ^prime in U ^prime that takes e to e ^prime . , m to m ^prime , and i to i ^prime , whenever the respective operations are defined. A local (left or right) action of a local Lie group on an open subset W ⊆ ℝ ^n is defined like an ordinary action, except that g · x ( o r x · g ) is required to be defined only for ( g , x ) in a neighborhood of e × W in U × W . A coordinate neighborhood of the identity in any Lie group is a local Lie group, and any smooth action of a Lie group on a smooth manifold restricts to a local action on any sufficiently small coordinate neighborhood. | — |
| `FC08-C20-U027` | **Theorem 20.22** | Ch. 20, § Lie’s Fundamental Theorems; source L16045 | (The Fundamental Theorems of Sophus Lie). (i) FIRST FUNDAMENTAL THEOREM: The set of left-invariant vector fields on a local Lie group is a finite-dimensional Lie algebra under Lie bracket, and two local Lie groups with isomorphic Lie algebras are locally isomorphic. (ii) SECOND FUNDAMENTAL THEOREM: Given an open subset W ⊆ ℝ ^n , there is a one-to-one correspondence between smooth right actions of local Lie groups on W and finite-dimensional Lie subalgebras of X.W /. (iii) THIRD FUNDAMENTAL THEOREM: Given any finite-dimensional abstract Lie algebra g, there exists a local Lie group whose algebra of left-invariant vector fields is isomorphic to g . . It is an interesting exercise to see if you can adapt the techniques of this chapter to prove these theorems. (See Problem 20-19.) | — |
| `FC08-C20-U028` | **Lemma 20.23** | Ch. 20, § Normal Subgroups; source L16061 | Let G be a connected Lie group, and let H ⊆ G be a connected Lie subgroup. Let g and h denote the Lie algebras of G and H , respectively. Then H is normal in G if and only if (exp X) (exp Y) (exp (- X) ) ∈ H f o r a l l X ∈ g and Y ∈ h.tag20.13 | — |
| `FC08-C20-U029` | **Definition/convention — ideal in g** | Ch. 20, § Normal Subgroups; source L16077 | Our next goal is a theorem that expresses a deep relationship between Lie groups and their Lie algebras. If g is a Lie algebra, a linear subspace h ⊆ g is called an ideal in g if [ X , Y ] ∈ h whenever X ∈ g and Y ∈ h (see Problem 8-31). Because ideals are kernels of Lie algebra homomorphisms and normal subgroups are kernels of Lie group homomorphisms, it should not be surprising that there is a connection between ideals and normal subgroups. The key to analyzing this connection is the adjoint representation, which we study next. | — |
| `FC08-C20-U030` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 20, § The Adjoint Representation; source L16081 | Let G be a Lie group and g be its Lie algebra. For any g ∈ G , the conjugation map C _g : G → G given by bar(C) _g ( h ) = g h g ^- 1 is a Lie group homomorphism (see Example 7.4(f)). We let A d ( g ) = ( C _g ) _* : g → g denote its induced Lie algebra homomorphism. | `FC08-C07-U006` |
| `FC08-C20-U031` | **Proposition 20.24** | Ch. 20, § The Adjoint Representation; source L16083 | (The Adjoint Representation). If G is a Lie group with Lie algebra g, the map Ad G → GL ( g ) is a Lie group representation, called the adjoint representation of G . | — |
| `FC08-C20-U032` | **Proposition 20.25** | Ch. 20, § The Adjoint Representation; source L16097 | For any Lie algebra g, the map ad g → g l ( g ) is a Lie algebra representation, called the adjoint representation of g . | — |
| `FC08-C20-U033` | **Theorem 20.27** | Ch. 20, § The Adjoint Representation; source L16103 | Let G be a Lie group, let g be its Lie algebra, and let Ad G GL ( g ) be the adjoint representation of G. The induced Lie algebra representation A d _* : g → g l ( g ) is given by A d _* = a d | — |
| `FC08-C20-U034` | **Theorem 20.28** | Ch. 20, § Ideals and Normal Subgroups; source L16135 | (Ideals and Normal Subgroups). Let G be a connected Lie group, and suppose H ⊆ G is a connected Lie subgroup. Then H is a normal subgroup of G if and only if Lie.H / is an ideal in Lie.G/. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U001` | **Convention/notation — orbit space of the action** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16299 | Suppose we are given an action of a group G on a topological space M; which we write either as θ : G × M → M or as ( g , p ) ↦ g · p . (For definiteness, let us assume that G acts on the left; similar considerations apply to right actions.) Recall that the orbit of a point p ∈ M is the set of images of p under all elements of the group: G · p = g · p: g ∈ G . Define a relation on M by setting p ∼ q if there exists g ∈ G such that g · p = q This is an equivalence relation, whose equivalence classes are exactly the orbits of G in M . The set of orbits is denoted by M / G ; with the quotient topology it is called the orbit space of the action. It is of great importance to determine conditions under which an orbit space is a smooth manifold. It is important to be aware that some authors use the notation G backslash M for an orbit space determined by a left action of G on M; reserving M / G for one determined by a right action. We use only the latter notation, relying on the context, not the notation, to distinguish between the two cases. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U002` | **Lemma 21.1** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16311 | For any continuous action of a topological group G on a topological space M; the quotient map π : M → M / G is an open map. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U003` | **Example 21.2** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16323 | (Orbit Spaces of Smooth Lie Group Actions). (a) Let G be any group and let M be any smooth manifold. The trivial action (given by g · p = p for all g ∈ G and p ∈ M ) has one-point sets as orbits and M / G = M; so the orbit space is a smooth manifold for silly reasons. (b) The simplest nontrivial example to keep in mind is the action of ℝ ^k on ℝ ^k × ℝ ^n by translation in the ℝ ^k factor: v · ( x , y ) = ( v + x , y ) . The quotient map π : ℝ ^k × ℝ ^n → ℝ ^n is a smooth submersion. (c) The circle group mathbb S ^1 acts on the plane C by complex multiplication: z · w = z w The orbits are circles centered at the origin and the singleton 0 . Thus, the orbit space is not a manifold. (d) An even more dramatic example of how an orbit space can fail to be a manifold is given by the natural action of GL ( n , ℝ ) on ℝ ^n by matrix multiplication. This orbit space is not even Hausdorff, let alone a manifold. (e) The restriction of the natural action of GL ( n , ℝ ) on ℝ ^n to O ( n ) × ℝ ^n → ℝ ^n defines a smooth left action of O ( n ) on ℝ ^n . (If v and v ^prime are such vectors, complete v / ∣ v ∣ and v ^prime / ∣ v ^prime ∣ to orthonormal bases and let A and A ^prime be the orthogonal matrices whose columns are these orthonormal bases; then it is easy to check that A ^prime A ^- 1 takes v to v ^prime . ) As in (c), the orbit space is homeomorphic to [ 0 , ∞ ) (f) If we delete the origin from each of the three preceding examples, we obtain orbit spaces that are manifolds: the quotient of ℂ setminus 0 by mathbb S ^1 is homeomorphic to ℝ ^+ , as is the quotient of ℝ ^n setminus 0 by O ( n ) ; and the quotient of ℝ ^n setminus 0 by GL ( n , ℝ ) is a single point. (g) Further restricting the natural action to O ( n ) × mathbb S ^n - 1 → mathbb S ^n - 1 , we obtain an action of O ( n ) on mathbb S ^n - 1 . It is smooth by Corollary 5.30, because mathbb S ^n - 1 is an embedded submanifold of ℝ ^n . | `FC08-C05-U043` |
| `FC08-C21-U004` | **Example 21.3** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16341 | Let ˛ be an irrational number, and let R act on mathbb T ^2 = mathbb S ^1 × mathbb S ^1 by t · (w, z) = (e ^2 π i t w, e ^2 π i α t z ). This is a smooth action, and the arguments of Example 4.20 and Problem 4-4 can be adapted easily to show that it is free and has dense orbits. This means that the only saturated open subsets of mathbb T ^2 are varnothing and mathbb T ^2 , so the orbit space mathbb T ^2 / ℝ has the trivial topology. In particular, it is not Hausdorff and therefore not a manifold. | `FC08-C04-U020` |
| `FC08-C21-U005` | **Definition/convention — a proper action** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16349 | To avoid pathological cases such as these, we need to introduce one more restriction on our group actions. A continuous left action of a Lie group G on a manifold M is said to be a proper action if the map G × M → M × M given by ( g , p ) ↦ ( g · p , p ) is a proper map. Note that this is generally a weaker condition than requiring that the map G × M → M defining the action be a proper map (see Problem 21-1). | — |
| `FC08-C21-U006` | **Proposition 21.4** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16351 | If a Lie group acts continuously and properly on a manifold, then the orbit space is Hausdorff. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U007` | **Proposition 21.5** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16363 | (Characterizations of Proper Actions). Let M be a manifold, and let G be a Lie group acting continuously on M . The following are equivalent. (a) The action is proper. (b) I f ( p _i ) is a sequence in M and ( g _i ) is a sequence in G such that both ( p _i ) and ( g _i · p _i ) converge, then a subsequence of ( g _i ) converges. (c) For every compact subset K ⊆ M ; the set G _K = g ∈ G : ( g · K ) ∩ K ≠ emptyset is compact. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U008` | **Corollary 21.6** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16385 | Every continuous action by a compact Lie group on a manifold is proper. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U009` | **Proposition 21.7** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16389 | (Orbits of Proper Actions). Suppose θ is a proper smooth action of a Lie group G on a smooth manifold M . For any point p ∈ M ; the orbit map θ ^( p ) : G → M is a proper map, and thus the orbit G · p = θ ^( p ) ( G ) is closed in M . If in addition G _p = e , then θ ^( p ) is a smooth embedding, and the orbit is a properly embedded submanifold. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U010` | **Corollary 21.8** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16395 | If a Lie group G acts properly on a manifold M; then each orbit is a closed subset of M; and each isotropy group is compact. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U011` | **Example 21.9** | Ch. 21, § Quotients of Manifolds by Group Actions; source L16399 | We can see in two ways that the action of ℝ ^+ on ℝ ^n given by t · (x ^1, … , x ^n) = (t x ^1, … , t x ^n)tag21.1 is not proper: the isotropy group of the origin is all of ℝ ^+ , which is not compact; and the orbits of other points are open rays, which are not closed in ℝ ^n | — |
| `FC08-C21-U012` | **Theorem 21.10** | Ch. 21, § The Quotient Manifold Theorem; source L16411 | (Quotient Manifold Theorem). Suppose G is a Lie group acting smoothly, freely, and properly on a smooth manifold M . Then the orbit space M / G is a topological manifold of dimension equal to dim M dim G, and has a unique smooth structure with the property that the quotient map π M → M / G is a smooth submersion. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U013` | **Proposition 4.40** | Ch. 21, § Covering Manifolds; source L16468 | showed that any covering space of a smooth manifold is itself a smooth manifold. It is often important to know when a space covered b y a smooth manifold is itself a smooth manifold. To understand the answer to this question, we need to study the action on a covering space by the automorphism group of the covering. We saw in Chapter 7 (Proposition 7.23) that for any smooth covering π E → M ; the automorphism group is a discrete Lie group acting smoothly and freely on the covering space E. Below, we will show that the action is also proper. Before proceeding, it is useful to have an alternative characterization of properness for free actions of discrete Lie groups. | `FC08-C07-U033` |
| `FC08-C21-U014` | **Lemma 21.11** | Ch. 21, § Covering Manifolds; source L16470 | Suppose a discrete Lie group η acts continuously and freely on a manifold E. The action is proper if and only if the following conditions both hold: (i) Every point p ∈ E has a neighborhood U such that for each g ∈ T , ( g · U ) ∩ U = varnothing unless g = e (ii) H p , p ^prime ∈ E are not in the same η -orbit, there exist neighborhoods V of p and V ^prime of p ^prime such that ( g · V ) ∩ V ^prime = emptyset for all g ∈ T | — |
| `FC08-C21-U015` | **Definition/convention — properly discontinuous** | Ch. 21, § Covering Manifolds; source L16488 | In the literature, a continuous discrete group action satisfying condition (i) of this lemma (or conditions (i) and (ii), or something closely related to them) has traditionally been called properly discontinuous. We avoid this terminology, because its meaning is not universally agreed upon, and because it leads to such oxymoronic expressions as “continuous properly discontinuous actions.” Instead, following Allan Hatcher [Hat02], we call a continuous action satisfying (i) a covering space action, and simply refer to actions satisfying (i) and (ii) as “free and proper actions.” It can be shown that any covering space action on a topological space yields a covering map, though the quotient space need not be Hausdorff (see, e.g., [LeeTM, Chap. 12]). | — |
| `FC08-C21-U016` | **Proposition 21.12** | Ch. 21, § Covering Manifolds; source L16490 | Let M be a smooth manifold, and let π : E → M be a smooth covering map. With the discrete topology, the automorphism group A u t _π ( E ) acts smoothly, freely, and properly on E | — |
| `FC08-C21-U017` | **Theorem 21.13** | Ch. 21, § Covering Manifolds; source L16496 | Suppose E is a connected smooth manifold and η is a discrete Lie group acting smoothly, freely, and properly on E. Then the orbit space E / cal T is a topological manifold and has a unique smooth structure such that π E → E / cal T is a smooth normal covering map. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U018` | **Example 21.14** | Ch. 21, § Covering Manifolds; source L16514 | (Proper Discrete Group Actions). (a) The discrete Lie group ℤ ^n acts smoothly and freely on ℝ ^n by translations (Example 7.22(e)). To verify that this smooth structure on mathbb T ^n is the same as the one we defined previously (thinking of mathbb T ^n as the product manifold mathbb S ^1 × ·s × mathbb S ^1 ) , we just check that the covering map ℝ ^n → mathbb T ^n given by ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) ↦ ( e ^ 2 π i x ^1 , … , e ^ 2 π i x ^n ) is a local diffeomorphism with respect to the product smooth structure on mathbb T ^n , and makes the same identifications as the quotient map ℝ ^n → ℝ ^n / ℤ ^n ; thus Theorem 4.31 implies that ℝ ^n / ℤ ^n is diffeomorphic to mathbb T ^n (b) The two-element group ± 1 acts on mathbb S ^n by multiplication. By Theorem 4.31, therefore, mathbb S ^n / ± 1 is diffeomorphic to mathbb R P ^n | `FC08-C07-U031`, `FC08-C21-U007`, `FC08-C21-U017`, `FC08-C04-U033`, `FC08-C02-U015` |
| `FC08-C21-U019` | **Definition/convention — homogeneous G -space (** | Ch. 21, § Homogeneous Spaces; source L16522 | Some of the most interesting group action are transitive ones. A smooth manifold endowed with a transitive smooth action by a Lie group G is called a homogeneous G -space (or a homogeneous space or homogeneous manifold if it is not important to specify the group). | — |
| `FC08-C21-U020` | **Example 21.15** | Ch. 21, § Homogeneous Spaces; source L16528 | (Homogeneous Spaces). (a) The natural action of O ( n ) on mathbb S ^n - 1 is transitive, as we observed in Example 21.2(g). Thus, mathbb S ^n - 1 is a homogeneous space of O ( n ) (b) The natural action of O ( n ) restricts to a smooth action of SO ( n ) on mathbb S ^n - 1 . Thus for n ≥ 2 , mathbb S ^n - 1 is also a homogeneous space of SO ( n ) (c) The Euclidean group E ( n ) defined in Example 7.32 acts on ℝ ^n by rigid motions. Because any point in ℝ ^n can be taken to any other by a translation, E ( n ) acts transitively on ℝ ^n , so ℝ ^n is a homogeneous E ( n ) -space. (d) The group SL ( 2 , ℝ ) acts smoothly and transitively on the upper half-plane mathbb U = z ∈ ℂ : Im z > 0 by the formula ( beginarrayc c a & b c & d endarray ) · z = (a z + b)/(c z + d). The resulting complex-analytic diffeomorphisms from U to itself are called Möbius transformations. (e) For n ≥ 1 , the natural action of GL ( n , ℂ ) on ℂ ^n restricts to natural smooth actions of both U ( n ) and S U ( n ) on mathbb S ^2 n - 1 , identified with the set of unit vectors in ℂ ^n . The next exercise shows when these actions are transitive. | `FC08-C21-U003`, `FC08-C07-U044` |
| `FC08-C21-U021` | **Convention/notation — left coset of H** | Ch. 21, § Homogeneous Spaces; source L16548 | Next we describe a construction that can be used to generate a great number of homogeneous spaces, as quotients of Lie groups by closed subgroups. Let G be a Lie group and H ⊆ G be a Lie subgroup. A subset of G of the form g H = g h: h ∈ H for some g ∈ G is called a left coset of H . The left cosets form a partition of G _: and the quotient space determined by this partition (i.e., the set of left cosets with the quotient topology) is called the left coset space of G modulo H , and is denoted by G / H . Two elements g _1 , g _2 ∈ G are in the same left coset of H if and only if g _1 ^- 1 g _2 ∈ H ; in this case we write g _1 equiv g _2 .mod H / and say g _1 and g _2 are congruent modulo H . | — |
| `FC08-C21-U022` | **Theorem 21.17** | Ch. 21, § Homogeneous Spaces; source L16556 | (Homogeneous Space Construction Theorem). Let G be a Lie group and let H be a closed subgroup of G. The left coset space G / H is a topological manifold of dimension equal to dim G dim H , and has a unique smooth structure such that the quotient map π G → G / H is a smooth submersion. The left action of G on G=H given by g _1 · (g _2 H) = (g _1 g _2) Htag21.3 turns G / H into a homogeneous G-space. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U023` | **Theorem 21.18** | Ch. 21, § Homogeneous Spaces; source L16578 | (Homogeneous Space Characterization Theorem). Let G be a Lie group, let M be a homogeneous G-space, and let p be any point of M . The isotropy group G _p is a closed subgroup of G, and the map F : G / G _p → M defined by F ( g G _p ) = g · p is an equivariant diffeomorphism. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U024` | **Definition/convention — homogeneous space to be a quotient manifold of the form G / H** | Ch. 21, § Homogeneous Spaces; source L16600 | Because of this identification, some authors define a homogeneous space to be a quotient manifold of the form G / H , where G is a Lie group and H is a closed subgroup of G. One advantage of that definition is that it makes explicit the intimate relationship between the homogeneous space and the Lie group that acts transitively on it. However, a disadvantage is that it suggests there is something special about the identity coset of H (the image of e ∈ G under the quotient map), while the essence of homogeneous spaces is that every point “looks the same” as every other. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U025` | **Example 21.19** | Ch. 21, § Homogeneous Spaces; source L16604 | (Homogeneous Spaces Revisited). (a) Consider again the natural action of O.n/ on mathbb S ^n - 1 . Thus mathbb S ^n - 1 is diffeomorphic to the quotient manifold O ( n ) / O ( n - 1 ) (b) For the action of SO ( n ) on mathbb S ^n - 1 , the isotropy group is SO ( n - 1 ) , so mathbb S ^n - 1 is also diffeomorphic to SO ( n ) / SO ( n - 1 ) when n ≥ 2 (c) Because the Euclidean group E.n/ acts smoothly and transitively on ℝ ^n , and the isotropy group of the origin is the subgroup O ( n ) ⊆ E ( n ) , ℝ ^n is diffeomorphic to E ( n ) / O ( n ) (d) Next, consider the transitive action of SL.2; R/ on the upper half-plane by Möbius transformations. This subgroup is exactly SO ( 2 ) ⊆ SL ( 2 , ℝ ) , so the characterization theorem gives rise to a diffeomorphism mathbb U approx SL ( 2 , ℝ ) / SO ( 2 ) (e) By virtue of the result of Exercise 21.16, we obtain diffeomorphisms mathbb S ^2 n - 1 approx U ( n ) / U ( n - 1 ) for all n ≥ 1 and mathbb S ^2 n - 1 approx S U ( n ) / S U ( n - 1 ) for all n ≥ 2 . | — |
| `FC08-C21-U026` | **Theorem 21.20** | Ch. 21, § Sets with Transitive Group Actions; source L16622 | Suppose X is a set, and we are given a transitive action of a Lie group G on X such that for some point p ∈ X , the isotropy group G _p is closed in G. Then X has a unique smooth manifold structure with respect to which the given action is smooth. With this structure, dim X = dim G - dim G _p | — |
| `FC08-C21-U027` | **Example 21.21** | Ch. 21, § Sets with Transitive Group Actions; source L16628 | (Grassmannians). Let G _k ( ℝ ^n ) / denote the Grassmannian of k - dimensional subspaces of ℝ ^n as in Example 1.36. Therefore, G _k ( ℝ ^n ) has a unique smooth manifold structure making the natural GL ( n , ℝ ) action smooth. Problem 21-12 shows that this is the same structure we defined in Example 1.36. | `FC08-C01-U048` |
| `FC08-C21-U028` | **Example 21.22** | Ch. 21, § Sets with Transitive Group Actions; source L16636 | (Flag Manifolds). Let V be a real vector space of dimension n > 1 and let K = ( k _1 , … , k _m ) be a finite sequence of integers satisfying 0 < k _1 < ·s < k _m < n . A flag in V of type K is a nested sequence of linear subspaces S _1 ⊆ S _2 ⊆ ·s ⊆ S _m ⊆ V , with dim S _i = k _i for each i. The set of all flags of type K in V is denoted by F _K ( V ) . (For example, if K = ( k ) , then F _K ( V ) is the Grassmannian G _k ( V ) . ) It is not hard to show that GL.V / acts transitively on F _K ( V ) with a closed subgroup as isotropy group (see Problem 21-16), so F _K ( V ) has a unique smooth manifold structure making it into a homogeneous GL.V /-space. With this structure, F _K ( V ) is called a flag manifold. // | — |
| `FC08-C21-U029` | **Theorem 21.23** | Ch. 21, § Quotient Groups; source L16646 | (Quotient Theorem for Abstract Groups). Suppose G is any group and K ⊆ G is a normal subgroup. Then the set G / K of left cosets is a group with multiplication given by ( g _1 K ) ( g _2 K ) = ( g _1 g _2 ) K , and the projection π : G → G / K sending each element of G to its coset is a surjective homomorphism whose kernel is K. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U030` | **Theorem 21.24** | Ch. 21, § Quotient Groups; source L16648 | (First Isomorphism Theorem for Abstract Groups). If G and H are groups and F : G → H is a group homomorphism, then the kernel of F is a normal subgroup of G, the image of F is a subgroup of H , and F descends to a group isomorphism tilde(F) : G / Ker F → Im F . If F is surjective, then G= Ker F is isomorphic to H . | — |
| `FC08-C21-U031` | **Theorem 21.26** | Ch. 21, § Quotient Groups; source L16654 | (Quotient Theorem for Lie Groups). Suppose G is a Lie group and K ⊆ G is a closed normal subgroup. Then G / K is a Lie group, and the quotient map π : G → G / K is a surjective Lie group homomorphism whose kernel is K. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U032` | **Theorem 21.27** | Ch. 21, § Quotient Groups; source L16658 | (First Isomorphism Theorem for Lie Groups). If F : G → H is a Lie group homomorphism, then the kernel of F is a closed normal Lie subgroup of G , the image of F has a unique smooth manifold structure making it into a Lie subgroup of H , and F descends to a Lie group isomorphism tilde(F) : G / Ker F → Im F If F is surjective, then G= Ker F is smoothly isomorphic to H . | — |
| `FC08-C21-U033` | **Definition/convention — subgroup that** | Ch. 21, § Quotient Groups; source L16662 | These results are particularly significant when we apply them to a discrete subgroup, that is, a subgroup that is a discrete space in the subspace topology. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U034` | **Proposition 21.28** | Ch. 21, § Quotient Groups; source L16664 | Every discrete subgroup of a Lie group is a closed Lie subgroup of dimension zero. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U035` | **Theorem 21.29** | Ch. 21, § Quotient Groups; source L16674 | (Quotients of Lie Groups by Discrete Subgroups). _I f G is a connected Lie group and T ⊆ G is a discrete subgroup, then G / cal T is a smooth manifold and the quotient map π : G → G / cal Gamma is a smooth normal covering map. | — |
| `FC08-C21-U036` | **Example 21.30** | Ch. 21, § Quotient Groups; source L16678 | Let C be a cube centered at the origin in ℝ ^3 . The set of orientationpreserving orthogonal transformations of ℝ ^3 that take C to itself is a finite subgroup T ⊆ SO ( 3 ) , and SO ( 3 ) / T is a connected smooth 3-manifold with finite fundamental group and with mathbb S ^3 as its universal covering space (see Problem 21-21). Similar examples are obtained from the symmetry groups of other regular polyhedra, such as a regular tetrahedron, octahedron, dodecahedron, or icosahedron. // | — |

Thus every nonzero 2-covector on a 2- dimensional vector space is symplectic. Thus ! is nondegenerate, and so is a symplectic tensor. / It is interesting to consider the special case in which dim V = 2 . If ( V , ω ) is a symplectic vector space and S ⊆ V is any linear subspace, we define the symplectic complement of S , denoted by S ^perp , to be the subspace S ^perp = v ∈ V: ω (v, w) = 0 text for all w ∈ S . As the notation suggests, the symplectic complement is analogous to the orthogonal complement in an inner product space. Just as in the inner product case, the dimension of S ^perp is the codimension of S, as the next lemma shows. | — |

| `FC08-C22-U016` | **Definition/convention — a symplectic** | Ch. 22, § Symplectic Structures on Manifolds; source L16973 | Suppose ( M , ω ) is a symplectic manifold. | `FC08-C22-U017` | **Convention/notation — tautological 1-form** | Ch. 22, § The Canonical Symplectic Form on the Cotangent Bundle; source L16982 | The most important symplectic manifolds are total spaces of cotangent bundles, which carry canonical symplectic structures that we now define. We define τ ∈ Omega ^1 ( T * M ) (a 1-form on the total space of T * M ) ) by tau_(q, φ) = d pi_(q, φ) * φ .tag22.3 (See Fig. 22.1.) In other words, the value of τ at ( q , φ ) ∈ T * M is the pullback with respect to π of the covector φ itself. If v is a tangent vector in T _( q , φ ) ( T * M ) , then tau_(q, φ) (v) = φ big (d pi_(q, φ) (v) big). | — |

| `FC08-C22-U031` | **Example 22.18** | Ch. 22, § Hamiltonian Vector Fields; source L17254 | (The n-Body Problem). Consider n physical particles moving in space, and suppose their masses are m _1 , … , m _n . We assume further that the forces are conservative, which by the results of Chapter 11 is equivalent to the existence of a smooth function V ∈ C ^∞ ( Q ) (called the potential energy of the system) such that F = - d V Under the physically reasonable assumption that all of the masses are positive, the matrix M is positive definite, and thus can be interpreted as a (constantcoefficient) Riemannian metric on Q . For many purposes, an effective model of such a system is obtained by idealizing the particles as points in ℝ ^3 , which we denote by bf q _1 , … , bf q _n . (A typical example is gravitational forces.) If we denote the components of the net force on the kth particle by F _k ( q ) = ( F _k ^1 ( q ) , F _k ^2 ( q ) , F _k ^3 ( q ) ) , then Newton’s second law of motion asserts that the particles’ motion satisfies m _k ddot mathbf q _k ( t ) = mathbf F _k big ( mathbf q ( t ) big ) for each k , which translates into the 3 n × 3 n system of second-order ODEs beginarrayl m _k ddot q _k ^1 (t) = F _k ^1 big (q (t) big), m _k ddot q _k ^2 (t) = F _k ^2 big (q (t) big), m _k ddot q _k ^3 (t) = F _k ^3 big (q (t) big), k = 1, … , n. endarray (There is no implied summation in these equations.) This can be written in a more compact form if we relabel the 3n position coordinates as q ( t ) = ( q ^1 ( t ) , … , q ^3 n ( t ) ) and the 3n components of the forces as F ( q ) = ( F _1 ( q ) , … , F _3 n ( q ) ) , and let M = ( M _i j ) denote the 3 n × 3 n diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are ( m _1 , m _1 , m _1 , m _2 , m _2 , m _2 , … , m _n , m _n , m _n ) . It therefore defines a smooth bundle isomorphism hat(M) : T Q → T * Q . If we denote the natural coordinates on T Q by ( q ^i , v ^i ) and those on T * Q by ( q ^i , p _i ) , then M ( v , w ) = M _i j v ^i w ^j , and hat(M) has the coordinate representation (q ^i, p _i ) = hat(M) (q ^i, v ^i ) = (q ^i, M _i j v ^j ). Define a function E ∈ C ^∞ ( T * Q ) , called the total energy of the system, by E (q, p) = V (q) + K (p), where V is the potential energy introduced above, and K is the kinetic energy, defined by K (p) = (1)/(2) M ^i j p _i p _j. Since ( q ^i , p _i ) are Darboux coordinates on T * Q , a comparison of (22.14) with (22.11) shows that (22.14) is precisely Hamilton’s equations for the Hamiltonian flow of E. The fact that E is constant along the trajectories of its own Hamiltonian flow is known as the law of conservation of energy. | — |

(a) A function f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) is a conserved quantity if and only i f f , H = 0 (b) The infinitesimal symmetries of ( M , ω , H ) are precisely the symplectic vector fields V that satisfy V H = 0 (c) If θ is the flow of an infinitesimal symmetry and is a trajectory of the system, then for each s ∈ ℝ , θ _s ∘ γ is also a trajectory on its domain of definition. | — |
| `FC08-C22-U041` | **Theorem 22.22** | Ch. 22, § Poisson Brackets; source L17408 | (Noether’s Theorem). L e t ( cal M , ω , cal H ) be a Hamiltonian system. If f is any conserved quantity, then its Hamiltonian vector field is an infinitesimal symmetry. Conversely, if H _d R ^1 ( M ) = 0 , then each infinitesimal symmetry is the Hamiltonian vector field of a conserved quantity, which is unique up to addition of a function that is constant on each component of M . | — |
| `FC08-C22-U042` | **Theorem 22.23** | Ch. 22, § Hamiltonian Flowouts; source L17420 | (Hamiltonian Flowout Theorem). Suppose ( M , ω ) is a symplectic manifold, H ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) , η is an embedded isotropic submanifold of M that is contained in a single level set of H , and the Hamiltonian vector field X _H is nowhere tangent to η . If ι is a flowout from η along X _H , then ι is also isotropic and contained in the same level set of H . | — |
| `FC08-C22-U043` | **Definition/convention — contact form for H** | Ch. 22, § Contact Structures; source L17442 | Suppose M is a smooth manifold of odd dimension 2 n + 1 . A contact form on M is a nonvanishing smooth 1-form θ with the property that for each p ∈ M the restriction of d θ _p to the subspace Ker θ _p ⊆ T _p M is nondegenerate, which is to say it is a symplectic tensor. A contact structure on M is a smooth distribution H ⊆ T M of rank 2n with the property that any smooth local defining form θ for H is a contact form. A contact manifold is a smooth manifold M together with a contact structure on M . If .M; H / is a contact manifold, any (local or global) defining form for H is called a contact form for H . It was proved in 1971 by Jean Martinet [Mar71] that every oriented compact smooth 3-manifold admits a contact structure; but the question of which higher-dimensional manifolds admit contact structures is still unresolved. | — |
| `FC08-C22-U044` | **Proposition 22.24** | Ch. 22, § Contact Structures; source L17444 | A smooth 1-form θ on a ( 2 n + 1 ) -manifold is a contact form if and only if θ ∧ d θ ^n is nonzero everywhere on M; where d θ ^n represents the n-fold wedge product dθ ∧ ·s ∧ d θ | — |
| `FC08-C22-U045` | **Example 22.27** | Ch. 22, § Contact Structures; source L17452 | (Contact Forms). (a) On ℝ ^2 n + 1 with coordinates ( x ^1 , … , x ^n , y ^1 , … , y ^n , z ) , define a 1-form θ by θ = d z - sum_i = 1 ^n y ^i d x ^i,tag22.19 and let H ⊆ T ℝ ^2 n + 1 be the rank-2n distribution annihilated by θ . . It follows just as in Example 22.2 that d θ ∣ _H is nondegenerate, so θ is a contact form. (b) Let M be a smooth n-manifold, and define a smooth 1-form θ on the ( 2 n + 1 ) manifold ℝ × T * M by θ = d z - τ , where z is the standard coordinate on R and τ is the tautological 1-form on T * M . In terms of natural coordinates ( x ^i , ξ _i ) for T * M ; the form θ has the coordinate representation θ = d z - sum_i = 1 ^n xi_i d x ^i, so it is a contact form by the same argument as in part (a). (c) On ℝ ^2 n + 2 with coordinates ( x ^1 , … , x ^n + 1 , y ^1 , … , y ^n + 1 ) , consider the 1-form Theta = sum_i = 1 ^n + 1 (x ^i d y ^i - y ^i d x ^i ). We compute beginarrayl N lrcorner d Theta = 2 sum_i = 1 ^n + 1 (x ^i d y ^i - y ^i d x ^i ) = 2 Theta , T lrcorner d Theta = 2 sum_i = 1 ^n + 1 (x ^i d x ^i + y ^i d y ^i ) = d (∣ x ∣ ^2 + ∣ y ∣ ^2 ). endarray It follows that S _p ^perp is the common kernel of Theta _p and d ( ∣ x ∣ ^2 + ∣ y ∣ ^2 ) _p , which is Ker Theta _p ∩ T _p mathbb S ^2 n + 1 = Ker θ _p . | `FC08-C22-U003`, `FC08-C22-U007` |
| `FC08-C22-U046` | **Theorem 22.28** | Ch. 22, § Contact Structures; source L17496 | (The Reeb Field). Let ( M , H ) be a contact manifold, and suppose θ is a contact form for H . There is a unique vector field T ∈ X ( M ) , called the Reeb field of θ, that satisfies the following two conditions: T lrcorner d θ = 0, θ (T) = 1.tag22.20 | — |
| `FC08-C22-U047` | **Theorem 22.31** | Ch. 22, § Contact Structures; source L17514 | (Contact Darboux Theorem). Suppose θ is a contact form on a ( 2 n + 1 ) -dimensional manifold M . For each p ∈ M ; there are smooth coordinates ( x ^1 , … , x ^n , y ^1 , … , y ^n , z ) centered at p in which θ has the form (22.19). | — |
| `FC08-C22-U048` | **Proposition 22.32** | Ch. 22, § Contact Structures; source L17520 | Suppose ( M , H ) is a contact manifold and θ is a contact form for H . For any function f ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) , there is a unique vector field X _f called the contact Hamiltonian vector field of f , that satisfies θ ( X _f ) = f and ( X _f lrcorner d θ ) ∣ _H = - d f ∣ _H | — |
| `FC08-C22-U049` | **Definition/convention — contact vector field** | Ch. 22, § Contact Structures; source L17524 | Suppose ( M , H ) is a contact manifold. A smooth vector field X ∈ X ( M ) is called a contact vector field if its flow θ preserves the contact structure, in the sense that d ( θ _t ) _p ( H _p ) = H _ θ _t ( p ) for all ( t , p ) in the domain of θ . | — |
| `FC08-C22-U050` | **Theorem 22.33** | Ch. 22, § Contact Structures; source L17526 | (Characterization of Contact Vector Fields). H ( M , H ) is a contact manifold and θ is a contact form for H , then a smooth vector field on M is a contact vector field if and only if it is a contact Hamiltonian vector field. | — |
| `FC08-C22-U051` | **Definition/convention — Legendrian submanifold** | Ch. 22, § Contact Structures; source L17530 | If .M; H / is a contact manifold, a smooth submanifold S ⊆ M is said to be isotropic if T S ⊆ H , or equivalently if iota * θ = 0 for any contact form θ, where iota : S ↪ M is inclusion. If S ⊆ M is isotropic, then iota * d θ = d ( iota * θ ) = 0 . This implies that for each p ∈ S , the tangent space T _p S is an isotropic subspace of the symplectic vector space H _p , and thus its dimension cannot be any larger than n, where 2 n + 1 is the dimension of M . An isotropic submanifold of the maximum possible dimension n is called a Legendrian submanifold. | — |
| `FC08-C22-U052` | **Theorem 22.34** | Ch. 22, § Contact Structures; source L17534 | (Contact Flowout Theorem). Suppose .M; H / is a contact manifold, F ∈ C ^∞ ( M ) , η is an embedded isotropic submanifold of M that is contained in the zero set of F , and the contact Hamiltonian vector field X _F is nowhere tangent to η . If ι is a flowout from η along X _F , then ι is also isotropic and contained in the zero set of H . | — |
| `FC08-C22-U053` | **Definition/convention — Hamilton** | Ch. 22, § Nonlinear First-Order PDEs; source L17542 | We begin with a somewhat special case. A first-order partial differential equation that involves only the first derivatives of the unknown function but not the values of the function itself is called a Hamilton–Jacobi equation. Such an equation for an unknown function u ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) can be written in the form F (x ^1, … , x ^n, (∂ u)/(∂ x ^1) (x), … , (∂ u)/(∂ x ^n) (x) ) = 0,tag22.21 where F is a smooth function defined on an open subset of ℝ ^2 n . (The terminology regarding Hamilton–Jacobi equations is not universally agreed upon. Some authors reserve the term Hamilton–Jacobi equation for the special case of an equation of the form (∂ u)/(∂ x ^1) + H (x ^1, … , x ^n, (∂ u)/(∂ x ^2) (x), … , (∂ u)/(∂ x ^n) (x) ) = 0.tag22.22 The implicit function theorem shows that an equation of the general form (22.21) can be locally rewritten in this special form if and only if the partial derivative of F with respect to its ( n + 1 ) st variable is nonzero. On the other hand, other authors use the term eikonal equation to refer to any equation of the form (22.21). <!-- pages 601-723 --> We reserve that term for another special case to which it was originally applied; see Problem 22-24.) | — |
| `FC08-C22-U054` | **Definition/convention — solution to the equation** | Ch. 22, § Nonlinear First-Order PDEs; source L17560 | More generally, if M is a smooth manifold, a Hamilton–Jacobi equation on M is given by a smooth real-valued function F defined on an open subset W ⊆ T * M and a solution to the equation is a smooth real-valued function u defined on an open subset U ⊆ M such that the image of du lies in the zero set of F : F (x, d u (x)) = 0 text for all x ∈ U.tag22.23 | — |
| `FC08-C22-U055` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Ch. 22, § Nonlinear First-Order PDEs; source L17566 | (We write the covector d u _x ∈ T _x * M as ( x , d u ( x ) ) , in order to be more consistent with the coordinate representation (22.21) of the equation.) We are interested in solving a Cauchy problem for (22.23): given an embedded hypersurface S ⊆ M and a smooth function φ : S ℝ , we wish to find a smooth function u defined on a neighborhood of S in M and satisfying (22.23) together with the initial condition | — |
| `FC08-C22-U056` | **Definition/convention — noncharacteristic** | Ch. 22, § Nonlinear First-Order PDEs; source L17588 | The Cauchy problem (22.23)–(22.24) is said to be noncharacteristic if there exists a smooth section σ ∈ Gamma ( T * M ∣ _S ) satisfying (22.25)–(22.26), with the additional property that if ( x ^i ) are any local coordinates on M and ( x ^1 , … , x ^n , ξ _1 , … , ξ _n ) are the corresponding natural coordinates on T * M ; the following vector field along S is nowhere tangent to S : . A ^σ ∣ _x = (∂ F)/(∂ xi_1) (x, σ (s)) (∂)/(∂ x ^1) + … + (∂ F)/(∂ xi_n) (x, σ (s)) (∂)/(∂ x ^n).tag22.27 | — |
| `FC08-C22-U057` | **Theorem 22.35** | Ch. 22, § Nonlinear First-Order PDEs; source L17596 | (The Cauchy Problem for a Hamilton–Jacobi Equation). Suppose M is a smooth manifold, W ⊆ T * M is an open subset, F : W → ℝ is a smooth function, S ⊆ M is an embedded hypersurface, and ' S → ℝ is a smooth function. If the Cauchy problem (22.23)–(22.24) is noncharacteristic, then for each p ∈ S there is a smooth solution defined on some neighborhood of p in M . | — |
| `FC08-C22-U058` | **Example 22.36** | Ch. 22, § Nonlinear First-Order PDEs; source L17636 | (A Hamilton–Jacobi Equation). Consider the following Cauchy problem in the plane: (∂ u)/(∂ x) - ((∂ u)/(∂ y) ) ^2 = 0, u (0, y) = y ^2. Solving ( x , y ) = ( t , s - 4 s t ) for ( t , s ) and inserting into the formulas for ( ξ , η ) , we find that ι is the image of the following 1-form: α = (4 y ^2)/((1 - 4 x) ^2) d x + (2 y)/(1 - 4 x) d y. Since x is a defining function for S _ i , we can write σ = d ( y ^2 ) + f ( y ) d x = 2 y d y + f ( y ) dx and solve the equation F ( 0 , y , f ( y ) , 2 y ) = f ( y ) - ( 2 y ) ^2 = 0 to obtain f ( y ) = 4 y ^2 , and thus we can set σ ( y ) = 2 y d y + 4 y ^2 d x . The initial curve S can be parametrized by X ( s ) = ( 0 , s ) , and therefore the initial curve T ⊆ T * ℝ ^2 (the image of σ ) can be parametrized by tilde(X) ( s ) = ( 0 , s , 4 s ^2 , 2 s ) The Hamiltonian field of F is . In principle, we might have to add a constant to u to satisfy the initial condition, but in this case u ( 0 , y ) = y ^2 already, so this is the solution to our Cauchy problem. This is indeed a closed 1-form, and using the procedure sketched at the end of Chapter 11 we find that α = d u on the set ( x , y ) : x < 1 / 4 , where u (x, y) = (y ^2)/(1 - 4 x). | — |
| `FC08-C22-U059` | **Comparison/convention — higher-order jet bundles that give invariant representations** | Ch. 22, § General Nonlinear Equations; source L17682 | Finally, we show how the preceding method can be adapted to solve arbitrary firstorder PDEs by using contact geometry in place of symplectic geometry. For this purpose, we introduce one last geometric construction. If M is a smooth manifold, the 1-jet bundle of M is the smooth vector bundle J ^1 M = ℝ × T * M → M ; whose fiber at x ∈ M is ℝ × T _x * M . (It is the Whitney sum of a trivial R-bundle with T * M . ) If u M R is a smooth function, the 1-jet of u is the section j ^1 u : M → J ^1 M defined by j ^1 u ( x ) = ( u ( x ) , d u ( x ) ) . A point in the fiber of J ^1 M over x ∈ M can be viewed as a first-order Taylor polynomial at x of a smooth function on M; represented invariantly as the values of the function and its differential at x. (One can also define higher-order jet bundles that give invariant representations of higherorder Taylor polynomials. They are useful for studying higher-order PDEs, but we do not pursue them here.) | — |
| `FC08-C22-U060` | **Definition/convention — Legendrian** | Ch. 22, § General Nonlinear Equations; source L17684 | The canonical contact form on J ^1 M is the 1-form θ = d z - τ defined in Example 22.27(b). A smooth (local or global) section η M J ^1 M is said to be Legendrian if its image is a Legendrian submanifold of J ^1 M . ; or equivalently if η * θ = 0 . The next proposition is a contact analogue of Proposition 22.12. | `FC08-C22-U045`, `FC08-C22-U020` |
| `FC08-C22-U061` | **Proposition 22.37** | Ch. 22, § General Nonlinear Equations; source L17686 | Let M be a smooth manifold. A smooth local section of J 1 M is the 1-jet of a smooth function if and only if it is Legendrian. | — |
| `FC08-C22-U062` | **Comparison/convention — solution** | Ch. 22, § General Nonlinear Equations; source L17690 | The 1-jet bundle provides the most general setting in which to consider firstorder partial differential equations. If M is a smooth manifold, a first-order PDE for a function u M → ℝ can be viewed as a real-valued function F on the 1-jet bundle of M; and a solution is a function whose 1-jet takes its values in the zero set of F . | — |
| `FC08-C22-U063` | **Definition/convention — noncharacteristic** | Ch. 22, § General Nonlinear Equations; source L17702 | This problem is said to be noncharacteristic if there exists a smooth section σ ∈ Gamma ( T * M ∣ _S ) taking its values in W and satisfying σ (x) big ∣ _ T _x S = d φ (x) text for all x ∈ S,tag22.31 F (x, φ (x), σ (x)) = 0 text for all x ∈ S,tag22.32 and such that the following vector field along S is nowhere tangent to S: . A ^φ , σ ∣ _x = (∂ F)/(∂ xi_1) (x, φ (x), σ (x)) (∂)/(∂ x ^1) + … + (∂ F)/(∂ xi_n) (x, φ (x), σ (x)) (∂)/(∂ x ^n).tag22.33 | — |
| `FC08-C22-U064` | **Theorem 22.39** | Ch. 22, § General Nonlinear Equations; source L17720 | (The General First-Order Cauchy Problem). Suppose M is a smooth manifold, W ⊆ J ^1 M is an open subset, F : W → ℝ is a smooth function, S ⊆ M is an embedded hypersurface, and ' S → ℝ is a smooth function. If the Cauchy problem (22.29)–(22.30) is noncharacteristic, then for each p ∈ S there is a smooth solution on some neighborhood of p in M . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U001` | **Definition/convention — open subsets** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17896 | We begin with the definitions. Let X be a set. A topology on X is a collection T of subsets of X , called open subsets, satisfying | — |
| `FC08-CA-U002` | **Definition/convention — topological space** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17904 | A pair .X; T / consisting of a set X together with a topology T on X is called a topological space. Ordinarily, when the topology is understood, one omits mention of it and simply says “X is a topological space.” | — |
| `FC08-CA-U003` | **Definition/convention — neighborhood of p** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17910 | A neighborhood of p is an open subset containing p. Similarly, a neighborhood of the set S is an open subset containing S. (Be warned that some authors use the word “neighborhood” in the more general sense of a subset containing an open subset containing p or S.) | — |
| `FC08-CA-U004` | **Definition/convention — closed** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17912 | S is said to be closed if X setminus S is open (where X setminus S denotes the set difference x ∈ X : x not ∈ S ) . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U005` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17914 | The interior of S , denoted by Int S, is the union of all open subsets of X contained in S. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U006` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17916 | The exterior of S , denoted by Ext S, is the union of all open subsets of X contained in X setminus S | — |
| `FC08-CA-U007` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17918 | The closure of S , denoted by bar(S) , , is the intersection of all closed subsets of X containing S. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U008` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17920 | The boundary of S , denoted by @S, is the set of all points of X that are in neither Int S nor Ext S. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U009` | **Definition/convention — an isolated point of S** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17922 | A point p ∈ S is said to be an isolated point of S if p has a neighborhood U ⊆ X such that U ∩ S = p | — |
| `FC08-CA-U010` | **Definition/convention — a limit point of S** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17924 | A point p ∈ X (not necessarily in S ) is said to be a limit point of S if every neighborhood of p contains at least one point of S other than p. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U011` | **Definition/convention — dense in X** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17926 | S is said to be dense in X if bar(S) = X , or equivalently if every nonempty open subset of X contains at least one point of S . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U012` | **Definition/convention — nowhere dense in X** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17928 | S is said to be nowhere dense in X if Sx contains no nonempty open subset. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U013` | **Definition/convention — next** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17930 | The most important concepts of topology are continuous maps and convergent sequences, which we define next. Let X and Y be topological spaces. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U014` | **Definition/convention — continuous** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17932 | A map F : X → Y is said to be continuous if for every open subset U ⊆ Y , the preimage F ^- 1 ( U ) is open in X . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U015` | **Definition/convention — homeomorphism** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17934 | A continuous bijective map F : X → Y with continuous inverse is called a homeomorphism. If there exists a homeomorphism from X to Y , we say that X and Y are homeomorphic. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U016` | **Definition/convention — a local homeomorphism** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17936 | A continuous map F : X → Y is said to be a local homeomorphism if every point p ∈ X has a neighborhood U ⊆ X such that F ( U ) is open in Y and F restricts to a homeomorphism from U to F ( U ) | — |
| `FC08-CA-U017` | **Convention/notation — source terminology/construction** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17938 | Given a sequence ( p _i ) _i = 1 ^∞ of points in X and a point p ∈ X , the sequence is said to converge to p if for every neighborhood U of p _: , there exists a positive integer N such that p _i ∈ U for all i ≥ N . In this case, we write p _i → p or scriptstyle operatorname* l i m _i → ∞ p _i = p | — |
| `FC08-CA-U018` | **Example A.4** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17958 | (Discrete Spaces). If X is an arbitrary set, the discrete topology on X is the topology defined by declaring every subset of X to be open. Any space that has the discrete topology is called a discrete space. // | — |
| `FC08-CA-U019` | **Example A.5** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17960 | (Metric Spaces). A metric space is a set M endowed with a distance function (also called a metric) d M × M → ℝ (where R denotes the set of rea numbers) satisfying the following properties for all x , y , z ∈ M (i) POSITIVITY: d ( x , y ) ≥ 0 , with equality if and only if x = y (ii) SYMMETRY: d ( x , y ) = d ( y , x ) (iii) TRIANGLE INEQUALITY: d ( x , z ) ≤ d ( x , y ) + d ( y , z ) If M is a metric space, x ∈ M ; and r > 0 , the open ball of radius r around x is the set B _r (x) = y ∈ M: d (x, y) < r , and the closed ball of radius r is bar(B) _r (x) = y ∈ M: d (x, y) ≤ r . The metric topology on M is defined by declaring a subset S ⊆ M to be open if for every point x ∈ S , there is some r > 0 such that B _r ( x ) ⊆ S | — |
| `FC08-CA-U020` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17986 | If S is a nonempty bounded subset of M; the diameter of S is the number diam S = operatorname* s u p d ( x , y ) : x , y ∈ S | — |
| `FC08-CA-U021` | **Definition/convention — source terminology/construction** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17988 | A sequence of points ( x _i ) _i = 1 ^∞ in M is a Cauchy sequence if for every ε > 0 , there exists an integer N such that i , j ≥ N implies d ( x _i , x _j ) < ε | — |
| `FC08-CA-U022` | **Definition/convention — complete** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17990 | A metric space M is said to be complete if every Cauchy sequence in M converges to a point of M . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U023` | **Example A.6** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17992 | Example A.6 (Euclidean Spaces). For each integer n ≥ 1 , the set ℝ ^n of ordered n-tuples of real numbers is called n-dimensional Euclidean space. We denote a point in ℝ ^n by ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) , ( x ^i ) , or x; the numbers x ^i are called the components or coordinates of x. (When n is small, we often use more traditional names such as ( x , y , z ) for the coordinates.) Notice that we write the coordinates of a point ( x ^1 , … , x ^n ) ∈ ℝ ^n with superscripts, not subscripts as is usually done in linear algebra and calculus books, so as to be consistent with the Einstein summation convention, explained in Chapter 1. By convention, ℝ ^0 is the one-element set 0 . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U024` | **Definition/convention — Euclidean norm** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L17994 | For each x ∈ ℝ ^n , the Euclidean norm of x is the nonnegative real number ∣ x ∣ = sqrt (x ^1 ) ^2 + ·s + (x ^n ) ^2, | — |
| `FC08-CA-U025` | **Definition/convention — Euclidean distance** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L18000 | and for x; y ∈ ℝ ^n , the Euclidean distance function is defined by d (x, y) = ∣ x - y ∣. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U026` | **Convention/notation — Euclidean topology** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L18006 | This distance function turns ℝ ^n into a complete metric space. The resulting metric topology on ℝ ^n is called the Euclidean topology. // | — |
| `FC08-CA-U027` | **Example A.7** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L18008 | (Complex Euclidean Spaces). We also sometimes have occasion to work with complex Euclidean spaces. We consider the set C of complex numbers, as a set, to be simply ℝ ^2 , with the complex number x + i y corresponding to ( x , y ) ∈ ℝ ^2 . For any positive integer n, the n-dimensional complex Euclidean space is the set ℂ ^n of ordered n-tuples of complex numbers. It becomes a topological space when identified with ℝ ^ 2 bar(n) via the correspondence (x ^1 + i y ^1, … , x ^n + i y ^n ) rightarrow (x ^1, y ^1, … , x ^n, y ^n ).tag | — |
| `FC08-CA-U028` | **Example A.8** | Appendix A, § Topological Spaces; source L18014 | (Subsets of Euclidean Spaces). Every subset of ℝ ^n or ℂ ^n becomes a metric space, and thus a topological space, when endowed with the Euclidean metric. Whenever we mention such a subset, it is always assumed to have this metric topology unless otherwise specified. It is a complete metric space if and only if it is a closed subset of ℝ ^n . Here are some standard subsets of Euclidean spaces that we work with frequently: The unit interval is the subset I ⊆ ℝ defined by I = [ 0, 1 ] = x ∈ ℝ: 0 ≤ x ≤ 1 . The (open) unit ball of dimension n is the subset mathbb B ^n ⊆ ℝ ^n defined by mathbb B ^n = x ∈ ℝ ^n: ∣ x ∣ < 1 . The closed unit ball of dimension n is the subset overline mathbb B ^n ⊆ ℝ ^n defined by overline mathbb B ^n = x ∈ ℝ ^n: ∣ x ∣ ≤ 1 . The terms (open) unit disk and closed unit disk are commonly used for mathbb B ^2 and overline mathbb B ^2 , respectively. For n ≥ 0 , the (unit) n-sphere is the subset mathbb S ^n ⊆ ℝ ^n + 1 defined by mathbb S ^n = x ∈ ℝ ^n + 1: ∣ x ∣ = 1 . Sometimes it is useful to think of an odd-dimensional sphere mathbb S ^2 n + 1 as a subset of ℂ ^n + 1 , by means of the usual identification of ℂ ^n + 1 with ℝ ^2 n + 2 The (unit) circle is the 1-sphere mathbb S ^1 , considered either as a subset of ℝ ^2 or as a subset of C. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U029` | **Definition/convention — a Hausdorff space** | Appendix A, § Hausdorff Spaces; source L18052 | To avoid pathological cases like this, which result when X does not have sufficiently many open subsets, we often restrict our attention to topological spaces satisfying the following special condition. A topological space X is said to be a Hausdorff space if for every pair of distinct points p , q ∈ X , there exist disjoint open subsets U , V ⊆ X such that p ∈ U and q ∈ V . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U030` | **Definition/convention — a basis for the topology of X** | Appendix A, § Bases and Countability; source L18060 | Suppose X is a topological space. A collection B of open subsets of X is said to be a basis for the topology of X (plural: bases) if every open subset of X is the union of some collection of elements of B . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U031` | **Definition/convention — topology generated by B** | Appendix A, § Bases and Countability; source L18062 | More generally, suppose X is merely a set, and B is a collection of subsets of X satisfying the following conditions: (i) beginarray r X = bigcup _B ∈ B B . endarray (ii) I f ~ B _1 , B _2 ∈ B and x ∈ B _1 ∩ B _2 , then there exists B _3 ∈ B such that x ∈ B _3 ⊆ B _1 ∩ B _2 . Then the collection of all unions of elements of B is a topology on X, called the topology generated by B , and B is a basis for this topology. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U032` | **Definition/convention — topology generated by B** | Appendix A, § Bases and Countability; source L18068 | Then the collection of all unions of elements of B is a topology on X, called the topology generated by B , and B is a basis for this topology. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U033` | **Definition/convention — neighborhood basis at p** | Appendix A, § Bases and Countability; source L18070 | If X is a topological space and p ∈ X a neighborhood basis at p is a collection B _p of neighborhoods of p such that every neighborhood of p contains at least one B ∈ B _p . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U034` | **Definition/convention — countably infinite** | Appendix A, § Bases and Countability; source L18072 | A set is said to be countably infinite if it admits a bijection with the set of positive integers, and countable if it is finite or countably infinite. A topological space X is said to be first-countable if there is a countable neighborhood basis at each point, and second-countable if there is a countable basis for its topology. Since a countable basis for X contains a countable neighborhood basis at each point, secondcountability implies first-countability. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U035` | **Definition/convention — sequence** | Appendix A, § Bases and Countability; source L18074 | The next lemma expresses the most important properties of first-countable spaces. To say that a sequence is eventually in a subset means that all but finitely many terms of the sequence are in the subset. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U036` | **Lemma A.12** | Appendix A, § Bases and Countability; source L18076 | (Sequence Lemma). Let X be a first-countable space, let A ⊆ X be any subset, and let x ∈ X (a) x ∈ bar(A) if and only if x is a limit of a sequence of points in A. (b) x ∈ Int A if and only if every sequence in X converging to x is eventually in A. (c) A is closed in X if and only if A contains every limit of every convergent sequence of points in A. (d) A is open in X if and only if every sequence in X converging to a point of A is eventually in A. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U037` | **Definition/convention — open cover** | Appendix A, § Bases and Countability; source L18092 | One of the most important properties of second-countable spaces is expressed in the following proposition. Let X be a topological space. A cover of X is a collection U of subsets of X whose union is X; it is called an open cover if each of the sets in U is open. A subcover of U is a subcollection of U that is still a cover. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U038` | **Proposition A.16** | Appendix A, § Bases and Countability; source L18094 | Let X be a second-countable topological space. Every open cover of X has a countable subcover. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U039` | **Definition/convention — relative topology) by declaring a subset U** | Appendix A, § Subspaces; source L18102 | Probably the simplest way to obtain new topological spaces from old ones is by taking subsets of other spaces. If X is a topological space and S ⊆ X is an arbitrary subset, we define the subspace topology on S (sometimes called the relative topology) by declaring a subset U ⊆ S to be open in S if and only if there exists an open subset V X such that U = V ∩ S A subset of S that is open or closed in the subspace topology is sometimes said to be relatively open or relatively closed in S , to make it clear that we do not mean open or closed as a subset of X. Any subset of X endowed with the subspace topology is said to be a subspace of X . Whenever we treat a subset of a topological space as a space in its own right, we always assume that it has the subspace topology unless otherwise specified. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U040` | **Definition/convention — topological embedding** | Appendix A, § Subspaces; source L18104 | If X and Y are topological spaces, a continuous injective map F : X → Y is called a topological embedding if it is a homeomorphism onto its image F ( X ) ⊆ Y in the subspace topology. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U041` | **Proposition A.17** | Appendix A, § Subspaces; source L18108 | (Properties of the Subspace Topology). Let X be a topological space and let S be a subspace of X. (a) CHARACTERISTIC PROPERTY: If Y is a topological space, a map F Y S is continuous if and only if the composition iota _S ∘ F : Y → X is continuous, where iota _S : S ↪ X is the inclusion map (the restriction of the identity map of X to S ). (b) The subspace topology is the unique topology on S for which the characteristic property holds. (c) A subset K ⊆ S is closed in S if and only if there exists a closed subset L ⊆ X such that K = L ∩ S (d) The inclusion map iota _S : S ↪ X is a topological embedding. (e) If Y is a topological space and F : X → Y is continuous, then F ∣ _S : S → Y (the restriction of F to S ) is continuous. (f) If B is a basis for the topology of X , then B _S = B ∩ S : B ∈ B is a basis for the subspace topology on S. (g) If X is Hausdorff, then so is S . (h) If X is first-countable, then so is S . (i) If X is second-countable, then so is S . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U042` | **Lemma A.19** | Appendix A, § Subspaces; source L18132 | (Continuity Is Local). Continuity is a local property, in the following sense: if F X Y is a map between topological spaces such that every poin p ∈ X has a neighborhood U on which the restriction F ∣ _U is continuous, then F is continuous. | — |
| `FC08-CA-U043` | **Lemma A.20** | Appendix A, § Subspaces; source L18134 | (Gluing Lemma for Continuous Maps). Let X and Y be topological spaces, and suppose one of the following conditions holds: (a) B _1 , … , B _n are finitely many closed subsets of X whose union is X. (b) B _i _i ∈ A is a collection of open subsets of X whose union is X. Suppose that for all i we are given continuous maps F _i : B _i → Y that agree on overlaps: F _i ∣ _ B _i ∩ B _j = F _j ∣ _ B _i ∩ B _j . Then there exists a unique continuous map F : X → Y whose restriction to each B _i is equal to F _i . | — |
| `FC08-CA-U044` | **Construction/definition — source terminology/construction** | Appendix A, § Product Spaces; source L18148 | Next we consider finite products of topological spaces.
